**6.1.1**  Having heard Hanūmān's words spoken exactly as they were, Rāma, filled with joy, replied with the following words.

**6.1.2**  Hanūmān has accomplished an exceedingly great and difficult deed on earth — one that no other being on this earth could even conceive of in mind.

**6.1.3**  I see no one who could cross the great ocean, save Garuḍa, or the Wind, or Hanūmān.

**6.1.4**  The city of Laṅkā, which is impregnable to gods, dānavas, yakṣas, gandharvas, nāgas, and rākṣasas, and which is well-guarded by Rāvaṇa —

**6.1.5**  — who, having entered it by courage, could emerge alive? Who could enter that city so very hard to assault, guarded by rākṣasas, if he were not equal to Hanūmān in valor and might?

**6.1.6**  Hanūmān has performed a great service for his master Sugrīva, deploying his own strength in a manner befitting his valor.

**6.1.7**  A servant who, when assigned by his master to a difficult task, accomplishes it with devotion — such a one is called a foremost among men.

**6.1.8**  A servant who, though capable, qualified, and charged with the king's task, yet does not perform it — such a one is called the lowest of men.

**6.1.9**  Hanūmān, appointed to that charge, has fulfilled his duty; he has not made himself small, and Sugrīva too is gratified.

**6.1.10**  I, scion of the Raghu lineage, and the mighty Lakṣmaṇa have today been saved — rightfully so — by the sight of Vaidehī.

**6.1.11**  Yet this thought afflicts my wretched heart anew: that here I can render no fitting gift to him who has brought such glad tidings.

**6.1.12**  This embrace of Hanūmān is my all — given by me to that great soul, availing myself of this moment as the only gift I have.

**6.1.13**  The search for Sītā has been accomplished — that much is well done. Yet now that we have come to the ocean, my heart is lost once more.

**6.1.14**  How shall the monkeys reach the southern shore of this vast, hard-to-cross sea? That is the question that weighs upon my mind.

**6.1.15**  Even though Vaidehī's news has been reported to me, what still remains unsolved is the matter of the monkeys crossing the ocean.

**6.1.16**  Having spoken thus, Rāma the destroyer of foes, agitated by grief, the long-armed hero, fell into deep contemplation, Hanūmān beside him.

**6.2.1**  Then glorious Sugrīva spoke words that dispel grief to Rāma, son of Daśaratha, who was scorched by sorrow.

**6.2.2**  Why do you afflict yourself, O hero, like any ordinary man? Do not be so; abandon your anguish as an ingrate abandons friendship.

**6.2.3**  I see no occasion for your anguish, O Rāghava, now that news of her has been received and the enemy's abode has been discovered.

**6.2.4**  You are steadfast, versed in the scriptures, wise, and learned, O Rāghava. Abandon this wicked thought that, like a foe to oneself, corrupts your purpose.

**6.2.5**  We shall cross the ocean, which teems with great crocodiles, scale Laṅkā, and slay your enemy.

**6.2.6**  For the man who has lost his energy, who is despondent, whose soul is overwhelmed by grief — all his purposes founder, and calamity overtakes him.

**6.2.7**  All our monkey chieftains are brave and capable; for your sake they have roused their courage even to the point of entering fire itself.

**6.2.8**  By their exuberance I know it, and my conviction in this is firm: I shall bring Sītā back by valor, having slain the enemy.

**6.2.9**  As to how a bridge may be built here, and how we may behold that city of the king of rākṣasas — give your command to that effect, O Rāghava.

**6.2.10**  Having seen that city of Laṅkā seated on the peak of Trikūṭa, consider Rāvaṇa already slain in battle — take that as certain from the very sight.

**6.2.11**  Once a bridge is built across the sea and Laṅkā is close at hand, and once the whole army has crossed — consider it conquered.

**6.2.12**  These monkeys who can assume any form are heroes in battle. Enough then of this faint-hearted thinking, O king, that destroys all purposes.

**6.2.13**  In this world grief diminishes a man's heroism. What a man must do is hold fast to courage — especially men who are heroes and great souls like yourself.

**6.2.14**  Whether in ruin or in loss, grief destroys all aims. But you are the foremost among the wise, thoroughly versed in the meaning of all the scriptures.

**6.2.15**  You ought to conquer the enemy here, together with ministers like me. I see no one in all three worlds, O Rāghava —

**6.2.16**  — who could stand before you in battle once you have taken up your bow. With the monkeys engaged, your purpose shall not fail.

**6.2.17**  You shall see Sītā soon, once you have crossed the boundless sea. Enough of clinging to grief — take hold of wrath instead, O lord of the earth.

**6.2.18**  Sluggish kṣatriyas who take no action — all of them tremble before the fierce. And as for crossing the dreadful lord of rivers, the ocean —

**6.2.19**  — having come here with us, consider the matter with your keen intellect. For these monkeys who can take any form are heroes in battle.

**6.2.20**  They will scatter those enemies with showers of rocks and trees. Somehow we do see our way across the abode of Varuṇa.

**6.2.21**  What more need be said at length? You shall be victorious in every way.

**6.3.1**  Having heard Sugrīva's well-reasoned speech, Kākutstha, knower of the highest truth, accepted it and then spoke to Hanūmān.

**6.3.2**  Whether by the swift building of a bridge or by drying up the ocean, I am fully capable in every way of crossing this sea.

**6.3.3**  How many fortifications does the fortified city of Laṅkā have? Tell me that. I wish to know all of it as if seeing it with my own eyes, O monkey.

**6.3.4**  The size of the army, the construction of the gates and fortifications, the defensive measures of Laṅkā, and the dwellings of the rākṣasas —

**6.3.5**  — all that you saw freely and exactly in Laṅkā, recount to me truly. For you are in every way an expert.

**6.3.6**  Having heard Rāma's words, Hanūmān, son of the Wind, foremost among those skilled in speech, again spoke to Rāma.

**6.3.7**  "Let me be heard; I shall relate everything, regarding the arrangement of the fortifications, how the city of Laṅkā is protected and how it is guarded by armed forces."

**6.3.8**  "The surpassing wealth of Laṅkā, the dreadfulness of the ocean, the arrangement of the army's divisions, and the disposition of the war-vehicles —"

**6.3.9**  "Laṅkā is jubilant and cheerful, crowded with rutting elephants, large, filled with chariots, and thronged with bands of rākṣasas."

**6.3.10**  "Her four vast gates are of great size, fitted with strongly fastened panels and great iron crossbars."

**6.3.11**  "On the ramparts stand great and powerful stone-throwing machines, by which any enemy force that arrives there is driven back."

**6.3.12**  "At the gates, dreadful śataghnis — heavy iron-bolt engines — forged of black iron, sharp and burnished, are ranged by the hundreds, mounted by warrior bands of rākṣasas."

**6.3.13**  "She has a great golden rampart, hard to assail, whose interstices are inlaid with gems, coral, vaidūrya, and pearls."

**6.3.14**  "All around are great and terrifying moats, auspicious and vast, filled with cool water, fathomless, and inhabited by crocodiles and fish."

**6.3.15**  "Over those moats are four very long drawbridges fitted with many great and tightly jointed mechanisms."

**6.3.16**  "Those drawbridges are raised for defense when an enemy army approaches, and by those mechanisms they are dropped all around into the moats."

**6.3.17**  "But one bridge is immovable and strong, exceedingly firm and massive, adorned with many golden pillars and parapets."

**6.3.18**  "Rāvaṇa himself, O Rāma, is gifted by nature with martial spirit — ever roused and vigilant in the inspection of his forces."

**6.3.19**  "The city of Laṅkā is self-sustaining, a fortress of the gods, fearsome; she has all four kinds of natural fortification: riverine, mountainous, forest-based, and artificial."

**6.3.20**  "She stands on the far shore of the ocean whose opposite shore is far away, O Rāghava; there is no sea-road to her, and she is inaccessible from every direction."

**6.3.21**  "That fortress-city is built on the summit of a mountain, comparable to the city of the gods, filled with horses and elephants; Laṅkā is supremely difficult to conquer."

**6.3.22**  "Iron crossbars, śataghnis, and various war-engines adorn the city of Laṅkā belonging to Rāvaṇa, that evil-souled one."

**6.3.23**  "At the western gate ten thousand rākṣasas are stationed — all of them wielding spears and hard to assail, foremost swordsmen."

**6.3.24**  "A hundred thousand rākṣasas are stationed at the southern gate — warriors with a four-fold army, and peerless combatants there as well."

**6.3.25**  "A million rākṣasas are posted at the eastern gate — all bearing shields and swords, and all of them skilled in every weapon."

**6.3.26**  "Ten million rākṣasas are stationed at the northern gate — charioteers and cavalry, all of them nobly born and highly honored."

**6.3.27**  "At the central garrison a hundred times a hundred thousand — that is, more than a crore — of yātudhānas, hard to assail, are posted."

**6.3.28**  "Those drawbridges I have broken, the moats I have filled up, the city of Laṅkā I have burned, and the ramparts I have shattered."

**6.3.29**  "Whatever route we take to cross the abode of Varuṇa, let it be settled among the monkeys that the city of Laṅkā is as good as slain."

**6.3.30**  "Aṅgada, Dvivida, Mainda, Jāmbavān, Panasa, Nala, and Nīla the general — with these remaining forces, what more do you need?"

**6.3.31**  "Leaping across, they will go to that great city of Rāvaṇa — ramparts, mansions and all — and bring back the Maithilī."

**6.3.32**  "Therefore, command quickly the full muster of the army and, choosing an auspicious hour, authorize the departure."

**6.4.1**  Having heard Hanūmat's words duly and in proper order, the greatly resplendent Rāma, whose valour is true, then spoke.

**6.4.2**  "That city of Laṅkā belonging to that fearsome rākṣasa, which you describe to me — I shall swiftly destroy it. This I say to you as truth."

**6.4.3**  "In this very moment, O Sugrīva, I choose to set out on the march. The hour is auspicious for victory; the sun has reached the meridian."

**6.4.4**  "Today is Uttara-Phālgunī; tomorrow the moon will be joined with Hastā. Let us set forth, O Sugrīva, encompassed by all our army divisions."

**6.4.5**  "And the auspicious omens that are manifesting before me — slaying Rāvaṇa, I shall bring back Sītā, the daughter of Janaka."

**6.4.6**  "Indeed, this upper eye of mine, twitching as it is, seems to herald my victory and the fulfilment of my heart's desire."

**6.4.7**  "Let Nīla go in advance of this army to reconnoitre the route, attended by a hundred thousand swift monkeys."

**6.4.8**  "O Nīla, O general, lead the army swiftly by a path furnished with fruits and roots, with cool woodland streams, and abounding in honey."

**6.4.9**  "Wicked rākṣasas may defile the roots, fruits, and water along the route; guard against them, remaining ever vigilant."

**6.4.10**  "Forest-dwellers lurking in ravines, woodland strongholds, and forests might spring forward and scout out the slaughter of our army by the enemy."

**6.4.11**  "Let the mighty lion-monkeys draw forth the fearsome vanguard, which resembles the flood of the ocean, by the hundreds and by the thousands."

**6.4.12**  "Let Gaja, who resembles a mountain, and the great-spirited Gavaya, and Gavākṣa march in front, like lordly bulls at the head of a herd of cattle."

**6.4.13**  "Let Ṛṣabha, the lord of leaping monkeys, that bull among monkeys, guard the right flank of the monkey host as it advances."

**6.4.14**  "Let swift Gandhamādana — fierce as a rutting elephant — take up his post guarding the left flank of the monkey army."

**6.4.15**  "I shall march in the midst of the army, gladdening the flood of warriors, mounted upon Hanūmat as Indra rides upon Airāvata."

**6.4.16**  "Let Lakṣmaṇa, who equals Death, advance with Aṅgada — as the lord of beings and the lord of wealth ride with the universal sovereign."

**6.4.17**  "And let those three — Jāmbavān, Suṣeṇa, and the monkey Vegadarśī, the mighty king of bears — protect the flanks of the army."

**6.4.18**  Having heard the words of Rāghava, Sugrīva, lord of the army and bull among monkeys, assigned posts to the exceedingly valiant monkeys.

**6.4.19**  Then all those eager-for-battle monkey hosts swiftly leapt up from caves and mountain peaks and bounded forward.

**6.4.20**  Then righteous Rāma, honoured by the monkey king and by Lakṣmaṇa, set forth with his army toward the southern quarter.

**6.4.21**  He marched then encompassed by monkeys resembling elephants, in their hundreds, their hundreds of thousands, their tens of millions, and their ten thousands.

**6.4.22**  The great monkey army followed behind him as he marched.

**6.4.23**  Joyful and exultant, all guarded by Sugrīva, the leaping monkeys bounded, leapt, roared, shrieked, and howled as they marched indeed toward the southern quarter.

**6.4.24**  Eating fragrant honeycombs and fruits, carrying large trees whose blossoms clustered in dense masses,

**6.4.25**  suddenly spotting one another they dashed and flung each other about; some fell and others leapt up, and still others knocked their companions to the ground.

**6.4.26**  "Rāvaṇa must be slain by us, and all the night-roaming rākṣasas too" — thus the monkeys roared in the presence of Rāghava.

**6.4.27**  In the van, the heroic Ṛṣabha and Nīla and also Kumuda were clearing the route together with many monkeys.

**6.4.28**  In the centre were King Sugrīva, Rāma, and Lakṣmaṇa — slayers of foes — surrounded by many powerful warriors.

**6.4.29**  The heroic monkey Śatabali, attended by ten crores of soldiers, guarded the entire monkey host single-handedly with his firm grasp.

**6.4.30**  Kesarī, Panasa, Gaja, and the exceedingly mighty Arka, attended by a hundred crores, guarded one flank of the army.

**6.4.31**  Suṣeṇa and Jāmbavān, surrounded by many bears, placed Sugrīva in front and together guarded the rear of the army.

**6.4.32**  Their general, heroic Nīla — bull among monkeys, foremost of leapers — guarded that army as it surged forward.

**6.4.33**  Darīmukha, Prajaṅgha, Jambha, and the monkey Rabhasa — those heroes went on every side urging the monkey soldiers onward.

**6.4.34**  Moving thus, those tiger-monkeys, swelling with strength, beheld the foremost of mountains, the Sahya, covered with trees and creepers.

**6.4.35**  That great, fearsome monkey army, resembling the flood of the ocean, poured forth with a tremendous roar, like the ocean with its terrifying momentum.

**6.4.36**  Those heroic elephant-like monkeys at the side of Daśaratha's son all leapt forward rapidly, like noble steeds urged on.

**6.4.37**  Borne by the two monkeys, those two bulls among men shone gloriously — as though the moon and the sun were touched by two great sea-monsters.

**6.4.38**  Then Lakṣmaṇa, riding on Aṅgada, addressed Rāma in auspicious words — he who was full of purposeful thought, possessed of memory, and ready of wit.

**6.4.39**  "Having recovered Vaidehī who was taken, and having slain Rāvaṇa, you shall return with accomplished purpose to Ayodhyā, which likewise waits with accomplished purpose."

**6.4.40**  "O Rāghava, I see great and auspicious omens in the sky and upon the earth — all of them heralding the accomplishment of your purpose."

**6.4.41**  "An auspicious breeze, gentle and pleasant, blows after the army; and these deer and birds cry out in full, sweet voices."

**6.4.42**  "All the quarters are serene; the sun shines clear and unclouded; and Venus — of brilliant, cloudless flame — follows behind you."

**6.4.43**  "The cluster of Brahmā shines pure; the great seers shine pure and radiant; all of them blaze forth steadily in the auspicious rightward direction."

**6.4.44**  "Triśaṅku the royal sage, together with his priest, shines without blemish — that most excellent ancestral star of our great-souled Ikṣvākus."

**6.4.45**  "Viśākhā also shines bright and undimmed, without affliction — that supreme constellation of our great-souled Ikṣvāku line."

**6.4.46**  "The constellation of the rākṣasas, Nairṛta, is tormented; Mūla, their own natal star, is afflicted and scorched by a comet with a smoky tail."

**6.4.47**  "This portends the destruction of the rākṣasas — whose time has come, seized by Death — their star tormented by a planet."

**6.4.48**  "The divine waters are pure and the rivers are serene; the forests are fruit-laden; fragrances waft most abundantly; trees bloom as in their proper season."

**6.4.49**  "The arrayed monkey armies blaze brilliantly, O master, like the armies of the gods in the war against Tārakā."

**6.4.50**  "Having observed all these omens, O noble one, you ought to be joyful." Thus Saumitri, himself elated, spoke these words to reassure his brother.

**6.4.51**  Then the great host, covering the entire earth, marched onward — accompanied by tiger-bears and tiger-monkeys, armed with claws and fangs.

**6.4.52**  The dust raised by the tips of the monkeys' hands and feet swallowed the earth, blocking the sun's radiance and enshrouding the world.

**6.4.53**  That great monkey army marched day and night — a force joyful and exultant, guarded by Sugrīva.

**6.4.54**  All the monkeys pressed forward swiftly, rejoicing in battle, eager to free Sītā — not halting anywhere even for a moment.

**6.4.55**  Then they reached the Sahya mountain, thick with trees and teeming with diverse animals, and also the great earth-bearing Malaya.

**6.4.56**  Even while beholding the variegated forests and the river-springs of Sahya and of Malaya, Rāma pressed on.

**6.4.57**  The leaping monkeys broke campaka trees, tilaka trees, mango trees, aśoka trees, sinduvāra trees, karavīra trees, and timiśa trees.

**6.4.58**  The powerful monkeys there consumed fruits with the fragrance of ambrosia, roots, and blossoms of the trees.

**6.4.59**  Those tawny-brown monkeys, joyful and of a golden hue, marched on drinking honeycombs the size of a droṇa measure that hung pendant from the branches.

**6.4.60**  The bull-monkeys pressed forward, breaking trees, tearing down creepers, and toppling mountain-boulders.

**6.4.61**  Other monkeys roared in the trees, drunk with honey; others ran to the trees and still others fell from them.

**6.4.62**  The earth was filled by those bull-monkeys — like the earth covered with fields of fully bloomed lotuses.

**6.4.63**  Then reaching Mount Mahendra, lotus-eyed Rāma, long-armed, ascended the summit adorned with trees.

**6.4.64**  Having climbed the summit, Rāma the son of Daśaratha beheld the expanse of waters teeming with tortoises and fish.

**6.4.65**  Passing beyond the Sahya and the great mountain Malaya, they reached in due course the ocean, roaring with a terrifying sound.

**6.4.66**  Rāma — foremost among those who bring delight — descended swiftly and came with Sugrīva and Lakṣmaṇa to the incomparable shore-forest.

**6.4.67**  Then, having reached the broad shoreline — its rocky surface washed by the sudden surges of water — Rāma spoke these words.

**6.4.68**  "We have now reached, O Sugrīva, the abode of Varuṇa. Here now arises that anxiety which did not arise before."

**6.4.69**  "Yonder is the far shore — this ocean, lord of rivers. And this sea cannot be crossed without a means."

**6.4.70**  "Therefore let the encampment be made here and let counsel be taken here — such that this monkey army may reach the far shore."

**6.4.71**  Speaking thus, long-armed Rāma — worn by grief over Sītā's abduction — upon reaching the ocean ordered the camp to be established.

**6.4.72**  "The time for counsel has arrived for us regarding the crossing of this ocean. Let each corps be dismissed to its own position; let no one stray in any direction. Let the heroic monkeys go and reconnoitre; the hidden dangers facing us must be known."

**6.4.73**  Having heard the words of Rāma, Sugrīva together with Lakṣmaṇa encamped the army on the tree-covered shore of the ocean.

**6.4.74**  That army, encamped near the ocean, blazed in splendour — like a second ocean, its waters pale gold as honey.

**6.4.75**  Then those bull-monkeys, having come to the shore-forest, settled down there, yearning for the far shore of the great deep.

**6.4.76**  That monkey army, having reached the great ocean and joyful, gazed upon the great ocean tossed by the force of the wind.

**6.4.77**  Gazing upon the abode of Varuṇa — its far shore distant, its expanse unobstructed, haunted by hosts of rākṣasas — the monkey troop-leaders sat down.

**6.4.78**  Fearsome with its violent crocodiles and sea-monsters, churned at the close of day and at moonrise, roiling with moon-halos,

**6.4.79**  scattered with monstrous sea-beasts lashed by fierce winds, strewn with timitimiṅgila creatures — the abode of Varuṇa seemed blanketed with serpents of blazing coils.

**6.4.80**  Plunged into by great creatures, filled with many underwater mountains, difficult to ford, treacherous, pathless, fathomless — that dwelling of the asuras.

**6.4.81**  Churned by makara sea-dragons and serpent coils, reddened by the wind — great masses of water rose up and fell down.

**6.4.82**  As if scattered with fire-powder, dazzling with the sun's reflections — the charming realm of the sun's enemy, fearsome, forever rendered rough by the underworld.

**6.4.83**  The ocean appeared like the sky and the sky appeared like the ocean — ocean and sky appeared indistinguishable from one another.

**6.4.84**  The waters were merged with the sky and the sky was merged with the waters; in that aspect they appeared alike — the stars mingled with the jewels.

**6.4.85**  Between the ocean with its billowing clouds and its rows of wave-garlands and the sky, no distinction could be made.

**6.4.86**  The waves of the ocean-king, crashing against one another and intertwined, rang out with terrifying sounds — like great war-drums in battle.

**6.4.87**  Resounding with the rush of jewel-strewn waters, as if lashed by the wind, as if leaping in rage, teeming with hosts of sea-creatures,

**6.4.88**  those great-souled heroes beheld the water-abode battered by the wind, as if hurled into the sky by the wind, as if bounding up with its waves — the ocean seemingly churning with the roar of its swirling, wave-tossed waters.

**6.5.1**  Then that army, with its proper guard duly arranged by Nīla, was well encamped on the northern shore of the ocean.

**6.5.2**  And both Mainda and Dvivida — those two bull-monkeys — roamed in every direction around that army for its protection.

**6.5.3**  When the army had encamped on the shore of the lord of rivers and lakes, Rāma, seeing Lakṣmaṇa at his side, spoke these words.

**6.5.4**  "They say that grief passes away with the passage of time; but mine, as I do not see my beloved, grows greater day by day."

**6.5.5**  "It is not that my beloved is far — that does not grieve me. It is not that she was taken — that does not grieve me. This alone is what I lament: her youth is passing away."

**6.5.6**  "Blow, O wind, from wherever that maiden is — touching her, touch me also. Through you I have contact with her body; through the moon I have a meeting of eyes."

**6.5.7**  "That burns my limbs like poison swallowed and lodged within me — what my beloved cried to me as she was being carried off: 'O my lord!'"

**6.5.8**  "Fed by that separation as by fuel, blazing with great flames of thought of her, by night and by day my body is consumed by the fire of love."

**6.5.9**  "I shall plunge into the ocean and sleep, O Saumitri, apart from you; perhaps the blazing passion might burn me even as I lie submerged in the water."

**6.5.10**  "There is much to sustain life for one who still desires — by this alone: that I and she of beautiful thighs are supported by one and the same earth."

**6.5.11**  "As a dry field lives by the moisture of the irrigated field beside it, so I live by the mere proximity of her vitality — for I hear that she is alive."

**6.5.12**  "When indeed shall I see fair-hipped Sītā — her eyes as wide as lotus petals — radiant as Śrī, after vanquishing my enemies?"

**6.5.13**  "When shall I, like a sick man drinking an elixir, drink from her face which is like a lotus, lifting it gently upward — from those beautiful lips red as the bimba fruit?"

**6.5.14**  "When indeed shall those two firm, full breasts of hers — resembling the fruits of the tāla palm — trembling, embrace me as she laughs?"

**6.5.15**  "She — that dark-eyed one with the long eyes, virtuous though surrounded by rākṣasas, who calls me her lord — no doubt finds no protector, as though she were lordless."

**6.5.16**  "When shall she, shaking off the rākṣasas and rising up free of them, soar aloft — as the crescent moon in autumn shakes free of blue storm-clouds?"

**6.5.17**  "Sītā, naturally slender, is surely grown yet more slender still through grief and fasting — and by the reversal of her rightful place and season."

**6.5.18**  "When indeed shall I, having fixed my arrows in the breast of the rākṣasa king, bring Sītā back and cast off this grief from my mind?"

**6.5.19**  "When indeed shall chaste Sītā — resembling the daughter of the immortals, filled with longing — cling to my neck and shed tears born of joy?"

**6.5.20**  "When shall I suddenly shed this dreadful grief born of separation from the Maithilī — as one sheds a white garment that has turned otherwise?"

**6.5.21**  While wise Rāma lamented thus at that place, the sun, his body dimming at the end of the day, descended to the west.

**6.5.22**  Comforted by Lakṣmaṇa, Rāma performed the evening rites — remembering Sītā of lotus-petal eyes, overwhelmed by grief.

**6.6.1**  In Laṅkā, having witnessed the fearsome, life-threatening deed wrought by Hanūmat — as by great-souled Śakra himself — the rākṣasa king, his face turned slightly downward in shame, addressed all his rākṣasas.

**6.6.2**  "Laṅkā, this city so difficult to assail, has been violated and penetrated — by a mere monkey, at that — and Sītā, daughter of Janaka, was seen."

**6.6.3**  "The pleasure-grove was ravaged, the sacred shrine was violated, eminent rākṣasas were slain, and all of Laṅkā was thrown into turmoil — all by Hanūmat."

**6.6.4**  "What shall I do? May good befall you all. What is the right course to follow now? Tell me what is fitting — let that be done which, having been done, will prove well done."

**6.6.5**  "The noble and wise declare that victory has counsel as its root; therefore I choose indeed to take counsel, O mighty ones, regarding Rāma."

**6.6.6**  "Men in the world are of three kinds — the highest, the lowest, and the middling; of these gathered together I shall speak of their virtues and defects."

**6.6.7**  "He who, together with counsellors who are well-disposed and capable in resolving deliberations — or with friends of equal purpose, or even with well-wishing kinsmen —"

**6.6.8**  "— deliberates and then sets forth his undertakings, and also makes effort in what pertains to fate — him they call the highest of men."

**6.6.9**  "He who deliberates alone on matters of interest, alone sets his mind on dharma, and alone performs his tasks — him they call the middling man."

**6.6.10**  "He who, without determining the merits and defects, abandoning recourse to the divine, neglects his task with the word 'I shall act' — he is the lowest of men."

**6.6.11**  "Just as men are forever of three kinds — highest, lowest, and middling — so too counsel itself must be understood to be highest, lowest, and middling."

**6.6.12**  "Counsel wherein the counsellors, having arrived at unity by means of the eye of śāstra, come to a settled conclusion — that they call the highest counsel."

**6.6.13**  "Counsel wherein the counsellors, even after many opinions have been advanced in determining what is beneficial, yet again arrive at a single conclusion — that is remembered as middling counsel."

**6.6.14**  "Counsel wherein each upholds his own opinion and speaks against the others, where there is no good outcome in any unity of view — that counsel is called the lowest."

**6.6.15**  "Therefore, O foremost of counsellors, let you all well deliberate and agree upon what should be done — this is the most urgent matter for me."

**6.6.16**  "Rāma, surrounded by thousands of heroic monkeys, is advancing upon our city of Laṅkā — he who would lay siege to us."

**6.6.17**  "And Rāghava shall most certainly cross the ocean with ease — with fitting swiftness, together with his brother and followed by his army."

**6.6.18**  "With affairs having reached this pass — opposed by the monkeys — let everything beneficial for the city and for the army be deliberated thoroughly on my behalf."

**6.7.1**  Having been thus addressed by the lord of rākṣasas, those mighty rākṣasas all spoke with folded hands to Rāvaṇa, the lord of rākṣasas.

**6.7.2**  O king, our great army is filled with iron clubs, spears, javelins, tridents, and broadswords — why then does despondency overtake you?

**6.7.3**  Kubera, who dwelt on the peak of Kailāsa surrounded by many yakṣas, was reduced to your subjection after great slaughter was wrought.

**6.7.4**  That guardian of the world, boasting of his friendship with Maheśvara, was defeated by you in fury in battle, O mighty one.

**6.7.5**  Having slain the hosts of yakṣas, having routed and seized them, you brought away this aerial chariot from the peak of Kailāsa.

**6.7.6**  Maya, the lord of dānavas, desiring alliance out of fear of you, gave his daughter to be your wife, O bull among rākṣasas.

**6.7.7**  Madhu by name, the lord of dānavas, proud of his valour and hard to withstand, was seized and brought under your control — he who was the source of joy to Kumbhīnasī.

**6.7.8**  The nāgas who had retreated to the netherworld were defeated by you, O great-armed one; Vāsuki, Takṣaka, Śaṅkha, and Jaṭī were brought under your subjection.

**6.7.9**  Those dānavas who are inexhaustible, powerful, heroic, and doubly blessed with boons — you fought them in battle for a full year, O mighty one.

**6.7.10**  Relying on your own strength, they were brought to subjection, O slayer of foes; and many magical powers were also obtained there, O lord of rākṣasas.

**6.7.11**  The heroic and mighty sons of Varuṇa, followed by their fourfold armies, were defeated by you in battle, O great-armed one.

**6.7.12**  Having plunged into the ocean of Yama's army — terrible as the great crocodile of death's rod, adorned by the Śālmalī island — O king,

**6.7.13**  great victory was obtained and death itself was repelled; by your glorious fighting all the worlds there were thoroughly gratified.

**6.7.14**  The earth was filled with many heroic kṣatriyas of valour equal to Śakra, like great trees filling a forest.

**6.7.15**  Those supremely hard-to-conquer ones, in whose might, virtue, and energy Rāghava has no equal in battle — they were forcibly slain by you, O king.

**6.7.16**  O king, this calamity that has come from an ordinary mortal is unworthy of concern; do not take it to heart — you shall slay Rāghava.

**6.8.1**  Then Prahasta, a rākṣasa dark as a rain-cloud, that hero who was commander of the army, spoke with folded hands:

**6.8.2**  Gods, dānavas, gandharvas, piśācas, birds, and serpents are incapable of assailing you — much less vānaras in battle.

**6.8.3**  All of us were careless and unsuspecting; we were deceived by Hanūmat. That forest-dweller shall not escape alive while I live.

**6.8.4**  I shall make all the earth up to the ocean, with its mountains, forests, and groves, free of vānaras — let your lordship command me.

**6.8.5**  I shall also provide protection from the vānaras, O ranger of the night; no grief arising from your own fault shall come upon you.

**6.8.6**  Then Durmukha, a rākṣasa in violent rage, declared: "This affront to all of us is truly intolerable —

**6.8.7**  this further insult to the prosperous lord of rākṣasas and to the city and its inner chambers — the brazen affront of the lord of vānaras.

**6.8.8**  In this very moment I shall slay the vānaras — whether they have entered the terrible sea, the sky, or the netherworld — and return alone."

**6.8.9**  Then Vajradaṃṣṭra, immensely strong and in violent rage, seizing a dreadful iron club reddened with flesh and blood, spoke:

**6.8.10**  "What need have you of that wretched, hapless Hanūmat? While formidable Rāma stands firm with Sugrīva and Lakṣmaṇa,

**6.8.11**  today I shall come back alone, having routed the army of vānaras and slain Rāma with Sugrīva and Lakṣmaṇa with my iron club."

**6.8.12**  Then Nikumbha, the heroic and mighty son of Kumbhakarṇa, in supreme fury, spoke to Rāvaṇa, the one who makes the world cry:

**6.8.13**  "Let all of you remain assembled together with the great king; I alone shall slay Rāghava with Lakṣmaṇa."

**6.8.14**  Then Vajrahanu, a rākṣasa like a mountain, enraged and licking his mouth with his tongue, spoke:

**6.8.15**  "Let your lordships attend freely to your affairs without anxiety; I alone shall devour all those chiefs of vānara troops."

**6.8.16**  "Be at ease, take sport without care, drink honey-wine; I alone shall slay Sugrīva with Lakṣmaṇa, and Aṅgada, Hanūmat, and Rāma, the elephant of the battlefield."

**6.9.1**  Then Nikumbha, Rabhasas, Sūryaśatru the mighty, Suptaghna, Yajñakopa, Mahāpārśva, and Mahodara —

**6.9.2**  Agniketu the fierce, Raśmiketu the rākṣasa, and Indrajit of great splendour, the powerful son of Rāvaṇa —

**6.9.3**  Prahasta, Virūpākṣa, Vajradaṃṣṭra the mighty, Dhūmrākṣa, Atikāya, and Durmukha the rākṣasa —

**6.9.4**  seizing iron clubs, broadswords, lances, spears, tridents, battle-axes, bows with arrows, and large sharp swords,

**6.9.5**  those rākṣasas, in supreme fury, leaping up and blazing as if with energy, all spoke to Rāvaṇa:

**6.9.6**  "Today we shall slay Rāma, Sugrīva with Lakṣmaṇa, and the wretched Hanūmat by whom Laṅkā was violated."

**6.9.7**  Having restrained all of them who had taken up weapons, Vibhīṣaṇa made them sit down again and spoke with folded hands:

**6.9.8**  "Dear one, the goal which cannot be attained by the three means — conciliation, gifts, and sowing division — has its proper times for the use of force, as the wise declare.

**6.9.9**  Feats of force succeed, dear one, when performed after due deliberation and in accordance with proper method, against those who are careless, beset by enemies, or struck down by fate.

**6.9.10**  But how do you wish to assault him who is vigilant, who desires victory, who stands firm in strength, who has conquered his wrath, and who is hard to assail?

**6.9.11**  Having crossed the terrible ocean, lord of rivers and streams — who could have imagined the feat accomplished by Hanūmat?

**6.9.12**  The strength and valour of enemies are immeasurable, O rangers of the night; sudden contempt for them must not be shown under any circumstances.

**6.9.13**  And moreover, what wrong was previously done to Rāma, the glorious one, by the king of rākṣasas — whose wife was carried away from Janasthāna?

**6.9.14**  If Khara overstepped and was slain by Rāma in battle, the lives of living beings must surely be protected to the best of one's ability.

**6.9.15**  On account of this, the greatest fear would befall us; the abducted Vaidehī should be surrendered — why perpetuate a quarrel that has been provoked?

**6.9.16**  It is not fitting for us to wage a pointless war against that valiant one who follows righteousness; let Maithilī be given to him.

**6.9.17**  Before he shatters the city — with its elephants, horses, and abundant treasures — with his arrows, let Maithilī be given to him.

**6.9.18**  Before his terrible, great, and invincible army of vānaras descends upon our Laṅkā, let Sītā be given back.

**6.9.19**  For the city of Laṅkā and all the heroic rākṣasas will surely perish if the beloved wife of Rāma is not willingly given back.

**6.9.20**  I implore you out of kinship — act upon my words; I speak what is beneficial and wholesome: let Maithilī be given to him.

**6.9.21**  Before the prince releases upon you his unfailing arrows — bright as the rays of the autumn sun, freshly fletched, well-strung — for your destruction, let Maithilī be given to the son of Daśaratha.

**6.9.22**  Abandon wrath, which destroys happiness and righteousness; embrace righteousness, which increases joy and fame; be gracious — let us live with our sons and kinsmen; let Maithilī be given to the son of Daśaratha.

**6.10.1**  To Vibhīṣaṇa, who had spoken words firmly grounded and well-meaning, Rāvaṇa — urged on by fate — spoke harsh words in return.

**6.10.2**  One might dwell with a rival, or even with an enraged venomous serpent, but one should never live with a pretended friend who serves the enemy.

**6.10.3**  I know the nature of kinsmen in all the worlds, O Rākṣasa — kinsmen always rejoice in the misfortunes of their own kin.

**6.10.4**  Those who are eminent, capable, skilled, or virtuous in conduct, O Rākṣasa — kinsmen despise them and treat even the brave with contempt.

**6.10.5**  Forever rejoicing in one another's afflictions, perpetrators of harm, concealing their hearts — kinsmen are terrible and cause fear.

**6.10.6**  Certain verses are said to have been sung by elephants somewhere in a lotus-forest, upon seeing men with nooses in their hands — hear them as I recite them.

**6.10.7**  Neither fire, nor other weapons, nor nooses are what we fear — but kinsmen, terrible and acting from self-interest, are what truly frighten us.

**6.10.8**  They will tell others the means of our capture — of that there is no doubt; it is known to us that, among all fears, fear from kinsmen is the most terrible.

**6.10.9**  Abundance is found in cattle, self-restraint in Brāhmaṇas, fickleness in women — and danger is found from one's own kinsmen.

**6.10.10**  Therefore, O gentle one, what is unwelcome to you is that I am honored in the world, that I am born of noble lineage, and that I stand with my foot on the heads of my enemies.

**6.10.11**  Were another to speak such words, O night-wanderer, he would not survive this moment — but shame on you, O defiler of your lineage!

**6.10.12**  Having been thus spoken to so harshly, Vibhīṣaṇa — the speaker of righteousness — rose up into the air, mace in hand, together with four Rākṣasas.

**6.10.13**  And then Vibhīṣaṇa — glorious, his anger now roused, stationed in the sky — spoke these words to his brother, the lord of Rākṣasas.

**6.10.14**  You are my brother, O King — say to me whatever you wish; but these harsh words of yours, untrue as they are, I cannot forgive.

**6.10.15**  The well-reasoned counsel spoken out of goodwill is not heeded by those of unformed souls who have come under the sway of Time, O ten-faced one.

**6.10.16**  Men who always speak pleasant things are easily found, O King; but one who speaks — or is willing to hear — what is unpleasant yet beneficial is rare.

**6.10.17**  I cannot stand by and watch you perish, bound in the noose of Time that carries off all beings, any more than one could ignore a burning dwelling.

**6.10.18**  I do not wish to see you struck down by Rāma's arrows — those bright shafts blazing like fire and adorned with golden ornaments.

**6.10.19**  Those who are brave, powerful, and skilled in arms on the field of battle — when fate overtakes them, they collapse like bridges of sand.

**6.10.20**  Protect yourself in every way, and this city along with all the Rākṣasas. Fare you well — I shall depart. Be happy without me.

**6.10.21**  When I, acting from goodwill, tried to restrain you, my counsel did not please you, O night-wanderer; for men whose time is run and whose life is spent do not accept benefit though offered by friends.

**6.11.1**  Having spoken these harsh words to Rāvaṇa, Rāvaṇa's younger brother came in a short time to where Rāma was, together with Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.11.2**  Those lords of monkeys standing on the earth beheld him stationed in the sky — massive as the peak of Meru, blazing like a bolt of lightning.

**6.11.3**  Seeing him together with four others as a group of five, Sugrīva — the irresistible lord of monkeys — pondered the matter with the other monkeys, being wise.

**6.11.4**  Having reflected for a moment, he spoke this excellent speech to all those monkeys, with Hanūmat foremost among them.

**6.11.5**  This Rākṣasa, bearing all weapons, advances with four Rākṣasas — see! He comes to kill us, there is no doubt.

**6.11.6**  Hearing Sugrīva's words, all those foremost of monkeys, raising sāla trees and boulders, spoke these words.

**6.11.7**  Quickly direct us, O King, to slay these wicked-souled ones — let these short-lived beings fall dead to the earth.

**6.11.8**  While they were thus talking among themselves, Vibhīṣaṇa reached the far shore and stood there, still stationed in the sky.

**6.11.9**  And Vibhīṣaṇa, greatly wise and great in stature, still stationed in the sky, looked at Sugrīva and those others, and spoke in a mighty voice.

**6.11.10**  There is a Rākṣasa of wicked conduct named Rāvaṇa, lord of Rākṣasas — I am his younger brother, known as Vibhīṣaṇa.

**6.11.11**  By him Sītā was carried off from Janasthāna, after slaying Jaṭāyu; she is confined, helpless and wretched, closely guarded by Rākṣasī women.

**6.11.12**  I remonstrated with him through reasoned arguments and various counsels, again and again: 'Let Sītā be properly restored to Rāma.'

**6.11.13**  But he, Rāvaṇa, driven by fate, did not accept the beneficial counsel being offered to him — as though it were a bitter medicine.

**6.11.14**  I, having been spoken to harshly by him and humiliated like a slave, abandoning sons and wife, have taken refuge with Rāghava.

**6.11.15**  Report to the great-souled Rāghava, the refuge of all worlds, that Vibhīṣaṇa stands present before him and seeks shelter, quickly.

**6.11.16**  Having heard these words, Sugrīva of swift valor spoke these agitated words to Rāma in the presence of Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.11.17**  One who is known as Vibhīṣaṇa, the younger brother of Rāvaṇa, together with four Rākṣasas has taken refuge with you, my lord.

**6.11.18**  Know this Vibhīṣaṇa to be a spy sent by Rāvaṇa — I consider his arrest to be fitting, O best of the forbearing.

**6.11.19**  This Rākṣasa, directed by a crooked mind, has presented himself — concealed by illusion — to strike once you trust him, O Rāghava.

**6.11.20**  Let him be seized with severe punishment, along with his counselors — for this is Vibhīṣaṇa, the brother of the cruel Rāvaṇa.

**6.11.21**  Having spoken thus to Rāma, the agitated commander of the army — himself skilled in speech and knowing the value of words — then fell silent.

**6.11.22**  Hearing that speech of Sugrīva, Rāma of great might spoke thus to the monkeys nearby, with Hanūmat foremost among them.

**6.11.23**  What the king of monkeys has said regarding the younger brother of Rāvaṇa — those well-reasoned words of great weight — you all have heard them.

**6.11.24**  In difficult situations, it is fitting even for a capable and wise friend of good character who desires lasting well-being to offer counsel.

**6.11.25**  Thus questioned, each of them, without delay, spoke their own opinion to Rāma with due courtesy, each desiring his welfare.

**6.11.26**  Nothing in the three worlds is unknown to you, O Rāghava — honoring us, O Rāma, you ask us out of friendship.

**6.11.27**  You are true to your vow, heroic, righteous, of firm valor — you act only after examining, you are mindful, and you give yourself fully to your friends.

**6.11.28**  Therefore, let your counselors speak one by one, those endowed with sound judgment and capable, with reasons, time and again.

**6.11.29**  When Rāghava had spoken thus, the wise monkey Aṅgada spoke first, for the purpose of examining Vibhīṣaṇa.

**6.11.30**  One who has come from the side of the enemy is in every way suspect — Vibhīṣaṇa should not hastily be made worthy of trust.

**6.11.31**  Those of deceitful mind move about having concealed their true intent, and they strike at moments of vulnerability — that calamity would be very great.

**6.11.32**  Having carefully weighed benefit and harm, one should adopt a course of action — accept someone on the strength of virtues, and reject on account of faults.

**6.11.33**  If the fault in him is great, let him be cast away without hesitation; or if many virtues are recognized, let acceptance be made, O King.

**6.11.34**  Then Śarabha, having reflected, spoke words full of meaning: 'Quickly, O tiger among men, let a spy be employed concerning him.'

**6.11.35**  Having properly set a spy of subtle intelligence, and having examined him thoroughly, then let reception of him be made as is proper.

**6.11.36**  Then Jāmbavān, discerning, examining with the intelligence of the śāstras, presented a speech full of virtue and free from fault.

**6.11.37**  Vibhīṣaṇa has come from a sworn enemy and an evil lord of Rākṣasas, arriving at an unsuitable time and place — let him be regarded with suspicion in every way.

**6.11.38**  Then Mainda, skilled in right and wrong policy, eloquent in speech, after deliberation, spoke words even more reasoned.

**6.11.39**  This Vibhīṣaṇa is, they say, merely a spokesman for Rāvaṇa — let him be questioned gently and with sweet words, O lord of foremost men.

**6.11.40**  Having learned his intent, you will then act in accordance with the truth — if he is seen to be innocent or guilty, do so with deliberate mind, O bull among men.

**6.11.41**  Then Hanūmān, foremost of counselors and endowed with refinement, spoke words that were smooth, meaningful, sweet, and concise.

**6.11.42**  Even Bṛhaspati himself, were he to speak, would not be able to surpass you — best of minds, most capable, foremost among speakers.

**6.11.43**  Neither for argument nor contention, neither from pride nor personal desire, will I speak, O King — I will speak truly out of reverence for Rāma.

**6.11.44**  In what has been said by your counselors on the grounds of benefit and harm, I see a fault — for the proposed course of action does not follow.

**6.11.45**  Without commission of a task, capability cannot be known — hasty deployment appears to me to be flawed.

**6.11.46**  What your counselors said — that deployment of a spy is fitting — the reason for that does not hold, given that the goal cannot be achieved thereby.

**6.11.47**  As for the charge that Vibhīṣaṇa has arrived at an unsuitable time and place — I have something to say about that; hear it as I understand it.

**6.11.48**  That very place and time become what they become depending on the person encountered; and so too do virtues and faults, passing from person to person.

**6.11.49**  Having seen the wickedness of Rāvaṇa, and likewise your own valor, his coming here is fitting and consistent with his own discernment.

**6.11.50**  Regarding what was said — 'Let him be questioned by men in disguised form' — I have a considered opinion about that, upon reflection.

**6.11.51**  A wise man, when questioned suddenly with suspicion, might speak guardedly — and thereby a friend who came in good faith would be alienated by false questioning.

**6.11.52**  It is not possible, O King, to know another's inner disposition suddenly — rather, it is by those inner traits that are revealed that one skilled in discernment perceives it deeply.

**6.11.53**  But even as he speaks, no wickedness of disposition is ever discernible in him — his countenance is serene — and therefore I have no doubt.

**6.11.54**  One of undisturbed mind, at ease — a deceitful person does not move about in such a manner; his speech too is not crooked — and therefore there is no doubt here.

**6.11.55**  Demeanor, even when one attempts to conceal it, cannot be fully hidden — it forcibly reveals the inner disposition of men.

**6.11.56**  An action suited to place and time, O best of those who know what must be done, when undertaken with purposeful application, quickly bears fruit.

**6.11.57**  Having seen your resolve, having seen Rāvaṇa's false conduct, and having heard of the slaying of Vālin and the consecration of Sugrīva —

**6.11.58**  — desiring a kingdom, he has come here with considered intent; keeping all this in view, his acceptance is appropriate.

**6.11.59**  What I have said to the best of my ability regarding the Rākṣasa's sincerity — you are the authority for the rest, O best of the wise, having heard it.

**6.12.1**  Then Rāma — of serene spirit, irresistible, learned, and established in his own self — having heard the son of the Wind, replied.

**6.12.2**  I too have something to say regarding Vibhīṣaṇa — I wish to hear all of that from you who stand in what is good.

**6.12.3**  One who has come in the spirit of friendship I would not abandon under any circumstances — even if fault there be in him, this is not censured among the good.

**6.12.4**  Hearing Rāma's words, Sugrīva — lord of the leaping monkeys — spoke back to the scion of Kākutstha, impelled by warm affection.

**6.12.5**  What is remarkable in this, O knower of dharma, O crest-jewel of the lords of the world, that you, possessed of true being and standing on the noble path, should speak what is noble?

**6.12.6**  My own inner self too perceives the purity of Vibhīṣaṇa — from inference, from his disposition, he has been thoroughly examined from every angle.

**6.12.7**  Therefore, O Rāghava, let the greatly wise Vibhīṣaṇa quickly become our equal and let him enter into friendship with us.

**6.12.8**  Rāma, having heard and reflected upon that speech of Sugrīva, then spoke more auspicious words to the bull among monkeys.

**6.12.9**  Whether this night-wanderer be utterly wicked or free of wickedness — how could he ever be capable of doing even the slightest harm to me?

**6.12.10**  Piśācas, Dānavas, Yakṣas, and all the Rākṣasas on earth — I could slay all of them with the tip of my finger, were I so inclined, O lord of the monkey host.

**6.12.11**  It is heard that a dove, when an enemy sought refuge with him, honored the enemy properly and even invited him with portions of his own flesh.

**6.12.12**  That dove accepted even him who had come to carry off his wife — then what of a man of my kind, O best of monkeys?

**6.12.13**  Hear the verses sung of old by Kaṇḍu, son of the sage Kaṇva, that great sage, righteous and truthful in speech.

**6.12.14**  One should not slay a suppliant who stands with palms joined, pitiable, begging for protection, come seeking refuge — even if he be the greatest enemy — for the sake of non-cruelty, O lord of birds.

**6.12.15**  Whether afflicted or proud, an enemy who has taken refuge with others must be protected by one of formed soul, even at the sacrifice of his own life.

**6.12.16**  If he does not protect — out of fear, or delusion, or desire — to the best of his ability and in accordance with truth, that is a sin censured by the world.

**6.12.17**  If the refugee perishes while the protector watches, the refugee — left unprotected — departs taking with him all the merit of that protector.

**6.12.18**  Such is the great fault in not protecting those who have surrendered — it leads to exclusion from heaven, to disgrace, and to destruction of strength and valor.

**6.12.19**  I will fulfill in all truth the excellent teaching of Kaṇḍu — righteous and glorious, it shall yield the fruit of heaven when the time of fruition comes.

**6.12.20**  To one who has surrendered even once and cries 'I am yours' — I grant freedom from fear from all beings: this is my vow.

**6.12.21**  Bring him here, O best of monkeys — I have given him my assurance of safety, O Sugrīva, whether it be Vibhīṣaṇa, or even Rāvaṇa himself.

**6.12.22**  Then, upon hearing those words spoken by the lord of men at the urging of the lord of monkeys, the king of men swiftly went to meet Vibhīṣaṇa, as Purandara meets the king of birds.

**6.13.1**  When Rāghava had granted him safety, Rāvaṇa's younger brother bowed and, overjoyed, descended from the sky to the earth together with his devoted attendants.

**6.13.2**  That righteous Vibhīṣaṇa, seeking refuge, fell at Rāma's feet together with four rākṣasas.

**6.13.3**  There Vibhīṣaṇa then spoke these words to Rāma — words that were righteous, fitting, timely, and gladdening.

**6.13.4**  "I am the younger brother of Rāvaṇa, and by him I have been dishonoured. I have come to you for refuge — you who are the refuge of all beings."

**6.13.5**  "I have abandoned Laṅkā, my friends, and my wealth. My kingdom, my life, and my pleasures are all now surrendered to you."

**6.13.6**  "I will render assistance in the slaying of the rākṣasas and in the assault upon Laṅkā with all the strength of my life, and I will enter your army."

**6.13.7**  As he spoke thus, Rāma embraced Vibhīṣaṇa and, filled with joy, said to Lakṣmaṇa: "Bring water from the ocean."

**6.13.8**  "With it, quickly anoint this greatly wise Vibhīṣaṇa as king of the rākṣasas, O bestower of honour, now that I am pleased with him."

**6.13.9**  Thus instructed, Saumitri anointed Vibhīṣaṇa as king in the midst of the foremost monkeys, acting on Rāma's command.

**6.13.10**  Witnessing that gracious act of Rāma, the monkeys at once raised great shouts and cried out, "Well done! Well done!"

**6.13.11**  Then Hanūmān and Sugrīva asked Vibhīṣaṇa: "How shall we cross the impassable ocean, the abode of Varuṇa?"

**6.13.12**  "Let us approach by means of some stratagem, so that we may all — together with the army — cross swiftly the lord of rivers and streams, the abode of Varuṇa."

**6.13.13**  Thus addressed, Vibhīṣaṇa, who knows what is right, replied: "King Rāghava ought to approach the ocean for refuge."

**6.13.14**  "This measureless great ocean was dug out by Sagara; the great ocean ought therefore to render service to Rāma, his kinsman."

**6.13.15**  When the wise rākṣasa Vibhīṣaṇa had spoken thus, this course also found favour with Rāghava, who was by nature righteous.

**6.13.16**  Then that great-spirited one, skilled in proper conduct and seeking to honour the occasion, spoke with a gentle smile to Lakṣmaṇa and to Sugrīva, lord of the monkeys.

**6.13.17**  "Lakṣmaṇa, this counsel of Vibhīṣaṇa pleases me. Speak, you together with Sugrīva — does it please you as well?"

**6.13.18**  "Sugrīva is ever wise, and you are expert in counsel; having considered the matter, both of you — say what seems best."

**6.13.19**  Thus addressed, those two heroes, Sugrīva and Lakṣmaṇa, both spoke this reply, combined with proper courteous form.

**6.13.20**  "Why would it not please us, O tiger among men, O Rāghava? What Vibhīṣaṇa has said is agreeable and suited to this moment."

**6.13.21**  "Without building a bridge across this dreadful ocean, the abode of Varuṇa, Laṅkā cannot be reached even by the gods and asuras together with Indra."

**6.13.22**  "Let the true word of the valiant Vibhīṣaṇa be carried out. Enough delay — let this ocean now be made to serve."

**6.13.23**  Thus counselled, Rāma then lay down upon the shore of the lord of rivers and streams, which was spread with kuśa grass, like fire resting on a sacrificial altar.

**6.14.1**  As Rāma lay there on the ground spread with kuśa grass, vigilant and self-restrained, three nights passed over him.

**6.14.2**  Yet the sluggish ocean would not reveal itself to Rāma, even though Rāma had worshipped it with all due observance and effort.

**6.14.3**  Then Rāma, enraged at the ocean, his eyes red at the corners, spoke thus to Lakṣmaṇa of auspicious marks, who stood nearby.

**6.14.4**  "Look, Lakṣmaṇa, at the arrogance of this ignoble ocean, which, though honoured, does not appear before me of its own accord."

**6.14.5**  "Composure, forbearance, uprightness, gentle speech — these virtues of good men bear the fruit of powerlessness when directed at those who are without virtue."

**6.14.6**  "The world honours a man who praises himself, who is wicked, bold, reckless, and who wields the rod of punishment on all sides."

**6.14.7**  "By gentleness, Lakṣmaṇa, fame cannot be won; by gentleness, glory cannot be won in this world, nor victory on the field of battle."

**6.14.8**  "Today, O Saumitri, look at the abode of the makaras with its waters blocked and its makaras pierced by my arrows, floating on all sides."

**6.14.9**  "Behold here, Lakṣmaṇa, the great fish, the trunks of elephants, and the coiled bodies of serpents, all pierced by me."

**6.14.10**  "Today, with great battle, I shall dry up with my arrows the ocean together with its conches, oysters, fish, and makaras."

**6.14.11**  "This abode of makaras takes me, endowed with forbearance, to be powerless. Fie upon forbearance toward such a person!"

**6.14.12**  "Bring my bow, O Saumitri, and arrows like venomous serpents. Today, in my fury, I shall agitate even the impassable ocean."

**6.14.13**  "With my arrows I shall render boundless this abode of Varuṇa, which abides within its shores, suddenly churning with waves."

**6.14.14**  Having spoken thus, with bow in hand and eyes dilated with rage, Rāma became terrible, blazing like the fire at the end of an age.

**6.14.15**  Straining that dreadful bow and shaking the world with his arrows, he released fierce shafts like Śatakratu hurling thunderbolts.

**6.14.16**  Those blazing, swiftly flying, supremely powerful arrows entered the waters of the ocean, terrifying the serpents therein.

**6.14.17**  Then a great and terrible agitation arose in the ocean, filled with crocodiles and makaras, accompanied by a roar like that of a tempest.

**6.14.18**  The great ocean, spread with garlands of mighty waves, teeming with conches and oysters, its waves suddenly wreathed in smoke, was thrown into turmoil.

**6.14.19**  The serpents were afflicted, their mouths and eyes blazing; and the dānavas of great might who dwell in the depths of Pātāla were terrified.

**6.14.20**  The waves of the lord of rivers, teeming with crocodiles and makaras, rose up by the thousands, resembling the Vindhya and Mandara mountains.

**6.14.21**  With its mass of waves churning turbulently, its serpents and rākṣasas thrown into confusion, its great sea-monsters surging upward, the ocean was overwhelmed.

**6.15.1**  Then the ocean rose of its own accord from its midst; the ocean appeared, ascending like the sun rising over the great mountains of Meru, accompanied by serpents with blazing mouths.

**6.15.2**  He had the glossy hue of vaidūrya gem, adorned with jāmbūnada gold, wearing a red garland and garments, his eyes resembling lotus petals.

**6.15.3**  The mighty ocean, having emerged and first paying homage, spoke with joined palms to Rāghava, who stood with arrow in hand.

**6.15.4**  "O Rāghava, earth, wind, sky, water, and fire abide, O gentle one, in their own nature, following the eternal path."

**6.15.5**  "This is my own nature too — that I am unfathomable and unfordable. But there could be a transformation, O Rādhā — this I declare to you."

**6.15.6**  "Neither out of desire, nor out of greed, nor out of fear, O son of a king, can I in any way restrain my waters, which are troubled by crocodiles."

**6.15.7**  "I shall make provision, O Rāma, for that by which I can bear it; the crocodiles shall not attack for as long as the army is crossing."

**6.15.8**  "This one, O gentle lord, is named Nala, the son of Viśvakarman; the illustrious one was granted a boon by his father and is equal to Viśvakarman."

**6.15.9**  "Let this monkey of great energy build a bridge upon me; I shall support it, for he is like his father."

**6.15.10**  Having spoken thus, the ocean vanished. Then Nala rose up and that foremost of monkeys, exceedingly mighty, spoke these words to Rāma.

**6.15.11**  "I shall build a bridge across the wide abode of Varuṇa, drawing upon the ability of my father. The great ocean has spoken the truth."

**6.15.12**  "A boon was granted to my mother at Mandara by Viśvakarman; I am his own son, equal to Viśvakarman."

**6.15.13**  "Nor indeed would I have proclaimed my own virtues without being asked. Let the foremost monkeys build the bridge this very day if they so wish."

**6.15.14**  Then, dispatched by Rāma, the leaders of the monkey troops rushed in all directions into the great forest with joy, by the hundreds of thousands.

**6.15.15**  Those monkeys, themselves like mountains, foremost among the hosts of tree-dwelling creatures, broke off trees there and dragged them to the ocean.

**6.15.16**  The monkeys brought sāla trees, aśvakarṇa trees, dhava trees, bamboos, kuṭaja, arjuna, tāla, tikala, and timiśa trees.

**6.15.17**  With bilva, saptaparṇa, and flowering karṇikāra trees, with mango and aśoka trees, they filled the ocean.

**6.15.18**  Those foremost monkeys, lifting trees both with roots and without roots like Indra's banners raised on high, carried and hurled them.

**6.15.19**  As mountains were hurled in, the water suddenly thrown upward leapt into the sky and scattered in all directions.

**6.15.20**  Nala built the great bridge in the midst of the lord of rivers and streams — ten yojanas in breadth and a hundred yojanas in length.

**6.15.21**  As rocks were thrown and mountains were cast down there upon that great ocean, a tumultuous sound arose.

**6.15.22**  That bridge built by Nala over the ocean, the abode of makaras, beautiful and glorious, shone like the path of Svāti in the sky.

**6.15.23**  Then the gods together with the gandharvas, the siddhas, and the foremost seers —

**6.15.24**  — and all living beings beheld the bridging of the ocean, which was inconceivable, unbearable to look upon, wondrous, and hair-raising, while the monkeys leapt, swam, and roared.

**6.15.25**  Those thousands of crores of mighty monkeys, building the bridge across the ocean, crossed to the further shore of the great sea.

**6.15.26**  That great bridge — wide, well-built, glorious, with level ground, firmly laid — shone in the ocean like a parting line in hair.

**6.15.27**  Then, on the further shore of the ocean, Vibhīṣaṇa with mace in hand stood with his ministers, ready to ward off attacks from enemies.

**6.15.28**  At the head of that army, the illustrious Rāma, righteous of soul, bearing his bow, accompanied by Lakṣmaṇa and Sugrīva, advanced.

**6.15.29**  Others went by the middle, others of the monkeys by the flanks; some fell into the water, others could not find the path; some, taking to the sky, leapt like garuḍas.

**6.15.30**  With its great roar, the terrible monkey army, as it crossed, drowned out the uplifted, terrible sound of the ocean.

**6.15.31**  That army of monkeys, having crossed by Nala's bridge, encamped at the far shore at the command of the king, in a place rich in roots and fruits.

**6.15.32**  Beholding that wondrous and difficult feat, the gods together with siddhas and cāraṇas came to Rāma accompanied by the great seers and each separately anointed him with beautiful waters.

**6.15.33**  "Conquer your enemies, O king of men; protect the earth with its ocean for eternal ages" — with such auspicious and varied words they honoured Rāma, who was thus celebrated as a king of men.

**6.16.1**  When Rāma, son of Daśaratha, had crossed the ocean with his army, the illustrious Rāvaṇa spoke to his two ministers, Śuka and Sāraṇa.

**6.16.2**  "The entire monkey army — so formidable and hard to overcome — has crossed the ocean, and a bridge across the sea has been built by Rāma, a feat without precedent."

**6.16.3**  "I could never have believed a bridge could be built across the ocean; and yet that monkey army must be counted and assessed by me."

**6.16.4**  "You two shall enter the monkey army undetected and learn its full measure and strength, and who the foremost among the leaping warriors are —"

**6.16.5**  "— who are the counselors trusted by Rāma and Sugrīva, who march in the vanguard, and which of the leaping monkeys are the true heroes."

**6.16.6**  "Determine how the bridge was constructed across the ocean's waters, and how the encampment of those great-souled monkeys is arranged."

**6.16.7**  "You must learn truly the resolve and might of Rāma, and the weapons of the hero Lakṣmaṇa as well."

**6.16.8**  "And who is the commander of that great-spirited monkey host? Having learned all this accurately, you should return without delay."

**6.16.9**  Thus instructed, those two rākṣasa heroes, Śuka and Sāraṇa, assumed the forms of monkeys and entered the monkey army.

**6.16.10**  But that monkey force was so inconceivable and hair-raising in its vastness that Śuka and Sāraṇa were unable to count it.

**6.16.11**  It was encamped on mountaintops, in ravines and caves, along the shores of the ocean, in forests and in groves.

**6.16.12**  Part was crossing, part had already crossed, and part still wished to cross; some were settling in camp, others still settling — that great, terribly roaring army.

**6.16.13**  The great and radiant Vibhīṣaṇa spotted those two in their disguise, seized Śuka and Sāraṇa, and reported to Rāma: "These two are spies come from Laṅkā — conquerors of enemy citadels."

**6.16.14**  Seeing Rāma, the two were filled with anguish and despaired of their lives; clasping their hands together in supplication, they stood in fear and spoke these words:

**6.16.15**  "We have come here, O gentle one, both of us sent by Rāvaṇa — to learn the full measure of your army, O joy of the Raghu line."

**6.16.16**  Having heard their words, Rāma, son of Daśaratha — he who is devoted to the welfare of all beings — spoke with a smile:

**6.16.17**  "If you have seen the whole of our army, and if you have examined us well, and if the task assigned to you is accomplished — then go freely as you please."

**6.16.18**  "Entering the city of Laṅkā, you two shall convey my message to the king of the rākṣasas, the younger brother of Kubera, exactly as I tell you:"

**6.16.19**  "'Whatever power you relied upon when you stole Sītā from me — show it now fully, together with your army and your kinsmen.'"

**6.16.20**  "'By tomorrow you shall see the city of Laṅkā with its ramparts and gateways, and the rākṣasa army, shattered by my arrows.'"

**6.16.21**  "'I shall unleash my terrible wrath — hold firm your strength, Rāvaṇa! — as Vāsava hurls his thunderbolt upon the Dānavas.'"

**6.16.22**  Thus dispatched, the two rākṣasas, Śuka and Sāraṇa, returned to the city of Laṅkā and addressed the lord of rākṣasas.

**6.16.23**  "We were seized by Vibhīṣaṇa and deserved death, O lord of rākṣasas; but Rāma of boundless splendor — righteous in spirit — saw us and set us free."

**6.16.24**  "There in one place stand four foremost men, like the guardians of the worlds — heroes, accomplished in arms, of firm and unwavering valor:"

**6.16.25**  "Rāma the illustrious son of Daśaratha, Lakṣmaṇa, Vibhīṣaṇa, and Sugrīva of great radiance — whose valor equals that of great Indra."

**6.16.26**  "These four alone are capable of uprooting the city of Laṅkā with its ramparts and gateways — let all the monkeys stand by idle; these suffice."

**6.16.27**  "Such is Rāma's form and such his weaponry, that he alone could destroy the city of Laṅkā — let the other three merely stand by."

**6.16.28**  "That army, protected by Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa and by Sugrīva, has become impossible to assail even by all the gods and asuras together."

**6.16.29**  "That bannered host of great-souled forest-dwellers stands eager to fight. Enough of opposition — let peace be made; let the princess of Mithilā be given back to the son of Daśaratha."

**6.17.1**  Having heard that salutary and courageous speech spoken by Sāraṇa, King Rāvaṇa replied to Sāraṇa:

**6.17.2**  "Even if the gods, gandharvas, and dānavas were to attack me, I would never give up Sītā — not even out of fear from all the worlds."

**6.17.3**  "But you, gentle Sāraṇa — thoroughly terrified, utterly routed by those monkeys — think it best to restore Sītā this very day. Who indeed among enemies is capable of vanquishing me in battle?"

**6.17.4**  Having spoken those harsh words, Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas, the illustrious one, wishing to see for himself, ascended a palace as white as snow and as tall as many palm trees.

**6.17.5**  Accompanied by those two spies, Rāvaṇa — overcome with rage — gazing at the ocean, the mountains, and the forests, saw the whole stretch of earth completely filled with the leaping warriors.

**6.17.6**  Beholding that vast and boundless, innumerable monkey force, King Rāvaṇa asked Sāraṇa in detail:

**6.17.7**  "Among these foremost monkeys, which are the heroes, which are the mighty ones? Which march in the vanguard on all sides with great energy?"

**6.17.8**  "To whom does Sugrīva listen? Who are the lords of lords of troops? Tell me everything, Sāraṇa — who are the foremost among the leaping warriors?"

**6.17.9**  Sāraṇa — a connoisseur of great men — then described to Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas, who had inquired so fully, those foremost of the forest-dwellers.

**6.17.10**  "This monkey who stands roaring and facing Laṅkā, surrounded by a hundred thousand troop commanders —"

**6.17.11**  "— at whose great roar all of Laṅkā trembles — its walls, gateways, hills, forests and groves —"

**6.17.12**  "— this hero who stands at the head of the entire army of the great-souled Sugrīva, king of all forest-dwellers: this is the troop-chieftain named Nīla."

**6.17.13**  "That valiant one who goes on foot with arms outstretched, facing Laṅkā, who keeps stretching himself again and again in his fury —"

**6.17.14**  "— he who resembles a mountain peak, his complexion like the filaments of a lotus, who in his excitement cracks his tail again and again —"

**6.17.15**  "— at the sound of whose tail the ten directions seem to ring — this is he who was consecrated as crown prince by Sugrīva, king of monkeys: Aṅgada by name. He challenges you to battle."

**6.17.16**  "Those foremost monkeys who stand with limbs tensed, snarling and roaring, who rise up and stretch themselves in their wrath —"

**6.17.17**  "— these are irresistible, terrible, fierce, of fierce valor: eight hundred thousand and a thousand crores of them."

**6.17.18**  "The heroes who dwell on the Candana mountain and follow him — this chieftain hopes to crush Laṅkā with his own division alone."

**6.17.19**  "White as silver, a powerful and terribly mighty monkey, wise, heroic, renowned in all three worlds —"

**6.17.20**  "— a monkey who goes quickly to Sugrīva and returns again, dividing and arranging the monkey host, heartening the divisions —"

**6.17.21**  "— he who formerly ranged the beautiful mountain called Saṃkocana on the banks of the Gomatī, a hill adorned with many kinds of trees —"

**6.17.22**  "— this is the troop-chieftain named Kumuda who rules there as king, he who draws along a thousand hundred-thousands of warriors."

**6.17.23**  "He whose tail hairs, many fathoms long, spread out along its great length — copper-red, yellow, dark and white, scattered in all directions — this doer of terrible deeds —"

**6.17.24**  "— undepressed, wrathful, fierce, craving battle: this same one hopes to crush Laṅkā with his own division."

**6.17.25**  "That one who resembles a lion, tawny-colored, with long mane, who stares fixedly at Laṅkā as if to burn it with his gaze —"

**6.17.26**  "— he who constantly dwells on the Vindhya, the Kṛṣṇagiri, the Sahya and the mountain Sudarśana: this is the troop-chieftain Rambha, O king."

**6.17.27**  "A hundred-hundred-thousand plus thirty monkey troop-leaders surround and follow him to crush Laṅkā by force."

**6.17.28**  "He who pricks up his ears and yawns again and again, who does not flinch at death and does not run from his troop —"

**6.17.29**  "— immensely strong, free from fear, who constantly dwells on the beautiful Sālveya mountain: this is the troop-chieftain Śarabha, O king."

**6.17.30**  "All the troop-chieftains who accompany this mighty one are called Vihāras, O king — forty hundred-thousands of them."

**6.17.31**  "He who stands like a great cloud filling the sky in the midst of monkey heroes, as Vāsava stands among the gods —"

**6.17.32**  "— whose great and terrible sound is heard like the beating of war drums by those foremost forest-dwelling lords longing for battle —"

**6.17.33**  "— he who constantly inhabits the incomparable Pāriyātra mountain, ever irresistible in battle: this is the troop-chieftain named Panasa."

**6.17.34**  "Fifty hundred-thousands of troop-chieftains attend upon him, that best of troop-chieftains, each with their separate divisions."

**6.17.35**  "He who stands on the shore of the ocean like a second sea, adorning and setting in motion that formidable army —"

**6.17.36**  "— he who resembles a split mountain and roams drinking from the Parṇāśā, the finest of rivers: this is the troop-chieftain named Vinatā."

**6.17.37**  "His army numbers sixty hundred-thousands of leaping warriors. The troop-chieftain named Krathana challenges you to battle."

**6.17.38**  "The monkey who bears a body of the color of red chalk, Gavaya by name, a man of great energy — he advances upon you in fury."

**6.17.39**  "Seventy hundred-thousands attend upon him. This one hopes to crush Laṅkā with his own division."

**6.17.40**  "These are the irresistible, terrible, mighty troop-chieftains who can assume any form — the best of troop-chieftains — whose number cannot be counted."

**6.18.1**  "I shall now tell you, as you look on, about those troop-chieftains who have exerted themselves for Rāghava's sake without regard for their own lives."

**6.18.2**  "He whose tail-hairs are sleek and very dark, spread all along its length — copper-red, yellow, dark and white, scattered out — a doer of terrible deeds —"

**6.18.3**  "— as they are raised up they blaze like the rays of the sun, and they trail along the ground: this is the troop-chieftain named Hara."

**6.18.4**  "Him whom hundreds and thousands follow from behind, united, lifting up trees, intent on scaling Laṅkā —"

**6.18.5**  "— this one, with a thousand crores of mighty monkeys, longs to conquer you in battle, O conqueror of enemy citadels."

**6.18.6**  "Those you see standing there like great dark storm clouds, black as a mass of people, whose valor in battle is true —"

**6.18.7**  "— heroes armed with claws and fangs, keen in anger, inspiring terror, countless and indescribable as the far shore of the ocean —"

**6.18.8**  "— those bears who inhabit rugged mountains and rivers, O king: these dreadful bears advance upon you."

**6.18.9**  "Standing in their midst, O king, with terrible eyes and terrible to behold, surrounded on all sides like a rain-cloud by storm clouds, is —"

**6.18.10**  "— he who inhabits the best of mountains, Ṛkṣavanta, drinking from the Narmadā: lord of all bears, this is the troop-chieftain named Dhūmra."

**6.18.11**  "His younger brother — behold him, mountain-like — resembles him in form yet surpasses him in valor:"

**6.18.12**  "This is he: Jāmbavān by name, a great lord of lords of troops — tranquil, obedient to his elders, yet impatient in the midst of battle."

**6.18.13**  "Great assistance was rendered by this wise Jāmbavān to Śakra during the war of gods and asuras, and many boons were obtained through him."

**6.18.14**  "Climbing to mountain peaks they hurl vast boulders broad as great clouds, and they do not flinch before death."

**6.18.15**  "In his army are many hairy ones resembling rākṣasas and piśācas, roaming about with the fiery energy of fire."

**6.18.16**  "He at whom all monkeys gaze — that lord of lords of troops who stands in his excitement as if about to leap —"

**6.18.17**  "— this lord of monkeys who attends upon thousand-eyed Indra, endowed with strength and power: this is the troop-chieftain named Rambha, O king."

**6.18.18**  "He who, while walking, passes by a mountain standing a league away by his side, and in the same manner reaches a league with a single upward spring of his body —"

**6.18.19**  "— than whom no form more excellent is found among four-footed beings: this famed one is called Saṃnādana, the forefather of monkeys."

**6.18.20**  "He who once gave battle to wise Śakra in the fight and was not defeated — this lord of lords of troops — whose valor in battle equals that of Śakra himself."

**6.18.21**  "This one was born of a gandharva maiden by Kṛṣṇavartman, long ago in the war of gods and asuras, for the purpose of aiding the denizens of the triple heavens."

**6.18.22**  "He whose Jambū mountain is frequented by king Vaiśravaṇa, that king of mountains' lords, attended by many kiṃnaras —"

**6.18.23**  "— ever giving pleasure of sport to your brother, O lord of rākṣasas: there dwells this illustrious, powerful bull among monkeys, who boasts not in battles: the troop-chieftain named Krathana."

**6.18.24**  "Surrounded and attended by a thousand crores of monkeys, this very one hopes to crush Laṅkā with his own division."

**6.18.25**  "He who ranges along the Gaṅgā, terrifying the leaders of elephant herds, remembering the ancient enmity between elephants and monkeys —"

**6.18.26**  "— this troop-lord and leader who dwells in mountain caves, the foremost in the monkey host, who goes along the Himālayan river —"

**6.18.27**  "— who takes his delight on the mountain Uśīrabīja, equal to Mandara, the best of monkeys, even as Śakra himself takes delight in heaven —"

**6.18.28**  "— a thousand hundred-thousands march with him: this is the intolerant chieftain, O king, the troop-chieftain named Pramāthī."

**6.18.29**  "That one you see following him, churning about repeatedly like a cloud lifted by the wind, where this great dust arises —"

**6.18.30**  "These black-faced, terrible, cow-tailed ones of great strength — a hundred hundred-thousands of them — having beheld the bridge —"

**6.18.31**  "— surrounding the cow-tailed, great-speed troop-chieftain named Gavākṣa, they advance to crush Laṅkā by force."

**6.18.32**  "Where bees circle all around and trees bear fruits of every wish — the mountain he circles round, equal in color to the sun itself —"

**6.18.33**  "— by whose light the beasts and birds constantly shine in that same hue, and whose plateau great-souled sages do not abandon —"

**6.18.34**  "— there, O king, on that beautiful Golden Mountain, this one takes his delight: the foremost of foremost monkeys, the troop-chieftain named Kesarī."

**6.18.35**  "Among sixty thousand beautiful golden mountains, that mountain stands supreme in their midst — like yourself, O sinless one, among rākṣasas."

**6.18.36**  "There on that best of mountains dwell these tawny-colored and white ones with copper-red faces, honey-brown, armed with sharp fangs and claws."

**6.18.37**  "Like lions with four fangs, hard to approach as tigers, all equal to the fire of universal dissolution, like blazing venomous serpents."

**6.18.38**  "With very long, curving tails, resembling rutting elephants, looking like great mountains, roaring with the sound of great clouds."

**6.18.39**  "Their lord stands in their midst, endowed with valor, renowned on earth by the name Śatabalī, O king. This very one hopes to crush Laṅkā with his own division."

**6.18.40**  "Gaja, Gavākṣa, Gavaya, Nala and the monkey Nīla — each one of these troop-leaders is attended by ten crores."
