**6.18.41**  "Likewise other foremost monkeys, dwellers on the Vindhya mountain — their numbers are so great they cannot be counted, though their valor is light-footed and swift."

**6.18.42**  "All are of great majesty, O great king; all have bodies resembling great mountains; all are capable of making the earth in a moment strewn and scattered with shattered mountains."

**6.19.1**  Having heard the words of Sāraṇa, Śuka then addressed Rāvaṇa, lord of the rākṣasas, as he surveyed the entire army.

**6.19.2**  Those whom you see standing there, like great elephants in rut, like banyan trees on the Gaṅgā's banks, like śāla trees of the Himālayas —

**6.19.3**  — these, O king, are irresistible, mighty, and can change form at will; they are comparable to the Daityas and Dānavas, and in battle their valor equals that of the gods.

**6.19.4**  Of these there are nine thousand koṭis, five and seven koṭis besides, thousands upon thousands of śaṅkhas, and hundreds of vṛndas.

**6.19.5**  These are the ministers of Sugrīva, ever dwelling in Kiṣkindhā, vānaras born of gods and gandharvas, capable of assuming any form at will.

**6.19.6**  Those two princes whom you see standing there, of divine appearance — they are Mainda and Dvivida; in battle there is none equal to them.

**6.19.7**  Both were granted leave by Brahmā and have drunk the nectar of immortality; these two aspire to crush Laṅkā in battle by their own might.

**6.19.8**  And those two who stand at their sides, resembling mountains — they are Sumukha and Vimukha, sons of Mṛtyu, equal to their father in power.

**6.19.9**  And that vānara whom you see standing there, like a great elephant in must — in his fury he could churn even the ocean by his strength.

**6.19.10**  This one came to Laṅkā before, O lord, on behalf of the Vaidehī and of you yourself — behold, this vānara has returned, the one you saw before.

**6.19.11**  He is the eldest son of Keśarin, known as the son of the Wind-god, celebrated by the name Hanūmān — the one by whom the ocean was leaped across.

**6.19.12**  A shape-shifter, foremost among the vānaras, endowed with strength and form, his movement is irresistible, like that of the ever-moving sun-god.

**6.19.13**  It is said that as a child, consumed by thirst, seeing the rising sun, he descended a path of three thousand yojanas —

**6.19.14**  — having resolved in his mind, 'I will seize the sun, and my hunger shall not return,' this one, drunk on his own power, leaped upward.

**6.19.15**  Unable to reach that god — unapproachable even by gods, seers, and dānavas — he fell upon the mountain at the sun's rising.

**6.19.16**  When this ape fell, one jaw was slightly fractured upon the rocky surface — he of the firm jaw — and it is for this that he is called Hanūmān.

**6.19.17**  Truly, through the authority of scripture this vānara is known to me — his strength, his form, his power are impossible to describe in words.

**6.19.18**  This one aspires to crush Laṅkā single-handedly by his might. And the next hero, the dark one with lotus-like eyes —

**6.19.19**  — a supreme chariot-warrior of the Ikṣvākus, whose valor is celebrated throughout the world, in whom dharma never wavers, and who never transgresses dharma —

**6.19.20**  — who knows the Brahmā-weapon and the Vedas, foremost among those versed in the Vedas, who could split the sky with his arrows and cleave the mountains asunder —

**6.19.21**  — whose wrath is like Death itself and whose valor rivals that of Śakra — that is Rāma, O king, who comes to fight you.

**6.19.22**  And he who stands at his right side, radiant as pure gold, broad-chested, copper-eyed, with dark and curling locks —

**6.19.23**  — this is his brother Lakṣmaṇa, dear to him as his own life, skilled in statecraft and in battle, versed in every science of weapons.

**6.19.24**  He is intolerant of insult, unconquerable, a victor, valiant, wise, and strong — the right arm of Rāma, ever his outer breath of life.

**6.19.25**  For the sake of Rāghava he does not guard his own life; this one himself aspires to slay all the rākṣasas in battle.

**6.19.26**  And he who stands supporting Rāma on his left flank, surrounded by a troop of rākṣasas — he is King Vibhīṣaṇa.

**6.19.27**  Consecrated as king of Laṅkā by the glorious king of kings, it is against you alone that he comes forward, filled with righteous fury, ready for battle.

**6.19.28**  And he whom you see standing in the center, immovable as a mountain — the undefeated lord of all the kings of the monkey-troops —

**6.19.29**  — who surpassed all the vānaras in splendor, fame, wisdom, knowledge, and nobility, as the Himālaya surpasses all other mountains —

**6.19.30**  — who rules Kiṣkindhā, that cave overgrown with dense trees, an inaccessible stronghold set within a mountain fastness, together with his chief commanders —

**6.19.31**  — upon whom this golden garland of a hundred lotuses shines beautifully, lovely to gods and men alike, in whom Lakṣmī herself is established —

**6.19.32**  — this garland, and Tārā, and the eternal kingdom of the vānara king — all this Sugrīva received from Rāma after slaying Vālin.

**6.19.33**  With such a force of a thousand koṭis and a hundred śaṅkus, Sugrīva, lord of the vānaras, advances against you for battle.

**6.19.34**  Seeing this army now arrayed before you, O great king, blazing like a meteor in its splendor, let the utmost effort be made, so that there be victory and not defeat at the hands of the enemy.

**6.20.1**  Having thus heard Śuka's account, and having seen those leaders of the vānara hosts, and his own brother Vibhīṣaṇa standing near Rāma —

**6.20.2**  — and Lakṣmaṇa of tremendous might, the right arm of Rāma, and Sugrīva, king of all the vānaras, of terrible valor —

**6.20.3**  — Rāvaṇa, his heart somewhat disturbed and anger rising within him, at the conclusion of the report threatened those two heroes, Śuka and Sāraṇa.

**6.20.4**  As Śuka and Sāraṇa bowed with downcast faces, he spoke to them harsh words, his voice choked with rage, beside himself with fury.

**6.20.5**  It is not fitting for ministers who live by the king's favor to speak what is disagreeable to him who holds the power of punishment and reward, who is their lord.

**6.20.6**  For you two, it is utterly unsuitable to sing the praises of enemies who advance adversarially intent on battle, and to do so out of season.

**6.20.7**  Teachers, preceptors, and elders have been attended upon by you in vain, since you have failed to grasp the essence of the science of kingship — that which ministers must live by.

**6.20.8**  Or perhaps it was grasped but not understood — such is called the burden of knowledge. By my fate I bear being yoked to foolish ministers such as these.

**6.20.9**  Is there no fear of death in you, that you would speak harsh words to me, whose very tongue, when I command it, delivers what is auspicious and inauspicious?

**6.20.10**  Trees may survive after touching fire and remain standing in the forest; but those who have been touched by a king's displeasure, if they have truly offended, do not survive.

**6.20.11**  I would slay these two sinners who sing the praises of the enemy's side, were my anger not softened by the remembrance of services they have rendered me before.

**6.20.12**  Begone — depart from my presence! I do not wish to slay you, remembering the services you have done for me. Those two ungrateful ones who have turned their affections away from me would be as good as slain.

**6.20.13**  Addressed thus, both Śuka and Sāraṇa, filled with shame, congratulated Rāvaṇa with cries of victory and departed.

**6.20.14**  Then the ten-necked one said to Mahodara who stood nearby: 'Quickly bring before me spies versed in statecraft.'

**6.20.15**  Then the spies, hastened by the king's command, arrived and stood before him with hands folded in salutation, wishing him victory and prosperity.

**6.20.16**  Rāvaṇa, lord of the rākṣasas, then spoke to those spies — trustworthy, brave, devoted, and fearless.

**6.20.17**  Go from here and ascertain Rāma's intentions; those who are privy to his counsels and have joined him with affection —

**6.20.18**  — how he sleeps, how he keeps watch, and what else he plans to do — learn all this with precision and return without omission.

**6.20.19**  An enemy known through espionage is, by wise kings, confronted and repelled in battle with very little effort.

**6.20.20**  The spies, saying 'So be it' and filled with enthusiasm, circumambulated the lord of the rākṣasas and went to where Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa were.

**6.20.21**  Going there, disguised, they beheld near Mount Suvela Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa together with Sugrīva and Vibhīṣaṇa.

**6.20.22**  Those rākṣasas, however, were spotted by Vibhīṣaṇa, that righteous lord of rākṣasas, and were apprehended where they stood, as if by chance.

**6.20.23**  Manhandled by the vānaras — valiant, swift in their movements — they managed to return to Laṅkā, panting and out of their senses.

**6.20.24**  Then those spies, those night-wanderers who move perpetually abroad, presented themselves before the ten-necked one and reported to him of the terrible force of the mighty one encamped near Mount Suvela.

**6.21.1**  Then the spies reported to the lord of Laṅkā of that unassailable army, and of Rāghava encamped upon Mount Suvela.

**6.21.2**  Rāvaṇa, hearing from the spies of Rāma's arrival with his great army, became somewhat agitated and then spoke to Śārdūla.

**6.21.3**  Your complexion is unnatural and you appear dejected, O night-wanderer — have you perhaps fallen into the power of angered enemies?

**6.21.4**  Thus instructed by him, Śārdūla, overwhelmed with fear, slowly began to speak to that tiger among rākṣasas.

**6.21.5**  Those foremost vānaras cannot be spied upon, O king — they are valiant, powerful, and are protected by Rāghava.

**6.21.6**  Nor can one speak with them; no inquiry can be obtained there. Every path is guarded on all sides by those vānaras who are like mountains.

**6.21.7**  The moment I entered that army, which had not yet been spied upon, I was recognized, seized by force by many, and torn apart in many ways.

**6.21.8**  I was beaten heavily with knees, fists, teeth, and open palms, and dragged about by those powerful, wrathful vānaras.

**6.21.9**  Having been dragged everywhere, I was led before Rāma's assembly, my whole body smeared with blood, stupefied, my senses reeling.

**6.21.10**  Even as the vānaras were killing me and I begged with folded hands, I was rescued by Rāghava — and so I live, it seems, by mere chance.

**6.21.11**  Having filled the great ocean with rocks and boulders, Rāma now stands armed at the very gate of Laṅkā.

**6.21.12**  Having deployed in the garuḍa battle-formation, surrounded on all sides by vānaras, the great-souled one released me and now advances directly upon Laṅkā.

**6.21.13**  Before he reaches the rampart, quickly do one of two things: either give Sītā back to him at once, or offer him worthy battle.

**6.21.14**  Inwardly burning with anguish upon hearing Śārdūla's momentous words, the lord of the rākṣasas, Rāvaṇa, then spoke.

**6.21.15**  Even if the gods, gandharvas, and dānavas were all to fight against me, I will not give back Sītā — not even out of fear of all the worlds.

**6.21.16**  Having spoken thus, the great-souled Rāvaṇa spoke again: 'The army you have scouted — who among the leaping ones are the heroes there?'

**6.21.17**  What are they like, and what is the power of those vānaras who are so difficult to assail? Whose sons and grandsons are they? Tell me the truth, O rākṣasa.

**6.21.18**  Having assessed their strength and weakness there, I shall formulate a response — for one who desires battle must necessarily take stock of the enemy's force.

**6.21.19**  Addressed thus by Rāvaṇa, Śārdūla, that foremost of spies, began to speak these words in Rāvaṇa's presence.

**6.21.20**  There is the son of Ṛkṣarajas, O king, who is exceedingly hard to conquer in battle; and the son of Gadgada, known as Jāmbavān.

**6.21.21**  Another son of Gadgada too is present, and a son of Bṛhaspati, the son of Śatakratu himself — by whose single son such slaughter of rākṣasas was wrought.

**6.21.22**  Suṣeṇa too, righteous of soul, a mighty son of Dharma, is there; and gentle Dadhimukha the vānara, son of the moon-god, O king.

**6.21.23**  Sumukha, Durmukha, and the vānara Vegadarśin are also there — Death itself, no doubt, fashioned in the form of vānaras by the Self-born.

**6.21.24**  Nīla, son of the fire-god, is there himself as commander of the army; and the son of the wind-god, known as Hanūmān, is also present.

**6.21.25**  Aṅgada, the young and powerful grandson of Śakra, who is irresistible, is there; and both Mainda and Dvivida, powerful sons of the Aśvins.

**6.21.26**  Sons of Vivasvat are there — five of them, each resembling Death at the end of an age: Gaja, Gavākṣa, Gavaya, Śarabha, and Gandhamādana.

**6.21.27**  Śveta and Jyotirmukha are there, both sprung from the sun-god himself; and Hemakūṭa the vānara, son of Varuṇa.

**6.21.28**  Nala, the valiant son of Viśvakarman, foremost among the vānaras, is there; and the son of the Vasus, the valiant and swift Sudurdhara.

**6.21.29**  Ten koṭis of heroic vānaras eager for battle, glorious sons of gods — the rest I dare not enumerate.

**6.21.30**  This son of Daśaratha is a young man with a lion's frame — the one by whom Dūṣaṇa was slain, and Khara, and Triśiras as well.

**6.21.31**  There is no one in this world equal to Rāma in valor — the one by whom Virādha was slain, and Kabandha, who was like Death itself.

**6.21.32**  No man on earth is capable of recounting Rāma's virtues — the one by whom so many rākṣasas dwelling in Janasthāna were slain.

**6.21.33**  And Lakṣmaṇa here, righteous of soul, like a bull among elephants — once having come within the path of his arrows, even Vāsava himself would not survive.

**6.21.34**  And foremost among rākṣasas, your own brother Vibhīṣaṇa, having accepted the city of Laṅkā, is devoted to the welfare of Rāghava.

**6.21.35**  Thus the entire vānara army encamped on Mount Suvela has been described to you — for what remains to be done, you yourself are the authority.

**6.22.1**  Then the king's spies reported to Rāvaṇa in Laṅkā that Rāghava, with his irresistible army, had encamped on the Suvela mountain.

**6.22.2**  Hearing from the spies that mighty Rāma had arrived, Rāvaṇa became somewhat agitated and spoke thus to his ministers.

**6.22.3**  "Let all the ministers come quickly, fully composed — for this moment of counsel has now arrived for us, O Rākṣasas."

**6.22.4**  Hearing that command of his, the ministers came swiftly; then he deliberated together with his Rākṣasa councillors.

**6.22.5**  That unconquerable one, having deliberated and resolved upon what was appropriate, dismissed his ministers and entered his own palace.

**6.22.6**  Then that great master of illusion, summoning the mighty Rākṣasa Vidyujjihvā, who was skilled in māyā, entered where Maithilī dwelt.

**6.22.7**  The lord of Rākṣasas spoke to Vidyujjihvā, who was versed in illusion: "We shall bewilder Sītā, the daughter of Janaka, through māyā."

**6.22.8**  "Taking a head fashioned of illusion belonging to Rāghava, O night-wanderer, present yourself before me, along with a great bow fitted with an arrow."

**6.22.9**  So addressed, the night-wanderer Vidyujjihvā said "So be it." The king was pleased with him and bestowed upon him an ornament.

**6.22.10**  That mighty one — the younger brother of Kubera — entered the Aśoka grove and there beheld her who was afflicted though undeserving of affliction, head bowed, overwhelmed by grief, seated on the ground.

**6.22.11**  She was meditating only upon her husband, having come to be in the Aśoka grove, and was being attended upon by terrible Rākṣasī women not far away.

**6.22.12**  Approaching Sītā then, proclaiming his joy by name, he spoke this bold speech to the daughter of Janaka.

**6.22.13**  "While I was comforting you, O fair one, he upon whom you relied and exulted — Rāghava, the slayer of Khara, your husband — has been slain in battle."

**6.22.14**  "Your root has been cut off on every side; your pride has been destroyed by me. Through my own supremacy, O Sītā, you shall become my wife."

**6.22.15**  "O woman of little merit, whose purpose is thwarted, foolish one who fancies herself wise — hear, O Sītā, of your husband's slaughter, dreadful as the slaying of Vṛtra."

**6.22.16**  "Rāghava had indeed come to the ocean's edge to slay me, accompanied by a great army led by the king of monkeys."

**6.22.17**  "Rāma, having encamped with his great army on the southern shore of the ocean, was there as the sun went to its setting."

**6.22.18**  "Then at midnight, with the army weary from the march and sleeping in ease — this was first reconnoitered by spies —"

**6.22.19**  "— my great army, led by Prahasta, slaughtered his army at night, there where Rāma was along with Lakṣmaṇa."

**6.22.20**  "Broad-bladed swords, iron clubs, scimitars, discuses, great iron maces, volleys of arrows, spears, gleaming hammers —"

**6.22.21**  "— staves, javelins, lances, discuses, and pestles — these were raised again and again by the Rākṣasas and hurled upon the monkeys."

**6.22.22**  "Then the crushing Prahasta, with a swift and practiced hand, severed with a great sword the head of Rāma while he slept."

**6.22.23**  "Vibhīṣaṇa, leaping up, was seized by chance and driven away in all directions; and Lakṣmaṇa was expelled along with all the monkeys."

**6.22.24**  "Sugrīva, the lord of monkeys, lies with his neck broken; Hanūmān lies with his jaw knocked out, slain by the Rākṣasas."

**6.22.25**  "Jāmbavān, having leaped up upon his knees, was struck down in battle, cut to pieces by many broad swords, felled like a tree."

**6.22.26**  "Both Mainda and Dvivida, those two bull-monkeys, have been slain, gasping and wailing, drenched in blood."

**6.22.27**  "Panasa, that destroyer of foes, struck by a sword and cut in two at the middle, groans upon the earth like a breadfruit tree."

**6.22.28**  "Darīmukha lies in a cave, cut through by many iron arrows; the mighty Kumuda lies crying out, struck by shafts."

**6.22.29**  "Aṅgada, assailed by the Rākṣasas, cut through by many arrows, has been felled — Aṅgada has fallen to the ground vomiting blood."

**6.22.30**  "Some monkeys were crushed by elephants, others laid low and trampled by a mass of chariots there, like storm clouds by the force of wind."

**6.22.31**  "Others fled in terror, struck down from behind, pursued by the Rākṣasas like great elephants by lions."

**6.22.32**  "Some fell into the ocean, some took refuge in the sky; the bears climbed trees, mixed together with the monkeys."

**6.22.33**  "On the shores of the ocean, on the mountains, and in the forests, many of those tawny-eyed ones were slain by the many misshapen-eyed Rākṣasas."

**6.22.34**  "Thus your husband along with his army has been slain by my army; and this — his head, wet with blood and covered with dust — has been brought here."

**6.22.35**  Then Rāvaṇa, most unconquerable lord of the Rākṣasas, while Sītā was listening, spoke thus to a Rākṣasī.

**6.22.36**  "Bring to me the Rākṣasa Vidyujjihvā, cruel in his deeds, the one by whom that head of Rāghava was personally brought from the battlefield."

**6.22.37**  Vidyujjihvā then, taking that head along with the bow and arrows, bowed his own head in salutation and stood before Rāvaṇa.

**6.22.38**  Then the king Rāvaṇa spoke to the Rākṣasa who stood near him — Vidyujjihvā of the great tongue, who hovered at his side.

**6.22.39**  "Place quickly before Sītā the head of the son of Daśaratha — let the wretched woman behold well her husband's final state."

**6.22.40**  So commanded, that Rākṣasa set down that fair-looking head before Sītā and swiftly vanished from sight.

**6.22.41**  Rāvaṇa also flung down a great gleaming bow famous throughout the three worlds, and spoke thus to Sītā.

**6.22.42**  "This is that bow of your Rāma, along with its bowstring — brought here by Prahasta after slaying that mortal in the night."

**6.22.43**  And Rāvaṇa, having together with Vidyujjihvā strewn that head and bow upon the ground, then spoke to that illustrious daughter of the king of Videha: "Come under my sway."

**6.23.1**  Sītā, seeing that head and that excellent bow — and the meeting with Sugrīva that had been reported by Hanūmān —

**6.23.2**  — the eyes, the complexion, and the face resembling her husband's face, the hair and the hairline, and that beautiful crest jewel —

**6.23.3**  — recognizing these by all these tokens, deeply grief-stricken, she then denounced Kaikeyī, crying out like an osprey.

**6.23.4**  "May you have your wish, O Kaikeyī — this joy of the family has been slain. The entire family has been destroyed by you, O quarrelsome one."

**6.23.5**  "What wrong indeed had the noble Rāma done to Kaikeyī, that she sent him from home, clad in bark garments, to the forest?"

**6.23.6**  Having spoken thus, the trembling, suffering Vaidehī, young as she was, fell to the earth like a plantain tree cut down.

**6.23.7**  She, having recovered after a moment and regaining her senses, smelled that head and lamented, that large-eyed one.

**6.23.8**  "Alas, I am undone, O mighty-armed one! I who faithfully followed a hero's vow — I have come to this final state of yours, made a widow."

**6.23.9**  "For a woman, they say, a husband's failing is the first death. You who were so nobly conducted toward one of noble conduct — you have preceded me."

**6.23.10**  "For me, who was plunged from sorrow into sorrow, submerged in an ocean of grief — even you who had set out to rescue me have been struck down."

**6.23.11**  "That mother-in-law of mine, Kausalyā, loving as she is, has been made, O Rāghava, bereft of her son by you, like a cow bereft of its calf."

**6.23.12**  "Those who decreed for you a long life, O you of inconceivable prowess — their words were false; you are short-lived, O Rāghava."

**6.23.13**  "Or perhaps even the wisdom of a wise and virtuous person is destroyed — for time, the origin of all beings, cooks them accordingly."

**6.23.14**  "How did you meet an unforeseen death, you who were versed in the science of statecraft, skilled in the means of countering adversities, deft at avoiding them?"

**6.23.15**  "Thus you, lotus-eyed one, embracing me, were torn away and taken by that savage, utterly cruel night of doom."

**6.23.16**  "You lie here, O mighty-armed bull among men, having abandoned me, the suffering one — as one abandons the beloved earth, that fair woman."

**6.23.17**  "This bow of yours, adorned with gold, beloved of me — O hero, it was always carefully honored by me with fragrant garlands."

**6.23.18**  "You have surely met in heaven, O sinless one, with your father Daśaratha — my father-in-law — and with your ancestral forebears."

**6.23.19**  "You have become a star in the heavens; great and beloved deeds have been done. You look down upon the holy lineage of royal sages that is your own."

**6.23.20**  "Why do you not look at me, O king? Why do you not speak to me — to me, your wife, your companion, the young one who was joined to you in youth?"

**6.23.21**  "What you promised while taking my hand — 'I shall go with you' — remember that, O Kākutstha, and take me too, who am in such distress."

**6.23.22**  "Why have you gone, abandoning me, O best among those who move — from this world to that other world, leaving me here in sorrow?"

**6.23.23**  "That body of yours, worthy of all auspicious things, embraced by me alone — that body is surely now being torn apart by flesh-eaters."

**6.23.24**  "You who had performed sacrifices such as the Agniṣṭoma and others, with proper dakṣiṇā given — why shall you not obtain the proper rites of the Agnihotra?"

**6.23.25**  "Kausalyā, frantic with grief, will question Lakṣmaṇa, the one who has come back of the three who set out into the forest."

**6.23.26**  "To her who questions, he will surely tell of the slaughter of Mitra and Bala, and of your slaughter by the Rākṣasas in the night."

**6.23.27**  "Hearing that you were slain while asleep, and that I have gone to the Rākṣasa's house — she will be no more, O Rāghava, her heart burst asunder."

**6.23.28**  "Do well, O Rāvaṇa — cast me quickly upon Rāma; unite wife with husband — do the highest good."

**6.23.29**  "Join my head to his head, my body to his body. I will follow, after Rāvaṇa, the course of my noble husband — I do not wish to live even a moment more with this sinful life."

**6.23.30**  "I heard in my father's house from Brāhmaṇas versed in the Vedas: those women whose husband is dear to them — for those women the worlds are of great glory."

**6.23.31**  "In whom forgiveness, self-restraint, generosity, truth, righteousness, gratitude, and non-injury toward all beings resided — without him, what course is there for me?"

**6.23.32**  Thus the large-eyed daughter of Janaka, scorched by grief, lamented there, gazing upon her husband's head and bow.

**6.23.33**  While Sītā was lamenting thus, a Rākṣasa who stood in the ranks approached his master there with hands cupped in reverence.

**6.23.34**  Saluting and propitiating him with "Be victorious, O son of a noble," he announced that Prahasta, the commander of the army, had arrived.

**6.23.35**  "Prahasta has come with all the ministers. There is some urgent matter — grant them an audience."

**6.23.36**  Hearing this, reported by the Rākṣasa, the ten-necked one left the Aśoka grove and went to give audience to the ministers.

**6.23.37**  He, having fully deliberated upon all his own duties with the ministers, entered the assembly hall and made his arrangements, knowing well Rāma's prowess.

**6.23.38**  That head and that excellent bow vanished into invisibility immediately upon Rāvaṇa's departure.

**6.23.39**  The lord of the Rākṣasas, together with those ministers of fearsome prowess, then deliberated upon and resolved the matter of what was to be done about Rāma.

**6.23.40**  Rāvaṇa, the lord of Rākṣasas, resembling Death itself, spoke to all the commanders of the army who stood not far away, those who sought his welfare.

**6.23.41**  "Swiftly, with the resounding beat of drums struck on their corners, muster my armies — no reason need be given."

**6.23.42**  Then those army commanders, accepting his word with "So be it," mustered their own great forces and reported to their lord — he who was eager for battle — that they had assembled.

**6.24.1**  Seeing Sītā bewildered, a Rākṣasī named Saramā — a dear and affectionate friend — quickly approached Vaidehī.

**6.24.2**  She had there been made a friend by Sītā even while Sītā was being guarded; she was beloved by Rāvaṇa, yet she protected Sītā, compassionate and firm in her purpose.

**6.24.3**  Saramā saw her friend Sītā senseless, having partially risen and then collapsed, like a mare in the dust.

**6.24.4**  That friend of noble vows comforted her with affection, knowing all that Rāvaṇa had said to her and all that she herself had said in reply.

**6.24.5**  "Out of a friend's love, O timid one, I listened to everything, concealed in the dense thicket, casting off fear of Rāvaṇa — for your sake, O large-eyed one, my life is not dear to me."

**6.24.6**  "And for what reason the lord of Rākṣasas departed in agitation — all that has become known to me upon his departure, O Maithilī."

**6.24.7**  "It is impossible to perform a night attack against Rāma, whose self is known and alert; and indeed the slaughter befits that tiger among men alone."

**6.24.8**  "Nor can the tree-dwelling monkeys be slain — they are well-guarded by Rāma, as the gods are guarded by the bull among gods."

**6.24.9**  "Long and rounded of arm, radiant, broad-chested, of great majesty, a bowman endowed with a firm physique, righteous in soul, renowned in the world —"

**6.24.10**  "— valiant, ever the protector of himself and others, together with his brother Lakṣmaṇa, skilled, versed in the science of statecraft —"

**6.24.11**  "— destroyer of enemy hosts, of inconceivable strength and valor — glorious Rāghava, the crusher of foes, has not been slain, O Sītā."

**6.24.12**  "This māyā has been deployed against you by that savage who is skilled in illusion, whose acts of mind are crooked, who is hostile to all beings."

**6.24.13**  "All your grief has departed; good fortune stands before you. Prosperity surely attends you — hear something dear and joyful."

**6.24.14**  "Rāma, having crossed the ocean with his army of monkeys, has encamped having reached the southern shore of the sea."

**6.24.15**  "I have seen Kākutstha, his purpose fully accomplished, together with Lakṣmaṇa, standing guarded, his forces arrayed at the ocean's edge."

**6.24.16**  "And the Rākṣasas sent out by Rāvaṇa — those swift ones — have brought back this intelligence: that Rāghava has crossed over."

**6.24.17**  "Having heard that intelligence, O large-eyed one, the lord of the Rākṣasas — this Rāvaṇa — is now deliberating with all his ministers."

**6.24.18**  As the Rākṣasī Saramā was speaking thus with Sītā, they heard the dreadful sound of armies in full mobilization.

**6.24.19**  Hearing the great sound of the drum struck by the drum-stick, the sweet-speaking Saramā said this to Sītā.

**6.24.20**  "This dread drum that calls men to armor — O timid one, hear the deep resonance of the drum, like the thunder of rain-clouds."

**6.24.21**  "Raging war-elephants are being harnessed, chariot-horses are being yoked; here and there armored infantry come rushing."

**6.24.22**  "The royal highways are filling with armies of wondrous appearance, swift and thundering, as the ocean fills with torrents of water."

**6.24.23**  "Of polished weapons, shields, and coats of mail, of chariots, horses, and elephants, and of the adorned Rākṣasas —"

**6.24.24**  "— behold the radiance of many colors rising up, like that of fire in its proper form when it burns a forest."

**6.24.25**  "Hear the clanging of bells, hear the rumble of chariots, hear the neighing of horses and the sound of martial instruments."

**6.24.26**  "This tumultuous, hair-raising commotion is that of the Rākṣasas, weapons raised in hand, following in the train of the lord of Rākṣasas."

**6.24.27**  "Prosperity, the destroyer of grief, attends you; fear has come upon the Rākṣasas from Rāma, O lotus-petal-eyed one, as fear came upon the Daityas from Vāsava."

**6.24.28**  "Conquering him, having mastered his wrath, that one of inconceivable prowess — your husband — will slay Rāvaṇa in battle and come to you."

**6.24.29**  "Your husband, together with Lakṣmaṇa, will wage valiant war against the Rākṣasas, as Vāsava together with Viṣṇu wages war against his enemies."

**6.24.30**  "When Rāma has arrived and the enemy has been laid low, I shall quickly see you — virtuous one, your purpose achieved — held in his embrace."

**6.24.31**  "You, O beautiful one, will shed tears born of joy, having met and been embraced upon the broad chest of that broad-chested one."

**6.24.32**  "Before long, O Sītā, O queen, mighty Rāma will free this braid that has fallen to your hips, kept these many months."

**6.24.33**  "Seeing his face, O queen, risen like the full moon, you will shed the water born of grief, as a serpent sheds its slough."

**6.24.34**  "Having slain Rāvaṇa in battle before long, O Maithilī, he who deserves happiness will attain happiness, complete, together with you, his beloved."

**6.24.35**  "Reunited with Rāma the great-souled one, you shall rejoice, as the earth rejoices when blessed with good rain and crops."

**6.24.36**  "He who swiftly circles the best of mountains all around, like a horse making its circuit — seek refuge here, O queen, in that maker of day, for he is the origin of all creatures."

**6.25.1**  Then Saramā comforted her who had grown distressed and bewildered by those words, even as the sky refreshes the earth with water.

**6.25.2**  Then that friend, wishing to do what was beneficial for her companion, and knowing the right moment, spoke words at the proper time, first smiling before she addressed her.

**6.25.3**  "O dark-eyed one, I am able to go, convey your message to Rāma, learn of his welfare, and return unseen."

**6.25.4**  "For when I move through the unsupported sky, neither the wind nor even Garuḍa is capable of tracking my course."

**6.25.5**  To Saramā speaking thus, Sītā, overcome by former grief, spoke again in sweet and gentle words.

**6.25.6**  "I know that you are capable of going to the sky or even to the netherworld; I understand both what should and should not be done on my behalf."

**6.25.7**  "If you wish to do what pleases me, and if your resolve is firm, I desire you to go and find out what Rāvaṇa is doing."

**6.25.8**  "For that cruel Rāvaṇa, whose power lies in illusion and who causes the enemy to cry out, bewilders me, wicked-souled as he is, just as wine intoxicates one who has drunk it."

**6.25.9**  "He has me threatened constantly and frightened repeatedly by the most terrible rākṣasī women who guard me at all times."

**6.25.10**  "I am anxious and fearful, and my mind finds no peace; it is out of that fear and anxiety that I have come to the Aśoka grove."

**6.25.11**  "If you were to report to me his counsel and whatever his settled resolve might be, that would be the greatest favor you could do for me."

**6.25.12**  Then Saramā of sweet speech, touching Sītā as she spoke thus, trembling with tears, addressed her in these words.

**6.25.13**  "If this is your wish, then I shall go, O Jānakī; wait and see me return with knowledge of the enemy's intention."

**6.25.14**  Having spoken thus, she went to the vicinity of that rākṣasa and heard what Rāvaṇa was saying together with his ministers.

**6.25.15**  She, who was skilled at discerning resolve, having heard the settled intention of that evil-souled one, quickly came back once more to the Aśoka grove.

**6.25.16**  Entering there again, she saw the daughter of Janaka waiting for her, like Śrī herself bereft of her lotus.

**6.25.17**  Sītā, warmly embracing Saramā of sweet speech who had now returned, herself offered her a seat.

**6.25.18**  "Sit here comfortably and tell me everything truly — the settled resolve of that cruel, evil-souled Rāvaṇa."

**6.25.19**  Thus addressed by Sītā who was trembling, Saramā related everything that had been said by Rāvaṇa together with his ministers.

**6.25.20**  "The lord of rākṣasas was indeed urged at length by his mother and by the senior minister for the purpose of releasing you, O Vaidehī, and advised by that wise elder of the council."

**6.25.21**  "'Let Maithilī be honourably given back to the lord of men; the marvel that occurred at Janasthāna is proof enough for you —'

**6.25.22**  "'— the crossing of the ocean, the appearance of Hanūmān, the slaying of the rākṣasas in battle: what mere mortal man on earth could do these things?'

**6.25.23**  "Though he was spoken to at length thus by the elder ministers and by his mother, he does not have the will to release you — like a miser who clings to wealth."

**6.25.24**  "'He will not release you without being slain in battle' — this, O Maithilī, is the firm resolve of that cruel one together with his ministers."

**6.25.25**  "This very firm determination has arisen from his greed driven by death; out of fear he is unable to release you unless he is repelled in battle — and that only through the slaying of all the rākṣasas and of himself."

**6.25.26**  "Having slain Rāvaṇa in battle by all means with his sharp arrows, Rāma will lead you back to Ayodhyā, O dark-eyed one."

**6.25.27**  In the meantime, the sound filled with the tumult of kettledrums and conches was heard throughout all the armies, making the earth tremble.

**6.25.28**  Hearing that sound of the vānara army, the servants of the rākṣasa king stationed in Laṅkā, their energy gone and their movements overcome by despondency, saw no good outcome — owing to the king's faults.

**6.26.1**  To the sound of those kettledrums mingled with conches, Rāghava, the mighty-armed Rāma, conqueror of enemy cities, drew near.

**6.26.2**  Upon hearing that tumult, Rāvaṇa, lord of the rākṣasas, sat in contemplation for a moment and then looked up at his ministers.

**6.26.3**  Then the greatly powerful Rāvaṇa, addressing all those ministers there and making the entire assembly resound, spoke thus.

**6.26.4**  "What you have reported regarding Rāma — the crossing of the ocean, his prowess, and the massing of his forces — I have heard; and I too know you to be those whose valour in battle is true."

**6.26.5**  Then a rākṣasa of very great wisdom named Mālyavān, the maternal great-grandfather of Rāvaṇa, having heard Rāvaṇa's words, spoke.

**6.26.6**  "O king, a king who is well trained in the sciences and follows sound statecraft rules his sovereignty long and brings his enemies under control."

**6.26.7**  "For one who makes peace at the right time, who fights with enemies when necessary, and who strengthens his own side, enjoys great sovereignty."

**6.26.8**  "A king who is weak should make peace, and even one who is equal in strength should do so; one should not despise an enemy, but one who is stronger may resort to open war."

**6.26.9**  "Therefore, O Rāvaṇa, a treaty with Rāma seems right to me; the Sītā for whose sake we have been assailed should be given back to him."

**6.26.10**  "All the divine sages and the gandharvas desire his victory; do not come into conflict with him — let a treaty with him be pleasing to you."

**6.26.11**  "For the venerable Pitāmaha created only two sides — that of the suras and that of the asuras — and upon these rested Dharma and Adharma respectively."

**6.26.12**  "Dharma is indeed said to be the side of the suras and the great-souled ones; Adharma is the side of the rākṣasas and the asuras, O Rāvaṇa."

**6.26.13**  "When Dharma swallows Adharma, the Kṛta age comes into being; when Adharma swallows Dharma, the Tiṣya (Kali) age commences."

**6.26.14**  "By your ranging through the worlds, great Dharma has been destroyed and Adharma has been embraced; on that account the others — our adversaries — have grown stronger than us."

**6.26.15**  "That Adharma, grown large through your negligence, swallows us like a serpent; and it enlarges the side of the suras, nurtured by the divine order."

**6.26.16**  "By you, who are given over to sense-pleasures and who acts impulsively, great distress has been caused to the sages who are like fire; their blazing power is irresistible, like a kindled conflagration."

**6.26.17**  "These twice-born ones, whose souls are refined by austerity and who delight in sustaining Dharma, worship perpetually with foremost sacrifices of every kind."

**6.26.18**  "They pour oblations into the sacred fires according to rule and chant the Vedas aloud; overpowering the rākṣasas, they raise the thunder of the brahman-sound, scattering in all directions like thunderclouds in the hot season."

**6.26.19**  "The smoke that rises from the Agnihotra of those sages who are like fire, pervading all ten directions, draws away the vitality of the rākṣasas."

**6.26.20**  "And in those various sacred regions, the fierce austerity practised by those firm in their vows torments the rākṣasas."

**6.26.21**  "Having seen the many and various dreadful portents of every kind, I foresee the destruction of all the rākṣasas."

**6.26.22**  "Dreadful and terrifying clouds thunder harshly, and they rain warm blood upon Laṅkā from every direction."

**6.26.23**  "Tears fall from the weeping draught-animals; the banners have fallen and are discoloured and do not gleam as before."

**6.26.24**  "Wolves, jackals, and vultures cry out most dreadfully; they enter Laṅkā incessantly and gather in assemblies."

**6.26.25**  "Dark-complexioned women with white teeth stand in front and laugh; and in dreams women despoil houses and jeer at them."

**6.26.26**  "Dogs devour the offering-oblations of households; donkeys are born of cows; rats consort with mongooses."

**6.26.27**  "Cats couple with leopards; pigs with dogs; kiṃnaras meet with rākṣasas, and rākṣasas with humans."

**6.26.28**  "Pale birds with red feet, impelled by fate, and doves, wander about for the destruction of the rākṣasas."

**6.26.29**  "Mynas perched in the houses cry 'cīkī kūcī'; and the quarrelsome ones, vanquished, fall down entangled together."

**6.26.30**  "A dreadful, misshapen, bald man, dark-tawny in colour — Time itself — surveys everyone's houses again and again at the appointed hour; and these and other evil omens keep arising."

**6.26.31**  "We believe Rāma to be Viṣṇu himself, who has assumed a human body; for that Rāghava of firm prowess is not a mere human being."

**6.26.32**  "By whom that most wondrous bridge across the ocean has been built — make peace with that king of men, Rāma, O Rāvaṇa."

**6.26.33**  Having spoken these words there and gauging the mind of the lord of rākṣasas, Mālyavān — that powerful one of supreme manly prowess among the greatest — fell silent and waited, watching Rāvaṇa.

**6.27.1**  But that beneficial speech spoken by Mālyavān, the ten-faced one, evil-souled and fallen under the sway of Time, did not receive with patience.

**6.27.2**  He, having knitted his brows on his face, having come under the sway of anger, his eyes rolling with indignation, then spoke to Mālyavān.

**6.27.3**  "That harsh speech which is spoken with an ostensibly beneficial intention but is actually harmful, spoken by one who has gone over to the enemy's side — this does not enter my ears."

**6.27.4**  "By what reasoning do you consider that wretched Rāma, a mere human, alone and dependent on tree-dwelling monkeys, abandoned by his father to a forest dwelling, to be capable?"

**6.27.5**  "By what reasoning do you consider me, lord of the rākṣasas and cause of terror to the gods, to be inferior — me who is not inferior in any of the heroic arts?"

**6.27.6**  "I suspect you have spoken these harsh words to me out of hatred for heroes, out of partiality toward the enemy, or out of encouragement by the other side."

**6.27.7**  "For who among the learned, who know the essence of the scriptures, would speak harshly to one who is powerful and established in his position, without being instigated by the enemy?"

**6.27.8**  "Having brought Sītā from the forest — like Śrī deprived of her lotus — why should I return her out of fear of Rāghava?"

**6.27.9**  "Within a few days you shall see Rāghava together with Sugrīva and Lakṣmaṇa, surrounded by tens of millions of vānaras, slain by me."

**6.27.10**  "Rāvaṇa, before whom even the gods do not stand firm in single combat and in battle — why should he harbour fear in war?"

**6.27.11**  "I would sooner be split in two than bow to anyone; this is an innate fault in me, a nature difficult to overcome."

**6.27.12**  "And if a bridge was built across the ocean by Rāma merely by chance, what wonder is there in that, that fear has come upon you because of it?"

**6.27.13**  "But that Rāma, having crossed the ocean with his vānara army — I promise you truly he shall not return alive."

**6.27.14**  Recognizing Rāvaṇa as he spoke thus, inflamed and enraged, Mālyavān, abashed, did not offer any reply.

**6.27.15**  Having duly pronounced the benediction of victory upon the king in the fitting manner, Mālyavān, having received leave to depart, went to his own dwelling.

**6.27.16**  But Rāvaṇa the rākṣasa, having deliberated and reflected together with his ministers, caused the incomparable fortification of Laṅkā to be arranged.

**6.27.17**  He assigned the rākṣasa Prahasta to the eastern gate, and the two great-valiant ones, Mahāpārśva and Mahōdara, to the southern gate.

**6.27.18**  And to the western gate he likewise assigned his son Indrajit, the master of great illusion, surrounded by many rākṣasas.

**6.27.19**  Having assigned Śuka and Sāraṇa to the northern city gate, he said to those ministers: "I myself shall be here."

**6.27.20**  And the rākṣasa Virūpākṣa, of great might and valour, he stationed in the central reserve force together with many rākṣasas.

**6.27.21**  Having made these arrangements for Laṅkā, that bull among rākṣasas considered his purpose accomplished — though he had come under the sway of Fate.

**6.27.22**  He then dismissed the ministers having commanded the complete arrangement of the city's defence; honoured with benedictions of victory and hymns by the assembly of counsellors, he entered his great and opulent inner palace.

**6.28.1**  Those two kings, of men and monkeys, and Hanūmat the son of the Wind, Jāmbavān king of the bears, and Vibhīṣaṇa the rākṣasa —

**6.28.2**  Aṅgada son of Vālin, Lakṣmaṇa son of Sumitrā, the monkey Śarabha, Suṣeṇa with his kinsmen, and Mainda and Dvivida —

**6.28.3**  Gaja, Gavākṣa, Kumuda, Nala, and Panasa — having reached the territory of the enemy, they assembled and deliberated.

**6.28.4**  "This is that Laṅkā, seen before us now, the city guarded by Rāvaṇa — unconquerable even by asuras, serpents, gandharvas, and the immortal gods."

**6.28.5**  "With success of our mission as the foremost aim, deliberate and reach a firm resolve, for Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas, is ever present here within."

**6.28.6**  While they were speaking thus, Vibhīṣaṇa, younger brother of Rāvaṇa, spoke words refined in diction and rich in meaning:

**6.28.7**  "Anala, Śarabha, Saṃpāti, and Praghasa — my ministers — went to the city of Laṅkā and have now returned here."

**6.28.8**  "Disguising themselves as birds, they all entered the enemy's forces, observed every arrangement that had been made, and then came back."

**6.28.9**  "O Rāma, hear from me all the dispositions of that evil-souled Rāvaṇa exactly as they have reported them:"

**6.28.10**  "At the eastern gate, Prahasta stands in force. At the southern gate stand the two of great valor, Mahāpārśva and Mahodara."

**6.28.11**  "Indrajit holds the western gate, surrounded by many rākṣasas bearing swords and bows, and carrying tridents and maces in their hands."

**6.28.12**  "Rāvaṇa's son is surrounded by heroes armed with various weapons, and by many thousands of rākṣasas with weapons in hand."

**6.28.13**  "Alert and extremely vigilant, surrounded by many rākṣasas, Rāvaṇa himself has taken his post at the northern gate of the city."

**6.28.14**  "Virūpākṣa, with a great force of rākṣasas bearing tridents, swords, and bows, has taken up position at the central reserve."

**6.28.15**  "Having observed all these guard-posts of this kind in Laṅkā, all my ministers swiftly returned here."

**6.28.16**  "In the city there are a thousand elephants, ten thousand chariots, twenty thousand horses, and more than a crore of rākṣasas."

**6.28.17**  "These night-rangers are valiant and powerful, aggressive in battle, and are ever the favorites of the king of rākṣasas."

**6.28.18**  "O lord of the people, for each single rākṣasa in battle readiness, a retinue of a thousand thousand stands in attendance."

**6.28.19**  Having reported this intelligence of Laṅkā as related by his ministers, Vibhīṣaṇa then spoke this reply to lotus-eyed Rāma:

**6.28.20**  "O Rāma, when Rāvaṇa fought against Kubera, sixty hundred thousand rākṣasas marched out to battle."

**6.28.21**  "He who equals Rāvaṇa the evil-souled in prowess, valor, energy, and the dignity of spirit — and matches him in pride —

**6.28.22**  "— let no anger be felt on this account; I do not mean to provoke or frighten you, for you are capable by your valor even of subduing the gods."

**6.28.23**  "Therefore, surrounded by a great fourfold army, you shall array this monkey host and crush Rāvaṇa."

**6.28.24**  As Rāvaṇa's younger brother was speaking thus, Rāghava spoke these words with the purpose of repelling his enemies:

**6.28.25**  "Let Nīla, foremost among monkeys, surrounded by many monkeys, be the combatant to oppose Prahasta at Laṅkā's eastern gate."

**6.28.26**  "And Aṅgada son of Vālin, attended by a great force, shall press hard at the southern gate against Mahāpārśva and Mahodara."

**6.28.27**  "Hanūmat, son of the Wind, of immeasurable soul, shall overwhelm the western gate and enter in, surrounded by many monkeys."

**6.28.28**  "He who delights in outraging hosts of daityas, dānavas, and great-souled sages — that vile one, emboldened by the boon he has received —

**6.28.29**  "— who roams through all the worlds tormenting its creatures: against that lord of rākṣasas I myself am resolved for his slaying."

**6.28.30**  "I, together with Lakṣmaṇa son of Sumitrā, shall assault and enter the northern gate of the city with my full force, where Rāvaṇa stands."

**6.28.31**  "The mighty king of monkeys, Sugrīva, the king of bears Jāmbavān, and the younger brother of the lord of rākṣasas, Vibhīṣaṇa — let them remain at the central reserve."

**6.28.32**  "The monkeys must not assume human form in battle — let this be our distinguishing mark for the monkey forces in this war."

**6.28.33**  "Monkeys alone shall be the distinction recognized among our own people; but we seven shall fight the enemies in human form."

**6.28.34**  "I myself, with my brother Lakṣmaṇa of great might — and this Vibhīṣaṇa, my friend, as the fifth of our own number —"

**6.28.35**  Having spoken thus to Vibhīṣaṇa with the aim of accomplishing his purpose, wise Rāma formed the resolve to ascend Mount Suvela.

**6.28.36**  Then great-souled Rāma, covering all the earth with his mighty host, joyous in bearing and with his mind fixed on the slaying of the enemy, great-souled as he was, advanced toward Laṅkā.

**6.29.1**  Having resolved upon ascending Suvela, Rāma, followed by Lakṣmaṇa, spoke these words to Sugrīva:

**6.29.2**  — and with gentle and excellent speech addressed Vibhīṣaṇa, that night-ranger who knows righteousness, who is devoted, learned in counsel, and skilled in procedure:

**6.29.3**  "Let us all ascend this lord of mountains, Suvela, resplendent with a hundred minerals, and spend this night here."

**6.29.4**  "And we shall survey Laṅkā, the abode of that rākṣasa, that evil-souled one who carried off my wife to bring about his own death."

**6.29.5**  "By whom neither righteousness was understood, nor conduct, nor lineage — by whom, with the vile intellect of a rākṣasa, that censurable deed was done."

**6.29.6**  "At whose very mention my fury mounts, that most base of rākṣasas — on account of whose transgression I shall witness the destruction of the rākṣasas."

**6.29.7**  "For one who is in the grip of the noose of Time commits sin alone, and through that base one's self-wrought transgression, his entire lineage perishes."

**6.29.8**  Speaking thus in counsel, his anger directed at Rāvaṇa, Rāma climbed Suvela with its variegated peaks for his night's lodging.

**6.29.9**  Behind him Lakṣmaṇa followed, composed and attentive, raising his great bow with its arrow nocked, delighting in great feats of valor.

**6.29.10**  Sugrīva ascended after him, along with his ministers, and Vibhīṣaṇa; Hanūmat, Aṅgada, Nīla, Mainda, and Dvivida as well.

**6.29.11**  Gaja, Gavākṣa, Gavaya, Śarabha, Gandhamādana, Panasa, Kumuda, and the troop-leader Hara and Rambha —

**6.29.12**  — these and many other monkeys, swift of movement, swift as the wind's rush, those frequenters of mountains, climbed by the hundreds Mount Suvela where Rāghava was.

**6.29.13**  In no long time, having climbed the mountain on all sides, they saw the city on its summit, hanging as it were suspended in the sky.

**6.29.14**  The leaders of monkey troops saw Laṅkā, beautiful, with its magnificent gates, graced by its excellent ramparts, and filled with rākṣasas.

**6.29.15**  Those foremost among monkeys saw another wall formed by dark-bodied night-rangers stationed upon the battlements — a living rampart.

**6.29.16**  All the monkeys, seeing those rākṣasas eager for battle, let out mighty roars there in Rāma's sight.

**6.29.17**  Then the sun set, flushed with the hues of dusk, and night drew near, lit by the full moon like a lamp.

**6.29.18**  Then Rāma, lord of the monkey host, greeted and honored by Vibhīṣaṇa, together with Lakṣmaṇa and surrounded by throngs of troop-leaders, rested comfortably on the crest of Suvela.

**6.30.1**  Having passed that night there on Suvela, those mighty monkey heroes, those warriors, beheld the groves and gardens of Laṅkā.

**6.30.2**  Even, pleasant, broad, and extensive — delightful to the eye — seeing them, they were filled with wonder.

**6.30.3**  Teeming with campaka, aśoka, puṃnāga, śāla, and palm trees, overhung with groves of tamāla, wrapped in garlands of nāga trees —

**6.30.4**  — with hintāla and arjuna trees, nīpa, and flowering saptaparṇa, tilaka, karṇikāra, and pāṭalī on every side —

**6.30.5**  — Laṅkā shone splendid with trees whose boughs were in bloom, entwined with creepers, and adorned with many kinds of divine plants, like Indra's Amarāvatī.

**6.30.6**  With variegated flowers, red and tender shoots, stretches of blue-green lawn, and colorful rows of forest trees —

**6.30.7**  — the trees there bore fragrant, beautiful flowers and fruits, wearing them like ornaments, as men do.

**6.30.8**  That forest, resembling Caitraratha, charming, comparable to Nandana, blooming in all seasons, lovely, swarming with bees, shone splendidly.

**6.30.9**  The sound of natyūha and koyaṣṭibhaka birds, of dancing peacocks, and the call of cuckoos was heard near the forest waterfalls.

**6.30.10**  The groves, ever resonant with intoxicated birds, frequented by bees, filled with flocks of cuckoos, and resounding with the calls of birds —

**6.30.11**  — sung over by bhrṅgarāja birds, attended by bumblebees, ringing with the cry of koṇālaka birds, and resonant with the calls of sārasa cranes —

**6.30.12**  — into those groves and gardens the monkey heroes, shape-changers at will, then entered, joyful and full of delight.

**6.30.13**  As those monkeys of great might entered there, a wind blew that was fragrant with the mingling of flowers, pleasant to the breath.

**6.30.14**  But other troop-leaders, issuing from the bands of monkey heroes with Sugrīva's permission, advanced toward banner-bearing Laṅkā.

**6.30.15**  Those foremost among the roaring ones frightened the birds, terrified the deer and elephants, and shook Laṅkā itself with their war-cries.

**6.30.16**  Those swift-moving ones caused the earth to tremble under the tread of their feet, and the dust raised by those feet leaped up into the sky all at once.

**6.30.17**  Bears, lions, wild boars, buffaloes, elephants, and deer, terrified by that noise, fled in panic in all ten directions.

**6.30.18**  And one tall summit of Trikūṭa, touching the sky, carpeted all around with flowers, resembling great silver —

**6.30.19**  — a hundred yojanas in breadth, pure, beautiful to behold, smooth, magnificent, vast, and inaccessible even to birds —

**6.30.20**  — difficult to scale even in the mind, how much more so by human physical effort — upon that summit sat Laṅkā, guarded by Rāvaṇa.

**6.30.21**  That city shone with tall gate-towers resembling white clouds, and with pillars of gold and silver.

**6.30.22**  Adorned supremely with palaces and aerial mansions, Laṅkā resembled at the end of summer the middle realm of Viṣṇu, laden with rain-clouds.

**6.30.23**  Within it a palace adorned with a thousand pillars was visible, shaped like the peak of Kailāsa, soaring upward as if to pierce the sky.

**6.30.24**  That sanctuary of the lord of rākṣasas was the ornament of the city, guarded at all times by a full hundred rākṣasas.

**6.30.25**  Rāma, elder brother of Lakṣmaṇa, possessing all prosperity, himself wealthy in purpose, beheld Rāvaṇa's city, rich and opulent, together with the monkeys.

**6.30.26**  Rāma beheld that city filled with jewels, of many elaborate arrangements, adorned with rows of palaces, its gates fortified with great siege-engines — and he beheld it with his mighty host.

**6.31.1**  Then, having observed those omens, the elder brother of Lakṣmaṇa spoke these words to the illustrious Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.31.2**  "Having secured cool water and fruit-bearing forests, let us divide our masses of troops, array them in formation, and stand ready, O Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.31.3**  I see a terrible danger approaching—one that will bring destruction to the world—the slaughter of mighty warriors among bears, monkeys, and rākṣasas alike.

**6.31.4**  The winds blow harshly, the earth quakes, the mountain peaks tremble, and great hills come crashing down.

**6.31.5**  Clouds resembling carrion-eaters, harsh and harsh-sounding, rain down cruelly, mixed with drops of blood.

**6.31.6**  The twilight glow is exceedingly dreadful, resembling red sandalwood, and a blazing disc of fire falls from the sun.

**6.31.7**  Wild beasts and birds, wretched and mournful in voice, cry toward the sun, portending great terror—they are inauspicious and grim.

**6.31.8**  At night the moon burns without proper light, its rays bordered with black and red, as at the dissolution of the world.

**6.31.9**  The solar halo is small, rough, inauspicious, and intensely red; and a dark mark is seen on the disc of the sun, O Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.31.10**  The stars do not appear as they should, and the moment at hand portends, as it seems, the end of an age for the world—behold, Lakṣmaṇa!

**6.31.11**  Crows, hawks, and vultures wheel low overhead, and jackals cry with great voices—but their cries are inauspicious omens.

**6.31.12**  Therefore, swiftly today let us advance upon that city difficult to assail, the city guarded by Rāvaṇa, surrounding it on all sides with our monkey forces."

**6.31.13**  Saying these words to Lakṣmaṇa, the mighty elder brother of Lakṣmaṇa—that hero of great strength—swiftly descended from the mountain summit.

**6.31.14**  Having descended from that mountain, the righteous-souled Rāghava reviewed his own army, which was utterly difficult for the enemy to assail.

**6.31.15**  Then Rāghava, who knew the right moment, girded himself together with Sugrīva, and at the proper time urged the vast army of the monkey king forward to battle.

**6.31.16**  Then the long-armed archer, surrounded by his great force, marched forth at the right hour, moving to the fore, his face turned toward the city of Laṅkā.

**6.31.17**  Vibhīṣaṇa, Sugrīva, Hanūmān, Jāmbavān, Nala, the king of bears, Nīla, and Lakṣmaṇa all followed him then.

**6.31.18**  Then behind them followed the vast army of bears and forest-dwelling monkeys, covering the great earth as they went, following Rāghava.

**6.31.19**  The monkeys, mighty as elephants and rivals of the greatest elephants, seized by the hundreds mountain peaks and great full-grown trees.

**6.31.20**  Within a short time those two brothers, Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa—tamers of foes—had reached Laṅkā, the city of Rāvaṇa.

**6.31.21**  That city, beautiful with its garlands of banners, adorned with gardens and groves, with variegated ramparts, exceedingly difficult to approach, with lofty walls and gateways—

**6.31.22**  —that city difficult to assail even for the gods: urged by Rāma's command, the forest-dwellers encircled it in their assigned positions and encamped.

**6.31.23**  Rāma, the archer, together with his younger brother, guarded and blockaded the northern gate of Laṅkā, which rose high like a mountain peak.

**6.31.24**  And Rāma, son of Daśaratha, that hero attended by Lakṣmaṇa, having encamped around Laṅkā, kept watch over the city ruled by Rāvaṇa.

**6.31.25**  Taking his stand at the northern gate where Rāvaṇa himself was stationed—for none other than Rāma was capable of guarding that gate.

**6.31.26**  That terrible gate held by Rāvaṇa—like the ocean held by Varuṇa—guarded on all sides by fearsome rākṣasas with weapons, strike terror into the timid as does Pātāla guarded by dānavas.

**6.31.27**  He saw arrayed there many varied masses of weapons belonging to the warriors, and likewise their armour.

**6.31.28**  At the eastern gate, Nīla, lord of the monkey host, valiant and mighty, took his stand together with Mainda and Dvivida.

**6.31.29**  Aṅgada, of immense strength, seized the southern gate, together with Ṛṣabha, Gavākṣa, Gaja, and Gavaya.

**6.31.30**  The powerful monkey Hanūmān guarded the western gate, accompanied by Pramāthin, Praghasa, and other heroes.

**6.31.31**  In the central reserve Sugrīva himself took his stand, together with all the foremost monkeys who were like Garuḍa and the wind in speed.

**6.31.32**  Thirty-six crores of monkeys, renowned troop-leaders, massed and encamped there where the monkey Sugrīva had stationed himself.

**6.31.33**  By Rāma's command, Lakṣmaṇa together with Vibhīṣaṇa stationed a crore of monkeys at each gate, gate by gate.

**6.31.34**  To the west of Rāma, Sugrīva along with Jāmbavān took his position not far away in the central reserve, followed by many forces.

**6.31.35**  Those tiger-like monkeys, like tigers with their fangs, seized trees and mountain peaks and stood ready for battle with joy in their hearts.

**6.31.36**  All had waving tails, all were armed with fangs and claws as weapons, all had strange and varied markings on their bodies, and all had formidable faces.

**6.31.37**  Some had the strength of ten elephants, some even ten times more than that; some equalled in valor a thousand elephants.

**6.31.38**  Some had the strength of a flood in spate; some were yet a hundredfold greater; and other monkey troop-leaders present there had immeasurable strength.

**6.31.39**  The gathering of those monkey armies there was wonderful and varied—like the swarming of locusts.

**6.31.40**  The sky seemed completely filled and the earth seemed entirely covered by the monkeys as they encamped around Laṅkā and leapt about.

**6.31.41**  A hundred times a hundred thousand bears and forest-dwellers separately came to the gates of Laṅkā; others came from all sides eager to fight.

**6.31.42**  That mountain was surrounded on all sides by those leaping beings, and ten million thousands of them advanced upon that city.

**6.31.43**  With mighty monkeys bearing trees, Laṅkā was hemmed in on all sides—impenetrable even to the wind.

**6.31.44**  The rākṣasas were struck with amazement when suddenly they were pressed in upon by monkeys resembling clouds in mass and equal to Indra in valor.

**6.31.45**  A mighty roar arose there as the flood of troops advanced, like the sound of the waters of the ocean when it is breached.

**6.31.46**  By that great sound, all of Laṅkā—with its ramparts and gateways, together with its mountains, forests, and groves—was set trembling.

**6.31.47**  That army, protected by Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa and by Sugrīva, became more difficult to assail than by all gods and asuras combined.

**6.31.48**  Having thus deployed his army for the destruction of the rākṣasas, and having deliberated with his counsellors and resolved upon a course of action again and again—

**6.31.49**  —intent on immediate action, knowing the true import of orderly method, mindful of royal duty with Vibhīṣaṇa's consent, Rāghava summoned Aṅgada, the son of Vālin, and spoke thus:

**6.31.50**  "Go, dear monkey, and speak these words of mine to the ten-necked one—leaping over the city of Laṅkā, free from fear, with no anxiety:

**6.31.51**  'O you whose glory has departed, whose sovereignty is gone, who desires death, whose sense has been destroyed—you who have wronged sages, gods, gandharvas, and apsarases,

**6.31.52**  nāgas, yakṣas, and kings, O night-roaming rākṣasa—the evil you have done through delusion and arrogance—

**6.31.53**  —that arrogance born of the Self-Existent's boon has surely now departed. I—I who wield the rod of punishment, worn down by the abduction of my wife—stand bearing the rod of punishment at the gate of Laṅkā.

**6.31.54**  You shall go, O rākṣasa, slain by me, to the same realm as the gods, the great seers, and all the royal sages.

**6.31.55**  O basest of rākṣasas, show now that strength and that māyā by which you carried off Sītā, eluding me.

**6.31.56**  With my sharpened arrows I shall make this world free of rākṣasas—unless you come to me for refuge, bringing the daughter of Mithilā.

**6.31.57**  The righteous-souled Vibhīṣaṇa, the best of rākṣasas, who has come to me—he will surely and without hindrance obtain the prosperous sovereignty of Laṅkā.

**6.31.58**  For you—with your foolish counsellors, sinful and with self unconquered—cannot enjoy the kingdom through unrighteousness even for a moment.

**6.31.59**  Or else, summoning your courage and holding fast to your valor, fight, O rākṣasa—slain in battle by my arrows, you shall then be purified.

**6.31.60**  If you should enter the three worlds transformed to a bird and swift as thought, once you fall within sight of my eyes you shall not return alive.'"

**6.31.61**  "I speak words of counsel to you: perform your last rites, take a good look at Laṅkā—your life rests in my hands."

**6.31.62**  Thus addressed by Rāma of untiring deeds, Aṅgada, son of Tārā, entered the sky and went forth like fire incarnate.

**6.31.63**  In a moment the illustrious one had overflown and beheld Rāvaṇa seated calmly in his palace together with his ministers.

**6.31.64**  Then the foremost monkey Aṅgada, wearing golden armlets, alighted not far from him and stood like a blazing fire.

**6.31.65**  He made known his own identity, and then caused Rāvaṇa and his ministers to hear all of Rāma's excellent message, neither less nor more.

**6.31.66**  "I am a messenger of Rāma, lord of Kosala, of untiring deeds. I am named Aṅgada, son of Vālin—if that name has reached your ears.

**6.31.67**  Rāma the Rāghava, increaser of Kausalyā's joy, says to you: 'Come out, O cruel lowest of men, and fight in return.'

**6.31.68**  'I shall slay you together with your ministers, your sons, kinsmen, and relatives. With you slain, the three worlds shall be free of anxiety.'

**6.31.69**  'Today I shall extract you, who are the enemy and thorn of gods, dānavas, yakṣas, gandharvas, serpents, rākṣasas, and sages.'

**6.31.70**  'And Vibhīṣaṇa shall obtain sovereignty when you are slain—unless you honor the daughter of Videha and return her with due reverence.'"

**6.31.71**  As the foremost monkey spoke these harsh words, the lord of the night-roaming host fell prey to indignation.

**6.31.72**  Then with eyes reddened by rage he commanded his ministers repeatedly: "Seize this fool! Kill him!"

**6.31.73**  Hearing Rāvaṇa's words—he whose splendor matched blazing fire—four terrible night-roaming rākṣasas then seized Aṅgada.

**6.31.74**  That hero, son of Tārā, allowed himself to be seized in order to display his strength before the host of yātudhānas.

**6.31.75**  Seizing those four who were clinging to his two arms as though they were birds, Aṅgada leapt up with them to the palace, which was like a mountain.

**6.31.76**  Shaken off in midair by his momentum, all those rākṣasas fell to the ground before the eyes of the lord of rākṣasas.

**6.31.77**  Then the pinnacle of the palace, lofty as a mountain peak, shattered under his feet before the eyes of the ten-necked Rāvaṇa.

**6.31.78**  Having shattered the palace pinnacle, having made his name resound, and having roared a mighty roar, he leapt up into the sky.

**6.31.79**  But Rāvaṇa burned with extreme anger at the violation of his palace, and foreseeing his own destruction, he fell to heavy sighing.

**6.31.80**  Meanwhile Rāma, desirous of slaying the enemy, advanced toward battle, surrounded by many joyfully roaring monkeys.

**6.31.81**  And the mighty Suṣeṇa, that monkey resembling a mountain peak, was surrounded there by many monkeys who could change form at will.

**6.31.82**  At Sugrīva's command, that invincible monkey ranged past all four gates, like the moon moving through the constellations.

**6.31.83**  Having reviewed a hundred akṣauhiṇī battalions of the forest-dwellers encamped around Laṅkā, and having crossed the ocean—

**6.31.84**  —the rākṣasas were struck with wonder; some were seized with fear; yet others, excited by battle-ardor, felt only the excitement.

**6.31.85**  The wretched rākṣasas saw the entire space within rampart and moat filled with monkeys—the walls had become a wall of monkeys.

**6.31.86**  When that great and fearful tumult broke out in the city of the rākṣasa king, the rākṣasas seized their great weapons and moved about like winds at the end of an age.

**6.32.1**  Then those rākṣasas went to Rāvaṇa's palace and reported that the city had been blockaded by Rāma together with the monkeys.

**6.32.2**  Hearing that the city was blockaded, the night-roaming Rāvaṇa was seized with anger; having ordered the deployment redoubled, he climbed to the palace roof.

**6.32.3**  He saw Laṅkā with its mountains, forests, and groves completely surrounded on all sides by innumerable bands of monkeys eager for battle.

**6.32.4**  Seeing the entire earth swallowed up by monkeys, he fell into deep thought as to how they might be destroyed.

**6.32.5**  Having reflected at length and steadying himself with composure, Rāvaṇa with his wide eyes gazed upon Rāghava and the monkey troops.

**6.32.6**  As the lord of rākṣasas watched, those armies in their divisions climbed upon Laṅkā then, eager to please Rāghava.

**6.32.7**  They—copper-faced, golden in hue, their lives surrendered for Rāma's sake—stormed Laṅkā itself, armed with sāla trees, palm trees, and boulders.

**6.32.8**  The leaping monkeys with trees, mountain peaks, and bare fists smashed the tall palace towers and the gateways.

**6.32.9**  The monkeys filled the moats—which had been full of clear water—with earth, mountain peaks, grass, and timber.

**6.32.10**  Then troop-leaders commanding a thousand troops each, and those commanding a crore each, and others commanding a hundred crore troops, all climbed upon Laṅkā.

**6.32.11**  The leaping monkeys crushed the golden gateways and destroyed the gopura towers, which resembled the peaks of mount Kailāsa.

**6.32.12**  Leaping, bounding, and roaring, the monkeys—resembling great elephants—advanced upon Laṅkā.

**6.32.13**  "Victory to Rāma, supremely mighty! Victory to the great-armed Lakṣmaṇa! Victory to king Sugrīva, protected by Rāghava!"

**6.32.14**  Shouting and roaring in this way, the monkeys who could take any form they wished rushed at the walls of Laṅkā.

**6.32.15**  Vīrabāhu, Subāhu, and Nala who ranged the forest—those monkey troop-leaders massed themselves and encamped against the rampart.

**6.32.16**  In the meantime they established an encampment of the main divisions.

**6.32.17**  The mighty Kumuda surrounded the eastern gate, taking his stand encircled by ten crores of monkeys who bore the look of conquerors.

**6.32.18**  The heroic monkey Śatabali came to the southern gate; the mighty one took his stand there encircled by twenty crores.

**6.32.19**  Suṣeṇa, the father of Tārā, that monkey, went to the western gate; the mighty one took his stand there surrounded by sixty crores.

**6.32.20**  Rāma, together with the son of Sumitrā, took his stand at the northern gate, mighty and encircling it; and Sugrīva, lord of monkeys, stood there too.

**6.32.21**  The great-bodied Golāṅgūla, and Gavākṣa of fearsome appearance—both mighty heroes—each surrounded by a crore, stood at Rāma's flank.

**6.32.22**  Dhūmra, destroyer of enemies, mightiest among the terrifyingly swift bears, surrounded by a crore, stood at Rāma's side.

**6.32.23**  Vibhīṣaṇa, fully armored, mighty and valiant, mace in hand, stood there surrounded by his ministers—that man of great strength.

**6.32.24**  Gaja, Gavākṣa, Gavaya, Śarabha, and Gandhamādana ranged about on all sides, guarding the monkey army.

**6.32.25**  Then Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas, his soul consumed by rage, commanded the immediate march out of all his forces.

**6.32.26**  Then the soldiers sallied forth joyfully, urged on by Rāvaṇa, like the surge of the great ocean at the moment of high tide.

**6.32.27**  In the meantime a terrible battle broke out between the rākṣasas and the monkeys, as of old between the gods and asuras.

**6.32.28**  Those terrible rākṣasas, proclaiming their own valor, struck down the monkeys with blazing maces, lances, spears, and battle-axes.

**6.32.29**  Likewise the great-bodied monkeys with trees and mountain peaks, and the rākṣasas with those same weapons—swift with claws and teeth—struck at one another.

**6.32.30**  Other fearsome rākṣasas standing on the ramparts tore apart those on the ground below with slings, swords, and spears.

**6.32.31**  And the monkeys too, greatly enraged—those on the ground—leapt up and hurled down the rākṣasas who stood on the ramparts.

**6.32.32**  That tumultuous clash between the rākṣasas and monkeys, a quagmire of flesh and blood, was like nothing ever seen before—a wonder beyond compare.

**6.33.1**  Then, as those great-souled monkeys were fighting, there arose among the rākṣasas an extremely fierce surge of force and fury.

**6.33.2**  With horses bearing golden trappings, with banners like flames of fire, with chariots brilliant as the sun, and with beautiful armor—

**6.33.3**  —the tiger-like rākṣasas of fearful deeds marched out, making the ten directions resound, desiring victory for Rāvaṇa.

**6.33.4**  The great army of monkeys, desiring victory, advanced against that army of rākṣasas who could take any form at will.

**6.33.5**  In the meantime, as they charged at one another—rākṣasas and monkeys—single combat broke out.

**6.33.6**  Indrajit the rākṣasa, of great splendor, fought with Aṅgada son of Vālin, as Tryambaka once fought with Andhaka.

**6.33.7**  Saṃpāti fought with Prajaṅgha, ever fierce in battle; and Hanūmān the monkey took on Jambūmālin.

**6.33.8**  Vibhīṣaṇa, Rāvaṇa's younger brother—the rākṣasa of immense wrath—met in battle the swift and fierce Mitraghna.

**6.33.9**  The mighty Gaja fought along with the rākṣasa Tapana, while the greatly splendorous Nīla fought with Nikumbha.

**6.33.10**  The monkey-lord Sugrīva came to blows with Praghasa, while the illustrious Lakṣmaṇa met Virūpākṣa in battle.

**6.33.11**  The invincible rākṣasas Agniketu and Raśmiketu, and Suptaghna and Yajñakopa, all came together against Rāma.

**6.33.12**  The two leading monkeys Mainda and Dvivida, that pair of dreadful rākṣasas Vajramuṣṭi and Aśaniprabha, came face to face with each other.

**6.33.13**  The heroic, terrible, and war-indomitable rākṣasa Pratapana fought in battle with the swift and fierce Nala.

**6.33.14**  Suṣeṇa, renowned as the powerful son of Dharma, that great monkey fought along with Vidyunmālin.

**6.33.15**  And other fearsome monkeys joined single combats in many forms with yet other rākṣasas—many engaging many.

**6.33.16**  There was waged a great, tumultuous, hair-raising battle between the rākṣasas and monkeys—heroes all, each desiring victory.

**6.33.17**  Rivers of blood flowed from the bodies of monkeys and rākṣasas, carrying masses of matted hair, bearing along heaps of bodies.

**6.33.18**  Indrajit, enraged, struck the hero Aṅgada—destroyer of enemy armies—with his mace, as Śatakratu might strike with the vajra.

**6.33.19**  Then the illustrious and swift monkey Aṅgada struck in battle Indrajit's chariot—golden and of splendid markings—together with its horses and charioteer.

**6.33.20**  Saṃpāti, struck by Prajaṅgha with three arrows, slew Prajaṅgha at the forefront of battle with an aśvakarṇa tree.

**6.33.21**  The mighty Jambūmālin, standing in his chariot, enraged, pierced Hanūmān through the chest with a chariot-lance in battle.

**6.33.22**  Then Hanūmān, son of the Wind, mounting that very chariot, quickly crushed Jambūmālin himself with his palm, along with the chariot.

**6.33.23**  His body pierced by sharp arrows from the swift-handed rākṣasa, Gaja struck Tapana with a mountain peak and then with his fist.

**6.33.24**  Sugrīva, lord of monkeys, pierced and slew Praghasa—who seemed to be swallowing the armies—with a saptaparṇa tree.

**6.33.25**  Overwhelming the fearsome-looking rākṣasa with a shower of arrows, Lakṣmaṇa slew Virūpākṣa with a single shaft.

**6.33.26**  The invincible rākṣasa Agniketu, and Raśmiketu, and Suptaghna, and Yajñakopa all pierced Rāma with arrows.

**6.33.27**  Then Rāma, enraged, severed in battle the heads of all four of them with four terrible arrows resembling flames of fire.

**6.33.28**  Vajramuṣṭi, struck down in battle by Mainda with his bare fist, fell to the ground together with his chariot and horses, like a palace tower collapsed.

**6.33.29**  And Dvivida—whose touch was like thunderbolt and lightning—struck Aśaniprabha with a mountain peak before the eyes of all the rākṣasas.

**6.33.30**  Then Aśaniprabha in turn pierced with arrows like thunderbolts the monkey-lord Dvivida, that fighter with trees, in battle.

**6.33.31**  His body pierced through and through by arrows, Dvivida—overcome with rage—slew Aśaniprabha along with his chariot and horses with a sāla tree.

**6.33.32**  Nikumbha in battle pierced Nīla—who had the luster of a heap of dark collyrium—with sharp arrows, as the sun pierces a cloud with its rays.

**6.33.33**  Then again the swift-handed night-roaming Nikumbha pierced Nīla in battle with a hundred arrows, and laughed.

**6.33.34**  Then Nīla—like Viṣṇu in battle—severed Nikumbha's head in combat with the wheel of Nikumbha's own chariot, and the head of his charioteer too.

**6.33.35**  Vidyunmālin, standing in his chariot, struck Suṣeṇa with arrows adorned with gold and roared again and again.

**6.33.36**  Seeing him in his chariot, the foremost monkey Suṣeṇa swiftly brought down the chariot with a great mountain peak.

**6.33.37**  Vidyunmālin the night-roamer, endowed with agility, quickly leapt from the chariot and stood on the ground, mace in hand.

**6.33.38**  Then Suṣeṇa, the foremost monkey, possessed by rage, seized an immense boulder and charged at the night-roamer.

**6.33.39**  As he came charging, the night-roaming Vidyunmālin quickly struck the best of monkeys, Suṣeṇa, in the chest with his mace.

**6.33.40**  Though struck by that terrible mace blow, the incomparable foremost of monkeys hurled that boulder onto Vidyunmālin's chest in the great battle.

**6.33.41**  Struck by the boulder's impact, Vidyunmālin the night-roamer fell dead to the ground, his heart crushed.

**6.33.42**  Thus those heroic night-roaming rākṣasas were ground down in single combat by those heroic monkeys, as the daityas were once by the gods.

**6.33.43**  Struck and pierced with war-headed arrows, swords, maces, lances, tomaras, and paṭṭasas—and with battle-chariots and war-horses—

**6.33.44**  —with slain maddened elephants, and with fallen monkeys and rākṣasas, and with broken chariot-wheels, axles, yokes, and poles lying on the earth—the dreadful battlefield was haunted by packs of jackals.

**6.33.45**  Headless trunks of monkeys and rākṣasas sprang up in all directions in that tumultuous and crushing engagement comparable to the war of gods and asuras.

**6.33.46**  The night-roamers, being torn asunder by the foremost monkeys, their bodies smeared with blood, swiftly resumed fierce fighting, longing for the setting of the sun.

**6.34.1**  While those monkeys and rākṣasas were still fighting, the sun set and night came on — a night that robs creatures of their lives.

**6.34.2**  A night battle then broke out between those monkeys and rākṣasas — terrible foes bound together in mutual hatred, each craving victory.
