**6.34.3**  "You are a rākṣasa!" cried the monkeys; "You are a monkey!" cried the rākṣasas — and so they slew one another in that dreadful darkness.

**6.34.4**  "Strike! Tear him apart!" — "Why do you flee?" — such a tremendous clamour was heard throughout that darkness.

**6.34.5**  Dark-complexioned rākṣasas clad in golden armour appeared in that darkness like mountain kings whose slopes blaze with luminous herbs.

**6.34.6**  In that impenetrable darkness, rākṣasas maddened by rage charged with great speed, devouring the monkeys.

**6.34.7**  Leaping upon horses adorned with golden crests and banners resembling tongues of fire, those rākṣasas, furious with terrible wrath, tore them apart with their sharp fangs.

**6.34.8**  Maddened with rage, they dragged and bit with their teeth at elephants, elephant riders, chariots flying banners and standards.

**6.34.9**  Lakṣmaṇa and Rāma, with arrows equal to venomous serpents, struck down the choicest rākṣasas whether visible or invisible.

**6.34.10**  The dust of the earth, churned up by the hooves of horses and thrown up by the rims of chariot wheels, choked the ears and eyes of the combatants.

**6.34.11**  While that dreadful, hair-raising battle raged, rivers of blood flowed swiftly there.

**6.34.12**  Then arose a din of kettle-drums, battle-drums, and paṇava drums, mingled with the sounds of conches and flutes — a sound beyond wonder.

**6.34.13**  The cries of rākṣasas who had been struck down and were groaning, and the clash of the monkeys' weapons, together raised an exceedingly dreadful sound.

**6.34.14**  The battlefield was strewn with an offering of weapons as flowers; it was hard to traverse, hard to enter, a quagmire of streaming blood.

**6.34.15**  That night was dreadful, sweeping away monkeys and rākṣasas alike — like the Night of Doom for all living beings, impossible to cross over.

**6.34.16**  Then in that dreadful darkness, the rākṣasas, filled with joy, charged straight at Rāma alone, raining arrows upon him.

**6.34.17**  The roar of those raging rākṣasas rushing forward was like the surging tumult of seven oceans.

**6.34.18**  Rāma felled six of those night-roamers with six sharp arrows keen as flames of fire — in the mere twinkling of an eye.

**6.34.19**  Yajñaśatru the irresistible, Mahāpārśva, Mahodara, Vajradaṃṣṭra, Mahākāya, and those two, Śuka and Sāraṇa —

**6.34.20**  — these were struck by Rāma's flood of arrows at every vital point, and withdrawing from the battle they survived with their remaining span of life.

**6.34.21**  Then the mighty Rāma, with arrows of golden-variegated shafts resembling tongues of fire, made the cardinal and intermediate directions clear.

**6.34.22**  Whatever other warrior rākṣasas stood facing Rāma also perished upon approaching him, like moths meeting a flame.

**6.34.23**  With thousands of shafts tipped with gold flying through the air, the night became brilliant, like an autumnal night lit with fireflies.

**6.34.24**  With the roaring of the rākṣasas and the battle-cries of the monkeys, that already dreadful night became even more dreadful.

**6.34.25**  By that great swelling sound on all sides, Mount Trikūṭa, filled with its caverns, seemed to roar back in echo.

**6.34.26**  Golāṅgūla monkeys of massive bodies, matching the darkness in their dark hue, seized night-roaming rākṣasas in their arms and devoured them.

**6.34.27**  Aṅgada, pressing forward in battle to slay the enemy, swiftly struck down Rāvaṇa's son Indrajit's charioteer and his horses.

**6.34.28**  But Indrajit, master of great illusion, his horses slain and his charioteer killed, abandoned his chariot and vanished right there before Aṅgada.

**6.34.29**  That wicked one, Rāvaṇa's son, fierce in battle, having vanished from sight — the heroic Rāvaṇi, empowered by the boon of Brahmā, maddened by rage — released sharp arrows blazing like thunderbolts while invisible.

**6.34.30**  That rākṣasa, enraged, pierced both Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa throughout all their limbs in battle with dreadful arrows made of nāgas.

**6.35.1**  The valiant prince Rāma, immensely powerful, seeking to track Indrajit's movements, dispatched ten monkey chieftains.

**6.35.2**  The two sons of Suṣeṇa, Nīla the foremost among monkeys, Aṅgada the son of Vālin, and swift Śarabha —

**6.35.3**  — Vinatā, Jāmbavān, the mighty Sānuprastha, Ṛṣabha, and Ṛṣabhaskandha — these the scorcher of foes commanded.

**6.35.4**  Those monkeys, greatly excited, lifting terrible trees, all leapt into the sky and searched the ten directions.

**6.35.5**  The son of Rāvaṇa, skilled in the science of missiles, checked the speed of those swift warriors with arrows even swifter, using his supreme weapon.

**6.35.6**  The monkeys of fearsome speed, wounded and struck by iron arrows, could not see him in the darkness — as one cannot see the sun covered by clouds.

**6.35.7**  Rāvaṇi, ever victorious in battle, drove deeply into Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa alone arrows that pierced every vital point.

**6.35.8**  The two heroic brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, their bodies covered without interval, were struck by the enraged Indrajit with serpent-arrows — arrows that had become serpents.

**6.35.9**  Blood flowed copiously through the wound-paths on both of them; those two shone like kiṃśuka trees in full bloom.

**6.35.10**  Then Rāvaṇi, his eyes reddened at the corners, resembling a heap of broken collyrium, invisible, spoke these words to the two brothers.

**6.35.11**  "Even Śakra, lord of the thirty gods, is incapable of seeing or approaching me when I fight invisibly — how much less you two?"

**6.35.12**  "I shall now, my soul seized with fury, send both Rāghava brothers — enveloped in a net of heron-feathered arrows — to the abode of Yama."

**6.35.13**  Having spoken thus, he pierced the two brothers — Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, knowers of dharma — with sharp arrows, and exulted and roared.

**6.35.14**  Dark as a heap of shattered collyrium, he drew his great bow and released dreadful arrows again and again in that great battle.

**6.35.15**  Then that heroic knower of vital points, driving sharp arrows into the vital points of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, roared again and again.

**6.35.16**  Those two, bound by the bond of arrows at the forefront of battle, could not even look up for the mere twinkling of an eye.

**6.35.17**  Then both of them, their every limb pierced, covered with arrow-thorns, swayed like the banner of great Indra released from its ropes.

**6.35.18**  Those two heroes, those two great bowmen, lords of the earth, staggering and weakened by the piercing of their vital points, fell to the ground.

**6.35.19**  Those two heroes lay on their hero's bed, drenched in blood, their every limb wrapped in arrows, agonized and in extreme pain.

**6.35.20**  Not a finger's breadth of their bodies was unpierced; not a part was not split, not a part was not transfixed from fingertip to the rest, by those straight-flying arrows.

**6.35.21**  Struck down by that cruel shape-shifting rākṣasa, the two of them bled profusely, like a pair of mountain springs flowing with water.

**6.35.22**  Rāma fell first, pierced in his vital points by the arrows of Indrajit who in his wrath had previously vanquished Śakra himself.

**6.35.23**  He shot him with nārāca arrows, ardha-nārāca arrows, bhalla arrows, añjalika arrows, vatsadanta arrows, siṃhadaṃṣṭra arrows, and kṣura arrows.

**6.35.24**  He lay on his warrior's bed, still holding his unstrung bow — its grip broken, triple-bent, ornamented with gold.

**6.35.25**  Seeing Rāma, that bull among men, fallen in the interval between the arrow-showers, Lakṣmaṇa lost all hope of life.

**6.35.26**  The two heroes, bound and fallen and lying there — the assembled monkeys, led by the son of the Wind, surrounded them and stood in utter sorrow, overwhelmed with the greatest grief.

**6.36.1**  Then the forest dwellers, looking over sky and earth, saw the two brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa sewn together by arrows.

**6.36.2**  When the rākṣasa had completed his deed and ceased — as a cloud ceases after raining — Sugrīva arrived at that place together with Vibhīṣaṇa.

**6.36.3**  Nīla, Dvivida, Mainda, Suṣeṇa, Sumukha, and Aṅgada quickly lamented for the two Rāghava princes together with Hanūmān.

**6.36.4**  Motionless, breathing faintly, drenched in floods of blood, covered with a mesh of arrows, rigid — they lay on their arrow-beds.

**6.36.5**  Hissing like serpents, motionless, their movement sluggish, their limbs smeared with flowing blood — they were like golden banners.

**6.36.6**  Those two heroes lay on the hero's bed, barely stirring, surrounded by monkey chieftains whose eyes were clouded with tears.

**6.36.7**  Seeing the two Rāghava princes fallen and enveloped in a net of arrows, all the monkeys along with Vibhīṣaṇa were stricken with anguish.

**6.36.8**  The monkeys scanned the sky and all directions but could not see Rāvaṇi in battle, veiled as he was by his māyā.

**6.36.9**  But Vibhīṣaṇa, looking through māyā with māyā, beheld his brother's son stationed there, though concealed by illusion.

**6.36.10**  By virtue of his boon, Vibhīṣaṇa saw that hero of incomparable deeds, unrivalled in combat, hidden from sight.

**6.36.11**  Indrajit, surveying his own deed and those two lying there, spoke in supreme delight, gladdening all the rākṣasas.

**6.36.12**  "The two mighty brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa — slayers of Dūṣaṇa and Khara — have been laid low by my arrows."

**6.36.13**  "These two cannot be freed from this bond of arrows even by all the gods, asuras, and hosts of sages combined."

**6.36.14**  "Because of these two, my father, brooding in grief, has been passing the night without his body touching his bed."

**6.36.15**  "Because of these two, all Laṅkā has been troubled like a river in the rains. That root-destroying calamity for all of us has been slain by me."

**6.36.16**  "All the exploits of Rāma, of Lakṣmaṇa, and of all the forest dwellers are now fruitless — like autumn clouds without rain."

**6.36.17**  Having spoken thus to all those rākṣasas standing around him, Rāvaṇi also struck all those monkey chieftains.

**6.36.18**  Having tormented them with floods of arrows and frightened the monkeys, the long-armed one laughed aloud and spoke this further word.

**6.36.19**  "Behold, rākṣasas! These two brothers together have been bound by me at the forefront of the army with a dreadful bond of arrows."

**6.36.20**  Thus addressed, all those rākṣasa fighters by cunning were filled with the greatest amazement and satisfied by that feat.

**6.36.21**  All those cloud-like rākṣasas roared great roars, and knowing Rāma to be slain, honoured Rāvaṇi.

**6.36.22**  Seeing both Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa motionless on the ground, barely breathing, he deemed them slain.

**6.36.23**  Filled with joy, Indrajit ever victorious in battle entered the city of Laṅkā, gladdening all the rākṣasas.

**6.36.24**  Seeing the bodies of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, their every limb and sub-limb heaped with shafts, fear overtook Sugrīva.

**6.36.25**  Vibhīṣaṇa spoke to that terrified king of monkeys, whose face was wet with tears, who was dejected, his eyes bewildered with grief.

**6.36.26**  "Enough of fear, Sugrīva; restrain the surge of tears. Such is the nature of battles — victory is not final for anyone."

**6.36.27**  "If, O hero, some portion of our fortune still remains, these two brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa will shake off their swoon."

**6.36.28**  "Steady yourself, O monkey, and steady me who am without a protector. For those devoted to truth and dharma there is no fear wrought by death."

**6.36.29**  Having spoken thus, Vibhīṣaṇa then wiped Sugrīva's beautiful eyes with his hand, wet with water.

**6.36.30**  Having wiped the face of that wise king of monkeys, he spoke these words, measured to the occasion and unperturbed.

**6.36.31**  "This is not the time, O king of monkeys, to give way to despondency. Even excessive love, at such an inopportune moment, conduces to death."

**6.36.32**  "Therefore, abandoning despondency — destroyer of every effort — let us consider what is beneficial for the armies led by Rāma."

**6.36.33**  "Or else, let Rāma be guarded until consciousness is lost no more; when the two Kākutsthas regain their senses, they will drive away our fear."

**6.36.34**  "This is nothing at all for Rāma; nor does Rāma wish to die. For the Fortune that abandons those whose life is spent will not abandon him."

**6.36.35**  "Therefore, comfort yourself and comfort your own forces, while I reassemble all the armies."

**6.36.36**  "These monkeys, O bull among monkeys, eyes dilated wide, seized with dread and fear, are whispering in each other's ears."

**6.36.37**  "Seeing me run forward to hearten the troops, let the monkeys abandon their fear as one discards a garland already worn."

**6.36.38**  Having heartened Sugrīva, the lord of rākṣasas Vibhīṣaṇa then heartened once more the scattered monkey army.

**6.36.39**  Meanwhile Indrajit, master of great illusion, surrounded by all his forces, entered the city of Laṅkā and approached his father.

**6.36.40**  There, saluting the seated Rāvaṇa with joined palms, he reported the joyful news to his father: Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa had been slain.

**6.36.41**  Rāvaṇa then leapt up with joy and embraced his son, in the midst of the rākṣasas, having heard that the two enemies had been laid low.

**6.36.42**  Smelling him on the head with affection and a gladdened heart, he questioned him; and to his father who questioned him, Indrajit reported everything just as it had occurred.

**6.36.43**  His inner heart carried away by the surge of joy, having heard the words of that great warrior, Rāvaṇa shed the fever that had arisen on account of Daśaratha's son; and rejoicing, he praised his son with words.

**6.37.1**  When the son of Rāvaṇa had re-entered Laṅkā, his mission accomplished, the foremost of the monkeys stood guard around Rāghava, anxious on his behalf.

**6.37.2**  Hanūmān, Aṅgada, Nīla, Suṣeṇa, Kumuda, Nala, Gaja, Gavākṣa, Gavaya, Śarabha, and Gandhamādana —

**6.37.3**  Jāmbavān, Ṛṣabha, Sunda, Rambha, Śatabali, and Pṛthu — all standing in battle array, alert, taking up trees on every side,

**6.37.4**  the monkeys scanned all directions, crosswise and above, and even when blades of grass stirred they thought it was a rākṣasa.

**6.37.5**  Rāvaṇa too, greatly pleased, having dismissed his son Indrajit, then summoned the rākṣasī women who guarded Sītā.

**6.37.6**  The rākṣasīs along with Trijaṭā appeared before him at his command. Then the lord of the rākṣasas, overjoyed, addressed those rākṣasī women.

**6.37.7**  "Announce to the daughter of Videha that Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa have been slain by Indrajit. Mount her on Puṣpaka and show her the two slain in battle."

**6.37.8**  "He who was her refuge and on whom she relied, the one for whose sake she would not come to me — that husband of hers, together with his brother, has been cast down on the battlefield."

**6.37.9**  "Sītā, the princess of Mithilā, free from doubt, free from distress, freed of all expectations, adorned with every ornament, will come to attend upon me."

**6.37.10**  "Having seen Rāma together with Lakṣmaṇa, come under the sway of death on the battlefield today, with her hope extinguished and seeing no other recourse, she will turn to me."

**6.37.11**  Hearing those words of the evil-souled Rāvaṇa, those rākṣasī women replied "So be it" and went to where Puṣpaka stood.

**6.37.12**  Then the rākṣasīs, taking Puṣpaka by Rāvaṇa's command, brought the princess of Mithilā who was in the Aśoka grove.

**6.37.13**  Taking her — Sītā, utterly absorbed in grief for her husband — the rākṣasīs had her ascend the aerial vehicle Puṣpaka.

**6.37.14**  Then, having placed Sītā together with Trijaṭā on Puṣpaka, Rāvaṇa had Laṅkā decorated with garlands of banners and flags.

**6.37.15**  And the lord of the rākṣasas, filled with joy, proclaimed throughout Laṅkā: "Rāghava and Lakṣmaṇa have been slain in battle by Indrajit."

**6.37.16**  And Sītā, traveling by that aerial vehicle with Trijaṭā, saw the entire monkey army laid low.

**6.37.17**  She saw the flesh-eating rākṣasas exulting in their minds, and the monkeys stricken with grief by the side of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.37.18**  Then Sītā beheld them both lying on their beds of arrows — Lakṣmaṇa and Rāma — unconscious, tormented by shafts.

**6.37.19**  The two heroes, their armor shattered, their bows flung apart, every limb of their bodies cut by arrows, lay on the ground like pillars fashioned of arrows.

**6.37.20**  Seeing those two brothers there, those two bull-like men, Sītā, deeply afflicted with grief, lamented piteously.

**6.37.21**  She, struck by tears and sorrow, gazing at those two brothers equal in splendor to gods, contemplating their death, overcome with grief, spoke these words.

**6.38.1**  Seeing her husband slain and the mighty Lakṣmaṇa, Sītā lamented piercingly and piteously, worn down by grief.

**6.38.2**  "Those who read auspicious marks who said to me, 'You shall have sons, you shall not be widowed' — all of them, now that Rāma is slain, were ignorant and spoke falsehood."

**6.38.3**  "Those who perform sacrifices who called me the chief queen, and those who perform soma rites who called me their patron's wife — all of them now that Rāma is slain were ignorant and spoke falsehood."

**6.38.4**  "Those who knew me as 'wife of a heroic king' and called me fortunate — all of them now that Rāma is slain were ignorant and spoke falsehood."

**6.38.5**  "Those twice-born readers of fate who declared me auspicious when I was presented — all of them now that Rāma is slain were ignorant and spoke falsehood."

**6.38.6**  "Indeed these lotus marks on the feet, by which women are said to be consecrated to sovereignty alongside their husbands, kings of men —

**6.38.7**  "and those ill-omened marks by which women become widows, unfortunate and deprived of good fortune — seeing as I am one whose auspicious signs have been destroyed, I do not see those in myself."

**6.38.8**  "These lotus marks declared true for women according to physiognomy — they become meaningless for me today, now that Rāma is slain."

**6.38.9**  "My hair is fine, even, and dark; my eyebrows are not joined; my calves are rounded and hairless; my teeth are without gaps."

**6.38.10**  "My temples, eyes, hands, feet, and ankles are well-formed; my thighs are full; my nails are curved, smooth, and even; my fingers are well-proportioned."

**6.38.11**  "My two breasts are close-set and full with sunken nipples; my navel is deep and set high; and my flanks and chest are well-formed."

**6.38.12**  "My complexion is like a jewel, and my body hair is fine. Those who read twelve signs declared me established in auspicious marks."

**6.38.13**  "My palms and soles, with unbroken barleycorn lines and beautiful in color, and my gentle smile — by these the readers of maidens' marks recognized me."

**6.38.14**  "A consecration to sovereignty alongside my husband — what was declared by those brāhmaṇas skilled in the knowledge of fate, all of that has been made false."

**6.38.15**  "After clearing out Janasthāna, after obtaining news of me, after crossing the unfordable ocean — the two brothers have been slain as if in a puddle of water."

**6.38.16**  "Did not the two Rāghavas indeed command the water-weapon, the fire-weapon, the Indra-weapon, the wind-weapon, and the Brahmaśiras missile as well?"

**6.38.17**  "By means of illusion, by one unseen in battle, Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa — the two lords equal to Vāsava, my protectors though I am without protection — have been slain."

**6.38.18**  "For no enemy, once he came within the field of vision of Rāghava in battle, could return alive, even if he were swift as thought."

**6.38.19**  "There is nothing too heavy for Time; Fate is utterly unconquerable — for here lies Rāma together with his brother, struck down in battle."

**6.38.20**  "I do not grieve so much for my slain husband, nor for Lakṣmaṇa, nor for myself, as I do for my mother-in-law, that ascetic woman."

**6.38.21**  "For she thinks constantly, 'When shall I see one whose vow is fulfilled and returned — when shall I see Sītā and Rāma together with Lakṣmaṇa?'"

**6.38.22**  As she lamented thus, the rākṣasī Trijaṭā spoke to her: "Do not despair, my lady — your husband is alive."

**6.38.23**  "And I shall tell you the great and fitting reasons why these two brothers, Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, are alive, my lady."

**6.38.24**  "For the faces of warriors in battle do not become flooded with anger and quivering with joy when their master has been slain."

**6.38.25**  "This divine aerial vehicle, called Puṣpaka by name, O daughter of Videha, would not bear you if these two were as dead as elephants slain."

**6.38.26**  "An army whose foremost heroes have been slain, with its energy destroyed and its effort collapsed, wanders about in battle like a rudderless ship on water."

**6.38.27**  "This army, on the other hand, unperturbed and undistressed, full of energy, protects the two Kākutsthas who have been overcome by illusion in battle."

**6.38.28**  "Therefore be completely reassured. By these signs from which good will arise, see that the two Kākutsthas are not slain. I tell you this out of affection."

**6.38.29**  "I have never spoken falsehood before, nor shall I ever speak it. Because of the pleasure I take in good conduct, you have entered my heart."

**6.38.30**  "These two cannot be conquered in battle even by the gods and asuras together with Indra. Having seen their faces, I have reported this to you."

**6.38.31**  "And observe this very great sign, O princess of Mithilā: though both of them are unconscious, their radiance does not depart from them."

**6.38.32**  "In general, in the faces of men who have lost their vital force and whose life has departed, a great change becomes visible."

**6.38.33**  "Abandon grief and pain and delusion, O daughter of Janaka. For the sake of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa it is not possible to cease living today."

**6.38.34**  Hearing those words of hers, Sītā, equal to the daughter of a god, joined her palms and said: "Let it be so" — the princess of Mithilā.

**6.38.35**  Then that swift Puṣpaka aerial vehicle was turned about, and Sītā, forlorn, was conducted back into Laṅkā itself by Trijaṭā.

**6.38.36**  Then, having descended from Puṣpaka together with Trijaṭā, she was conducted by the rākṣasī women back into the Aśoka grove itself.

**6.38.37**  Entering that pleasure-ground of the lord of the rākṣasas dense with many groves of trees, and gazing upon and reflecting on the two princes, Sītā fell into the deepest despair.

**6.39.1**  The two sons of Daśaratha, bound by a dreadful net of arrows, lay breathing like great serpents, drenched in blood.

**6.39.2**  All those foremost of the monkeys, the mighty ones with Sugrīva, stood surrounding the two great-souled princes, overwhelmed with grief.

**6.39.3**  In the meantime, the valiant Rāma regained consciousness, even though bound by arrows, through steadiness and the force of his vital spirit.

**6.39.4**  Then, seeing his brother — covered in blood, deeply afflicted, pierced through, his face downcast — he lamented, overcome with anguish.

**6.39.5**  "What need have I of Sītā, what need of life itself, I who now see my brother lying overcome in battle?"

**6.39.6**  "A woman equal to Sītā can be found by searching in the world. A brother equal to Lakṣmaṇa, a counselor and companion in war, cannot."

**6.39.7**  "I shall abandon my life before the eyes of the monkeys, if Sumitrā's joy-increasing son has attained the five elements."

**6.39.8**  "What shall I say to Kausalyā my mother? What to Kaikeyī? How shall I console mother Sumitrā, who longs to see her son?"

**6.39.9**  "How shall I comfort her, bereft of her child, trembling and crying out like an osprey, if I go back without him?"

**6.39.10**  "How shall I speak to Śatrughna and the illustrious Bharata? He went to the forest with me, and I have returned without him."

**6.39.11**  "Alas, I shall not be able to bear Sumitrā's reproach. I shall abandon my body here; I have no desire to live."

**6.39.12**  "Shame on me, a doer of evil deeds, ignoble — for whose sake this Lakṣmaṇa has fallen and lies on his bed of arrows, as if his life has departed."

**6.39.13**  "You, Lakṣmaṇa, always used to console me when I was deeply despondent. Now, your life gone, you are not able today to address me in my anguish."

**6.39.14**  "By whom many rākṣasas were struck down to the earth in battle today — on that same earth the hero himself lies, struck down by his enemies."

**6.39.15**  "Lying on this bed of arrows, drenched in his own blood, covered with a web of arrows, he shines like the sun going to its setting."

**6.39.16**  "Because his vital spots are struck by arrows, he cannot look up; and though he speaks no word of his pain, it is indicated by the redness of his gaze."

**6.39.17**  "Just as this great-splendored one followed me when I went to the forest, so too shall I follow him to the abode of Yama."

**6.39.18**  "A dear kinsman, ever devoted to me and ever in attendance on me — he has come to this condition today through the wicked conduct of an ignoble one like me."

**6.39.19**  "Even when provoked, the hero Lakṣmaṇa — I do not recall that he ever caused me to hear a harsh or displeasing word."

**6.39.20**  "He who could release five hundred arrows in a single surge, surpassing Kārtavīrya himself in the science of archery — that is Lakṣmaṇa."

**6.39.21**  "He who could counter weapons with weapons even against the great-souled Śakra — that same man now lies struck down on the earth, though fit for the most honored bed."

**6.39.22**  "And that false boast will surely consume me — that I did not make Vibhīṣaṇa king of the rākṣasas."

**6.39.23**  "At this very moment, Sugrīva, you ought to depart from here. The mighty lord of the rākṣasas may attack, considering me to be diminished."

**6.39.24**  "Placing Aṅgada at the fore, together with your army and your allies, Sugrīva, cross the ocean again by that same bridge."

**6.39.25**  "The deed accomplished by Hanūmān was one difficult for others in battle. I am satisfied with the king of the bears and with the lord of the golāṅgūla monkeys."

**6.39.26**  "The deed done by Aṅgada, and by Mainda and Dvivida, and the terrible battle fought in the conflict by Keśarin and Saṃpāti —"

**6.39.27**  "and the battle of Gavaya, Gavākṣa, Śarabha, Gaja, and other monkeys who abandoned their lives for my sake —"

**6.39.28**  "Fate cannot be overcome by human beings, O Sugrīva. But whatever was possible for a friend and companion to do, all of that, O scorcher of foes, has been done by you, who fear adharma."

**6.39.29**  "This duty of friendship has been done by all of you, O foremost of monkeys. Dismissed by me, you are all free to go wherever you wish."

**6.39.30**  All those monkeys heard his lament and let tears flow from their eyes, whose whites were darker than dark.

**6.39.31**  Then Vibhīṣaṇa, having stationed all the troops, came swiftly with mace in hand to where Rāghava was.

**6.39.32**  Seeing him advancing swiftly, dark as a mass of blue collyrium, all the monkeys fled, thinking him to be Rāvaṇa's son.

**6.40.1**  Then the great-spirited lord of monkeys, greatly powerful, spoke: "Why is this army distressed, like a ship tossed on water by a confused wind?"

**6.40.2**  Having heard the words of Sugrīva, Aṅgada son of Vālin said: "You do not see Rāma, nor Lakṣmaṇa of great might,

**6.40.3**  The two heroes, both sons of Daśaratha, covered over with a net of arrows — those two great-souled ones lying on a bed of shafts, drenched with blood."

**6.40.4**  Then Sugrīva, lord of the vānaras, spoke to his son Aṅgada: "I do not think this [distress] is without cause — there must be something to fear.

**6.40.5**  For see — these monkeys with downcast faces, having abandoned their weapons, flee in all directions out of terror, their eyes wide open with fright.

**6.40.6**  They feel no shame before one another, they do not look back behind them; they push each other aside and leap over the fallen."

**6.40.7**  At that moment the hero Vibhīṣaṇa, mace in hand, greeted Sugrīva and then looked toward Rāghava.

**6.40.8**  Seeing that Vibhīṣaṇa, who struck terror into the monkeys, Sugrīva spoke to Jāmbavān, the king of bears, who stood nearby:

**6.40.9**  "This one who has arrived is Vibhīṣaṇa, upon seeing whom the foremost of monkeys scatter in utter terror, suspecting him to be Rāvaṇa's son.

**6.40.10**  Quickly rally these thoroughly terrified ones who have fled in all directions, and announce that Vibhīṣaṇa has come."

**6.40.11**  Thus addressed by Sugrīva, Jāmbavān, king of the bears, calmed the fleeing monkeys and turned them back.

**6.40.12**  All those monkeys turned back once more, their agitation laid aside, having heard the words of the king of bears and having seen Vibhīṣaṇa himself.

**6.40.13**  But Vibhīṣaṇa, righteous in soul, seeing the bodies of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa covered with arrows, became distressed in all his senses.

**6.40.14**  Wiping their eyes with his hand moistened with tears, his heart crushed by grief, he wept and lamented aloud.

**6.40.15**  "These two, endowed with valor, brave in battle, fond of combat — they have been brought to this state by demons who fight with treachery.

**6.40.16**  These two, whose valor is straightforward, have been deceived by that wicked son of my brother, that evil-souled one, through the crooked cunning that is the way of rākṣasas.

**6.40.17**  Pierced through and through by arrows, drenched in blood, lying on the earth, these two appear like porcupines.

**6.40.18**  Those two foremost of men, relying on whose valor I had hoped to establish myself — they both lie as if in the sleep that destroys the body.

**6.40.19**  Living yet I am now ruined, my hope of regaining my kingdom lost, and the enemy Rāvaṇa made to fulfill his vow and have his desire."

**6.40.20**  Sugrīva, the lord of monkeys, possessed of fortitude, embraced the lamenting Vibhīṣaṇa and spoke these words:

**6.40.21**  "You shall obtain the kingdom in Laṅkā — of this there is no doubt, O knower of dharma. That Rāvaṇa, along with his son, shall not enjoy his kingdom here.

**6.40.22**  These two, Rāghava and Lakṣmaṇa, though oppressed by arrows, will cast off their swoon and slay Rāvaṇa along with his host in battle."

**6.40.23**  Having thus consoled and reassured the rākṣasa, Sugrīva spoke to Suṣeṇa, his father-in-law, who stood at his side:

**6.40.24**  "Take the two brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, those tamers of foes when they regain consciousness, along with the host of brave monkeys, and go to Kiṣkindhā.

**6.40.25**  I, however, shall slay Rāvaṇa together with his son and kinsmen, and bring back the Maithilī, as Śakra [would recover] lost glory."

**6.40.26**  Having heard this, Suṣeṇa spoke a reply to the lord of the vānaras: "I myself witnessed the great and utterly dreadful war between the gods and asuras,

**6.40.27**  When the dānavas, skilled in the touch of arrows, experienced in weapons, struck the gods repeatedly, covering them with shafts again and again.

**6.40.28**  Those afflicted ones, unconscious and seemingly dead, Bṛhaspati treated with incantations imbued with mantras, and with medicinal herbs.

**6.40.29**  To fetch those herbs, let the monkeys — Saṃpāti, Panasa, and others — go swiftly with speed to the Milk Ocean.

**6.40.30**  The monkeys know of those great mountain-herbs — the divine Saṃjīvakaraṇī [life-restorer] and Viśalyā [arrow-extractor], fashioned by the gods.

**6.40.31**  [On] the mountains named Candra and Droṇa, foremost in the ocean — where the amṛta was churned forth — there those supreme medicinal herbs [grow].

**6.40.32**  Those supreme herbs placed there on the mountains by the gods — let this son of the Wind, Hanūmān, go there, O king."

**6.40.33**  At that very moment the wind swept through the clouds along with their lightning, and, stirring the waters of the ocean, seemed to shake the very mountains.

**6.40.34**  By the mighty gale of his wings, all the great island trees fell, their branches broken, with their roots, into the salt sea.

**6.40.35**  The hooded serpents dwelling there were terrified, and all the creatures of the sea swiftly entered the salt ocean.

**6.40.36**  Then after a moment all the monkeys beheld Garuḍa, son of Vinatā, of great might, blazing as it were like fire.

**6.40.37**  Seeing him arrive, those nāgas that had bound the two noble men, who had become mighty arrows, fled in all directions.

**6.40.38**  Then Suparṇa [Garuḍa], seeing the two of the Kākutstha line, greeted them warmly, and stroked their faces — which shone like the moon — with both his hands.

**6.40.39**  Touched by Vainateya [Garuḍa], their wounds closed up at once; their bodies became smooth and golden immediately.

**6.40.40**  Radiance, heroic energy, strength, vital power, and enterprise — those great qualities — as well as their perception, intelligence, and memory, were doubled in the two of them.

**6.40.41**  Garuḍa raised up those two of great valor, who were equal to Vāsava [Indra], and embraced them both joyfully; then Rāma addressed him:

**6.40.42**  "By your grace the great calamity arising from Rāvaṇa's son has been overcome by us here, and swiftly have we been made strong.

**6.40.43**  Just as my heart rejoices on meeting my father Daśaratha and grandfather Aja, so too does it rejoice in meeting you — be gracious.

**6.40.44**  Who are you, endowed with a beautiful form, wearing divine garlands and unguents, clothed in spotless garments, adorned with divine ornaments?"

**6.40.45**  Then Vainateya [Garuḍa], of great splendor and great might, king of birds, his heart full of affection, his eyes brimming with joy, spoke to him:

**6.40.46**  "I am your friend, O Kākutstha — your dear prāṇa that moves outside the body. I am Garuṭmān, come here for the sake of helping you both.

**6.40.47**  Neither the asuras of great valor, nor the dānavas of great power, nor even the gods along with the Gandharvas with the Hundred-Sacrificed Indra at their head —

**6.40.48**  None of these would have been capable of releasing [you from] this arrow-bondage, so dreadful, constructed by the power of illusion by Indrajit of cruel deeds.

**6.40.49**  These nāgas, sons of Kadrū, with sharp fangs and venom overflowing, by the power of rākṣasa-illusion became arrows and clung to you.

**6.40.50**  You are truly fortunate, O Rāma, knower of dharma, one whose valor is real, together with your brother Lakṣmaṇa, slayer of foes in battle.

**6.40.51**  Having heard of this event, I came in haste, swiftly, out of affection for you both, honoring the bond of friendship.

**6.40.52**  And now that you have been released from this most terrible arrow-bondage, you both must always maintain vigilance without fail.

**6.40.53**  By their very nature, all rākṣasas fight with treachery in battle; the strength of brave ones like you, who are pure in spirit, lies in straightforwardness.

**6.40.54**  Therefore you must not trust rākṣasas on the battlefield — by this very [reasoning]: rākṣasas are always crooked."

**6.40.55**  Having spoken thus, Suparṇa [Garuḍa] of very great power embraced Rāma with the affection of a dear friend, and then made ready to take his leave.

**6.40.56**  "Friend Rāghava, O knower of dharma, tender even toward enemies — I desire your leave; I shall go as I came.

**6.40.57**  Having made Laṅkā desolate but for children and the aged with waves of arrows, and having slain the enemy Rāvaṇa, you shall recover Sītā."

**6.40.58**  Having spoken these words, Suparṇa of swift motion, having made Rāma whole and free of wounds in the midst of those forest-dwellers,

**6.40.59**  Suparṇa, the valiant one, then circumambulated him and embraced him, entered the sky, and departed like the wind.

**6.40.60**  Then the leaders of the monkey troops, seeing the two Rāghavas healed of wounds, roared with lion-roars and waved their tails.

**6.40.61**  Then they beat kettledrums and sounded the mṛdaṅgas, blew their conches in great exultation, and howled as was their wont.

**6.40.62**  The valiant tree-fighting monkeys, slapping their arms in challenge again and again, uprooted various trees and stood ready by the hundreds of thousands.

**6.40.63**  The leaping monkeys, desirous of battle, emitting great roars and terrifying the night-wanderers, advanced toward the gates of Laṅkā.

**6.40.64**  Then arose a terrible, tumultuous roar from the hosts of the monkey leaders — like the most fearsome sound of thunderclouds roaring at midnight in the waning of the summer heat.

**6.41.1**  The tumultuous sound of those swift-moving monkeys roaring together with the rākṣasas was then heard by Rāvaṇa.

**6.41.2**  Having listened to that roar, which was deep, resonant, and very powerful, he spoke a word in the midst of those his ministers:

**6.41.3**  "This very great uproar of the many exultant monkeys has arisen, like the roaring of thunderclouds —

**6.41.4**  Clearly these ones are in very great delight — of this there is no doubt; for indeed with their vast roars the abode of Varuṇa [the ocean] is shaken.

**6.41.5**  Yet the two brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa were bound with sharp arrows — and this very great uproar seems to arouse suspicion in me."

**6.41.6**  Having spoken that word to his ministers, the lord of the rākṣasas then addressed the nairṛtas [demon-warriors] who attended nearby:

**6.41.7**  "Let it be found out quickly — what cause of joy has arisen for all these forest-wanderers at a time when sorrow should have prevailed?"

**6.41.8**  So commanded by him, they hastened and climbed the rampart, and saw the army marshaled by the great-souled Sugrīva.

**6.41.9**  And seeing the two Rāghavas of great dignity freed from that most terrible arrow-bondage and risen up again, the rākṣasas were stricken with dismay.

**6.41.10**  All their hearts struck with fear, they descended from the rampart and stood before the lord of rākṣasas, every face cast down.

**6.41.11**  Those night-wanderers, skilled in speech, with wretched faces, reported to Rāvaṇa that unwelcome news fully and accurately:

**6.41.12**  "Those two brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa who were bound in battle by Indrajit with the arrow-bondage, rendered immovable of arm —

**6.41.13**  — those two are seen freed from the arrow-bondage on the battlefield, like two great elephants equal in valor to lords of elephants having snapped their fetters."

**6.41.14**  Hearing their words, the lord of rākṣasas of great power, overwhelmed by anxious grief, spoke with a downcast face:

**6.41.15**  "Bound by those terrible arrows bestowed by [divine] boons, equal in venom to the āśīviṣa serpents, unfailing, blazing like the sun — having been struck down in battle by Indrajit —

**6.41.16**  If my foes have been freed after falling into that weapon-bondage, then I see all my [military] power as standing in doubt.

**6.41.17**  Truly those arrows of Vāsuki's [serpent-] power have proved fruitless — those with which the life of my enemies was seized in battle.

**6.41.18**  Having spoken thus, furious and hissing like a serpent, he spoke in the midst of the rākṣasas to the rākṣasa named Dhūmrākṣa:

**6.41.19**  "Go forth, furnished with a great force of rākṣasas of terrible deeds, to slay Rāma together with the monkeys!"

**6.41.20**  Thus commanded by the wise lord of rākṣasas, Dhūmrākṣa made obeisance, and exulting he departed from the royal palace.

**6.41.21**  Having gone out through the gate, he spoke to the marshal of troops: "Quickly muster the army — why this delay for one who desires battle?"

**6.41.22**  Hearing the words of Dhūmrākṣa, the marshal, who commanded the troops, quickly set the army in motion by Rāvaṇa's command.

**6.41.23**  Those powerful night-wanderers of dreadful forms, their bells fastened, roaring and exultant, surrounded Dhūmrākṣa.

**6.41.24**  Bearing various weapons in their hands — holding lances and hammers — [armed] greatly with clubs, broadswords, iron staffs, maces,

**6.41.25**  Iron bars, sling-stones, bhalla-arrows, javelins, and battle-axes — the terrible rākṣasas went forth roaring like thunderclouds.

**6.41.26**  Others, clad in armor on chariots adorned with banners, fitted with golden trelliswork, [drew by] asses of various faces,

**6.41.27**  [Some drawn] by supremely swift horses and by maddened lordly elephants — those tiger-rākṣasas went forth, unapproachable as tigers.

**6.41.28**  Dhūmrākṣa, loud-braying as an ass, mounted a divine chariot yoked to asses with the faces of wolves and lions, adorned with gold.

**6.41.29**  That great hero Dhūmrākṣa, surrounded by rākṣasas, went forth laughing toward the western gate, where Hanūmān was the troop-commander.

**6.41.30**  As that most dreadful rākṣasa of fearsome appearance proceeded forth, cruel birds moving through the sky obstructed his path.

**6.41.31**  A most terrible vulture fell upon the top of his chariot, and carrion-eaters fell clustered at the tip of his banner-staff.

**6.41.32**  A great white headless trunk, drenched in blood, fell to the earth near Dhūmrākṣa, emitting a discordant cry.

**6.41.33**  The sky rained blood and the earth trembled; the wind blew in the wrong direction with a sound like a thunderbolt; the quarters there were wrapped in masses of darkness and did not shine forth.

**6.41.34**  Seeing those terrifying, most dreadful portents of evil manifested for the rākṣasas, Dhūmrākṣa was distressed.

**6.41.35**  Then the most terrible and mighty one, eager for battle, went forth surrounded by many night-wanderers, and beheld that vast army of many monkeys, protected by the arm of Rāghava, vast as the ocean.

**6.42.1**  Seeing Dhūmrākṣa the rākṣasa, of fearsome sound, going forth, all the monkeys, joyful and eager for battle, roared out.

**6.42.2**  Then arose a tumultuous battle between the monkeys and the rākṣasas, each striking the other with terrible trees and with lances and hammers.

**6.42.3**  The terrible monkeys were cut down on all sides by the rākṣasas; and the rākṣasas in turn were leveled to the ground by the monkeys with trees.

**6.42.4**  And the rākṣasas, enraged, pierced the monkeys with sharp arrows — those of terrible appearance, feathered with heron-feathers, flying straight.

**6.42.5**  With those dreadful clubs and broadswords, iron hammers, with terrible iron bars of various kinds, and with sharpened tridents —

**6.42.6**  Though torn apart by the rākṣasas, those monkeys of great might, their spirit roused by indignation, performed their deeds without fear.

**6.42.7**  Their bodies pierced through with arrows, their frames transfixed with lances, those leaders of monkey troops nonetheless seized trees and boulders there.

**6.42.8**  Those monkeys of terrible velocity, roaring here and there, churned through the dreadful rākṣasas and called out their own names.

**6.42.9**  The battle between the monkeys and rākṣasas was wondrous and dreadful, fought with boulders of various kinds and with trees of many branches.

**6.42.10**  Some rākṣasas, crushed by the victorious-seeming monkeys, gushed blood; some, blood-eaters, rained it from their mouths.

**6.42.11**  Some were ripped open in the flanks, some were piled in heaps by trees, some were pulverized by boulders, and some were torn apart by teeth.

**6.42.12**  With banners shattered and broken, with asses brought down and slain, with chariots smashed and destroyed, with night-wanderers fallen —

**6.42.13**  The rākṣasas were slashed in the face by the sharp nails of the swift-moving monkeys of terrible valor, who bounded upon them again and again.

**6.42.14**  Their faces discolored, their hair disheveled, stupefied by the smell of blood, they fell to the surface of the earth.

**6.42.15**  Yet some rākṣasas, supremely enraged and of terrible valor, attacked the monkeys with open palms equal to thunderbolts in their touch.

**6.42.16**  Those [rākṣasas] who charged were beaten back by the swifter-moving monkeys with fists, feet, teeth, and trees.

**6.42.17**  Seeing the army in rout, Dhūmrākṣa, foremost of rākṣasas, in his anger wrought carnage among the battle-seeking monkeys.

**6.42.18**  Some monkeys were struck down with javelins and streamed with blood; some struck with hammers fell to the ground.

**6.42.19**  Some were crushed with iron bars, some were split open with sling-stones, some were struck with broadswords and expired in agony.

**6.42.20**  Some of the forest-dwellers lay slain on the ground, drenched in blood; some were routed and lost sight of, scattered by the furious rākṣasas in battle.

**6.42.21**  Some lay with hearts split open, fallen on one side; some were torn open by weapons and lances, their entrails spilling out.

**6.42.22**  That most terrible great battle, crowded with monkeys and rākṣasas, blazed forth — abounding in weapons, thronging with boulders and trees.

**6.42.23**  It blazed as a gandharva-music of war — sweet with the strain of bowstrings, accompanied by yells and the beat of time, a symphony of deep rumbling thunder.

**6.42.24**  But Dhūmrākṣa, bow in hand, laughing, scattered the monkeys in all directions at the forefront of battle with showers of arrows.

**6.42.25**  Seeing the army tormented and afflicted by Dhūmrākṣa, Māruti [Hanūmān] came forward in great fury, grasping an enormous boulder.

**6.42.26**  His eyes doubly reddened with rage, his valor equal to his father's, he hurled that boulder against the chariot of Dhūmrākṣa.

**6.42.27**  Seeing the boulder rushing down, Dhūmrākṣa in alarm lifted his mace, leaped swiftly from his chariot, and stood on the ground.

**6.42.28**  That boulder, having smashed his chariot, fell upon the stony ground along with its wheels and pole, its horses, its banner, and its bow.

**6.42.29**  Having shattered his chariot, Hanūmān son of the Wind then wrought carnage among the rākṣasas with trees bearing their branches and boughs.

**6.42.30**  Their heads split open, the rākṣasas, drenched in blood, and others crushed by the trees, fell to the ground.

**6.42.31**  Routing the rākṣasa army, Hanūmān son of the Wind took a mountain peak and rushed at Dhūmrākṣa.

**6.42.32**  Seeing him charging, Dhūmrākṣa, valiant, mace raised, suddenly rushed roaring at Hanūmān.

**6.42.33**  Then in rage Dhūmrākṣa with great force brought down that spike-studded mace upon the head of Hanūmān.

**6.42.34**  Struck by that mace of fearsome form, the monkey of wind-like might disregarded that blow and brought the mountain peak down on the very crown of Dhūmrākṣa's head.

**6.42.35**  His every limb convulsed, struck down by the mountain peak, he fell suddenly to the earth like a mountain shattered asunder.

**6.42.36**  Seeing Dhūmrākṣa slain, the surviving night-wanderers, terrified, fled back into Laṅkā even as they were being cut down by the leaping monkeys.

**6.42.37**  The great-souled son of the Wind, having slain the enemy and scattered rivers of blood from wounds, wearied by the slaughter of the foe, rejoiced and was honored by the monkeys.

**6.43.1**  Having heard that Dhūmrākṣa was slain, Rāvaṇa, lord of the Rākṣasas, spoke these words to the commander of the army who stood before him with palms joined.

**6.43.2**  "Let the invincible Rākṣasas of terrible valor go forth swiftly, placing at their head Akampana, who is skilled in all weapons."

**6.43.3**  Then the foremost Rākṣasas, bearing various weapons, with fearsome eyes and fearsome appearance, rushed forth, urged on by the commander of the army.

**6.43.4**  Mounting a great chariot, his earrings of molten gold gleaming, Akampana then marched forth, surrounded by fearsome Rākṣasas.

**6.43.5**  Indeed Akampana could not be made to tremble even by the gods in great battle; blazing with the splendor of the sun, he shone before them.

**6.43.6**  Yet as he rushed forward, seized with eagerness for battle, a sudden distress came over the horses that drew his chariot.

**6.43.7**  His left eye twitched, of him who delighted in battle; his face grew pale, and his voice became faltering.

**6.43.8**  Though the day had been auspicious, a harsh wind arose making it inauspicious; all the birds and beasts cried out with cruel cries foreboding fear.

**6.43.9**  Yet he, with shoulders as massive as a lion's, his valor equal to a tiger's, gave no thought to those ill omens and marched out onto the battlefield.

**6.43.10**  As that Rākṣasa marched out together with his Rākṣasas, a tremendous roar arose, as if shaking the ocean itself.

**6.43.11**  Alarmed by that sound, the great army of monkeys, armed with trees and rocks, stood ready for battle.

**6.43.12**  Between those monkeys and Rākṣasas a terrible battle arose, for the sake of Rāma and Rāvaṇa, among those who had utterly cast away their lives.

**6.43.13**  All were exceedingly strong, all were heroic, all — monkeys and Rākṣasas alike — resembled mountains, and all were eager to slay one another.

**6.43.14**  The tremendous sound of those swift combatants roaring in battle could be heard as they bellowed furiously at one another.

**6.43.15**  Dust of a deep reddish hue, exceedingly dreadful and thick, stirred up by the monkeys and Rākṣasas, blocked all ten directions.

**6.43.16**  In that dust, white as silk shaken out, the beings on the battlefield could not see one another at all, so thoroughly were they covered.

**6.43.17**  Neither banner, nor flag, nor armor, nor horse, nor weapon, nor chariot — none could be seen in that dust.

**6.43.18**  In that tumultuous battle only the tremendous sound of those roaring warriors charging could be heard; their forms were not visible.

**6.43.19**  In that darkness, furious monkeys struck down monkeys in battle, and Rākṣasas likewise slew Rākṣasas.

**6.43.20**  Slaying enemies and allies alike, the monkeys and Rākṣasas at that time made the earth sodden with blood, smeared as if with mud.

**6.43.21**  Then, drenched by the flood of blood, the dust settled; and the earth became strewn with corpses and body parts.

**6.43.22**  With trees, spears, rocks, javelins, clubs, iron bars, and pikes, the monkeys and Rākṣasas quickly struck each other with might.

**6.43.23**  Fighting with arms shaped like iron bars, those monkeys like mountains, performers of terrible deeds, struck down the Rākṣasas in battle.

**6.43.24**  The furious Rākṣasas too, javelins and pikes in hand, slew the monkeys there with exceedingly dreadful weapons.

**6.43.25**  But the monkeys, advancing against the Rākṣasas with great trees and great boulders, tore them apart and shattered their weapons by force.

**6.43.26**  Meanwhile, the heroic monkeys Kumuda, Nala, and Mainda, exceedingly enraged, exerted their utmost speed.

**6.43.27**  Those monkey chiefs, with great speed, wrought tremendous slaughter at the forefront of the Rākṣasa army with trees, as if in sport.

**6.44.1**  Witnessing that great feat accomplished by those foremost of monkeys, Akampana summoned fierce anger in battle.

**6.44.2**  His form overcome with rage, twanging his supreme bow, and having witnessed the deeds of the enemies, he spoke these words to his charioteer:

**6.44.3**  "Drive the chariot quickly to that very spot, charioteer — these many foes are slaying very many Rākṣasas in battle."

**6.44.4**  "These monkeys here are powerful and of fearsome frame; armed with trees and rocks they stand before me."

**6.44.5**  "I, who glory in battle, wish to slay them — for the entire Rākṣasa force is seen to have been shattered by them."

**6.44.6**  Then Akampana, best of chariot warriors, borne in his chariot by swift horses, struck the monkeys in anger with nets of arrows.

**6.44.7**  The monkeys were unable to stand their ground, much less fight in battle; shattered by Akampana's arrows, they all fled in panic.

**6.44.8**  Seeing those kinsmen fallen under the sway of death, come under Akampana's power, the mighty Hanūmān stood forth to protect them.

**6.44.9**  Seeing that great monkey, all the heroic monkey chiefs assembled in battle and surrounded Hanūmān together.

**6.44.10**  Those monkey leaders, seeing Hanūmān stationed there, became strong themselves — for the strong take refuge with the strong.

**6.44.11**  But Akampana poured arrows down upon Hanūmān standing there like a mountain, just as Mahendra pours rain with streams of water.

**6.44.12**  Giving no thought to the sharp arrows that had fallen upon his body in floods, the mighty one fixed his mind on killing Akampana.

**6.44.13**  Then, laughing aloud, the mighty Hanūmān, son of the Wind, charged at that Rākṣasa, shaking the very earth as he ran.

**6.44.14**  Of him who charged with a roar, blazing with splendor, there appeared a form irresistible, like that of blazing fire.

**6.44.15**  Knowing himself to be without a weapon at hand, that bull among monkeys, filled with rage, uprooted a mountain with speed.

**6.44.16**  Taking that great mountain in one hand, the mighty son of the Wind, letting out a tremendous roar, began to whirl it.

**6.44.17**  Then he charged at Akampana, lord of Rākṣasas, just as Purandara charged at Namuci in battle with his thunderbolt.

**6.44.18**  But Akampana, seeing that mountain peak raised aloft, split it from a distance with great crescent-headed arrows.

**6.44.19**  Seeing that mountain peak split by the Rākṣasa's arrows and falling scattered through the sky, Hanūmān was overcome with rage.

**6.44.20**  That monkey, filled with fury and pride, quickly went to an Aśvakarṇa tree and uprooted it as if it were a great risen mountain.

**6.44.21**  Taking that Aśvakarṇa tree of great trunk, that one of great splendor, laughing with extreme delight, whirled it in battle.

**6.44.22**  Running with great speed, swiftly shattering trees, Hanūmān, supremely enraged, split the earth with his feet.

**6.44.23**  The wise Hanūmān slew elephants with their riders, charioteers with their chariots, and Rākṣasa foot soldiers as well.

**6.44.24**  Seeing Hanūmān in battle, angry as Death itself, a taker of life, the Rākṣasas fled in all directions.

**6.44.25**  The heroic Akampana saw him charging, supremely wrathful, the terror of Rākṣasas; he grew furious and bellowed.

**6.44.26**  Akampana pierced the mighty Hanūmān with fourteen sharp arrows that rent the body.

**6.44.27**  Though thus pierced by those many showers of arrows, the heroic Hanūmān appeared like a mountain in full bloom.

**6.44.28**  Then, uprooting another tree, and exerting his utmost speed, he quickly struck Akampana, lord of Rākṣasas, on the head.

**6.44.29**  Struck by that tree by the great-souled lord of monkeys in his wrath, that Rākṣasa fell and died.

**6.44.30**  Seeing Akampana, lord of Rākṣasas, struck down upon the earth, all the Rākṣasas were shaken like trees in an earthquake.

**6.44.31**  All those defeated Rākṣasas, having abandoned their weapons, fled terrified to Laṅkā, chased by those monkeys.

**6.44.32**  They fled in panic, hair loose, pride shattered, defeated, panting, their bodies streaming with sweat.

**6.44.33**  They stumbled against one another and entered the city out of fear, completely bewildered, glancing back again and again.

**6.44.34**  When those Rākṣasas had entered Laṅkā, all the mighty monkeys assembled and honored Hanūmān.

**6.44.35**  And Hanūmān, endowed with excellence, joyful in return, honored all those monkeys fittingly and graciously.

**6.44.36**  The monkeys roared with the breath of victory and dragged back there the Rākṣasas who were still alive.

**6.44.37**  That great monkey, the son of the Wind, having slain the Rākṣasas and that terrible great Asura, destroyer of enemies, bore the glory of a hero — just as Viṣṇu at the forefront of battle slew the mighty Bali.

**6.44.38**  The hosts of gods honored that monkey at that time; and mighty Rāma himself, and Lakṣmaṇa of exceeding strength, and likewise the monkeys headed by Sugrīva, and the mighty Vibhīṣaṇa — all honored him then.

**6.45.1**  Having heard of the slaying of Akampana, the lord of Rākṣasas grew furious; his face somewhat downcast, he looked upon his ministers.

**6.45.2**  After reflecting for a moment and deliberating with his ministers, he went around the city of Laṅkā to inspect all the garrisons.

**6.45.3**  He saw the city of Laṅkā, protected by hosts of Rākṣasas, surrounded by many garrisons, garlanded with banners and flags.

**6.45.4**  Seeing the city besieged, Rāvaṇa, lord of Rākṣasas, in his anger spoke at the right moment to Prahasta, skilled in warfare:

**6.45.5**  "For a city suddenly besieged and oppressed, I see no other deliverance than battle, O master of warfare.

**6.45.6**  "I, or Kumbhakarṇa, or you my general, or Indrajit, or Nikumbha — one of us must bear such a burden.

**6.45.7**  "Therefore, taking and marshaling the army swiftly from here, go forth for victory to where all those forest dwellers are."

**6.45.8**  "Surely upon your mere march out, the fickle monkey host will flee upon hearing the roar of the Rākṣasa lords."

**6.45.9**  "For monkeys are fickle, undisciplined, and unstable of mind; they will not endure your roar, just as elephants cannot endure a lion's roar."

**6.45.10**  "And when that army is scattered in flight, Rāma together with the son of Sumitrā will remain helpless without support, and will come under Prahasta's power."

**6.45.11**  "In calamity, the better course is the one fraught with doubt, not the certain one — or weigh what you consider favorable, for or against."

**6.45.12**  Thus addressed by Rāvaṇa, Prahasta, commander of the army, spoke these words to the lord of Rākṣasas, as Uśanā speaks to the lord of Asuras:

**6.45.13**  "O king, we took counsel before with skilled ministers, and debate too arose among us as we examined the matter from every side.

**6.45.14**  "By the return of Sītā I determined that the better course lay; but in the case of not returning her, battle was likewise seen by us to be the outcome.

**6.45.15**  "I, who have been constantly honored by you with gifts, honor, and various conciliations — how at this moment should I not do what is dear to you?

**6.45.16**  "My life is not to be guarded, nor my sons, wife, or wealth — behold me offering up my life in battle for your sake."

**6.45.17**  Having thus spoken to his lord Rāvaṇa, the army commander said: "Bring me quickly the great force of Rākṣasas."

**6.45.18**  "Today let the birds feast on the flesh of those forest dwellers slain in battle by the thunderbolt speed of my arrows."

**6.45.19**  Thus addressed by Prahasta, the army commanders, making haste, mobilized the army in that Rākṣasa palace.

**6.45.20**  Within a moment Laṅkā was crowded with those heroic Rākṣasas bearing sharp and various weapons, thronging like elephants.

**6.45.21**  As they propitiated the sacred fire and paid homage to the Brāhmaṇas, a fragrant wind blew carrying the scent of clarified butter.

**6.45.22**  The Rākṣasas, ready for battle and joyful, took garlands of various kinds consecrated with sacred formulas and wore them then.

**6.45.23**  The Rākṣasas, bearing bows and clad in armor, leaped up with speed, and gazing upon King Rāvaṇa, surrounded Prahasta.

**6.45.24**  Then, having taken leave of the king and beating the dreadful war drum, Prahasta mounted his divine chariot, fully equipped and made ready.

**6.45.25**  Yoked with horses of great speed, properly fitted with a skilled charioteer, its sound like a great cloud's thunder, blazing like the sun and moon themselves —

**6.45.26**  — Irresistible with its serpent-banner, well-fitted with a hood, bearing good arms, covered with a net of gold, and as if laughing with splendor —

**6.45.27**  Then Prahasta, executing the command entrusted by Rāvaṇa, mounted that chariot and quickly marched out of Laṅkā, surrounded by a great army.

**6.45.28**  Then as the army commander marched forth, the sound of kettledrums like the rumble of rain clouds was heard, and the sound of conches as well.

**6.45.29**  Rākṣasas of fearsome form and massive frame, roaring with terrible cries, marched at the fore as Prahasta's vanguard.

**6.45.30**  He marched out by the eastern gate in battle-array, exceedingly terrible, surrounded by a great army resembling a herd of elephants.

**6.45.31**  Surrounded by that army resembling the ocean's flood, Prahasta marched out swiftly, wrathful, his anger like that of Death at the end of an age.

**6.45.32**  At the sound of his march and the roaring of the Rākṣasas, all creatures in Laṅkā cried out with distorted voices.

**6.45.33**  Birds that feed on flesh and blood, entering the cloudless sky, circled in inauspicious spirals toward his chariot.

**6.45.34**  Jackals, dreadful and terrible, howled, vomiting flames of fire.

**6.45.35**  A meteor fell from the sky, a harsh wind blew, and the planets, in conflict with one another, shone not at all.

**6.45.36**  Rain of blood fell upon him and drenched his vanguard; a vulture perched atop his standard facing south.

**6.45.37**  As his charioteer plunged repeatedly into battle, the goad fell from the hand of the chariot-driver who guided the horses.

**6.45.38**  The radiant glory of victory that had attended his march — dazzling and exceedingly rare — vanished in a moment, and on level ground his horses stumbled.

**6.45.39**  But against Prahasta who marched forth, renowned for his strength and valor, the monkey army bearing various weapons advanced for battle.

**6.45.40**  Then a tremendous din arose from the monkeys as they tore up trees and seized heavy boulders.

**6.45.41**  Both armies were eager — the hosts of Rākṣasas and the forest dwellers — swift, capable, each hungering to slay the other; a great din was heard as they challenged one another.

**6.45.42**  Then Prahasta, of evil mind, charged at the monkey king's army for victory, and entered that swelling force with its growing momentum — as a moth seeking death enters fire.

**6.46.1**  Then the great monkey army beheld Prahasta marching forth — terrible, of terrible valour, roaring, of immense body, surrounded by rākṣasas.

**6.46.2**  The mighty army of the most powerful monkeys, exceedingly enraged, saw Prahasta and roared back at him.

**6.46.3**  Swords, lances, staffs, arrows, spears, clubs, maces, iron bars, javelins, and axes of many kinds —

**6.46.4**  — and variously adorned bows, all raised aloft by victory-seeking rākṣasas rushing at the monkeys, shone brilliantly.

**6.46.5**  The bull-like monkeys, eager to fight, seized flowering trees and broad, long boulders.

**6.46.6**  When the two sides met, a tremendous battle arose, with many warriors pouring down showers of stones and showers of arrows upon one another.

**6.46.7**  Many rākṣasas in battle slew many monkey troop-leaders, and many monkeys likewise slew many rākṣasas.

**6.46.8**  Some were slaughtered by spears, some struck down by mighty weapons, some beaten with iron bars, and some hewn apart by axes.

**6.46.9**  Some fell breathless upon the ground; some had their hearts cloven asunder; some were pierced through by a continuous stream of arrows.

**6.46.10**  Some were split in two by swords and fell quivering upon the earth; monkeys were ripped open through the flanks by the spears of the rākṣasas.

**6.46.11**  And by the enraged monkeys, great swarms of rākṣasas on all sides were crushed to the ground with trees and mountain peaks.

**6.46.12**  Struck hard with palms and fists that hit like thunderbolts, they cried out and vomited blood from their mouths, their teeth and eyes shattered.

**6.46.13**  Of the monkeys and rākṣasas in battle — some shrieking cries of pain, some roaring lion-roars — a tumultuous din arose.

**6.46.14**  The furious monkeys and rākṣasas, devoted to the path of heroes, with eyes wide open, fierce, performed their deeds fearlessly.

**6.46.15**  Narāntaka, Kumbhahanu, and the towering Mahānāda — all these ministers of Prahasta struck down the forest-dwellers.

**6.46.16**  As they rushed forward swiftly and struck down the monkeys, Dvivida slew Narāntaka alone with a mountain peak.

**6.46.17**  Then the monkey Durmukha, quick of hand, uprooted a great tree and smashed the towering rākṣasa Mahānāda.

**6.46.18**  Then Jāmbavān, greatly enraged and full of brilliance, seized an enormous boulder and hurled it onto the chest of Mahānāda.

**6.46.19**  Then Kumbhahanu was encountered there by the valiant Tāra; struck on the head with a tree, the rākṣasa gave up his life.

**6.46.20**  Prahasta, unable to endure this, mounted his chariot and, bow in hand, wrought terrible slaughter among the forest-dwellers.

**6.46.21**  Then between the two armies there arose what seemed like a whirlpool, and a sound like that of the immeasurable, churning ocean.

**6.46.22**  For Prahasta, skilled in warfare and greatly wrathful, afflicted the monkeys with a great torrent of arrows in that supreme battle.

**6.46.23**  The dreadful earth was heaped with the bodies of monkeys and rākṣasas, as if with fallen mountains.

**6.46.24**  That earth, covered by a great flood of blood, shone like the month of Mādhava covered with the blossoms of the palāśa tree.

**6.46.25**  The embankments of that river were heaps of slain heroes; its great trees were shattered weapons; its great waters were floods of blood — a river flowing to the ocean of Yama —

**6.46.26**  — its great mud was made of livers and spleens; its aquatic moss was scattered entrails; its fish were severed bodies and heads; its grass was made of limbs and body-parts —

**6.46.27**  — thronged with flocks of vultures and herons, frequented by kaṅka-birds and sārasas, covered with the foam of fat, resounding with cries of agony —

**6.46.28**  — that river formed of the battlefield, impossible for cowards to cross, like a river at the end of the rains frequented by swans and sārasas —

**6.46.29**  — that impassable river the rākṣasas and the leading monkeys crossed, as elephant herd-lords cross a lotus pool whose pollen has been scattered.

**6.46.30**  Then Nīla swiftly saw Prahasta standing in his chariot, releasing torrents of arrows and slaying the monkeys.

**6.46.31**  The mighty great monkey Nīla, valiant, uprooted a tree and struck Prahasta who was rushing toward him — utterly difficult to overcome.

**6.46.32**  Struck by him, the enraged bull among rākṣasas, roaring, poured showers of arrows upon the commander of the monkey army.

**6.46.33**  Unable to ward it off, Nīla received it with eyes half-closed, as a bull-ox receives the autumn rain that has arrived swiftly.

**6.46.34**  In exactly this way, Nīla with eyes half-closed suddenly endured the fearsome, difficult-to-withstand shower of arrows from Prahasta.

**6.46.35**  Enraged by the shower of arrows, the great Nīla struck Prahasta's mind-swift horses with a great sāla tree.

**6.46.36**  Prahasta, the army commander, thus rendered bowless by him, seized a terrible club and leaped down from his chariot.

**6.46.37**  Both commanders of the armies, roused to wrath and swift, stood with bodies smeared with blood, like two maddened elephants.

**6.46.38**  Raking each other with their very sharp tusks — those two resembling lions and tigers, moving like lions and tigers.

**6.46.39**  Those two heroes, victorious in their prowess, never turning back in battle, eager to attain glory — equals of Vṛtra and Vāsava.

**6.46.40**  Then Prahasta, greatly exerting himself, struck Nīla on the forehead with his club; blood flowed from that blow.

**6.46.41**  Then the great monkey, his body smeared with blood, furious, seized an enormous tree and hurled it at Prahasta's chest.

**6.46.42**  He — Prahasta, himself powerful — seized his great club to strike an inconceivable blow and charged at the mighty monkey Nīla.

**6.46.43**  Then the great monkey, himself of great speed, watching him — fierce and furious — as he rushed forward with terrible velocity, seized an enormous boulder.

**6.46.44**  Upon Prahasta — who was eager for battle, fighting with his club — Nīla swiftly brought the boulder crashing down upon his head.

**6.46.45**  That great, terrible boulder, released by the foremost of monkeys, shattered Prahasta's head into many pieces.

**6.46.46**  He — his life gone, his glory gone, his vitality gone, his senses gone — suddenly fell to the earth like a tree with its roots cut.

**6.46.47**  From his shattered head blood flowed copiously; it flowed also from his body, like a mountain spring.

**6.46.48**  When Prahasta was slain by Nīla, that great, formerly immovable army of the rākṣasas, their joy gone, made its way back to Laṅkā.

**6.46.49**  They could not stand their ground once their army-commander was slain — like water that, upon reaching a broken dam, scatters away.

**6.46.50**  When that army-commander was slain, those rākṣasas, their effort spent, went to the dwelling of the rākṣasa-lord and fell into a mute silence of dejection.

**6.46.51**  Then Nīla, victorious and of great strength, praised for the deed he had accomplished, rejoined Rāma together with Lakṣmaṇa, and the troop-lord was filled with joy.

**6.47.1**  When that guardian of the rākṣasa army had been slain in battle by the foremost of the leaping monkeys, the army of the rākṣasa king, bearing terrible weapons and moving with the speed of the ocean, fled in panic.

**6.47.2**  Going to the lord of rākṣasas, they reported that the general had been struck down by the son of Pāvaka; and upon hearing those words of theirs, the lord of rākṣasas fell under the sway of rage.

**6.47.3**  Having learned that Prahasta had been slain in battle, tormented by grief and with his mind consumed by anger, he addressed those foremost warriors of the rākṣasas even as Indra addresses the foremost warriors of the immortals.

**6.47.4**  "No contempt is to be shown toward the enemy, by whom my general — the destroyer of Indra's host — has been slain along with his retinue and his elephants.

**6.47.5**  I myself, without deliberation, shall go to that wondrous forefront of battle for the destruction of the enemy and for victory.

**6.47.6**  Today I shall burn down that monkey army and Rāma together with Lakṣmaṇa with torrents of arrows, as blazing fires burn a forest."

**6.47.7**  Having spoken thus, the enemy of the king of immortals mounted his chariot that blazed like fire, yoked with the finest horses, radiant and blazing with its own splendor.

**6.47.8**  Honored with the sounds of conches, kettledrums, and war drums, with the snapping of fingers, war-cries, and lion-roars, and with auspicious hymns of praise, the foremost king of rākṣasas then set forth.

**6.47.9**  Surrounded by the foremost kings of rākṣasas whose forms resembled mountains and storm-clouds, flesh-eating, with eyes blazing like fire, he shone like Rudra attended by spirits and like the lord of immortals.

**6.47.10**  Then, having sallied forth swiftly from the city in great might, he beheld that terrible monkey army, roaring like the ocean and storm-clouds, standing ready with trees and boulders in hand.

**6.47.11**  Beholding that most ferocious rākṣasa force, Rāma, whose arms were like serpent-lords, attended by his army and endowed with great glory, spoke to Vibhīṣaṇa, the foremost among those who bear weapons.

**6.47.12**  "Whose is this army, unshakeable and fearless, adorned with many banners and standards and weapons, equipped with spears, swords, tridents, missiles, and discuses, furnished with elephants resembling lordly mountains?"

**6.47.13**  Then, having heard Rāma's words, Vibhīṣaṇa, equal in valor to Śakra, described to Rāma the foremost champions among those great-souled rākṣasa bulls.

**6.47.14**  "That great-souled one seated on an elephant's shoulder, whose face is copper-red like the newly risen sun, causing the elephant's head to tremble as he approaches — know him, O king, as Akampana.

**6.47.15**  That one standing in his chariot with a lion as his emblem, brandishing a bow bright as Śakra's bow, who gleams like an elephant with fiercely bared tusks — he is the foremost of the great ones named Indrajit.

**6.47.16**  And this one, comparable to Vindhya, Asta, and Mahendra, a bowman standing in his chariot, a surpassing chariot-fighter of surpassing valor, drawing his matchless bow — by name Atikāya, of exceedingly large body.

**6.47.17**  That great-souled one with copper-colored eyes like the newly risen sun, mounted on a harsh elephant resounding with the clanging of bells, who roars mightily — that hero is known as Mahodara.

**6.47.18**  That one mounted on a horse with golden and variegated trappings, gleaming like clouds and mountains at twilight, raising a spear bound with rays of light — this apparition moves with the speed of a thunderbolt.

**6.47.19**  And this illustrious one who comes grasping a sharp trident bright as lightning, swift as the vajra of a servant of death, mounted on a lordly bull gleaming like a mountain — he is Triśirā.

**6.47.20**  And that one of cloud-like form is Kumbha, with a broad and well-formed chest, collected and steady, with the king of serpents as his emblem, who shines as he draws and brandishes his bow.

**6.47.21**  And this one who comes bearing a blazing, smoke-trailing iron club adorned with gold and vajra-gems, who stands as the standard of the rākṣasa host — he is Nikumbha, of wondrous and terrible deeds.

**6.47.22**  And this proud one who stands shining on a banner-flying chariot blazing like fire, furnished with bows, swords, and torrents of arrows — that is Narāntaka, who fights on mountain peaks.

**6.47.23**  And this one who shines surrounded by spirits of many terrible forms — with faces of tigers, camels, serpent-lords, and lion-lords — with rolling eyes, he is the very crusher of the pride of the gods.

**6.47.24**  Where that foremost white umbrella shines, resembling the moon, with fine ribs — there the great-souled lord of rākṣasas shines, surrounded by spirits, like Rudra himself.

**6.47.25**  That diademed one with swinging earrings on his face, of terrible form comparable to the lord of serpents and to Vindhya, the crusher of the pride of Mahendra and Vaivasvata — the lord of rākṣasas shines like the sun."

**6.47.26**  Then Rāma replied to Vibhīṣaṇa, the subduer of foes: "Ah, how blazing and of great splendor is Rāvaṇa, the lord of rākṣasas!

**6.47.27**  Rāvaṇa blazes, hard to look upon like the sun with its rays. I perceive his form clearly — it is enveloped in splendor.

**6.47.28**  The form of the heroes among gods and dānavas could not be like the form of the lord of rākṣasas that shines forth here.

**6.47.29**  All his warriors resemble mountains, all fight like mountains, all bear blazing weapons, and all are of great might.

**6.47.30**  That king of rākṣasas shines, surrounded by blazing, terribly valiant spirits with embodied forms, like Death himself."

**6.47.31**  Having spoken thus, the valiant Rāma, accompanied by Lakṣmaṇa, took up his bow and stood firm, raising an excellent arrow.

**6.47.32**  Then that great-souled lord of rākṣasas addressed those mighty rākṣasas: "Stand content and without fear at the gates, the patrol-houses, and the city towers."

**6.47.33**  Having swiftly dismissed them, and when the rākṣasas had gone to their posts, he split apart the ocean-like tide of monkeys as a great fish splits the full surge of the sea.

**6.47.34**  Beholding that lord of rākṣasas rushing forward swiftly in battle with blazing arrows and bow, the lord of monkeys uprooted a great mountain peak and charged toward the lord of rākṣasas.

**6.47.35**  Grasping that mountain summit with its many trees and ridges, he hurled it at the night-roaming rākṣasa; but seeing it rushing down, Rāvaṇa quickly split it with arrows fletched with gold.

**6.47.36**  When that peak, with its great ridge-trees, was shattered and fell to the earth, the lord of the world of rākṣasas took up an arrow resembling a great serpent and resembling Death himself.

**6.47.37**  Seizing that arrow swift as the wind, blazing with sparks and flames, with a speed matching the thunderbolt of Mahendra, he hurled it in rage for the destruction of Sugrīva.

**6.47.38**  That arrow, released from Rāvaṇa's arm, with a form like Śakra's thunderbolt and a sharp tip, reached Sugrīva and pierced him with force, as the fierce javelin hurled by Guha pierces a krauñca bird.

**6.47.39**  That hero, stricken by the arrow, with his senses overthrown, moaning, fell to the earth; and seeing him fallen senseless on the ground, the yātudhānas rejoiced and shouted in the battle.

**6.47.40**  Then Gavākṣa, Gavaya, Sudaṃṣṭra, Ṛṣabha, Jyotimukha, and Nala, their bodies grown enormous, lifted up boulders and charged toward that lord of rākṣasas.

**6.47.41**  The lord of rākṣasas made those attacks like clouds of theirs [dissolve] with masses of sharp-tipped arrows, and he also pierced those lords of monkeys with networks of arrows with gold-variegated fletching.

**6.47.42**  Those lords of monkeys, terrible in form, pierced by the arrows of the foe of the thirty gods, fell to the ground; then he covered that fierce monkey army with nets of arrows.

**6.47.43**  Those monkeys, being slaughtered, their foremost heroes fallen, crying out, pierced with the dart of fear, tormented by Rāvaṇa's arrows, fled to Rāma as their only refuge and shelter.

**6.47.44**  Then the great-souled Rāma, that archer, taking up his bow with arrows, advanced; and Lakṣmaṇa, approaching with joined palms, spoke words of highest significance.

**6.47.45**  "Though the noble one is certainly fully capable of slaying this wicked-souled foe, I shall destroy this base creature — grant me leave, O lord."

**6.47.46**  The great-splendored Rāma of true valor said to him: "Go, and be most diligent in the battle, O Lakṣmaṇa."

**6.47.47**  "For Rāvaṇa is of great might, of wondrous valor in battle; even if provoked by the three worlds together, he is difficult to withstand — there is no doubt of this.

**6.47.48**  Seek out his vulnerabilities and guard your own; with eye and bow alike, carefully and with collected mind, protect yourself."

**6.47.49**  Having heard the words of Rāghava, embracing him with honor and bowing to Rāma, the son of Sumitrā then went to battle.

**6.47.50**  He, whose arms were like an elephant's trunk, beheld Rāvaṇa with his terrible bow raised and blazing, covering those monkeys — their bodies shattered and scattered — with nets of arrow-showers.

**6.47.51**  Beholding him, the great-splendored Hanūmān, son of the wind-god, warding off nets of arrows, charged toward Rāvaṇa.

**6.47.52**  Having approached his chariot and raising his right arm, the wise Hanūmān, threatening Rāvaṇa, spoke these words:

**6.47.53**  "Gods, dānavas, gandharvas, yakṣas together with rākṣasas — these you have defeated by virtue of your boon of invulnerability, but you have fear from monkeys.

**6.47.54**  This right arm of mine, five-branched, raised on high, shall drive the soul long lodged within your body from its dwelling."

**6.47.55**  Having heard Hanūmān's words, Rāvaṇa of terrible valor, with eyes reddened by anger, spoke these words:

**6.47.56**  "Strike quickly and without hesitation — win enduring glory; then I shall destroy you as a hero among your kin, O monkey."

**6.47.57**  Having heard Rāvaṇa's words, the son of Vāyu spoke in reply: "I have struck before — remember your son Akṣa!"
