**6.47.58**  Thus addressed, the great-splendored lord of rākṣasas Rāvaṇa, full of valor, struck the son of the wind-god on the chest with his palm.

**6.47.59**  Struck by that palm blow, he swayed again and again; then, greatly enraged, he struck the enemy of the immortals in return with his very palm.

**6.47.60**  Then Daśagrīva, struck by the palm of the great-souled monkey, was shaken like a mountain in an earthquake.

**6.47.61**  Seeing Rāvaṇa struck by that palm blow in battle, the sages, monkeys, siddhas, gods together with asuras, all shouted.

**6.47.62**  Then the great-splendored Rāvaṇa, recovering, spoke: "Well done! By your monkey valor you are a praiseworthy foe of mine."

**6.47.63**  Thus addressed by Rāvaṇa, the son of Māruta replied: "Shame upon my valor, in that you still live, O Rāvaṇa!

**6.47.64**  "Strike just once now, O foolish one — why do you boast? After that, my fist will bring you to destruction." At the words of the son of Māruta, his anger then blazed up.

**6.47.65**  With eyes reddened, straining hard, he raised his right fist and with force brought it down on the monkey's chest; the valiant Hanūmān, struck on his broad chest, again staggered.

**6.47.66**  Seeing the mighty Hanūmān then dazed, that warrior supreme among chariot-fighters quickly turned his chariot toward Nīla.

**6.47.67**  With terrible arrows resembling serpents that pierced vital points in the deepest marrow, he set Nīla, the commander of the monkey host, ablaze.

**6.47.68**  Afflicted by that flood of arrows, Nīla, commander of the monkey host, hurled the peak of a mountain at the lord of rākṣasas with a single hand.

**6.47.69**  Hanūmān too, splendid and great-minded, having recovered, looking about and eager for battle, spoke these words in anger:

**6.47.70**  "It is not proper to rush against Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas, who is engaged in fighting with Nīla — to attack one already occupied with another."

**6.47.71**  Rāvaṇa of great blazing energy then struck that peak with seven arrows tipped with keen points, and shattered it fell to the ground.

**6.47.72**  Seeing that mountain-peak shattered, the commander of the monkey host — that slayer of enemy heroes — blazed up with fury like the fire of universal dissolution.

**6.47.73**  Then Nīla hurled in battle aśvakarṇa trees, dhava trees, śāla trees, and well-blossoming mango trees, as well as many other diverse trees.

**6.47.74**  Rāvaṇa met those trees and cut them apart, then pelted the son of Agni with a most terrible shower of arrows.

**6.47.75**  Pelted by that torrent of arrows as a great mountain is by a storm-cloud, he contracted his form to a tiny size and alighted on the tip of Rāvaṇa's flagstaff.

**6.47.76**  Seeing the son of Agni perched upon the top of his flagstaff, Rāvaṇa blazed with anger — and then Nīla let out a roar.

**6.47.77**  Seeing that monkey leap from flagstaff-tip to bow-tip to crown-tip, Lakṣmaṇa, Hanūmān, and Rāma were all filled with wonder.

**6.47.78**  Rāvaṇa of great blazing energy, astonished at the monkey's agility, then invoked the flaming and wondrous Āgneya weapon.

**6.47.79**  Then the monkeys, having achieved their aim, shouted with joy, seeing Rāvaṇa thrown into confusion in battle by Nīla's nimbleness.

**6.47.80**  Enraged by the clamour of the monkeys, Rāvaṇa at that moment was seized with confusion of heart and could not recover his composure.

**6.47.81**  Then the night-ranger Rāvaṇa took up an arrow charged with the Āgneya weapon and looked up at Nīla, who stood upon the top of the flagstaff.

**6.47.82**  Then Rāvaṇa, the great-blazing lord of rākṣasas, spoke: "You are possessed of agility, O monkey, by some supreme magical power.

**6.47.83**  Protect your life if you can, O monkey — you multiply those forms of yours time and again.

**6.47.84**  Even so, the arrow discharged by me and empowered by this weapon shall dislodge you from your life, however much you strive to preserve it."

**6.47.85**  Having spoken thus, mighty-armed Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas, fitted the arrow with the weapon and struck the commander of the host.

**6.47.86**  Nīla, struck in the chest by that arrow empowered with the weapon, burned as it were and suddenly fell to the ground.

**6.47.87**  Through the greatness of his father's glory and his own inherent energy, he fell only to his knees on the earth and was not parted from his life.

**6.47.88**  Seeing the monkey lying senseless, ten-necked Rāvaṇa, eager for battle, charged toward Saumitri in his chariot that roared like a thundercloud.

**6.47.89**  Saumitri, of undaunted spirit, spoke to him as he drew his immeasurable bow: "Come against me alone, O lord of the night-rangers — you should not fight against the monkeys."

**6.47.90**  Hearing his words, resonant and full-toned, and the fierce twang of his bowstring, the king approached Saumitri where he stood, and the rākṣasa spoke in anger.

**6.47.91**  "Fortunate for me that you have come within my sight, O Rāghava — you who are perverse of judgment and rushing toward your end. In this very instant you shall journey to the realm of death, battered by my net of arrows."

**6.47.92**  Saumitri, unperturbed, spoke to him as he roared with upturned white fangs: "The truly mighty do not bellow, O king. You boast too much, O foremost among evil-doers."

**6.47.93**  "I know your valour, O lord of rākṣasas — your strength, your power, and your prowess. I stand here, bow and arrows in hand. Come! What purpose is served by empty boasting?"

**6.47.94**  Provoked by these words, the angry lord of rākṣasas discharged seven well-feathered arrows; Lakṣmaṇa cut them apart with sharp-edged arrows adorned with golden plumage.

**6.47.95**  Seeing them suddenly cut down like great serpents with severed coils, the lord of Laṅkā fell under the sway of fury and launched other sharp-pointed arrows.

**6.47.96**  Rāma's younger brother unleashed a fierce rain of arrows from his bow, cutting apart those shafts with razor-headed, crescent-headed, karna, and broad-headed arrows, and remained undisturbed.

**6.47.97**  And Lakṣmaṇa swiftly fitted to his bow sharp-tipped arrows blazing like fire, equal in speed to the thunderbolt of great Indra, and discharged them to slay the lord of rākṣasas.

**6.47.98**  The lord of rākṣasas cut those arrows apart, and having cut them, struck Lakṣmaṇa in the forehead with an arrow blazing like the fire of final destruction — an arrow given by Svayambhū himself.

**6.47.99**  Lakṣmaṇa, wounded by Rāvaṇa's arrow, reeled and grasped his bow feebly; then recovering his senses with difficulty, he cut apart the bow of the enemy of the lord of the thirty gods.

**6.47.100**  Then the son of Daśaratha struck him — now bereft of his bow — with three sharp-pointed arrows. The king, tortured by those shafts, staggered and only with difficulty regained his senses.

**6.47.101**  His bow severed and his body struck by arrows, his limbs damp with sweat and drenched in blood, the enemy of the gods — of supreme power — seized in battle the śakti given by Svayambhū.

**6.47.102**  The lord of the rākṣasa realm swiftly hurled that blazing śakti — like smokeless fire and terrifying to the monkey host — at Saumitri.

**6.47.103**  The younger brother of Bharata struck that oncoming weapon with missile-charged arrows equal to consecrated fire; yet even so that śakti entered the broad chest of the son of Daśaratha.

**6.47.104**  Struck in the chest by that bewildering śakti, Saumitri contemplated within himself his own inconceivable nature as a portion of Viṣṇu.

**6.47.105**  Then the thorn of the gods tried to seize Saumitri — destroyer of the pride of dānavas — in his arms, but found himself powerless to lift him.

**6.47.106**  Himavān, Mandara, Meru, or even the three worlds together with the immortals might be lifted by his arms in battle — but not the younger brother of Bharata.

**6.47.107**  Then, seeing Lakṣmaṇa — that portion of Viṣṇu dwelling in a human body — lying senseless, Rāvaṇa was filled with astonishment.

**6.47.108**  Then the son of Vāyu, filled with fury, charged at Rāvaṇa and struck him in the chest with a clenched fist like a thunderbolt.

**6.47.109**  By that blow of the fist, Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas, fell to the ground on his knees — he tottered and he fell.

**6.47.110**  Seeing Rāvaṇa of terrible prowess lying senseless in battle, the sages, the monkeys, and the gods together with Vāsava all shouted with joy.

**6.47.111**  And Hanūmān, full of energy, took up Lakṣmaṇa — who had been tormented by Rāvaṇa — in his arms and carried him to Rāghava's side.

**6.47.112**  By the friendship of the son of Vāyu and his supreme devotion, he who was utterly unshakeable by enemies became light — light as a monkey.

**6.47.113**  Having released Saumitri, that invincible one in battle, the śakti returned again to its place on Rāvaṇa's chariot.

**6.47.114**  Rāvaṇa of great blazing energy, having regained his senses in the great battle, took up sharp arrows and grasped his great bow.

**6.47.115**  And Lakṣmaṇa the slayer of enemies, comforted and freed from the arrow's pain, contemplated within himself his own nature as the unquestionable portion of Viṣṇu.

**6.47.116**  But Rāghava, seeing the great host of monkeys with their mighty heroes struck down in battle, charged toward Rāvaṇa.

**6.47.117**  Then Hanūmān approached him and spoke: "Mount upon my back and punish that rākṣasa."

**6.47.118**  Hearing those words spoken by the son of Vāyu, the heroic Rāghava swiftly mounted great Hanūmān, and the lord of men beheld Rāvaṇa standing in his chariot on the battlefield.

**6.47.119**  Beholding him, Rāghava of great blazing energy charged forward, as Viṣṇu in fury charged at Vairocana with weapon upraised.

**6.47.120**  Rāma made a fierce twang of his bowstring like the crash of a thunderbolt, and spoke to the lord of rākṣasas with a deep and resonant voice.

**6.47.121**  "Stand! Stand! You have done me such grievous wrong — where, O tiger among rākṣasas, can you go to find escape?"

**6.47.122**  "Even if you flee to Indra, Vaivasvata, or Bhāskara, to Svayambhū, Vaiśvānara, or Śaṃkara, or to the ten directions — even so, having gone from me today, you shall not be released."

**6.47.123**  "And he whom you struck today with the śakti and who has suddenly fallen into affliction — he, O king of the rākṣasa host, is your death in today's battle, the death of you and your sons and wives."

**6.47.124**  Hearing Rāghava's words, the lord of rākṣasas struck great Hanūmān with sharp arrows resembling the flames of the fire of final dissolution.

**6.47.125**  Even as he was struck by those arrows in the rākṣasa battle, his energy — joined to his innate blazing power — only grew greater still.

**6.47.126**  Then Rāma of great blazing energy, seeing that tiger among monkeys wounded by Rāvaṇa, came under the sway of wrath.

**6.47.127**  Advancing against his chariot, Rāma with well-feathered arrows cut apart its wheels, horses, flagstaff, parasol, great banner, charioteer, and thunderbolt, spear, and sword.

**6.47.128**  Then he swiftly struck the enemy of Indra with an arrow like a thunderbolt and lightning-flash in the broad well-formed expanse of his chest — as divine Indra strikes Meru with his thunderbolt.

**6.47.129**  He who had never trembled or stirred at the fall of thunderbolt and lightning — that king, that hero, when struck by Rāma's arrow, reeled greatly in pain and dropped his bow.

**6.47.130**  Seeing him reeling, great-souled Rāma took up a blazing crescent-headed arrow and with it suddenly cut away the crown of the lord of rākṣasas — brilliant as the sun.

**6.47.131**  Seeing him like a venomous serpent whose venom is spent, like the sun with dimmed rays and extinguished glory, his crown-peak severed, Rāma spoke to the lord of rākṣasas in battle.

**6.47.132**  "You have done a great and most terrible deed, and you have laid low my foremost heroes. Therefore, judging you to be exhausted, I shall not consign you to the power of death by my arrows today."

**6.47.133**  Thus addressed, his pride and joy shattered, his bow severed, his horses and charioteer slain, tormented by arrows, his great crown cut away, the king swiftly retreated into Laṅkā.

**6.47.134**  As that lord of the night-rangers — mighty, the enemy of dānavas and gods — entered the city, Rāma, together with Lakṣmaṇa, at the forefront of the supreme battle, made the monkeys free from wounds.

**6.47.135**  As that enemy of the lord of the thirty gods was put to flight, the gods and asuras, the host of beings, the directions with their oceans, the great serpents together with the sages, and likewise the creatures of earth and water — all were filled with joy.

**6.48.1**  Having entered the city of Laṅkā, tormented by fear of Rāma's arrows, the king was then of broken pride, his senses distraught.

**6.48.2**  As an elephant overwhelmed by a lion, as a serpent overwhelmed by Garuḍa, so the king was overpowered by Rāghava, that great-souled one.

**6.48.3**  Recalling Rāghava's arrows — blazing like the rod of Brahmā, brilliant as lightning — the lord of the rākṣasas trembled with anguish.

**6.48.4**  Resting on his divine golden throne of the highest order, casting his gaze about at the rākṣasas, Rāvaṇa spoke these words.

**6.48.5**  All that supreme austerity I performed has surely been rendered fruitless, since I, equal to great Indra, have been defeated by a mere human.

**6.48.6**  That dreadful word of Brahmā has now come upon me: "Know that danger to you will come from humans" — and so it has come to pass.

**6.48.7**  Invulnerability from gods, dānavas, gandharvas, yakṣas, rākṣasas, and serpents was obtained by me, but I did not beg it from humans.

**6.48.8**  Recognising this, you must make effort here; and let the rākṣasas stand guard atop the patrol towers and city gates.

**6.48.9**  And that one of incomparable depth, crusher of the pride of gods and dānavas, yet now overcome by Brahmā's curse — let Kumbhakarṇa be awakened.

**6.48.10**  Knowing himself defeated and Prahasta slain, the mighty one commanded the terrible army of rākṣasas.

**6.48.11**  "Let vigil be kept at the gates, let the ramparts be mounted, and let Kumbhakarṇa, seized by the power of sleep, be awakened."

**6.48.12**  "That rākṣasa sleeps for nine, six, seven, or eight months at a stretch — quickly rouse that mighty Kumbhakarṇa now."

**6.48.13**  "For he, the long-armed pinnacle of all rākṣasas, will swiftly slay the monkeys and those two princes in battle."

**6.48.14**  "Kumbhakarṇa always sleeps, that dull one addicted to base pleasures — but in this most dreadful battle against one driven back by Rāma —

**6.48.15**  "there will be no grief for me once Kumbhakarṇa is awakened. What indeed could I not accomplish with one whose strength equals Indra's?"

**6.48.16**  "One who does not rise to aid when such a calamity has arrived—" Having heard that command of the lord of rākṣasas, those rākṣasas

**6.48.17**  departed in great haste to the dwelling of Kumbhakarṇa — those appointed by Rāvaṇa who fed on flesh and blood.

**6.48.18**  Seizing perfumes, garlands, and food, they quickly went and entered through that great gate extending a full yojana in every direction.

**6.48.19**  Moving onward toward that beautiful cave of Kumbhakarṇa, fragrant with all manner of scents, they entered the cave with great effort and difficulty.

**6.48.20**  Entering that beautiful and auspicious cave with its golden floor, they beheld that tiger among rākṣasas lying there, terrible to behold.

**6.48.21**  Together they attempted to wake that grotesquely sleeping Kumbhakarṇa, who lay like a scattered mountain, sunk in deep slumber.

**6.48.22**  His body with hair erect, breathing like a great serpent, terrifying all with his mighty exhalations — he lay there, dreadful to look upon.

**6.48.23**  They beheld that tiger among rākṣasas, the mighty Kumbhakarṇa — with his fearsome nostrils and his mouth as vast as the netherworld.

**6.48.24**  Those great ones then piled before Kumbhakarṇa a heap of flesh resembling Mount Meru — the most gratifying of offerings.

**6.48.25**  Those tigers among rākṣasas assembled heaps of deer, buffaloes, and boars — a wondrous pile placed before him.

**6.48.26**  Then those enemies of the gods placed before Kumbhakarṇa jars of blood and various kinds of wine.

**6.48.27**  They anointed that scorcher of foes with the finest sandalwood paste and adorned him with divine garlands and sweet-smelling fragrances.

**6.48.28**  They wafted fragrant incense about and praised that scorcher of foes; the yātudhānas roared in their thousands like rain-clouds.

**6.48.29**  They filled conch shells bright as the moon and simultaneously raised a tumultuous clamor, and in their fury they roared all at once.

**6.48.30**  Those night-roamers shouted, clapped their arms, hurled things about, and made a vast din for the purpose of waking Kumbhakarṇa.

**6.48.31**  The din of conches, war-drums, and kettledrums, along with clapping, battle-cries, and lion-roars filling all directions and piercing the heavens — hearing it, birds fell suddenly to the ground.

**6.48.32**  When even with those great noises the mighty Kumbhakarṇa, deep in slumber, did not awake, then all those companies of rākṣasas seized clubs, pestles, and maces.

**6.48.33**  Those towering rākṣasas then struck Kumbhakarṇa, lying at ease in deep slumber on the ground, with mountain peaks, pestles, maces, trees, palm-blows, sledge-hammers, and fists.

**6.48.34**  Even the mighty rākṣasas could not stand before the blast of the breath of that rākṣasa Kumbhakarṇa's exhalations.

**6.48.35**  Then those rākṣasas of terrible prowess beat hard before him mṛdaṅgas, paṇavas, war-drums, conches, and kettledrums — ten thousand rākṣasas all sounding them simultaneously.

**6.48.36**  They tried to rouse that one who resembled a heap of dark collyrium, striking him and shouting — but he was utterly unaware.

**6.48.37**  When they were unable to wake him, they then undertook a more severe and terrible effort.

**6.48.38**  They beat horses, camels, donkeys, and elephants with sticks, whips, and goads, and sounded the war-drums, conches, and mṛdaṅgas with all their might.

**6.48.39**  They struck his limbs with great timbers, logs, sledge-hammers, and pestles, exerting their full strength.

**6.48.40**  With that tremendous noise all of Laṅkā was filled, along with its mountains and forests — yet he still did not awaken.

**6.48.41**  Then a thousand war-drums fitted with polished golden strikers were beaten simultaneously, all around him without pause.

**6.48.42**  Even so, when that one of extreme slumber, fallen under the power of the curse, did not wake, the night-roamers grew angry.

**6.48.43**  Possessed by great rage, all of them of terrible valor, others again made fresh exertion to rouse that rākṣasa.

**6.48.44**  Some beat war-drums, others raised a mighty clamor, some pulled at his hair, others bit at his ears — yet Kumbhakarṇa, overcome by deep sleep, did not stir.

**6.48.45**  Other strong ones, holding iron sledge-hammers, rained down blows upon his head, chest, and limbs.

**6.48.46**  Though struck all around with śataghnis bound with rope-ties, that great-bodied rākṣasa did not wake.

**6.48.47**  Then a thousand elephants were driven to run across his body — and only then did Kumbhakarṇa stir, becoming faintly conscious of the touch.

**6.48.48**  With mountain peaks and trees being hurled upon him, heeding not those great blows, his sleep at last exhausted, tormented by hunger, he stretched himself yawning and suddenly sprang up.

**6.48.49**  That night-roamer flung wide his two arms — each like the body of a great serpent or the summit of a mountain — and opening his mouth, which resembled the submarine fire, yawned in a monstrous fashion.

**6.48.50**  As he yawned, his mouth resembling the netherworld was seen like the sun risen above the peak of Mount Meru.

**6.48.51**  That night-roamer of tremendous strength, now awake and stretching, breathed out a breath that arose like wind from a mountain.

**6.48.52**  The form of Kumbhakarṇa as he rose up shone like a rain-laden cloud, crowded with lightning, pouring down rain at the end of summer.

**6.48.53**  His two great eyes, blazing like fierce fire, brilliant as lightning, were seen like two great planets burning bright.

**6.48.54**  Hungry, he devoured the flesh; thirsty, he drank blood; and that enemy of Indra then drank from jars of fat and wine.

**6.48.55**  Then, knowing him satiated, the night-roamers arose and, bowing their heads in reverence, surrounded him on all sides.

**6.48.56**  That bull among rākṣasas consoled all the rākṣasas, and being also astonished at being awakened, spoke these words to the rākṣasas.

**6.48.57**  "For what reason have you beaten and awakened me? Is the king in good health? Is there no danger anywhere here?"

**6.48.58**  "Or has some great danger surely arisen from others — for which reason you have awakened me in such haste?"

**6.48.59**  "This very day I shall root out the danger of the lord of rākṣasas — I shall strike down great Indra or I shall extinguish Agni himself."

**6.48.60**  "For no trifling reason would you wake me so forcefully in my sleep. Therefore, tell me truly the cause for my awakening."

**6.48.61**  To Kumbhakarṇa, that subduer of enemies, speaking thus in agitation, Yūpākṣa, minister of the king, spoke with joined palms.

**6.48.62**  "No danger to us has ever come from the gods, nor is there any such danger from daityas or dānavas — as the human danger, O king, that has now come upon us."

**6.48.63**  "This Laṅkā is surrounded by mountain-like monkeys; a tumultuous danger to us comes from Rāma, who is burning with grief over the abduction of Sītā."

**6.48.64**  "This great city was previously burned by a single monkey; Prince Akṣa was slain along with his retinue and his elephant."

**6.48.65**  "And the lord of rākṣasas himself, descendant of Pulastya, thorn in the side of the gods, was left for dead in battle when Rāma, blazing like the sun, released him."

**6.48.66**  "He whom neither the gods, daityas, nor dānavas could reduce to such a state — he was here released by Rāma from peril to his very life."

**6.48.67**  Having heard Yūpākṣa's words about his brother's defeat in battle, Kumbhakarṇa, with rolling eyes, spoke thus to Yūpākṣa.

**6.48.68**  "This very day, O Yūpākṣa, I shall slay the monkey army along with Lakṣmaṇa and Rāghava in battle — and after that I shall go to see Rāvaṇa."

**6.48.69**  "I shall satisfy the rākṣasas with the flesh and blood of the monkeys, and I myself shall drink the blood of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa."

**6.48.70**  Hearing those proud words of his, whose fault was the swelling of wrath, Mahodara, foremost warrior among the rākṣasas, spoke these words with joined palms.

**6.48.71**  "Having heard Rāvaṇa's words and deliberated on merits and faults, you may thereafter conquer the enemies in battle, O long-armed one."

**6.48.72**  Having heard Mahodara's words, Kumbhakarṇa of great brilliance, the mighty one, surrounded by rākṣasas, set out.

**6.48.73**  Having roused him of terrible eyes and terrifying form and valor, the rākṣasas hastened to the dwelling of the ten-necked Rāvaṇa.

**6.48.74**  Then, going to the ten-necked one seated on his supreme throne, all the night-roamers with folded palms spoke.

**6.48.75**  "Kumbhakarṇa your brother is awakened, O bull among rākṣasas. Please let him come forth from there — or you will see him arrived here."

**6.48.76**  Rāvaṇa then said, delighted: "I wish to see him here, duly honored — let him be received with fitting honor."

**6.48.77**  Saying "So be it," all those rākṣasas returned and, urged by Rāvaṇa, spoke these words to Kumbhakarṇa.

**6.48.78**  "The king, foremost of all rākṣasas, desires to see you — resolve to go and gladden your brother."

**6.48.79**  Kumbhakarṇa, that irresistible one, accepting his brother's command, said "So be it," and that mighty one sprang up from his bed.

**6.48.80**  Washing his face, joyful, having bathed and adorned himself with the finest ornaments, thirsty, he urgently called for drink that would restore his strength.

**6.48.81**  Then those rākṣasas, acting swiftly and at Rāvaṇa's command, quickly brought him wine and all manner of food.

**6.48.82**  Having drunk a thousand jars, he made ready to depart.

**6.48.83**  Slightly intoxicated, exhilarated, endowed with blazing energy and strength, Kumbhakarṇa shone joyfully, resembling Yama at the end of a cosmic age.

**6.48.84**  As Kumbhakarṇa went to his brother's palace, accompanied by the rākṣasa forces, his footfalls made the earth tremble.

**6.48.85**  Illuminating the royal road with his form as the sun with a thousand rays illumines the earth, he went there surrounded by garlands of joined-palm salutations — as Indra goes to the house of Svayambhū.

**6.48.86**  Some, distressed, sought refuge in Rāma the refuge; others fell overwhelmed; others, overcome, fled in all directions; others, stricken with fear, lay prostrate on the ground.

**6.48.87**  The forest-dwellers, seeing that one resembling a mountain peak, crowned and touching the sun as it were with his own blazing splendor, that wondrous giant, fled this way and that, tormented by fear.

**6.49.1**  Then mighty Rāma, full of valour, took up his bow and beheld Kumbhakarṇa, the crown-wearing, immense-bodied one.

**6.49.2**  Seeing that foremost of rākṣasas, who in appearance resembled a mountain, striding as if across the sky — like lord Nārāyaṇa of old —

**6.49.3**  — like a rain-laden cloud, adorned with golden armlets — the great army of monkeys, beholding him, fled again in all directions.

**6.49.4**  Seeing the army in flight and the rākṣasa looming ever larger, Rāma spoke in wonder to Vibhīṣaṇa.

**6.49.5**  "Who is that hero visible in Laṅkā, resembling a mountain, wearing a crown, with tawny eyes, like a storm-cloud charged with lightning?"

**6.49.6**  "That great one, sole banner of the earth, is seen here — at whose very sight all the monkeys scatter hither and thither."

**6.49.7**  "Tell me — who is this great being, rākṣasa or asura? Never before have I seen a creature of this kind."

**6.49.8**  Thus questioned by the royal prince Rāma, the tireless doer, the supremely wise Vibhīṣaṇa spoke to the descendant of Kākutstha.

**6.49.9**  "He by whom Vaivasvata Yama and Vāsava Indra were defeated in battle — he is that son of Viśravas, the mighty Kumbhakarṇa."

**6.49.10**  "By him, O Rāghava, gods, dānavas, yakṣas, serpents, flesh-eaters, gandharvas, vidyādharas, and kiṃnaras — by the thousands — have been utterly routed in battle."

**6.49.11**  "The thirty gods could not slay the great-armed Kumbhakarṇa, trident in hand, of misshapen eyes — deluded, they thought: 'This is but Time itself.'"

**6.49.12**  "For Kumbhakarṇa is by nature radiant and mighty beyond measure; the power of other rākṣasa lords comes only from boons granted."

**6.49.13**  "By this great soul, even as a newborn tormented by hunger, thousands upon thousands of living creatures were devoured."

**6.49.14**  "As creatures were being consumed, the people, oppressed by fear, took refuge with Śakra and laid their grievance before him too."

**6.49.15**  "Enraged, great Indra the thunderbolt-wielder struck Kumbhakarṇa with the sharp vajra; struck by Śakra's thunderbolt, that great soul reeled and roared mightily in fury."

**6.49.16**  "Hearing the roar of the wise Kumbhakarṇa as he bellowed, the earth was terrified and trembled anew."

**6.49.17**  "Then, in anger at great Indra, the mighty Kumbhakarṇa wrenched a tusk from Airāvata and struck Vāsava upon the chest."

**6.49.18**  "Wounded by Kumbhakarṇa's blow, Vāsava staggered; thereupon gods, brahmarṣis, and dānavas all fell into sudden despair."

**6.49.19**  "And Śakra, together with the people, went to the abode of the Self-born; they reported to Prajāpati Kumbhakarṇa's wickedness, his devouring of creatures, and his assaulting of the gods."

**6.49.20**  "If he continues to devour creatures thus without cease, in no long time the world will be empty."

**6.49.21**  "Hearing Vāsava's words, the Grandfather of all worlds summoned the rākṣasas and beheld Kumbhakarṇa."

**6.49.22**  "On beholding Kumbhakarṇa, Prajāpati was seized with fear; having looked and sighed, the Self-born spoke these words."

**6.49.23**  "'Surely you were fashioned by the Progenitor for the destruction of worlds; therefore from this day forth you shall sleep as one dead.' Overcome by Brahmā's curse, he fell prostrate before the lord."

**6.49.24**  "Then greatly agitated, Rāvaṇa spoke: 'A golden tree grown to fullness is cut down at the very time of its fruit!'"

**6.49.25**  "'It is not right, O Prajāpati, to curse one's own grandson thus — and your word is never false; he shall sleep, there is no doubt; but let a term be set for his sleeping and his waking.'"

**6.49.26**  "Hearing Rāvaṇa's words, the Self-born spoke: 'He shall sleep for six months at a stretch and wake for a single day.'"

**6.49.27**  "Within that one day that hero, roaming the earth in hunger, with gaping mouth shall devour the worlds, like fire blazing in fury."

**6.49.28**  "That king Rāvaṇa, having fallen into calamity, and now afraid of your might, has wakened Kumbhakarṇa."

**6.49.29**  "He, the hero of terrifying valour, has now emerged from the camp and runs about devouring the monkeys in great fury."

**6.49.30**  "Simply at the sight of Kumbhakarṇa, the monkeys have fled in terror — how shall the vānaras restrain him when he is enraged in battle?"

**6.49.31**  "Let all the monkeys be told: 'This is a machine set up' — knowing this, the monkeys will be fearless."

**6.49.32**  Hearing the reasoned words issuing clearly from Vibhīṣaṇa's mouth, Rāghava then spoke to Nīla the army commander.

**6.49.33**  "Go, array all the troops and stand firm in battle formation; seize the gates of Laṅkā, and likewise her highways and bridges."

**6.49.34**  "Gathering mountain peaks, trees, and boulders, let all the monkeys stand ready, armed, rocks in hand."

**6.49.35**  Instructed by Rāghava, Nīla, commander of the monkey host, that elephant among monkeys, duly marshalled the vānara army.

**6.49.36**  Then Gavākṣa, Śarabha, Hanūmān, Aṅgada, and Nala — mountain-like in form — grasping mountain peaks, advanced upon the gate.

**6.49.37**  Then that fierce array of monkeys, trees and upraised peaks in their hands, drawn up near the mountain, shone resplendent — like a vast, threatening mass of storm-clouds.

**6.50.1**  That tiger among rākṣasas, still stupefied with the intoxication of sleep, strode with vast steps along the royal road, adorned in splendour.

**6.50.2**  Surrounded by thousands of rākṣasas, utterly invincible, with a rain of flowers poured upon him from the houses, he went on his way.

**6.50.3**  He beheld the vast and beautiful dwelling of the rākṣasa lord, spread over with nets of gold, blazing like the sun.

**6.50.4**  Entering that abode of the rākṣasa lord like the sun passing through a canopy of clouds, he saw his elder brother seated in the distance, like the Self-born enthroned, like Śakra upon his throne.

**6.50.5**  Approaching his brother's house and penetrating to the inner court, he saw his overlord seated in anxious grief upon the Puṣpaka aerial palace.

**6.50.6**  Then seeing Kumbhakarṇa arrived before him, the ten-necked one rose quickly, delighted, and drew him near.

**6.50.7**  Then mighty Kumbhakarṇa, as his brother sat upon the couch, bowed at his feet and asked: "What is to be done?" Springing up, Rāvaṇa joyfully embraced him.

**6.50.8**  Embraced by his brother and duly honoured, Kumbhakarṇa took his seat upon a fair and splendid throne.

**6.50.9**  Seated there, the mighty Kumbhakarṇa, his eyes flushed red with anger, addressed Rāvaṇa with these words.

**6.50.10**  "For what purpose have I, O king, been respectfully roused by you? Tell me — from what do you fear? Who is to become a ghost today?"

**6.50.11**  Rāvaṇa spoke to his brother Kumbhakarṇa, who sat there in anger, with eyes slightly averted.

**6.50.12**  "A very great time has passed while you slept, O mighty one; complacent, you have not known the fear that Rāma has brought upon me."

**6.50.13**  "This Rāma, son of Daśaratha, mighty with Sugrīva at his side, has crossed the ocean with his army and is cutting at our very root."

**6.50.14**  "Look — see how the groves and gardens of Laṅkā are now an ocean of monkeys, arrived with ease across the bridge."

**6.50.15**  "The foremost of the rākṣasas have been slain by the monkeys in battle; a diminishing of the monkeys in combat I see never at all."

**6.50.16**  "With the treasury entirely exhausted, you have come to me — protect this city of Laṅkā, left with only the young and the old."

**6.50.17**  "For your brother's sake, O mighty-armed one, perform this most difficult deed — I have never before spoken thus to anyone, O brother, scorcher of foes; in you I place my love and my highest hope."

**6.50.18**  "In the many fierce battles between gods and asuras, O bull among rākṣasas, you have stood against the gods and conquered asuras in battle — among all beings no equal to your might is seen."

**6.50.19**  "Do for me this highest deed of love and benefit, O dear one who loves dear battle-comrades — scatter the enemy host by your own brilliance, like a great wind rising to dispel the autumn clouds."

**6.51.1**  Hearing the lamentation of the rākṣasa king, Kumbhakarṇa then spoke, and laughed.

**6.51.2**  "The fault we foresaw long ago in the counsel chamber — you who were not intent on what is beneficial have now encountered that very consequence."

**6.51.3**  "Indeed the fruit of evil action has swiftly come upon you — like the fall into hell of one who has done wicked deeds."

**6.51.4**  "In the first place, O great king, this deed was undertaken without thought; relying solely on pride in your prowess, you gave no consideration to its consequences."

**6.51.5**  "He who, placed in sovereignty, does first what should be done later and does later what should be done first — such a one knows nothing of sound policy and its contrary."

**6.51.6**  "Actions performed in the wrong place and time, done in a contrary manner, come to ruin — like oblations offered by one who has not purified himself."

**6.51.7**  "He who clearly sees the fivefold application of the three types of action and, having taken counsel with his ministers, follows the right course — such a one walks the proper path of governance."

**6.51.8**  "A king who seeks to devise policy in accordance with scripture, who understands his ministers with discernment, and who keeps watch over his allies —"

**6.51.9**  "— that man, O lord of rākṣasas, pursues all three — dharma, artha, and kāma — in their season, or again in their three paired combinations."

**6.51.10**  "A king or one of royal standing who, after hearing what is foremost among these three, does not comprehend it — his wide learning is in vain."

**6.51.11**  "Conciliation by gifts, conciliation by gentle speech, sowing dissension, and bold action at the right moment — these strategies, O foremost of rākṣasas, and both sound and unsound policy —"

**6.51.12**  "— he who, self-possessed, takes counsel with his ministers in due season regarding dharma, artha, and kāma, and then acts upon them, encounters no calamity in this world."

**6.51.13**  "A king lives well who, with ministers versed in the true meaning of policy, examines what is to be done and what is not, in view of the chain of consequences beneficial to himself."

**6.51.14**  "Men of animal understanding, ignorant of the import of the śāstras, yet presume to speak in councils to which they have been admitted — solely out of arrogance."

**6.51.15**  "The words of those who do not know the śāstras, who are ignorant of the science of polity and yet covet great prosperity, should not be made welcome."

**6.51.16**  "Those who, through impudence, prattle that the harmful is beneficial must be watched and excluded from counsel as destroyers of enterprise."

**6.51.17**  "Ministers who, in league with clever enemies, cause the destruction of their master by setting contrary courses of action in motion here —"

**6.51.18**  "— a master should recognise those ministers, who resemble friends but are enemies in counsel, through their conduct, even as they are brought close."

**6.51.19**  "The affairs of a fickle man who rushes headlong into things are exploited by others, as birds enter the gap in the Krauñca mountain."

**6.51.20**  "He who, while disregarding the enemy, does not protect himself, truly incurs disaster and is dislodged from his position."

**6.51.21**  Hearing Kumbhakarṇa's speech, the ten-necked one drew his brows together and, enraged, spoke to him thus.

**6.51.22**  "Like a venerable teacher and preceptor — why do you lecture me? Why this laborious rhetoric? Let what is proper be done now, at this moment."

**6.51.23**  "Whether it was done through confusion, delusion of mind, or reliance on strength and valour — what is now done cannot be undone; do not pointlessly repeat it."

**6.51.24**  "Let what is now fitting at this juncture be enacted; remedy by your valour the fault arising from my ill-counsel."

**6.51.25**  "If indeed you have affection for me, if you recognise the bond of brotherhood, or if this matter is regarded by you at heart as most urgent —"

**6.51.26**  "— he is a friend who comes to the aid of one whose affairs have gone to ruin and who is brought low; he is a kinsman who lends his help when things have gone wrong."

**6.51.27**  Then perceiving that Rāvaṇa was offended by these words — firm yet harsh — Kumbhakarṇa spoke softly and soothingly.

**6.51.28**  Observing too keenly that his brother's senses were agitated, Kumbhakarṇa spoke gently, consoling him.

**6.51.29**  "Enough — O lord of rākṣasa kings, do not give yourself over to anguish; casting aside your rage, you ought to be at peace."

**6.51.30**  "While I yet live, O king, this matter ought not to weigh upon your mind; I shall destroy him on whose account you grieve."

**6.51.31**  "Surely I must in every situation speak what is beneficial to you — what I said was spoken from the bond of kinship and from brotherly love, O king."

**6.51.32**  "What now befits a devoted kinsman at this moment — behold it being done by me: the destruction of enemies in battle."

**6.51.33**  "This very day, O mighty-armed one, behold — with Rāma and his brother slain by me at the front of battle — the monkey host in flight."

**6.51.34**  "Today, seeing Rāma's head brought from the battlefield by me, be glad, O mighty-armed one; let Sītā be made to grieve."

**6.51.35**  "Today let all the rākṣasas in Laṅkā — those whose kinsmen have been slain — behold the greatly desired death of Rāma."

**6.51.36**  "Today, by the destruction of the enemy in battle, I shall wipe away the tears of those overcome with grief at the slaying of their own kinsmen."

**6.51.37**  "Today behold Sugrīva, lord of the leaping monkeys, mountain-like, like a cloud bearing the sun, shattered in battle."

**6.51.38**  "No other need be sent by you to battle, O one of incomparable prowess — I myself, O mighty one, shall annihilate your enemies."

**6.51.39**  "Whether it be Śakra, or Yama, or fire and wind, or Kubera and Varuṇa — I shall fight all of them."

**6.51.40**  "Even Puraṃdara Indra should fear me, whose body is the size of a mountain, who wields a sharp trident, who roars, whose fangs are keen."

**6.51.41**  "Or else, abandoning weapons, treading down the enemies with swift force — no one who wishes to live is capable of standing before me in battle."

**6.51.42**  "Neither with spear, nor with mace, nor with sword, nor with keen arrows — but with my two bare hands, in fury, I shall slay even the thunderbolt-wielder."

**6.51.43**  "If that Rāghava today withstands the force of my fist, then let the torrents of arrows drink Rāghava's blood."

**6.51.44**  "Why are you tormented by anxiety, O king, when I am at hand? I am ready to go forth for the destruction of your enemy."

**6.51.45**  "Release your fear of Rāma, O king; in combat I shall slay Rāghava, Lakṣmaṇa, and the mighty Sugrīva — I wish to bestow upon you extraordinary and great glory."

**6.51.46**  "To bring you pleasure-bringing joy through the slaying of the son of Daśaratha, I go forth; having slain Rāma along with Lakṣmaṇa, I shall devour all the chiefs of the monkey host."

**6.51.47**  "Revel at will, drink choice wine, attend to your affairs, let your fever be allayed; when I have this very day dispatched Rāma to Yama's realm, Sītā will at last be yours to command."

**6.52.1**  Having heard those words spoken by mighty Atikāya, the powerful, long-armed Kumbhakarṇa, Mahodara then replied.

**6.52.2**  "Born in the clan of Kumbhakarṇa, you are insolent and of common understanding; filled with conceit, you are unable to discern what is proper in every matter.

**6.52.3**  The king surely knows what is sound policy and what is not, O Kumbhakarṇa; but you, insolent since boyhood, desire only to bluster.

**6.52.4**  One who understands the division of place and time discerns what is stable, what is growth, and what is decline — both for himself and for others, O bull among rākṣasas.

**6.52.5**  What a mighty one of ordinary intellect, who has not attended upon the wise elders, might do — what wise man would do likewise?

**6.52.6**  As for the three — dharma, artha, and kāma — which you expound as separate pursuits to be understood: by your very nature you show no sign of comprehending them.

**6.52.7**  For action is the purpose of all causes; and here the fruit of actions falls out as either good or evil.

**6.52.8**  Dharma and artha both yield the fruit of the highest good; the opposite pair — adharma and anartha — bear only harmful fruit.

**6.52.9**  Men perform action for this world and the next; and by fixing upon the ordained actions, one also attains kāma.

**6.52.10**  In that regard, what the king has determined in his heart is deemed proper by us; for if there is rashness toward the enemy, what harm could be removed from it?

**6.52.11**  As for the reasoning you have advanced for going alone against the enemy — even in that I will tell you what is improper and what is not sound.

**6.52.12**  He by whom many exceedingly mighty rākṣasas were slain formerly at Janasthāna — how will you alone conquer that Rāghava?

**6.52.13**  Those great-spirited rākṣasas who were defeated by him at Janasthāna — even today you see them all in the city, still in fear.

**6.52.14**  That Rāma, son of Daśaratha, who is like an enraged lion — you wish, like one who unknowingly stirs a sleeping serpent, to rouse him.

**6.52.15**  Who is fit to approach him who always blazes with splendour and is unapproachable in wrath — approaching him is like approaching unbearable Death?

**6.52.16**  All of this is fraught with doubt in confronting that enemy; your going there alone does not at all please me.

**6.52.17**  Who, like a common man of diminished resources, would seek to bring under control a powerful enemy clearly at the cost of his own life?

**6.52.18**  He who has no equal among men, O foremost of rākṣasas — how do you hope to fight one who is the peer of Indra and Vivasvat?

**6.52.19**  Having thus addressed the enraged Kumbhakarṇa, Mahodara then spoke in the midst of the rākṣasas to Rāvaṇa, who makes the worlds cry out.

**6.52.20**  "Having regained Vaidehī, why do you babble on? When you desire it, Sītā will be in your power.

**6.52.21**  A certain stratagem has occurred to me for bringing Sītā to compliance; if it pleases you, lord of rākṣasas, hear it.

**6.52.22**  Have it proclaimed: 'I — Dvijihva — and Saṃhrādī, Kumbhakarṇa, and Vitardana go forth now to slay Rāma.'

**6.52.23**  Then, going forth, we shall give him battle with full effort; if we conquer your enemies, stratagem is unnecessary for us.

**6.52.24**  But if the enemy survives and we have engaged in battle, then we shall execute what has been deliberated in the mind.

**6.52.25**  We shall return here from the battle drenched in blood, our own bodies pierced by the sharp arrows bearing the name of Rāma.

**6.52.26**  Declaring 'We have devoured Rāghava and Lakṣmaṇa,' we shall grasp your feet; you, fulfil our desire.

**6.52.27**  Then proclaim throughout the city from the back of an elephant, O king: 'Rāma has been slain along with his brother and his army.'

**6.52.28**  Then, O subduer of foes, becoming indeed pleased with your servants, bestow upon them enjoyments, retinues, and desires.

**6.52.29**  Then give garlands, garments, and ointments to the heroes; give abundant drink to the warriors, and yourself drink in joy.

**6.52.30**  Then, when this merriment has multiplied and spread everywhere, entering in private, reassure and soothe Sītā, and entice her with wealth, grain, pleasures, and jewels.

**6.52.31**  By this ruse, O king — bound to fear and grief — Sītā, bereft of her lord and unwilling, will come under your sway.

**6.52.32**  Learning that her worthy husband is destroyed, she, out of despair and the lightness of a woman's nature, will submit to your will.

**6.52.33**  She who was formerly reared in happiness and worthy of happiness, now tormented by misery — knowing that happiness depends on you — will in every way come to you.

**6.52.34**  This, in my view, is sound policy; for upon merely seeing Rāma, disaster would befall. Here your great longing shall be fulfilled — gain of happiness without battle.

**6.52.35**  A lord of men who, without losing his army and without encountering doubt, conquers his enemies without battle, O master of the earth, long enjoys great glory, merit, prosperity, and fame."

**6.53.1**  Thus spoken to, Kumbhakarṇa rebuked Mahodara, then addressed his brother Rāvaṇa, foremost among rākṣasas.

**6.53.2**  "I shall this day wipe away your terrible fear of death at the hands of that wicked Rāma; be free from enmity and dwell in happiness.

**6.53.3**  Heroes do not roar in vain, as waterless clouds do; see now my roar being fulfilled in battle by deed.

**6.53.4**  Heroes neither endure passively nor glorify themselves; without showing off, they perform deeds of great difficulty.

**6.53.5**  This is what first befalls kings who are confused, devoid of wisdom, and fancying themselves learned — those who listen to the likes of you, Mahodara.

**6.53.6**  This matter has been ruined by cowards like you, ever speaking pleasant words, following the king about in battle.

**6.53.7**  Laṅkā has been reduced to but the shadow of a kingdom; the treasury is exhausted, the army slaughtered — all through attending upon this king who wears the mark of a friend but is an enemy.

**6.53.8**  I now march forth for battle, intent on conquering the enemy, to rectify today in the great conflict the ill counsel of yourselves.

**6.53.9**  Having heard these words of the wise Kumbhakarṇa, the lord of rākṣasas then replied, laughing.

**6.53.10**  "This Mahodara is certainly terrified of Rāma — no doubt; he does not relish battle, dear one, though expert in warfare.

**6.53.11**  There is no one equal to you for me in affection and in strength; go for the slaying of the enemy and for the triumph of Kumbhakarṇa.

**6.53.12**  The swift annihilator of enemies then took up the sharp spear — made entirely of iron, blazing, and adorned with beaten gold —

**6.53.13**  fearsome as Indra's thunderbolt, equal in weight to the vajra, the slayer of gods, dānavas, gandharvas, yakṣas, and kiṃnaras —

**6.53.14**  garlanded with red wreaths of great length, shooting forth flames of itself, stained with the blood of enemies. Taking up that sharp spear, the greatly resplendent Kumbhakarṇa spoke to Rāvaṇa.

**6.53.15**  "I shall go alone; let the great army remain here. Today, hungry and wrathful, I shall devour those monkeys.

**6.53.16**  Hearing Kumbhakarṇa's words, Rāvaṇa replied: "Go surrounded by troops bearing spears and clubs in their hands.

**6.53.17**  For the monkeys are noble-souled, swift, and resolute; they could bring even one alone or careless to destruction with their teeth.

**6.53.18**  Therefore go surrounded by utterly irresistible troops; annihilate the entire enemy host, which is hostile to the rākṣasas."

**6.53.19**  Then, springing up from his seat, the greatly resplendent Rāvaṇa bound upon Kumbhakarṇa a garland set with jewels at intervals.

**6.53.20**  He fastened armlets, finger-rings, and choice ornaments upon the great-souled one, as well as a necklace bright as the moon.

**6.53.21**  Rāvaṇa also attached divine, fragrant garland-chains, and fastened upon his ears his resplendent earrings.

**6.53.22**  Adorned with golden armlets and upper-arm bands, decorated with necklace-ornaments, large-eared Kumbhakarṇa blazed like a well-fed fire.

**6.53.23**  Resplendent with a great dark waist-cord, he shone like Mount Mandara when it was bound with the serpent at the churning of the nectar.

**6.53.24**  As the golden armour — capable of bearing great weight, windproof, bright as lightning, blazing with its own radiance — was being fastened upon him, he shone like the king of mountains wrapped in twilight clouds.

**6.53.25**  His body bound with every ornament, spear in hand, that rākṣasa, his valour akin to that of the three-striding one, appeared like Nārāyaṇa himself.

**6.53.26**  Embracing his brother, circling him clockwise, and bowing his head to him, the mighty one set forth; and Rāvaṇa sent him off with auspicious benedictions.

**6.53.27**  With the blare of conches and kettledrums, and with troops bearing choice weapons, with elephants, horses, and chariots thundering like rain-clouds, chariot-warriors followed that great-souled one, the foremost of chariot-fighters.

**6.53.28**  With serpents, camels, donkeys, horses, lions, elephants, deer, and birds, they followed that terrible and mighty Kumbhakarṇa.

**6.53.29**  Showered with flowers of varied colours, holding an umbrella aloft, sharp spear in hand, intoxicated with frenzy, maddened by the scent of blood, the foe of dānavas and gods marched forth.

**6.53.30**  Many foot-soldiers followed him — fearsome rākṣasas of great strength and terrible eyes, roaring loudly, weapons in hand.

**6.53.31**  Red-eyed, of immense bodies, resembling mounds of blue collyrium, brandishing sharp swords and axes —

**6.53.32**  and great missiles many fathoms long, vast and irresistible, and broad palm-tree trunks as well, vast and unapproachable —

**6.53.33**  Then, assuming another terrible form that made the hair stand on end, the greatly resplendent and mighty Kumbhakarṇa burst forth.

**6.53.34**  His girth measured a hundred bow-lengths around; he rose to six hundred bow-lengths in height — a fearsome one, his eyes like chariot-wheels, resembling a great mountain.

**6.53.35**  Joining the rākṣasas, the great one resembling a scorched mountain, wide-mouthed Kumbhakarṇa, laughing, spoke thus:

**6.53.36**  "Today, enraged, I shall burn those hosts of monkey-chiefs troop by troop, as fire burns locusts.

**6.53.37**  The forest-roaming monkeys have done me no real wrong; creatures like them are the natural ornament of parks and gardens.

**6.53.38**  But the root of the siege of the city is Rāghava together with Lakṣmaṇa; when he is slain, all is slain — him I shall kill in battle."

**6.53.39**  As Kumbhakarṇa spoke thus, the rākṣasas set up a mighty and terrible roar, as if shaking the ocean.

**6.53.40**  As the wise Kumbhakarṇa swiftly burst forth, terrible omens appeared on all sides.

**6.53.41**  Clouds mixed with meteors and thunderbolts rumbled most dreadfully; the earth with its oceans and forests trembled.

**6.53.42**  Jackals of fearsome aspect howled with flaming morsels in their mouths; birds wheeled in inauspicious circles.

**6.53.43**  A vulture alighted upon his spear as he walked the road; his eye throbbed and his left arm trembled.

**6.53.44**  A blazing meteor fell then with a fearsome sound; the sun lost its lustre, and no pleasant breeze blew.

**6.53.45**  Heeding not the great and hair-raising portents that had arisen, Kumbhakarṇa marched out, impelled by the force of Death.

**6.53.46**  Stepping over the rampart on foot, mountain-like, he beheld the wondrous monkey-army, dense as a mass of clouds.

**6.53.47**  Seeing that foremost rākṣasa who was like a mountain, the monkeys fled in all directions then, like storm-driven clouds.

**6.53.48**  Seeing that exceedingly fierce monkey-army scatter in all directions like a broken mass of clouds, Kumbhakarṇa, who resembled a cloud, roared again in joy, like a thunder-cloud.

**6.53.49**  Hearing his terrible roar, as though it were the rumble of a cloud in the sky, many leaping monkeys fell to the ground, like sāla-trees with their roots cut.

**6.53.50**  That great-souled Kumbhakarṇa, bearing his broad iron club, having gone forth to slay the enemy, struck profound terror into the monkey-hosts, like a master wielding his rod of punishment at the world's end.

**6.54.1**  He roared a mighty roar, resounding over the ocean, as if generating thunderclaps, as if shattering mountains.

**6.54.2**  Seeing him approach with his terrible eyes — he who was invincible by Indra, Yama, or Varuṇa — the monkeys fled in all directions.

**6.54.3**  Seeing them running away, Aṅgada, son of Vālin, addressed Nala, Nīla, Gavākṣa, and the mighty Kumuda:

**6.54.4**  "Forgetting yourselves here — your valour and your noble birth — where do you go, panicked with fear, like common monkeys?

**6.54.5**  Turn back, gentle ones; why do you protect your lives? This rākṣasa is not fit for battle — this great menace is mere terror.

**6.54.6**  This great terror of the rākṣasas that has arisen — we shall scatter it by our valour; turn back, O leaping ones."

**6.54.7**  With great difficulty reassuring them and rallying them from here and there, the monkeys, with trees and boulders in hand, set forth toward the battle-field.

**6.54.8**  Turning back, those forest-dwellers, exceedingly enraged, struck Kumbhakarṇa — like rut-maddened elephants — hurling tall mountain-peaks and boulders, the mighty ones.

**6.54.9**  Though struck with flowering-topped trees, he did not tremble; the boulders that fell upon his limbs shattered into hundreds of pieces, and the flowering-topped trees broke and fell to the ground.

**6.54.10**  He too, enraged, with utmost effort, ground down the armies of the mighty monkeys, like a blazing fire consuming forests.

**6.54.11**  Many foremost monkeys, drenched in blood, lay prostrate — cast down and fallen upon the earth like trees with copper-red blossoms.

**6.54.12**  The monkeys, leaping and running, looked not back; some fell into the ocean, some took refuge in the sky.

**6.54.13**  Those heroes, being slaughtered by the mighty rākṣasa, fled by the very path by which they had crossed the ocean.

**6.54.14**  With dejected faces, in fear, the bears and monkeys took to open ground and valleys; some climbed into trees, some took refuge on a mountain.

**6.54.15**  Some plunged into the sea, some took shelter in caves; some monkeys sat still, some did not stand their ground at all.

**6.54.16**  Seeing those monkeys routed, Aṅgada spoke to them: "Stand firm; let us fight; turn back, O leaping ones.

**6.54.17**  Searching this entire earth, I see no refuge for you who are routed; all of you, turn back — why do you protect your lives?

**6.54.18**  For those who run unarmed with unsupported flight and no valour — their own wives shall mock them; that truly is a blow against life.

**6.54.19**  We are all born in great and illustrious clans; it is surely ignoble that, in fear, abandoning your valour, you run away.

**6.54.20**  Those boasts of yours in the assembly of the people — where are they now? Where have those great and exalted resolves gone?

**6.54.21**  The taunts of 'coward' are heard — he who lives disgraced is cursed; follow the path trodden by men of honour; abandon fear.

**6.54.22**  Let us lie slain upon the earth — we who have short lives — or, cut down in battle, attain the world of Brahmā, hard to reach; or let us gain glory by slaying the enemy in combat.

**6.54.23**  Kumbhakarṇa, having seen Kākutstha, shall not go away living — like a moth approaching a blazing fire.

**6.54.24**  We who were pointed at for fleeing — shall we protect our lives? Many routed by one — our glory shall come to nothing."

**6.54.25**  As heroic Aṅgada of the golden armlets spoke thus, those still fleeing uttered words censured by the brave:

**6.54.26**  "Terrible carnage has been wrought upon us by the rākṣasa Kumbhakarṇa; this is no time to stand — we go; life is dear to us."

**6.54.27**  Having spoken only this much, all those monkey-troop leaders fled in all directions, seeing the fearsome, terrible-eyed one approaching.

**6.54.28**  But those fleeing heroes, the broad-faced monkeys, were then all turned back by Aṅgada with gentle words and great respect.

**6.54.29**  Ṛṣabha, Śarabha, Mainda, Dhūmra, and Nīla; Kumuda, Suṣeṇa, Gavākṣa, Rambha, and Tāra; Dvivida, Panasa, and Vāyuputra as chief — all set out swiftly toward the battle.

**6.55.1**  Those mighty-bodied ones, having heard the words of Aṅgada, turned back and assumed a resolute mind, all eager for battle.

**6.55.2**  Their valor stirred up and their courage heightened, the monkeys were rallied by the words of Aṅgada.

**6.55.3**  Setting forth, the monkeys became joyful, having resolved on death; they fought a most tumultuous battle, having cast aside all concern for their lives.

**6.55.4**  Then those mighty-bodied monkeys, quickly lifting up trees and great mountain peaks, rushed toward Kumbhakarṇa.

**6.55.5**  That valiant Kumbhakarṇa, exceedingly wrathful, lifting up his mace, of tremendous body, crushed and scattered the enemies on every side.

**6.55.6**  Seven hundred, eight hundred, thousands of monkeys lay scattered on the ground, crushed by Kumbhakarṇa.

**6.55.7**  Sixteen, eight, ten, twenty, thirty at a time — seizing them with his arms and devouring them, he ran about eating them; exceedingly enraged, like Garuḍa among serpents.

**6.55.8**  Hanūmān, stationed in the sky, rained down mountain peaks and many kinds of trees upon the head of Kumbhakarṇa.

**6.55.9**  Kumbhakarṇa of great strength shattered those mountain peaks with his spear and broke apart the shower of trees.

**6.55.10**  Then that fierce army of monkeys fled; but Hanūmān stood before him as he charged, grasping a mountain peak.

**6.55.11**  Enraged, he struck Kumbhakarṇa of terrible frame swiftly with the finest of mountains; Kumbhakarṇa was shaken thereby and overcome, his body dripping with fat and drenched in blood.

**6.55.12**  He whirled his spear, blazing like lightning, and struck the son of the Wind between his arms, as Guha once struck the Krauñca mountain with his fierce lance.

**6.55.13**  Hanūmān, his great chest pierced by the spear, staggered and vomiting blood from his mouth, let out a terrible roar in the great battle, like the sound of thunder at the end of an age.

**6.55.14**  Thereupon the hosts of rākṣasas suddenly roared with delight seeing him afflicted; the monkeys, distressed and overwhelmed with fear, fled from Kumbhakarṇa in the battle.

**6.55.15**  Nīla hurled a mountain peak at the wise Kumbhakarṇa; seeing it coming toward him, Kumbhakarṇa struck it with his fist.

**6.55.16**  That mountain peak, struck by the blow of his fist, shattered and fell to the earth with sparks and flames.

**6.55.17**  Ṛṣabha, Śarabha, Nīla, Gavākṣa, and Gandhamādana — these five foremost monkeys rushed upon Kumbhakarṇa.

**6.55.18**  Those mighty ones struck the great-bodied Kumbhakarṇa from all sides with rocks, trees, palms, feet, and fists.

**6.55.19**  Feeling those blows as if mere touches, he did not flinch; but the swift Ṛṣabha he clasped in his arms.

**6.55.20**  That terrible bull among monkeys, crushed by Kumbhakarṇa's arms, fell, with blood flowing from his mouth.

**6.55.21**  The enemy of Indra then struck Śarabha with his fist, Nīla with his knee in battle, and Gavākṣa with his palm.

**6.55.22**  Tormented by the blows given to them, drenched in blood, they fainted and fell to earth like felled kiṃśuka trees.

**6.55.23**  When those great-souled monkey chiefs had fallen, thousands of monkeys fled from Kumbhakarṇa.

**6.55.24**  All those mighty bull-monkeys, themselves resembling mountains, leapt upon him as upon a mountain, climbed him and bit him.

**6.55.25**  Those bull-monkeys struck the great-bodied Kumbhakarṇa with nails, teeth, fists, and knees.

**6.55.26**  Covered by those thousands of monkeys, that tiger among rākṣasas, who resembled a mountain, shone like a hill covered with its own trees.

**6.55.27**  Seizing all the monkeys with his arms, that mighty one devoured them, enraged, like Garuḍa devouring serpents.

**6.55.28**  Monkeys thrown by Kumbhakarṇa into his mouth, which resembled the underworld, emerged through his nostrils and his ears.

**6.55.29**  Devouring the monkeys with extreme fury, that foremost of rākṣasas, who resembled a mountain, smashed all the monkeys, exceedingly enraged.

**6.55.30**  Making the ground a mire of flesh and blood, that rākṣasa moved through the monkey army like the fire of universal dissolution blazing intensely.

**6.55.31**  The mighty Kumbhakarṇa in that battle shone forth, spear in hand, as Śakra with the thunderbolt in his hand, as Antaka with his noose in hand.

**6.55.32**  As fire burns dry forests in summer, so Kumbhakarṇa consumed the monkey armies.

**6.55.33**  Then those monkeys, being slaughtered, their herd-leaders slain, trembling with fear, cried out discordantly and piteously.

**6.55.34**  The monkeys, being slaughtered repeatedly by Kumbhakarṇa, afflicted and downcast in heart, sought refuge with Rāghava.

**6.55.35**  Seeing that mighty Kumbhakarṇa charging forward, the heroic Sugrīva, lord of the monkeys, leapt up.

**6.55.36**  That great monkey, lifting a mountain peak and whirling it about, rushed swiftly at the mighty Kumbhakarṇa.

**6.55.37**  Seeing that monkey rushing upon him, Kumbhakarṇa stood with all his limbs exposed, facing the king of the monkeys.

**6.55.38**  Seeing Kumbhakarṇa standing there with his body smeared with the blood of monkeys, devouring great monkeys, Sugrīva spoke.

**6.55.39**  "Warriors have been felled by you; a most difficult feat has been accomplished; armies have been devoured; supreme glory has been won by you.

**6.55.40**  Abandon that monkey army — what use have you for these ordinary ones? Bear one blow from me, rākṣasa, with this mountain."

**6.55.41**  Hearing that speech of the monkey king, imbued with courage and steadfastness, Kumbhakarṇa, tiger among rākṣasas, replied.

**6.55.42**  "You are indeed the grandson of Prajāpati and the son of Ṛkṣaraja; you are endowed with famed valor — that is why you roar so, monkey."

**6.55.43**  Hearing Kumbhakarṇa's words, he suddenly whirled the mountain and released it; with that mountain, resembling a thunderbolt or lightning, he struck Kumbhakarṇa in the chest.

**6.55.44**  That mountain peak was instantly shattered against his broad chest; thereupon the monkeys were suddenly downcast while the hosts of rākṣasas cried out with joy.

**6.55.45**  Struck by the mountain peak, he became enraged and roared in his fury, opening wide his mouth; whirling his lightning-bright spear, he hurled it to slay the lord of the monkeys.

**6.55.46**  That sharp spear hurled by Kumbhakarṇa's arms, adorned with a golden garland, was quickly leapt upon by the son of Anila, who caught and swiftly broke it with his arms.

**6.55.47**  That great spear of dark iron, made to bear a thousand loads, was broken by that bull among monkeys, who placed his knee upon it, delighted.

**6.55.48**  Seeing his spear broken, the great-souled lord of the rākṣasas became enraged; uprooting a peak from Laṅkā's Malaya range, he approached and struck Sugrīva with it.

**6.55.49**  The king of the monkeys, struck by the mountain peak, fell insensible to the ground in battle; seeing him fallen insensible to the ground, the rākṣasas roared with delight in battle.

**6.55.50**  Approaching that king of monkeys with his wondrously terrible might, Kumbhakarṇa seized and carried off Sugrīva in battle, even as the exceedingly violent wind carries off a cloud.

**6.55.51**  Kumbhakarṇa, tearing him away and moving in battle, of a form comparable to Meru, shone forth with the one who resembled a great cloud — like Meru with its terribly high peaks.

**6.55.52**  Then the hero tore him away and went, being praised by the lords of rākṣasas in battle, hearing the cries of the gods in their heaven, who were astonished at the capture of the monkey king.

**6.55.53**  Then taking him, the enemy of Indra thought regarding that Indra-like lord of monkeys: "With this one taken captive, the entire army together with Rāghava would be undone."

**6.55.54**  Seeing the monkey army fleeing in every direction and Sugrīva the monkey seized by Kumbhakarṇa,

**6.55.55**  the wise Hanūmān, son of the Wind, began to reflect: "With Sugrīva seized in this manner, what is it that I should do?"

**6.55.56**  "Whatever is right for me to do, that I shall do by every means; assuming a form like a mountain, I shall destroy the rākṣasa.

**6.55.57**  "When the mighty Kumbhakarṇa has been slain by me in battle, his body crushed by my fists, and the monkey king freed, let all the monkeys be glad."

**6.55.58**  "Or else the king himself will gain release — even if he were seized by the gods together with asuras and serpents,

**6.55.59**  "I think the lord of monkeys does not yet regain his senses, having been struck by the mountain blow from Kumbhakarṇa in battle.

**6.55.60**  "In a moment Sugrīva, having regained consciousness in the great battle, will do what is beneficial for himself and for the monkeys.

**6.55.61**  "But if I were to rescue the great-souled Sugrīva, both his displeasure would result and the permanent loss of his glory would be grievous.

**6.55.62**  "Therefore I shall wait a moment for the valor of our king; in the meantime I shall reassure the scattered monkey army."

**6.55.63**  Having thus reflected, Hanūmān, son of the Wind, once again steadied the great army of monkeys.

**6.55.64**  Then Kumbhakarṇa entered Laṅkā carrying that great monkey who was writhing and struggling, showered with the finest flowers by those standing in the aerial cars, mansions, and gatehouses.

**6.55.65**  Then the great-souled one, held between the arms of the stronger foe, with difficulty regaining his senses, and surveying the royal roads of the city, reflected again and again.

**6.55.66**  "Being seized in this way, what can I possibly do now? I shall act in such a way that what is desired and beneficial for the monkeys will be accomplished."

**6.55.67**  Then the king of the monkeys, suddenly striking with the tips of his fingers, bit with his nails into the ears and with his teeth into the nose of the enemy of Indra, and tore at his flanks with his claws.

**6.55.68**  Kumbhakarṇa, his ears and nose torn away, ripped and pounded by him, overwhelmed with rage, his body wet with blood, hurled Sugrīva down and crushed him to the ground.

**6.55.69**  Crushed on the ground by those enemies of the gods with their terrible strength, beaten by them, he swiftly took to the sky and returned to Rāma.

**6.55.70**  The mighty Kumbhakarṇa, deprived of his ears and nose, drenched in blood, shone forth like a mountain with its streams.

**6.55.71**  Then the great-souled Kumbhakarṇa suddenly sallied from the city and devoured that fierce monkey army in battle, blazing like the fire of universal destruction.

**6.55.72**  Hungry and greedy for blood and flesh, Kumbhakarṇa penetrated that fierce monkey army and in his frenzy devoured in battle rākṣasas, monkeys, piśācas, and bears alike.

**6.55.73**  Enraged, he seized one, two, three, many — monkeys along with rākṣasas in one hand — and swiftly flung them into his mouth.

**6.55.74**  Dripping with fat and blood, that mighty one, even while being struck by the tips of great mountains, devoured the monkeys; and those monkeys who were being devoured then fled to Rāma for refuge.

**6.55.75**  At that time the son of Sumitrā, Lakṣmaṇa, crusher of enemy forces, conqueror of enemy cities, waged battle in anger.

**6.55.76**  That valiant Lakṣmaṇa drove seven arrows deep into Kumbhakarṇa's body, took up other arrows, and released them.

**6.55.77**  Passing by Saumitri, the mighty Kumbhakarṇa charged straight at Rāma alone, as if splitting the earth.

**6.55.78**  Then Rāma, son of Daśaratha, employing the dread weapon, shot sharp arrows into Kumbhakarṇa's heart.

**6.55.79**  From the mouth of the one pierced by Rāma and rushing forward, flames mixed with embers streamed forth in his rage.

**6.55.80**  The peacock-plumed arrows sank into his chest, and the great mace fell from his hand to the earth.

**6.55.81**  When that mighty one realized he was weaponless, he wrought great havoc with his two fists and with his feet.

**6.55.82**  His body pierced all over with arrows, soaked with gore, he oozed blood like a mountain with its springs.

**6.55.83**  Maddened by intense rage and by blood, he ran about devouring monkeys, rākṣasas, and bears.

**6.55.84**  At that time the righteous Lakṣmaṇa, engaged in the slaying of Kumbhakarṇa and considering many strategies, spoke to Rāma.

**6.55.85**  "O king, he no longer distinguishes between monkeys and rākṣasas; maddened by the smell of blood he devours friend and foe alike.

**6.55.86**  "It is best that all the bull-monkeys climb upon him from every side, and let the herd-leaders, all the chiefs, stand around him.

**6.55.87**  "Perhaps this foolish rākṣasa, pressed down by the heavy burden in due time and falling to the earth, will not then slay any more monkeys."

**6.55.88**  Hearing that speech of the wise prince, those monkeys gladly climbed upon Kumbhakarṇa.

**6.55.89**  Kumbhakarṇa, exceedingly enraged, with the monkeys clambering all over him, shook them off with speed, as a rogue elephant shakes off its mahouts.

**6.55.90**  Seeing them shaken off, Rāma thought "this rākṣasa is enraged" and leapt up swiftly, taking up his finest bow.

**6.55.91**  Taking up that bow, resembling a great serpent, with a firm string, fierce, adorned with gold, and having reassured the monkeys, Rāma leapt up with his best quiver of arrows strapped on.

**6.55.92**  That mighty Rāma, supremely difficult to conquer, surrounded by those bands of monkeys and attended by Lakṣmaṇa, set forth.

**6.55.93**  He beheld the great-souled, diadem-wearing, enemy-subduing, mighty Kumbhakarṇa, his entire body drenched in blood.

**6.55.94**  He saw him charging all directions like a maddened elephant, stalking the monkeys in fury, surrounded by rākṣasas.

**6.55.95**  He resembled the Vindhya or Mandara mountain, adorned with golden armlets, blood streaming from his mouth like a rain-cloud risen up.

**6.55.96**  He licked the blood with his tongue, drenched in blood, crushing the monkey army, comparable to Yama at the end of an age.

**6.55.97**  Seeing that foremost of rākṣasas, blazing with the splendor of a lit fire, that bull among men then twanged his bow.

**6.55.98**  At the sound of his bow, that bull among rākṣasas became enraged, unable to endure that sound, and rushed at Rāghava.

**6.55.99**  Then Rāma spoke in battle to Kumbhakarṇa who charged like a cloud swept up by the wind, with arms like the coils of a great serpent king, and resembling a mountain.

**6.55.100**  "Come, lord of rākṣasas, do not despair — I stand here, bow in hand. Know me as Rāma, rival of Śakra; in a moment you will be senseless."

**6.55.101**  Recognizing this as Rāma, he laughed with a hideous sound, as if making the hearts of all the forest-dwellers sink.

**6.55.102**  Laughing hideously and fearfully with a sound like thunder, the mighty-splendored Kumbhakarṇa spoke to Rāghava.

**6.55.103**  "I am not to be thought of as Virādha, nor as Kabandha, nor as Khara, nor as Vālin, nor as Mārīca — I am Kumbhakarṇa who has come.

**6.55.104**  "Behold my terrible mace of solid iron — with this I formerly conquered the gods and dānavas.

**6.55.105**  "You should not despise me as one without ears and nose — even the loss of ears and nose gives me no pain worth mentioning.

**6.55.106**  "Show your valor upon my body freely, O tiger of the Ikṣvākus; then I shall devour you once your heroic prowess has been displayed."

**6.55.107**  Hearing Kumbhakarṇa's words, Rāma released well-feathered arrows; struck by them, equal in speed to the thunderbolt, the enemy of the gods neither wavered nor was pained.

**6.55.108**  Those arrows by which the finest śāla trees were severed, by which Vālin the bull among monkeys was slain — those thunderbolt-like arrows then could not distress Kumbhakarṇa's body.

**6.55.109**  The enemy of great Indra, drinking up those arrows with his body as if they were streams of water, countered Rāma's arrow-force by whirling and hurling that mace with terrible speed.

**6.55.110**  Then that rākṣasa, smeared with blood, terror of the great divine armies, whirling that mace of terrible velocity, put the monkey army to flight.

**6.55.111**  Then Rāma took up the excellent Vāyavya weapon and shot it at the night-ranger; with it he severed the arm together with the mace; with his arm cut off, he let out a tumultuous roar.

**6.55.112**  That arm of his, like a mountain peak, together with the mace, severed by Rāghava's arrow, fell upon the monkey-king's army and struck that monkey force.

**6.55.113**  Those monkeys, the remnant of the broken and slain, retreating to the flanks, downcast and trembling-limbed, beheld that most terrible clash of the king of men and the lord of rākṣasas.

**6.55.114**  Kumbhakarṇa, that great one with his arm cut off by the weapon, like a great mountain with its peak severed, uprooted a tree with his remaining hand and then charged at the king of men in battle.

**6.55.115**  Rāma severed that arm of his raised along with the śāla tree, resembling a serpent's coil, with an arrow charged with the Aindra weapon, decorated with gold.

**6.55.116**  That arm of Kumbhakarṇa, cut off, fell to the earth, resembling a mountain; writhing, it struck trees, hills, rocks, monkeys, and rākṣasas.

**6.55.117**  Seeing him with both arms severed, charging suddenly and roaring, Rāma seized two sharp crescent-shaped arrows and cut off the feet of the rākṣasa in battle.

**6.55.118**  His arms cut off and his feet severed, gaping open his mouth which resembled the mare's fire at ocean's end, he rushed at Rāma with a sudden roar, as Rāhu rushes at the moon in the sky.

**6.55.119**  Rāma filled his mouth with arrows of sharp points, with gold-bound shafts; with his mouth full he could not speak, he gurgled with difficulty and even fainted.

**6.55.120**  Then Rāma took up that arrow comparable to the rays of the sun, comparable to the rod of Brahmā and the end of time, unerring, charged with Aindra energy, sharp, well-feathered, equal in speed to the wind.

**6.55.121**  Rāma shot at the night-ranger that arrow with beautiful shaft of thunderbolt and gold, with the brilliance of the blazing sun, equal in speed to the bolt of great Indra.

**6.55.122**  That arrow, impelled by Rāghava's arm, illuminating the ten directions with its own light, appearing blazing like a smokeless fire, went forth equal in might to the thunderbolt of Śakra.

**6.55.123**  It severed the head of the lord of rākṣasas — that head resembling a great mountain peak, with bared tusks and swaying lovely earrings — even as Puraṃdara once severed the head of Vṛtra.

**6.55.124**  That rākṣasa's head, struck by Rāma's arrow, fell, mountain-like, smashing the promenades, mansions, and gatehouses, and toppling the high rampart.

**6.55.125**  That enormous rākṣasa body, resembling the Himālaya, fell into the ocean, crushed crocodiles, great masses of fish, and serpents, and submerged into the earth as well.

**6.55.126**  When the mighty Kumbhakarṇa, enemy of the Brāhmaṇas and gods, had been slain in battle, the earth and all its mountains shook, and the gods cried out in tumultuous joy.

**6.55.127**  Then the divine sages, great sages, serpents, gods, spirits, Suparṇas, Guhyakas, bands of Yakṣas and Gandharvas dwelling in the sky were filled with joy at Rāma's valor.

**6.55.128**  Many monkeys were filled with delight, their faces like blooming lotuses, and they praised Rāghava, the beloved, with the enemy of terrible strength and unapproachable prowess slain.

**6.55.129**  Having slain in battle Kumbhakarṇa, crusher of divine armies, unwearied and unconquered in great battles, Bharata's elder brother rejoiced, as the lord of the immortals rejoiced upon slaying the great asura Vṛtra.

**6.56.1**  Having seen Kumbhakarṇa slain by the great-souled Rāghava, the rākṣasas reported it to Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas.

**6.56.2**  Hearing that the mighty Kumbhakarṇa had been slain in battle, Rāvaṇa, consumed by grief, fainted and fell.

**6.56.3**  Hearing that their paternal uncle had been slain, Devāntaka, Narāntaka, Triśiras, and Atikāya wept, afflicted with grief.

**6.56.4**  Hearing that their brother had been slain by the tireless Rāma, Mahodara and Mahāpārśva were overcome by grief.

**6.56.5**  Then that foremost of rākṣasas, with great difficulty regaining his senses, Rāvaṇa, broken by the death of Kumbhakarṇa, lamented.

**6.56.6**  "Ah, hero, crusher of enemy pride, mighty Kumbhakarṇa — having alone scorched the enemy army, where do you go, abandoning me?

**6.56.7**  "Now indeed I am as nothing, for my right arm has fallen — that arm on which I relied and feared not gods nor asuras.

**6.56.8**  "How could such a hero, crusher of the pride of gods and dānavas, like the fire of time, be slain today by Rāghava in battle?

**6.56.9**  "You for whom the crushing blow of the thunderbolt never caused suffering — how are you now laid low by Rāma's arrows, sleeping on the earth?

**6.56.10**  "These hosts of gods standing in the sky together with seers, seeing you slain in battle, cry out overjoyed.

**6.56.11**  "Surely today the monkeys, their goal achieved and overjoyed, will climb the fortifications and scale all the gates of Laṅkā.

**6.56.12**  "I have no need of the kingdom, what shall I do with Sītā — without Kumbhakarṇa I find no pleasure in life.

**6.56.13**  "If I do not slay Rāghava, the slayer of my brother, in battle, then surely death is better for me; otherwise this life is meaningless.

**6.56.14**  "Today I shall go to that place where my younger brother is; for I cannot bear to live even a moment having abandoned my brothers.

**6.56.15**  "The gods will mock me, seeing one who has previously offended them. How shall I conquer Indra, with you, Kumbhakarṇa, slain?

**6.56.16**  "That good counsel of Vibhīṣaṇa has now come back to me — which I, out of ignorance, did not accept from that great-souled one.

**6.56.17**  "This destruction that has arisen for both Kumbhakarṇa and Prahasta, because of my not heeding Vibhīṣaṇa's counsel, fills me with bitter shame.

**6.56.18**  "This is the fruit, come to me in due course and causing grief, of that deed — when I expelled the righteous and glorious Vibhīṣaṇa."
