**6.56.19**  Having thus lamented most pitifully in many ways, his inner soul in turmoil, knowing his younger brother the enemy of Indra to be slain, the ten-faced Rāvaṇa fell down, sorely afflicted.

**6.57.1**  As the evil-souled Rāvaṇa lamented thus, scorched by grief, Triśiras heard him and spoke.

**6.57.2**  "Our middle uncle of great valor has indeed been slain, father; but righteous men, O king, do not lament as you do.

**6.57.3**  "Surely you, O lord, are sufficient even for the three worlds; why then do you subject yourself to grief like an ordinary man?

**6.57.4**  "You have the lance granted by Brahmā, a coat of armor, arrows, a bow, a chariot yoked to a thousand asses and roaring like thunder.

**6.57.5**  "By you the gods and dānavas have been repeatedly slaughtered even without weapons; so fully equipped with every weapon, you are fit to chastise Rāghava.

**6.57.6**  "Please remain, O great king; I shall go forth to battle and shall pull out your enemies here like Garuḍa pulling out serpents.

**6.57.7**  "As Śambara was laid low by the king of gods and Naraka by Viṣṇu, so today shall Rāma be laid low and slain by me in battle."

**6.57.8**  Hearing Triśiras's words, Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas, impelled by fate, felt as if he had been born again.

**6.57.9**  Hearing Triśiras's words, Devāntaka, Narāntaka, and the mighty Atikāya became filled with battle-joy.

**6.57.10**  Then, each roaring "I, I" — the sons of Rāvaṇa, heroes among rākṣasas, equal in valor to Śakra,

**6.57.11**  All of them moving through the sky, all skilled in illusion, all crushers of divine pride, all maddened by battle,

**6.57.12**  All endowed with the power of weapons, all of wide-spread fame, all having engaged in battle — never had they been heard of as defeated,

**6.57.13**  All heroes versed in arms, all skilled in warfare, all eager to excel, all having obtained boons —

**6.57.14**  Surrounded thus by those sons of solar radiance, crushers of enemy forces, the king shone forth as Maghavan shines surrounded by the immortals, destroyers of the pride of great dānavas.

**6.57.15**  He embraced his sons, adorned them with ornaments, and sent them forth to battle with auspicious blessings.

**6.57.16**  Rāvaṇa also sent his brothers Mahodara and Mahāpārśva to battle for the protection of the princes.

**6.57.17**  They saluted the great-souled Rāvaṇa, terror of enemies, performed circumambulation, and those great-bodied ones set forth.

**6.57.18**  Anointed with all medicinal herbs and perfumes, these six foremost rākṣasas of great strength set out, eager for battle.

**6.57.19**  Then Mahodara mounted an elephant named Sudarśana, blue-cloud-like, born of the lineage of Airāvata.

**6.57.20**  Equipped with all weapons and well adorned with quivers, he shone mounted on the elephant like the sun on the mountaintop at setting.

**6.57.21**  Triśiras, son of Rāvaṇa, mounted the finest chariot, yoked with best horses and full of all weapons.

**6.57.22**  Triśiras, bow in hand, mounted on his chariot, shone forth like a storm cloud with lightning, meteor, flames, and the rainbow of Indra.

**6.57.23**  Triśiras with his three diadems gleamed on that finest chariot like the Himālaya, king of mountains, with three golden peaks.

**6.57.24**  The mighty Atikāya, son of the lord of rākṣasas, best of all archers, also mounted the finest chariot.

**6.57.25**  That chariot was perfectly wheeled and well-equipped, with a strong axle and yoke, blazing with quivers, bows, and arrows, crowded with spears, swords, and clubs.

**6.57.26**  With his shining golden diadem and ornaments, he blazed forth like Mount Meru luminous with its own light.

**6.57.27**  That mighty son of the king shone on that chariot, surrounded by tiger-like rākṣasas, like Vajrapāṇi surrounded by the immortals.

**6.57.28**  Narāntaka mounted a white horse resembling Uccaiḥśravas, adorned with gold, swift as thought, of great frame.

**6.57.29**  Grasping a gleaming lance, Narāntaka shone forth, that mighty one taking his śakti, like Guha among enemies in battle.

**6.57.30**  Devāntaka took up his iron club adorned with thunderbolt-marks, grasped a mountain in his arms, mimicking the form of Viṣṇu.

**6.57.31**  The greatly splendid and valiant Mahāpārśva, mace in hand, shone forth in battle like Kubera with his mace.

**6.57.32**  Those great-souled ones set forth, surrounded by matchless forces, like gods in Amarāvatī surrounded by matchless forces.

**6.57.33**  Those great-souled rākṣasas bearing the finest weapons followed them with elephants, horses, and chariots thundering like rain-clouds.

**6.57.34**  Those great-souled princes, of solar radiance, diademed, endowed with glory, blazed like brilliant planets in the sky.

**6.57.35**  The white row of parasols held aloft over them shone like a row of autumn clouds, like a line of swans in the sky.

**6.57.36**  Having resolved on death or on the defeat of enemies, those heroes set forth with their minds made up, eager for battle.

**6.57.37**  They roared, they shouted, they shot arrows, and those great-souled ones maddened by battle rejoiced as they marched out.

**6.57.38**  With the hissing, snapping, and thunderous sounds the earth seemed to quake; with the lion-roars of the rākṣasas the sky seemed to burst.

**6.57.39**  Those mighty lords of rākṣasas, joyful, sallying forth, beheld the monkey army with its rocks and trees raised aloft.

**6.57.40**  The great-souled monkeys too beheld the rākṣasa army — dense with elephants, horses, and chariots, resounding with hundreds of bells.

**6.57.41**  Resembling blue storm-clouds, with great weapons raised, surrounded on all sides by rākṣasas blazing like fire and sun.

**6.57.42**  Seeing that army coming, the monkeys, their goal attained, with great boulders raised aloft, shouted again and again.

**6.57.43**  Then hearing the loud clamor of the monkey herd-leaders, the hosts of rākṣasas, unable to endure the others' joy, those mighty ones cried out yet more fiercely.

**6.57.44**  The monkey herd-leaders penetrated that dreadful rākṣasa force and ranged about with boulders raised, like peaked mountains moving.

**6.57.45**  Some of the monkeys entered the sky, some ranged on the earth, moving furiously through the rākṣasa armies with trees and stones as weapons.

**6.57.46**  With trees, stones, and boulders they made an unsurpassed rain of missiles; and the monkeys of terrible valor, even as they were restrained by showers of arrows,

**6.57.47**  Both rākṣasas and monkeys uttered lion-roars in battle; the monkeys pulverized the rākṣasas with rocks.

**6.57.48**  Enraged in battle, they struck those clothed in armor and ornaments — some heroes in chariots, some riding elephants and horses as well.

**6.57.49**  The monkeys leapt up and swiftly smote the rākṣasas with drops of mountain peaks and with fists; their eyes rolling, the foremost rākṣasas staggered, fell, and roared there.

**6.57.50**  Then in a moment the ground was covered and flooded with blood by the rocks and swords hurled by monkeys and rākṣasas.

**6.57.51**  Hurled and being hurled by the enemy-crushing rākṣasas of mountain-like scattered bodies, and with their spears broken by the monkeys,

**6.57.52**  Those night-rovers slew monkeys thinking they were monkeys; those monkeys too slew rākṣasas thinking they were rākṣasas.

**6.57.53**  Hurling rocks at them, the rākṣasas slew the monkeys; and seizing the weapons from them, the monkeys slew the rākṣasas.

**6.57.54**  They slew one another with boulders, spears, and weapons, piercing each other mutually; the monkeys and rākṣasas uttered lion-roars in battle.

**6.57.55**  Rākṣasas slain by monkeys, their armor and body-guards cut away, oozed blood there like trees their finest sap.

**6.57.56**  Some monkeys in battle struck chariot against chariot, elephant against elephant, horse against horse.

**6.57.57**  The rākṣasas with razor-edged, crescent-shaped, and broad-headed sharp arrows cut down the trees and rocks of the monkey lords.

**6.57.58**  With scattered mountain peaks, trees severed in battle, and slain monkeys and rākṣasas, the earth became impassable.

**6.57.59**  As that tumultuous slaughter raged, with the monkey-faced ones rejoicing and the rākṣasas being laid low, the great sages and hosts of gods cried out.

**6.57.60**  Then Narāntaka, mounting a horse of wind-like speed and grasping a sharp lance, plunged into the army of the monkey king like a fish into a great ocean.

**6.57.61**  That hero, the great-souled enemy of Indra, alone with his blazing lance in a single moment pierced seven hundred monkeys and slaughtered the army of the bull-monkeys.

**6.57.62**  The Vidyādharas and great sages beheld that great-souled one mounted on his horse, ranging through the monkey army.

**6.57.63**  His path was seen as a mire of flesh and blood, covered with fallen monkeys of mountain-like size.

**6.57.64**  Whenever the bull-monkeys set their minds to counter-attack, Narāntaka, surpassing them, would pierce them before they could act.

**6.57.65**  Narāntaka, at the edge of the battle raising his flaming lance, scorched the monkey armies like fire scorching forests.

**6.57.66**  Whenever the forest-dwellers began to uproot trees and boulders, that soon they fell, struck by his lance, like mountains cleft by a thunderbolt.

**6.57.67**  The powerful Narāntaka ranged in all directions, crushing on every side in battle like the wind in the monsoon season.

**6.57.68**  The heroes could not flee, could not stand, much less stir; the valiant one pierced all — leaping, standing, and moving.

**6.57.69**  With a single lance like Death, of solar radiance, the monkey armies were cleft and fell to the earth.

**6.57.70**  The monkeys could not bear the descent of his lance, which resembled the crushing blow of the thunderbolt; they cried out with great sound.

**6.57.71**  The forms of the falling monkey heroes blazed forth like mountains with peaks split by the thunderbolt falling.

**6.57.72**  Those great-souled foremost monkeys who had previously been felled by Kumbhakarṇa, still unwell, attended upon Sugrīva.

**6.57.73**  Sugrīva, surveying all around, saw the monkey army terrified by Narāntaka and fleeing this way and that.

**6.57.74**  Seeing the army in flight, he beheld Narāntaka, lance in hand, coming mounted on his horse.

**6.57.75**  Then the greatly splendid Sugrīva, lord of the monkeys, spoke to the heroic prince Aṅgada, equal in valor to Śakra.

**6.57.76**  "Go, hero, to that rākṣasa there who is mounted on a horse, disturbing the monkey force — quickly deprive him of his life."

**6.57.77**  Hearing his master's words, Aṅgada leapt out then from among the cloud-like armies like the sun from behind a bank of clouds.

**6.57.78**  Aṅgada, foremost of the monkeys, resembling a mass of mountains, wearing his armlets, shone like a mountain with its minerals.

**6.57.79**  Weaponless, greatly splendid, armed only with his nails and teeth, the son of Vālin approached Narāntaka and spoke.

**6.57.80**  "Stand! What will you do with these ordinary monkeys? Hurl your lance on my chest — its touch is like the thunderbolt."

**6.57.81**  Hearing Aṅgada's words, Narāntaka became exceedingly enraged; he bit his lip with his teeth and hissed like a serpent.

**6.57.82**  He whirled his blazing lance and suddenly hurled it at Aṅgada; it struck the chest of the son of Vālin, became like a thunderbolt, shattered, and fell to the ground.

**6.57.83**  Seeing that lance shattered, resembling a serpent's coil cut by Suparṇa, the son of Vālin raised his palm and struck the crown of Narāntaka's horse.

**6.57.84**  His hooves buried in the ground, his eyeballs burst, his tongue hanging out, that horse, mountain-like, fell to the earth at the palm-blow, its skull scattered.

**6.57.85**  Narāntaka, overcome by rage, seeing his horse slain and fallen, raised his fist and the mighty one struck the son of Vālin on the head in battle.

**6.57.86**  Then Aṅgada, his head cleft by the fist, streamed intensely hot blood; he blazed repeatedly, fainted, and then regaining consciousness was astonished.

**6.57.87**  Then Aṅgada, the great-souled son of Vālin, clenching his fist equal in speed to the thunderbolt and like a mountain peak, drove it down upon the chest of Narāntaka.

**6.57.88**  His chest crushed and split by the fist, vomiting flame, his body smeared with blood, Narāntaka fell to the surface of the earth like a mountain broken by the descent of the thunderbolt.

**6.57.89**  Then in the sky there arose a great roar from the foremost of gods and from the forest-dwellers, as the foremost hero Narāntaka was slain in battle by the son of Vālin.

**6.57.90**  Then Aṅgada had accomplished that most difficult feat which was a delight to Rāma's heart; and he of surpassing valor was himself amazed at that act of valor, and once again became joyful in battle.

**6.58.1**  Seeing Narāntaka slain, the bull-chiefs among the Rākṣasas cried out — Devāntaka, Triśiras, and Mahodara, descendant of Pulastya.

**6.58.2**  Mahodara, mighty in valour, mounted upon an elephant lord resembling a thundercloud and charged at Aṅgada, son of Vālin, of great heroic power.

**6.58.3**  Devāntaka, the powerful one, scorched by grief at his kinsman's misfortune, took up a blazing iron club and rushed at Aṅgada.

**6.58.4**  Triśiras the hero, mounting a chariot bright as the sun and yoked with the finest horses, then advanced against the son of Vālin.

**6.58.5**  Assailed simultaneously by those three lord-Rākṣasas who crush the pride of the gods, Aṅgada uprooted a great tree with massive branches.

**6.58.6**  That hero Aṅgada hurled that enormous tree with its great boughs at Devāntaka swiftly, like Indra hurling a blazing thunderbolt.

**6.58.7**  Triśiras cut that tree to pieces with arrows like venomous serpents. Seeing the tree severed, Aṅgada then leapt up.

**6.58.8**  Then that bull among monkeys showered trees and rocks upon them; Triśiras, filled with rage, cut them all down with sharp arrows.

**6.58.9**  Devāntaka also shattered those trees with the tip of his club, and Triśiras charged at the hero Aṅgada with arrows.

**6.58.10**  Mahodara, mounted on his elephant, charged at the son of Vālin in rage and struck him on the chest with javelins like thunderbolts.

**6.58.11**  Devāntaka too, filled with fury, swiftly approached Aṅgada, struck him hard with his club, and then withdrew with speed.

**6.58.12**  Assailed simultaneously by those three foremost Rākṣasas, the great-spirited, illustrious son of Vālin did not waver.

**6.58.13**  Leaping up forcefully, he struck the great elephant with his palm; the beast's eyes fell out and it trumpeted in agony.

**6.58.14**  The mighty son of Vālin then wrenched out its tusk and, charging at Devāntaka, struck him in battle.

**6.58.15**  He, his entire body staggered like a tree shaken by the wind, poured blood from his mouth the colour of lac-juice.

**6.58.16**  Then the mighty Devāntaka, recovering with great difficulty from that blow, whirled his terrible club and struck Aṅgada.

**6.58.17**  Struck by the club, the son of the lord of monkeys fell to the ground on his knees, but sprang up again at once.

**6.58.18**  As he leapt up, Triśiras struck the son of the lord of monkeys on the forehead with three terrible arrows like venomous serpents.

**6.58.19**  Then Hanūmān and Nīla, perceiving that Aṅgada was being overwhelmed by those three bull-Rākṣasas, advanced to help.

**6.58.20**  Then Nīla hurled a mountain peak at Triśiras; but that wise son of Rāvaṇa shattered it with sharp arrows.

**6.58.21**  The peak of the mountain, pierced by hundreds of arrows, its stony surface split asunder, with sparks and flames, fell to the ground.

**6.58.22**  Then Devāntaka, seeing that gap in the attack, charged joyfully with his iron club at the son of the Wind in battle.

**6.58.23**  Leaping to meet his charge, Hanūmān son of the Wind struck him then on the crown of his head with a fist of thunderbolt force.

**6.58.24**  His skull crushed and shattered by that fist, his teeth, eyes, and tongue dangling out, Devāntaka, son of the king of Rākṣasas, fell to the earth lifeless at once.

**6.58.25**  When that foremost Rākṣasa warrior, mighty and a foe of the gods, was slain in battle, Triśiras in rage showered a fierce torrent of sharp-tipped arrows upon Nīla's chest.

**6.58.26**  Nīla, guardian of the monkey army, his body pierced and his limbs gone limp from those showers of arrows, was thus rendered inert by the might of that powerful one.

**6.58.27**  But then Nīla, regaining his senses, uprooted a mountain with its cluster of trees, and leaping up with fierce tremendous speed, struck Mahodara on the head with it.

**6.58.28**  Then Mahodara, shattered by the mountain's impact and pulverised together with his elephant, fell to the ground bereft of life, like a mountain struck by a thunderbolt.

**6.58.29**  Seeing his uncle slain, Triśiras took up his bow and, filled with fury, pierced Hanūmān with sharp arrows.

**6.58.30**  But Hanūmān, springing forward in wrath, tore apart Triśiras's horses with his claws, as a lion king tears an elephant lord.

**6.58.31**  Then Triśiras, son of Rāvaṇa, took up a śakti-spear like the Night of Cosmic Dissolution wielded by Death itself, and hurled it at the son of the Wind.

**6.58.32**  That śakti, hurled and still hurtling through the air like a meteor launched into the sky, was caught by that tiger among monkeys; he broke it and roared.

**6.58.33**  Seeing that fearsome śakti broken by Hanūmān, the monkey host exulted and shouted like thunderclouds.

**6.58.34**  Then Triśiras, foremost among Rākṣasas, raised his sword and in fury drove it into the chest of the lord of monkeys.

**6.58.35**  Struck by the sword blow, Hanūmān son of the Wind, still mighty, struck Triśiras on the chest with his open palm.

**6.58.36**  Struck by that palm blow, the great-spirited Triśiras fell to the ground, his arms hanging limp, his clothing dishevelled, his senses departed.

**6.58.37**  That great monkey, mountain-like, snatched the sword from the fallen one and roared, terrifying all the Rākṣasas.

**6.58.38**  Unable to endure that roar, the night-ranger leapt up and, springing at Hanūmān, struck him with his fist.

**6.58.39**  At that fist-blow the great monkey blazed with rage, and in his fury seized that bull among Rākṣasas by his crown.

**6.58.40**  With a sharp sword the son of Anila, enraged, cut off those heads adorned with crowns and earrings, just as Indra once cut off the heads of the son of Tvaṣṭṛ.

**6.58.41**  Those heads of Indra's foe fell to the earth — their elongated eyes like mountains, their pupils blazing like Vaiśvānara — like celestial lights released from the sun's path.

**6.58.42**  When that three-headed foe of the gods was slain by Hanūmān with the might of Indra, the monkeys shouted, the earth trembled, and the Rākṣasas fled on all sides.

**6.58.43**  Seeing Triśiras slain, and likewise Mahodara, and beholding the slaughter of Devāntaka and Narāntaka — those two who were hard to assail —

**6.58.44**  the supremely wrathful Mahāpārśva, mighty in strength, blazed with anger and seized an all-iron mace, gleaming with flame.

**6.58.45**  It was encased in bands of gold, smeared with flesh and blood, resplendent in its form, dyed with the blood of enemies.

**6.58.46**  Its tip ablaze with energy, adorned with garlands of red flowers, inspiring terror in Airāvata, Mahāpadma, and Sārvabhauma alike.

**6.58.47**  Seizing that mace and blazing with fury, the mighty Mahāpārśva rushed at the monkeys, blazing like the fire at the end of an age.

**6.58.48**  Then that monkey Ṛṣabha, son of Varuṇa, leapt up and, approaching Rāvaṇa's brother Mahāpārśva, stood before him powerfully.

**6.58.49**  Seeing that mountain-like monkey standing before him, Mahāpārśva, enraged, struck him on the chest with his mace equal to a thunderbolt.

**6.58.50**  Struck by that mace, the bull among monkeys, his chest cloven and his body shaken, poured forth a great flow of blood.

**6.58.51**  Ṛṣabha, that bull among monkeys, regaining his senses after some time, gazed at Mahāpārśva in fury with quivering lips.

**6.58.52**  Seizing that terrible mace and whirling it again and again, he struck the arrogantly arrayed Mahāpārśva at the front of the battle.

**6.58.53**  Mahāpārśva, cleaved by his own mace, his teeth and eyes scattered, fell like a mountain struck by a thunderbolt.

**6.58.54**  When that brother of Rāvaṇa was slain, that ocean-like Rākṣasa host, casting aside its weapons, fleeing with life as its only goal, scattered like a sea whose shores had been broken.

**6.59.1**  Seeing his own army in chaos and torment, hair-raising to behold, and seeing his brothers slain — those whose valour matched Indra's —

**6.59.2**  and beholding both his uncles cut down in battle — Mahodara and Mahāpārśva, those two bull-Rākṣasa brothers —

**6.59.3**  the great-spirited Atikāya, who had received boons from Brahmā, mountain-like in form, crusher of the pride of gods and demons, blazed with fury in battle.

**6.59.4**  Mounting his chariot blazing like a thousand suns together, Indra's foe rushed at the monkeys.

**6.59.5**  Wearing his crown and gleaming earrings, he drew his great bow to its full span, proclaimed his name aloud, and roared with a mighty sound.

**6.59.6**  With that lion's roar, with the proclamation of his name, and with the fearsome twang of his bowstring, he struck terror into the monkeys.

**6.59.7**  Seeing his form, just as they might Viṣṇu in his three-stride stride, all the monkeys, seized by fear, scattered in the ten directions.

**6.59.8**  Those monkeys, their minds bewildered at the sight of Atikāya in battle, fled for refuge to the one worthy of refuge — the elder brother of Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.59.9**  Then Kākutstha (Rāma) beheld from afar Atikāya mounted in his chariot, mountain-like, bearing his bow, roaring like a storm cloud of Doomsday.

**6.59.10**  Seeing that great being, Rāghava was filled with wonder. Having calmed the monkeys, he addressed Vibhīṣaṇa.

**6.59.11**  "Who is that one, mountain-like, bearing a bow, with tawny eyes, standing in a vast chariot yoked with a thousand horses?

**6.59.12**  He who, surrounded by sharp spears, keen lances, and flaming javelins, shines like Maheśvara surrounded by supernatural beings —

**6.59.13**  who blazes forth, enveloped by chariot-spears gleaming like the tongues of Kāla, like a thundercloud enveloped by lightning?

**6.59.14**  His strung bows with golden backs adorn his excellent chariot on all sides, like Indra's bow adorns the sky.

**6.59.15**  Who is this tiger among Rākṣasas who blazes upon the field of battle, advancing as the foremost of chariot warriors, with a chariot of solar splendour?

**6.59.16**  Rāhu set upon the peak of his standard blazes forth; with arrows bright as the rays of the sun he illumines the ten directions.

**6.59.17**  His bow, triply curved, its sound like the rumble of a thundercloud, its back adorned with gold, resplendent like the bow of Śatakratu (Indra), shines forth.

**6.59.18**  His great chariot bears a standard and pennant, has a dragging-pole, is yoked with four horses, and gives forth a sound like the roar of a thundercloud.

**6.59.19**  Thirty-eight quivers are ranged in the chariot; terrible bows and bowstrings tawny-gold are mounted there.

**6.59.20**  Two swords in the chariot, placed at his flanks, adorning each side, fitted with hilts of four handbreadths, clearly ten handbreadths long.

**6.59.21**  Composed and resolute, wearing a crimson neck-cord, resembling a great mountain, like Kāla of mighty jaws — like the sun dwelling within a thundercloud.

**6.59.22**  He is resplendent with his arms bound with golden armlets, like the great mountain Himavān with its two towering peaks.

**6.59.23**  His face, with auspicious eyes and earrings, shines like the full disc of the moon stationed between the twin stars Punarvasu.

**6.59.24**  O mighty-armed one, tell me who is this foremost of Rākṣasas — at whose sight all the monkeys, seized by fear, have fled in all directions?"

**6.59.25**  Thus questioned by the royal prince Rāma of boundless splendour, the great-spirited Vibhīṣaṇa described him to Rāghava.

**6.59.26**  "The ten-necked one, great in splendour, king, younger brother of Vaiśravaṇa, terrible in his deeds, of enormous enterprise — Rāvaṇa, lord of Rākṣasas —

**6.59.27**  had a mighty son, equal to Rāvaṇa in battle, respectful of elders, a retainer of sacred learning, foremost among all who know the science of weapons —

**6.59.28**  honoured in the arts of horsemanship, chariot, elephant, sword, archery, in dissension, conciliation, giving, statecraft, and counsel —

**6.59.29**  relying on whose arm Laṅkā becomes fearless. This is Atikāya, known as the son of Dhānyamālinī.

**6.59.30**  By him Brahmā was propitiated through austerity and a disciplined self; weapons too were obtained and enemies were vanquished.

**6.59.31**  The Self-born (Brahmā) granted him invulnerability before gods and demons; this divine armour and this chariot blazing like the sun were also given by him.

**6.59.32**  By him hundreds of gods and demons have been defeated; Rākṣasas have been protected, Yakṣas have been slaughtered.

**6.59.33**  By him the thunderbolt of the wise Indra was checked with arrows, and the noose of the lord of waters was repelled in battle.

**6.59.34**  This is that powerful Atikāya, foremost bull among Rākṣasas, the wise son of Rāvaṇa, crusher of the pride of gods and demons.

**6.59.35**  Therefore, O bull among men, let effort be made against him quickly, before he brings the monkey armies to destruction with his arrows."

**6.59.36**  Then powerful Atikāya, entering the monkey army, drew his bow taut and roared again and again.

**6.59.37**  Seeing him of fearsome form standing in his chariot, the foremost of charioteers, those great-spirited monkeys who were the foremost leaders rushed against him.

**6.59.38**  Kumuda, Dvivida, Mainda, Nīla, and Śarabha charged together simultaneously with trees and mountain peaks.

**6.59.39**  Those trees and mountains of theirs Atikāya, the great-spirited, foremost among those versed in weapons, cut to pieces with gold-ornamented arrows.

**6.59.40**  And those monkeys themselves, the mighty night-ranger of fearsome form, pierced with all-iron arrows as they advanced before him in battle.

**6.59.41**  Those monkeys, tormented by the shower of arrows, their bodies pierced, could not oppose Atikāya in the great battle.

**6.59.42**  That Rākṣasa struck terror into the army of monkey heroes, as a lion in the prime of youth strikes terror into a herd of deer.

**6.59.43**  That lord of Rākṣasas, having not struck down anyone who stood unarmed in the monkey host, approached Rāma with bow and quiver and spoke this proud challenge.

**6.59.44**  "I stand in my chariot, arrows and bow in hand — I do not fight with ordinary men. Let him who has the strength and determination to give me swift battle today step forward now."

**6.59.45**  Hearing those words as he spoke them, Saumitri, slayer of foes, blazed with anger; refusing to endure it, he sprang forward, and then smiling, took up his bow.

**6.59.46**  Enraged, Saumitri leapt forward, drew an arrow from his quiver, and drew his great bow in front of Atikāya.

**6.59.47**  The fierce twang of Lakṣmaṇa's bowstring filled the earth, the mountains, the sky, the ocean, and all the directions, striking terror into the night-rangers.

**6.59.48**  Hearing the fearsome crack of Saumitri's bow then, the mighty and powerful son of the lord of Rākṣasas was struck with wonder.

**6.59.49**  Then Atikāya, angered at seeing Lakṣmaṇa step forward, took up a sharp arrow and spoke these words.

**6.59.50**  "You are a boy, O Saumitri, unskilled in feats of valour — go away. Why do you wish to fight me, who am like Death itself?

**6.59.51**  Even Himavān cannot bear the speed of the weapons discharged from my arms, nor the sky, nor the earth.

**6.59.52**  You wish to rouse the fire of Doomsday from its easy sleep. Lay down your bow and turn back — do not lose your life by coming against me.

**6.59.53**  Or if you are so resolute you refuse to turn back — then stand, and abandoning your life, you shall go to the realm of Yama.

**6.59.54**  Behold my sharp arrows, crushers of the enemy's pride, resembling the weapons of the Lord, adorned with heated gold.

**6.59.55**  This arrow of mine, like a serpent, shall drink your blood, as an enraged lion-king drinks the blood of the lord of elephants."

**6.59.56**  Hearing Atikāya's words, spoken in anger and pride in battle, that royal prince, the supremely powerful and greatly glorious one, blazed with fury and then spoke words of great import.

**6.59.57**  "One does not become eminent by words alone — good men are not made by boasting. With me standing here, bow in hand with arrow ready, show your own strength, O evil one!

**6.59.58**  Demonstrate yourself through deeds — you should not merely brag. He who is endowed with manliness is remembered as a hero.

**6.59.59**  Fully armed, a bowman, mounted in your chariot — show your prowess, whether with arrows or with supernatural weapons.

**6.59.60**  Then I shall cut off your head with sharp arrows, as the wind fells a ripe palm-fruit from its stalk.

**6.59.61**  Today my arrows, adorned with heated gold, shall drink blood gushing from your body among the arrow-tips.

**6.59.62**  Knowing me to be a boy, you should not despise me. Whether boy or elder, know death in this battle."

**6.59.63**  Hearing Lakṣmaṇa's words, reasoned and of supreme import, Atikāya blazed with fury and took up a supreme arrow.

**6.59.64**  Then Vidyādharas, Bhūtas, gods, Daityas, great seers, and great-spirited Guhyakas all watched that battle.

**6.59.65**  Then Atikāya, enraged, strung an arrow to his bow and shot it at Lakṣmaṇa, seeming to compress the very sky.

**6.59.66**  That sharp arrow like a venomous serpent as it came flying — Lakṣmaṇa, slayer of enemy heroes, cut it with a crescent-headed arrow.

**6.59.67**  Seeing that arrow cut down like a serpent whose hood had been severed, Atikāya, greatly enraged, took up five arrows.

**6.59.68**  Those arrows the night-ranger shot at Lakṣmaṇa; before they could arrive, the younger brother of Bharata cut them down with sharp arrows.

**6.59.69**  Having cut those down with sharp arrows, Lakṣmaṇa, slayer of enemy heroes, took up a sharp arrow blazing as if with energy.

**6.59.70**  Taking it and fitting it to his excellent bow, Lakṣmaṇa drew it swiftly and released the arrow.

**6.59.71**  With that arrow released at full draw, its joints smooth, that valorous one struck the foremost of Rākṣasas on the forehead.

**6.59.72**  The arrow, sunk into the forehead of that fearsome Rākṣasa, was seen red with blood in battle, like the lord of serpents.

**6.59.73**  The Rākṣasa shook, struck by Lakṣmaṇa's arrows, as the terrible gate-tower of Tripura shook when struck by Rudra's arrow.

**6.59.74**  Recovering himself, the mighty one reflected and thought: "By this fall of arrows you are truly a foe worthy of being slain tomorrow."

**6.59.75**  Having considered thus, he bent his head, bowed both his arms, and taking his stance on the chariot-platform, maneuvered with his chariot.

**6.59.76**  One, three, five, seven — arrows the bull among Rākṣasas took up, fitted, drew to the full, and discharged.

**6.59.77**  Those arrows, like Death himself, discharged from the bow of the lord of Rākṣasas, with golden fletching, bright as the sun, made the sky blaze as if on fire.

**6.59.78**  Then Rāvaṇa's younger brother (Lakṣmaṇa), unperturbed, cut down those streams of arrows released by the Rākṣasa with many sharp arrows of his own.

**6.59.79**  Seeing those arrows cut down in battle, the son of Rāvaṇa, foe of the lord of the thirty gods, blazed with fury and seized a sharp arrow.

**6.59.80**  The great-spirited one, fitting that arrow, suddenly released it; he struck Saumitri then in the chest as he came forward.

**6.59.81**  Struck by Atikāya in battle on the chest, Saumitri poured forth copious blood, like a rutting elephant in rut.

**6.59.82**  That mighty one instantly extracted the arrow from himself, took up a keen arrow, and charged it with a supernatural weapon.

**6.59.83**  He charged that arrow with the Āgneya weapon of fire; the arrow then blazed, and so did the bow of that great-spirited one.

**6.59.84**  The supremely brilliant Atikāya charged the Saura weapon of the sun, and with it fitted an arrow like a serpent with golden fletching.

**6.59.85**  Then Lakṣmaṇa hurled that blazing, terrible charged arrow at Atikāya, like Death hurling his rod of Kāla.

**6.59.86**  Seeing the arrow charged with the Āgneya weapon coming at him, the night-ranger discharged his own blazing arrow charged with the Saura solar weapon.

**6.59.87**  Those two arrows in the sky struck each other, their tips blazing with energy, like two enraged serpents.

**6.59.88**  Having consumed each other mutually, those two supreme arrows fell to the surface of the earth, reduced to ash, without flame, their glory extinguished.

**6.59.89**  Then Atikāya, greatly enraged, discharged the Aiṣīka reed-weapon; the valorous Saumitri cut it down with the Aindra weapon of Indra.

**6.59.90**  Seeing the Aiṣīka weapon destroyed, that young son of Rāvaṇa, greatly enraged, charged an arrow with the Yāmya weapon of Yama.

**6.59.91**  The night-ranger then hurled that weapon at Lakṣmaṇa; but Lakṣmaṇa destroyed that weapon with the Vāyavya weapon of Vāyu.

**6.59.92**  Then enraged Lakṣmaṇa showered the son of Rāvaṇa with streams of arrows like a thundercloud showering streams of rain.

**6.59.93**  Those arrows, reaching Atikāya's armour adorned with diamond-hard metal, instantly fell to the ground with their tips and shafts broken.

**6.59.94**  Seeing those arrows made fruitless, Lakṣmaṇa the slayer of enemy heroes, of great fame, showered him with thousands of arrows.

**6.59.95**  Even drenched with streams of arrows, Atikāya the mighty Rākṣasa, shielded by his impenetrable armour, did not even flinch in battle.

**6.59.96**  That foremost of men could not inflict a wound upon him in battle. Then the Wind-god himself approached and spoke to him.

**6.59.97**  "He has received a boon from Brahmā and is protected by impenetrable armour. Pierce him with the Brahmā weapon — he can be slain by no other means."

**6.59.98**  Then Saumitri, whose valour matches Indra's, hearing the words of Vāyu, instantly took up an arrow of unfailing speed and charged it at once with that Brahmā weapon.

**6.59.99**  As that supreme weapon was being charged by Saumitri onto his best arrow with sharp tip, the directions with moon, sun, and great planets, the sky itself, trembled and roared, and the earth shook.

**6.59.100**  Lakṣmaṇa charged that well-fletched arrow, the equal of Yama's messenger, with Brahmā's weapon on his bow and discharged it in battle at the son of Indra's foe — an arrow like a thunderbolt.

**6.59.101**  That arrow discharged by Lakṣmaṇa, of unfailing speed, as it came flying, bright as fire, with fine golden and diamond-decorated fletching — Atikāya then beheld it in battle.

**6.59.102**  Even as Atikāya was watching it, he instantly struck it with many sharp arrows; yet that arrow, swift as Garuḍa, came to his side with yet greater speed.

**6.59.103**  Seeing that arrow come — like blazing Death and Kāla combined — Atikāya struck at it with spears, iron darts, maces, axes, pikes, and ploughs, his efforts unimpeded.

**6.59.104**  Making all those weapons of wondrous form futile, that arrow blazing with fire forcefully took off Atikāya's head adorned with its crown then and there.

**6.59.105**  That head together with its helmet-armour, driven by Lakṣmaṇa's arrow, fell suddenly to the earth like a peak of Himavān.

**6.59.106**  Then many monkeys, filled with great joy, their faces like blossomed lotuses, honoured Lakṣmaṇa, the fortunate one, upon the slaying of that fearsome, formidable foe.

**6.60.1**  Then the surviving Rākṣasa bands there, those who remained after the slaughter, swiftly reported to Rāvaṇa the killing of those bull-Rākṣasas — Devāntaka, Triśiras, Atikāya, and the rest.

**6.60.2**  Then the king, hearing at once of those who were slain, fell into a swoon, his eyes flooded with tears; pondering the destruction of his sons and the terrible killing of his brothers, the king brooded deeply.

**6.60.3**  Then seeing the king so wretched, tossed in the ocean of grief, that bull among the sons of the king of Rākṣasas — Indrajit — spoke these words.

**6.60.4**  "O father, you should not fall into bewilderment while Indrajit yet lives, O lord of Rākṣasas — no one struck by the arrows of Indra's foe is capable of preserving his life in battle.

**6.60.5**  See today Rāma together with Lakṣmaṇa, their bodies pierced and shattered by my arrows, lying on the ground deprived of life, their every limb covered with sharp arrows.

**6.60.6**  Hear this vow of Indra's foe, firmly resolved and joined with manly power and destiny: this very day I shall torment Rāma together with Lakṣmaṇa with unfailing arrows.

**6.60.7**  Today Indra, Vivasvat's son (Yama), Viṣṇu, Mitra, the Sādhyas, the Aśvins, Vaiśvānara, Moon, and Sun shall see my immeasurable valour, fierce as Viṣṇu's at the sacrificial grounds of Bali."

**6.60.8**  Having spoken thus, Indra's foe, undaunted in spirit, took leave of the king and mounted his chariot swift as the wind, equipped with the finest mules.

**6.60.9**  The great-spirited one, mounting his chariot rivalling Hari's own, swiftly went to where the battle was, O subduer of foes.

**6.60.10**  Many mighty warriors, exulting, followed that great-souled one as he set forth, their hands holding excellent bows.

**6.60.11**  Some were mounted on the backs of elephants, some on the finest horses, bearing lances, hammers, swords, axes, and maces.

**6.60.12**  With fearsome blasts of conch-shells and the great roar of kettledrums, Indra's foe advanced, praised by the night-rangers.

**6.60.13**  That slayer of foes blazed resplendent beneath a parasol white as the conch or the moon, like the sky with the full moon.

**6.60.14**  Then that hero, foremost among all bowmen, was fanned with golden fly-whisks adorned with gold, with beautiful tufts of yak-tail.

**6.60.15**  Then Laṅkā with Indrajit, whose energy rivals the sun, whose valour is unmatched, blazed resplendent as the sky with the shining sun.

**6.60.16**  Seeing him going forth surrounded by a great army, the glorious lord of Rākṣasas Rāvaṇa spoke to his son.

**6.60.17**  "You are a warrior without a match in chariot-battle, my son; you have conquered Vāsava (Indra) in battle. Why would you not slay the audacious human Rāghava?"

**6.60.18**  Thus addressed by the lord of Rākṣasas, the hero received his great blessings, and going swiftly with his horse-yoked chariot to Nikumbhilā —

**6.60.19**  that great-spirited subduer of foes, having reached the sacred battle-ground, stationed Rākṣasas all around the chariot as guard.

**6.60.20**  Then that foremost of Rākṣasas, whose lustre equals fire, invoked the consumer of oblations — fire — with sacred mantras and proper ritual.

**6.60.21**  That mighty lord of Rākṣasas, with the rites of a network of oblations, preceded by garlands and fragrant substances, poured libations into the fire there.

**6.60.22**  Weapons, arrow-feathers, and kindling-sticks of vibhītaka wood, garments dyed red, and a ladle of black iron were offered.

**6.60.23**  Having spread the fire there with arrow-feathers and javelins, he grasped the throat of an all-black living goat.

**6.60.24**  Even as the fire was kindled just once, smokeless and blazing high with great flames, those omens appeared which foretold victory.

**6.60.25**  With its flames turning clockwise, resembling heated gold, fire itself rose up and accepted those oblations.

**6.60.26**  He, foremost among those versed in weapons, summoned the Brahmā weapon and consecrated his bow, his chariot, and all his armaments with sacred mantras there.

**6.60.27**  As that weapon was being invoked and as the fire was being fed with oblations, the sky together with sun, planets, moon, and stars trembled with fear.

**6.60.28**  Having made oblation into the fire, he whose energy blazes like fire, whose power rivals great Indra's, bearing bow, arrows, sword, chariot, horses, and charioteer, concealed himself in the sky — he of inconceivable form.

**6.60.29**  Leaving his army behind and swiftly entering the monkey armies in the great battle while remaining invisible, he rained down a fierce web of arrows, as a dark rain-cloud rains water.

**6.60.30**  Those monkeys, mountain-like, their bodies shattered by the arrows of Indra's conqueror, struck down by māyā, crying out in discordant voices, fell in battle like great elephants struck by Indra's thunderbolt.

**6.60.31**  They saw only the sharp-tipped arrows in the monkey armies in battle; they could not see the foe of the lord of gods, hidden by māyā — they did not perceive that Rākṣasa there at all.

**6.60.32**  Then that great-souled lord of Rākṣasas covered all the directions with swarms of sharp-tipped arrows bright as the sun and filled the lords of monkeys with despair.

**6.60.33**  He whirled and rained down fiercely upon the army of monkey lords — spears, swords, and battle-axes resembling blazing fire, blazing with flames and sparks.

**6.60.34**  Then the leaders of the monkey troops, struck by the arrows of Indra's conqueror, blazing like fire, bloomed like kimśuka flowers in full bloom.

**6.60.35**  Crawling toward one another and crying out in discordant voices, bull-monkeys pierced by the weapons of the lord of Rākṣasas fell down.

**6.60.36**  Some gazing up at the sky were struck in the eyes; pierced by arrows they collapsed into one another and fell to the ground.

**6.60.37**  Hanūmān, Sugrīva, Aṅgada, Gandhamādana, Jāmbavān, Suṣeṇa, and Vegadarśin —

**6.60.38**  Mainda, Dvivida, Nīla, Gavākṣa, Gaja, Gomukha, Keśarin, Hariloman, and the monkey Vidyuddaṃṣṭra —

**6.60.39**  Sūryānana, Jyotimukha, Dadhimukha the monkey, Pāvakākṣa, Nala, and the monkey Kumuda —

**6.60.40**  — all these tiger-monkeys that foremost of Rākṣasas pierced with lances, pikes, and sharp arrows charged with Indrajit's mantras.

**6.60.41**  Having shattered the monkey troop-leaders with maces and pierced them with gold-fletched arrows, he rained webs of arrow-showers upon Rāma together with Lakṣmaṇa — arrows like the rays of the sun.

**6.60.42**  Drenched in those showers of arrows, reflecting on them as if they were streams of falling rain, Rāma of supremely wondrous glory, surveying all around him, then spoke to Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.60.43**  "This lord of Rākṣasas again, O Lakṣmaṇa — Indra's foe — having felled the fierce monkey army, relying on the Brahmā weapon, torments us continually with his arrows.

**6.60.44**  He is great-souled, bearing a boon from the Self-born, stationed in the sky with his fearsome body concealed — how then can Indrajit, invisible in form and weapons raised, be killed today in battle?

**6.60.45**  I think the blessed Self-born, who is inconceivable, is the source of this weapon and of him who wields it. You, O wise one, endure here with me today without anxiety the falls of his arrows.

**6.60.46**  For this lord of Rākṣasas covers all the directions with webs of arrow-showers; and this entire army of the monkey king no longer shines, its foremost heroes having fallen.

**6.60.47**  And seeing the two of us fallen and senseless, withdrawn from battle, our fury and joy extinguished, he will surely enter the abode of the enemies of the immortals, having taken away the glory of the battle's vanguard.

**6.60.48**  Then those two were there struck down and rendered helpless by Indrajit's web of weapons; and having brought them to despair there, the lord of Rākṣasas roared with joy in battle.

**6.60.49**  Having thus brought the army of the monkey king and Rāma together with Lakṣmaṇa to despair in battle, he swiftly entered the city protected by the arms of the ten-necked one.

**6.61.1**  When those two were struck down at the forefront of battle, the army of the monkey-chieftains fell into confusion; Sugrīva, Nīla, Aṅgada, and Jāmbavān — none of them could act in any way.

**6.61.2**  Then Vibhīṣaṇa, foremost among the wise, seeing the army in despair, spoke to the heroic monkey-chieftains, comforting them with incomparable words.

**6.61.3**  "Do not be afraid; this is not a time for despair even though the two noble princes lie helpless and afflicted — it is because they are honoring the word of the Self-Born One that they have been struck down by Indrajit's web of weapons."

**6.61.4**  "This supreme Brahmā weapon of unfailing force was granted by the Self-Born; if the two princes have been laid low while honoring it, what cause is there for despair here?"

**6.61.5**  Then the wise Māruti, having honored the Brahmā weapon, and having heard the words of Vibhīṣaṇa, spoke to him.

**6.61.6**  "Now that this army of swift monkeys has been struck down, let us go and reassure each one who still holds to life."

**6.61.7**  Then those two heroes together, Hanūmān and the foremost of rākṣasas, bearing torches, roamed over the front of the battlefield that night.

**6.61.8**  Strewn all about lay bodies with severed tails, hands, thighs, feet, fingers, and heads, with limbs streaming blood, dripping on every side —

**6.61.9**  — they beheld the earth crowded with fallen monkeys huge as mountains and with fallen blazing weapons.

**6.61.10**  Sugrīva, Aṅgada, Nīla, Śarabha, Gandhamādana, Jāmbavān, Suṣeṇa, Vegadarśī, and Āhuka —

**6.61.11**  — Mainda, Nala, Jyotimukha, Dvivida, Panasa, and the rest — Vibhīṣaṇa and Hanūmān saw them lying struck down in battle.

**6.61.12**  Sixty-seven crores of swift monkeys had been slain within the remaining fifth part of the day by the weapon dear to the Self-Born.

**6.61.13**  Seeing that dreadful army, vast as an ocean flood, tormented by arrows, Hanūmān together with Vibhīṣaṇa searched for Jāmbavān.

**6.61.14**  They found the aged one, afflicted by natural old age, pierced by hundreds of arrows, the heroic son of Prajāpati, like a fire dying down.

**6.61.15**  Seeing him, the descendant of Pulastya approached and spoke: "O noble one, have your vital breaths not been destroyed by these sharp arrows?"

**6.61.16**  Hearing Vibhīṣaṇa's words, Jāmbavān, the foremost of bears, with great difficulty drew out a breath and spoke these words:

**6.61.17**  "By your voice — the voice of a great hero, lord of rākṣasas — I recognize you; tormented as I am by sharp arrows, I cannot see you with my eyes."

**6.61.18**  "Tell me, O lord of rākṣasas — is Añjanā's noble son, through whom Mātariśvan lives again, that best of monkeys, Hanūmān, alive somewhere?"

**6.61.19**  Hearing Jāmbavān's words, Vibhīṣaṇa said: "Passing over the two noble princes, why do you ask after the son of the Wind?"

**6.61.20**  "Not toward king Sugrīva, not toward Aṅgada, not even toward Rāghava, O noble one, is love displayed as greatly as toward the son of the Wind."

**6.61.21**  Hearing Vibhīṣaṇa's words, Jāmbavān replied: "Hear, O tiger among rākṣasas, why I ask after Māruti —"

**6.61.22**  "While that hero lives, even an army slain is as if unslain; but if Hanūmān has abandoned his life, then though we live, we are as the dead."

**6.61.23**  "If Māruti, who is like the Wind itself, equal to fire in valor, still lives, dear friend, then there is hope of life."

**6.61.24**  Then the son of the Wind, Hanūmān, came to the aged one and paid him due reverence, grasping the feet of Jāmbavān.

**6.61.25**  Hearing Hanūmān's voice, though his senses were afflicted, the foremost of bears felt himself reborn as it were.

**6.61.26**  Then the greatly resplendent Jāmbavān said to Hanūmān: "Come, O tiger among monkeys — you must save the vānaras."

**6.61.27**  "No other is equal to the task in prowess; you are their supreme protector. This is the moment for your valor — I see no one else."

**6.61.28**  "Gladden the troops of bear and monkey heroes; and remove the arrows from these two who have been struck — Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa."

**6.61.29**  "Travelling a great distance, high above the ocean, O Hanūmān, you must go to the Himālaya, the foremost of mountains."

**6.61.30**  "Then, O destroyer of foes, you will see the supremely towering golden Ṛṣabha mountain, and also the peak of Kailāsa."

**6.61.31**  "Between those two peaks, blazing with incomparable splendor, O hero, you will see the Herb Mountain, replete with all medicinal plants."

**6.61.32**  "O tiger among monkeys, on its summit you will see four blazing herbs that illuminate all ten directions."

**6.61.33**  "There is Mṛtasaṃjīvanī that revives the dead, Viśalyakaraṇī that removes arrows, Sauvarṇakaraṇī that restores golden color, and Saṃdhānī the great healing herb."

**6.61.34**  "Gathering all of them, you must come quickly, O Hanūmān, son of the Wind-god; reuniting the monkeys with their lives, revive them."

**6.61.35**  Hearing Jāmbavān's words, Hanūmān, the bull among monkeys, swelled with an upsurge of strength like an ocean swelling with rushing waters.

**6.61.36**  Standing at the mountain's edge and pressing down upon that great mountain, the hero Hanūmān appeared himself like a second mountain.

**6.61.37**  That mountain, shattered by the monkey's feet, sank down; it could not withstand itself under such tremendous pressure.

**6.61.38**  The trees fell to the ground and burst into flame from the monkey's force; its peaks were scattered as Hanūmān pressed upon it.

**6.61.39**  As the great mountain was being pressed down, its floor of trees and rocks shattered, the monkeys could not stand upon it as it whirled about.

**6.61.40**  Laṅkā with its great gates whirling, its houses and gateways shattered, filled with terror in the night, seemed as though it were dancing.

**6.61.41**  Resembling a mountain himself, the son of Māruta, pressing down on the mountain-bearer, caused the earth together with its oceans to shake.

**6.61.42**  Pressing upon the mountain with his feet and opening his terrible mouth like the submarine fire, he roared mightily as if to terrify the rākṣasas.

**6.61.43**  Hearing that wondrous roar as he bellowed, all the rākṣasas dwelling in Laṅkā could not stir from fear.

**6.61.44**  Then Māruti of terrible might, the scorcher of foes, having paid homage to Rāma, set himself to the supreme task for Rāghava's sake.

**6.61.45**  Raising his tail like a great serpent, bending his back, contracting his ears, opening his mouth like the submarine fire, he of fierce speed leaped into the sky.

**6.61.46**  He swiftly swept away groves of trees, mountains, and rocks; and ordinary monkeys, struck and sent flying by the force of his arms and thighs, fell spent into the water.

**6.61.47**  Stretching out his two arms like the coils of a great serpent, valiant as the enemy of serpents, the son of the Wind went toward Meru, king of mountains, seemingly drawing the directions behind him.

**6.61.48**  Gazing upon the ocean with its tossing rows of waves, its every creature thrown into agitation, he went suddenly like the discus released from the tip of Viṣṇu's hand.

**6.61.49**  Beholding mountains, forests, lakes, rivers, reservoirs, the finest cities, and even prosperous tracts of collyrium, he sped onward with speed equal to his father's.

**6.61.50**  Taking the path of the sun, he went without fatigue; that foremost of monkeys beheld the Himālaya, highest of mountains.

**6.61.51**  It was adorned with many springs, filled with numerous gorges and cascades, with peaks resembling heaps of white clouds and fair to behold.

**6.61.52**  Approaching that lord of great mountains with its very lofty, foremost, and terrible peaks, he saw the holy great hermitages frequented by the foremost hosts of gods and seers.

**6.61.53**  He saw Brahmā's treasury, the Silver Abode, Indra's dwelling, the spot where Rudra released his arrow, the horse-faced Hayaśīrṣa, the blazing Brahmaśiras, and the attendants of Vaivasvata Yama.

**6.61.54**  He saw the abode of Vajra, the dwelling of Vaiśravaṇa bright as the sun, the tethering place of the sun, the seat of Brahmā, the bow of Śaṃkara, and the navel of the earth.

**6.61.55**  He saw the foremost Kailāsa, the Himālayan rock, the Ṛṣabha mountain foremost of golden peaks, blazing with every herb ablaze, the king of the Herb Mountain.

**6.61.56**  Beholding it blazing with rays like fire, the son of Indra's messenger was filled with wonder; leaping upon that king of herb mountains, he set about gathering the herbs there.

**6.61.57**  That great monkey, the son of Māruta, having crossed thousands of yojanas, roamed over the mountain bearing divine herbs.

**6.61.58**  But all those great herbs on that best of mountains, knowing that a seeker was approaching, then became invisible.

**6.61.59**  The great-souled Hanūmān, not seeing them, became enraged; seething with anger, he roared mightily; with eyes blazing like fire, unable to endure it, he spoke to that lord of mountains:

**6.61.60**  "What is this that you have so firmly resolved — that you show no compassion for Rāghava? Behold yourself now scattered asunder, overcome by the strength of my arms, O lord of mountains!"

**6.61.61**  Seizing at once with speed its peak with all its trees, elephants, gold, and thousands of minerals — its summit scattered, its upper ridges shaking — he uprooted it.

**6.61.62**  Having uprooted it, he leaped into the sky, terrifying the worlds along with the gods and lords of gods; praised by many sky-dwellers, he went with the fierce power of Garuḍa.

**6.61.63**  Entering the path of the sun, holding that peak bright as the sun, he shone then, himself like the sun, near the sun, reflecting back the sun's brightness.

**6.61.64**  The son of the Wind-god, like a mountain himself, shone magnificently with that mountain, like Viṣṇu in the sky holding aloft his discus with its thousand edges blazing with fire.

**6.61.65**  The monkeys, seeing him, cried out; he too, seeing them, roared with joy. Hearing their mingled cries, those dwelling in Laṅkā cried out even more terribly.

**6.61.66**  Then the great-souled one descended with that foremost mountain into the midst of the monkey army, bowing his head to the foremost monkeys, and there he embraced Vibhīṣaṇa.

**6.61.67**  Those two also, the princes born as men, inhaling the fragrance of the great herbs, became free of their arrow-wounds; and other heroic monkeys rose up.

**6.61.68**  Then Hari, the son of the Wind-god, of exalted valor, swiftly carried that Herb Mountain back to the Himālaya itself, and returned again to Rāma.

**6.62.1**  Then the greatly resplendent Sugrīva, lord of the monkeys, also communicating his purpose, spoke to the mighty Hanūmān:

**6.62.2**  "Since Kumbhakarṇa has been slain and the princes have been killed, Rāvaṇa is no longer in a position to deliver reinforcements now."

**6.62.3**  "Let all the monkeys who are mighty and swift leap at Laṅkā quickly, O bulls among monkeys, taking up torches."

**6.62.4**  Then, when the sun had set at that terrible twilight, those bulls among monkeys, bearing torches, went facing toward Laṅkā.

**6.62.5**  Charged on all sides by the monkey troops holding torches, the disfigured-eyed guards stationed at the walls suddenly fled in all directions.

**6.62.6**  Exulting, the monkeys set fire to the gateways, watchtowers, wide avenues, patrol-paths of various kinds, and palaces.

**6.62.7**  Then the fire devoured thousands of those houses and the dwellings of all the rākṣasas, of every householder.

**6.62.8**  Of those whose armor was adorned with gold, who wore garlands of flowers and fine garments, whose eyes were unsteady with the drinking of wine, who walked reeling with intoxication —

**6.62.9**  — whose garments clung to their beloved consorts, who were filled with fury toward their enemies, who held maces, spears, and swords in their hands, who were still eating and drinking —

**6.62.10**  — who lay asleep on their fine couches together with their beloved ones, or who fled in terror swiftly, taking their children with them, in all directions —

**6.62.11**  — the fire burned thousands of those houses of the Laṅkā-dwellers, and blazed again and again.

**6.62.12**  The solid, greatly valuable dwellings, possessed of deep merits, adorned with golden full moons and half-moons, with their lofty moonlight chambers —

**6.62.13**  — with their lattice-windows inlaid with gems, with their bases firm on all sides, painted with jewels and coral, seeming to touch the sun —

**6.62.14**  — the mansions that resounded with the sounds of curlews, peacocks, lutes, and ornaments, immovable like mountains — the fire devoured them.

**6.62.15**  The gateways, encircled by fire, shone like masses of clouds bound with lightning in the summer season.

**6.62.16**  Noble women who had been sleeping in the aerial palaces, being burned, shedding their jewelry, cried out loudly: "Alas! Alas!"

**6.62.17**  There the mansions, enveloped in fire, collapsed, like the peaks of a great mountain struck by Indra's thunderbolt.

**6.62.18**  Those blazing mansions shone from afar like the peaks of the Himālaya with their forests of blazing herbs.

**6.62.19**  With the palace-tops burning and blazing with flames, Laṅkā at night appeared as if covered with flowering kiṃśuka trees.

**6.62.20**  With elephants released by their keepers and horses set free, Laṅkā became like an ocean at the end of the world with sharks swimming in disorder.

**6.62.21**  Seeing a horse set free, an elephant perhaps turns aside in fear; a horse, frightened, seeing a frightened elephant, turns back somewhere.

**6.62.22**  That city, set ablaze by the monkeys in a moment, became like the earth blazing at the terrible dissolution of this world.

**6.62.23**  The cries of the women, enveloped in smoke, wailing loudly, the sound of those scorched by fire, were heard for ten yojanas.

**6.62.24**  The monkeys, eager for battle, suddenly leaped upon other rākṣasas whose bodies had been scorched and who had come outside.

**6.62.25**  The battle-cries of the monkeys and the shrieks of the rākṣasas resounded through the ten directions, through the ocean, and through the earth.

**6.62.26**  Those two great-souled ones, Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, freed from their arrows, unperturbed, took up their two excellent bows.

**6.62.27**  Then as Rāma twanged his excellent bow, a tumultuous sound arose, causing terror among the rākṣasas.

**6.62.28**  Then Rāma shone brilliantly, drawing back his great bow, like the blessed and furious Bhava with his bow that is the Veda.

**6.62.29**  The terrible cry of the monkeys, the wail of the rākṣasas, and the twang of Rāma's bowstring — these three filled the ten directions.

**6.62.30**  By the arrows released from his bow, the city gate of that fortress, resembling a peak of Kailāsa, was shattered and fell to the earth.

**6.62.31**  Then seeing Rāma's arrows, tumultuous arming of the rākṣasa lords occurred in the aerial palaces and houses.

**6.62.32**  As they armed themselves and raised their lion-roars, the night of the rākṣasa lords became as it were dreadful.

**6.62.33**  Those lords of monkeys, commanded by the great-souled Sugrīva, having approached close, reaching the gate: "Fight, O bulls among monkeys!"

**6.62.34**  "And whoever stationed at his post violates his duty — he must be killed outright, as one who violates the king's command."

**6.62.35**  As those foremost monkeys stood at the gate with blazing torches flaming in their hands, fury entered Rāvaṇa.

**6.62.36**  From the spreading convulsions of his agitation the ten directions were thrown into confusion; his fury was visible in his limbs as if it had taken the form of Rudra's wrath.

**6.62.37**  Seething with rage, he dispatched Nikumbha and Kumbha, both sons of Kumbhakarṇa, together with many rākṣasas.

**6.62.38**  The lord of rākṣasas commanded all those rākṣasas: "Go there at once, rākṣasas!" — and they roared with lion-roars.

**6.62.39**  Then those heroes, spurred on by him, the rākṣasas with their weapons ablaze, issued forth from Laṅkā, roaring again and again.

**6.62.40**  With terrible horses, chariots, and elephants, swarming with infantry of various kinds, armed with blazing spears, maces, swords, javelins, darts, and bows —

**6.62.41**  — that dreadful rākṣasa army of terrible prowess and valor was seen blazing with javelins, resounding with hundreds of small bells.

**6.62.42**  Arms latticed with golden networks, battle-axes bound tight, great weapons swung in sweeping arcs, bows pressed together with arrows —

**6.62.43**  — with a great wind fragrant with perfumes, garlands, and the effusion of honey, dreadful, swarming with brave men, roaring like a great storm-cloud —

**6.62.44**  Seeing that very terrible army of the rākṣasas advancing, the monkey army stirred and cried out loudly.

**6.62.45**  Leaping forward swiftly, the great monkey army charged the enemy force like moths rushing into fire.

**6.62.46**  The foremost force of the rākṣasas, with its iron bars and thunderbolts sweeping and striking from the swinging of their arms, shone even more magnificently.

**6.62.47**  Likewise other night-wanderers of terrible form struck the foremost of those monkeys on every side with sharp swords.

**6.62.48**  One struck him who was striking another; one felled him who was felling; others reviled the one reviling; others bit the one biting.

**6.62.49**  "Give it!" some cried; "He's giving it!" another; "I'll give it!" yet another again; "Why do you trouble me? Stand!" — thus they spoke to one another there.

**6.62.50**  With great javelins raised aloft, with fists, spears, and swords in confusion, a mighty and dreadful battle of the monkeys and rākṣasas commenced.

**6.62.51**  The rākṣasas attacked ten, seventeen monkeys at a time; the monkeys slew ten and seventeen rākṣasas in battle.

**6.62.52**  The monkeys, seizing the rākṣasa army with its manes and harnesses fallen loose, its armor and standards discarded, surrounded it on all sides.

**6.63.1**  As that terrible confused slaughter of heroes raged, the battle-eager Aṅgada encountered the hero Kampana.

**6.63.2**  Challenging Aṅgada, Kampana in his fury swiftly struck him first with his mace; struck mightily, Aṅgada staggered.

**6.63.3**  Recovering consciousness, the valiant one hurled a mountain peak; and Kampana, tormented by the blow, fell to the earth.

**6.63.4**  With its heroes slain and afflicted, the rākṣasa king's army then turned toward where the son of Kumbhakarṇa was; as it rushed forward with speed, Kumbha calmed that army.

**6.63.5**  That best of archers, taking up his bow with perfect composure, released arrows like venomous serpents that tear through bodies.

**6.63.6**  His excellent bow with its arrows shone still more brilliantly, like a second Indra's bow blazing with the light of Airāvata's lightning.

**6.63.7**  Released from a full draw to the ear, with that arrow with golden shaft, feathered, clad in feathers, he then struck Dvivida.

**6.63.8**  Suddenly struck by him, his footing lost, convulsing, bewildered, that foremost of monkeys fell like a mountain peak.

**6.63.9**  But Mainda, seeing his brother broken in that great battle, charged forward swiftly, grasping a great boulder.

**6.63.10**  That mighty one hurled the boulder at the rākṣasa; Kumbha split that boulder with five well-aimed arrows.

**6.63.11**  Then nocking another fine-tipped arrow like a venomous serpent, the greatly resplendent one struck Dvivida's elder brother in the chest.

**6.63.12**  Mainda, the monkey troop-leader, struck in a vital spot by that blow, fell to the earth in a swoon.

**6.63.13**  Aṅgada, seeing those two mighty maternal uncles fallen, charged swiftly at Kumbha with his bow raised.

**6.63.14**  As he came rushing, Kumbha pierced him with five iron arrows, and three other sharp arrows — as an elephant is pierced with darts.

**6.63.15**  The valiant Kumbha pierced Aṅgada with various arrows, unblunted and sharp, keen and gold-adorned.

**6.63.16**  Aṅgada, the son of Vālin, though his body was pierced through, did not waver; he rained down upon Kumbha's head a shower of rocks and trees.

**6.63.17**  The glorious son of Kumbhakarṇa cut down all of them and shattered again the rocks hurled by the son of Vālin.

**6.63.18**  And seeing the monkey troop-leader rushing at him, Kumbha pierced him between the eyebrows with two arrows, as if striking an elephant with two torches.

**6.63.19**  Aṅgada, covering his eyes drenched with blood with one hand, seized with the other hand a nearby śāla tree.

**6.63.20**  That tree resembling Indra's banner post, like Mandara mountain — as he hurled it with force before the eyes of all the rākṣasas —

**6.63.21**  — Kumbha severed it with seven sharp arrows that pierce the body; Aṅgada was tormented again and again, sank down, and fell into a swoon.

**6.63.22**  Seeing Aṅgada tormented and sinking as if in an ocean, the foremost monkeys reported it to Rāghava — he was unapproachable.

**6.63.23**  Hearing that the son of Vālin was tormented in the great battle, Rāma then directed the foremost monkeys, headed by Jāmbavān.

**6.63.24**  Those tigers among monkeys, hearing Rāma's command, charged furiously at Kumbha with his bow raised.

**6.63.25**  Then the bulls among monkeys, with trees and stones in their hands, their eyes reddened with fury, rushed forward wishing to protect Aṅgada.

**6.63.26**  Jāmbavān, Suṣeṇa, and the monkey Vegadarśī — all angry — ran together toward the heroic son of Kumbhakarṇa.

**6.63.27**  But seeing those greatly powerful lords of monkeys advancing, he blocked them with a flood of arrows as a mountain blocks a reservoir of water.

**6.63.28**  Meeting his mass of arrows, the great-souled monkey lords could not pass beyond, like the great ocean unable to pass beyond its shore.

**6.63.29**  Seeing those monkey troops afflicted by the rain of arrows, placing his nephew Aṅgada behind him, the lord of monkeys —

**6.63.30**  — Sugrīva charged swiftly at Kumbha in battle, like a swift-moving lion against an elephant roaming the mountain ridges.

**6.63.31**  The mighty one uprooted great mountains, many aśvakarṇa and dhava trees, and various other trees, and hurled them.

**6.63.32**  That unapproachable rain of trees that covered the sky, the glorious son of Kumbhakarṇa severed with his sharp arrows.

**6.63.33**  Struck by Kumbha's sharp, unerring, fierce arrows, those trees shone like terrible śataghnī engines of war.

**6.63.34**  Seeing that rain of trees severed by the valiant Kumbha, the glorious lord of monkeys, of great essence, did not waver.

**6.63.35**  Suddenly pierced through and enduring those arrows, he snatched Kumbha's bow and broke it — that bow bright as Indra's rainbow.

**6.63.36**  Then leaping forward swiftly and having performed that most difficult deed, he angrily addressed Kumbha, who was like an elephant with a broken tusk:

**6.63.37**  "O elder brother of Nikumbha, that valor of yours, that wonderful speed of your arrows, that skill and power — it is yours or Rāvaṇa's."

**6.63.38**  "O equal of Prahrāda, Bali, the slayer of Vṛtra, Kubera, and Varuṇa — you alone have been born surpassing your father in strength."

**6.63.39**  "You alone, O mighty-armed one, spear in hand, subduer of foes, the thirty gods cannot overcome, as diseases cannot overcome one who has conquered his senses."

**6.63.40**  "Your paternal uncle can endure gods and dānavas by virtue of boons received; but Kumbhakarṇa endured gods and asuras through sheer valor."

**6.63.41**  "Equal to Indrajit with the bow, equal in glory to Rāvaṇa — today you are the foremost in the world of rākṣasas in strength and valor."

**6.63.42**  "Let the beings of the world witness today this wonderful great clash between you and me in battle, as they once witnessed that between Indra and Śambara."

**6.63.43**  "An incomparable feat has been accomplished, skill with weapons has been displayed, and these monkey heroes of terrible prowess have been felled by you."

**6.63.44**  "Nor have I slain you yet for fear of censure, O hero — you have done your work and are weary; rest, and then witness my strength."

**6.63.45**  Honored — though with contempt — by those words of Sugrīva, his energy grew like the flame of a fire fed with clarified butter.

**6.63.46**  Then Kumbha leaped up, fell upon Sugrīva, and furiously struck him on the chest with a fist of thunderbolt force.

**6.63.47**  The skin of Sugrīva's chest burst open and blood appeared; and that fist of great momentum rebounded from his firm circle of bones.

**6.63.48**  Then from that force a flame was kindled there again and again — like the flames born from the crushing of a thunderbolt on Mount Meru.

**6.63.49**  Struck there by him, Sugrīva, the bull among monkeys, the mighty one, gathered his fist into a thunderbolt-like shape.

**6.63.50**  Blazing with a thousand flames, bright as the solar orb, the valiant one drove that fist down onto Kumbha's chest.

**6.63.51**  Struck by that fist, the rākṣasa fell at once, like the planet Mars with its blazing rays falling from the sky by chance.

**6.63.52**  The appearance of Kumbha as he fell, his chest shattered by the fist, resembled the form of the lord of cattle assailed by Rudra.

**6.63.53**  When he was slain by the bull among leapers, of terrible valor, in battle, the earth with its mountains and forests trembled, and a great fear entered the rākṣasas.

**6.64.1**  Nikumbha, seeing his brother struck down by Sugrīva, glared at the lord of the monkeys as if burning with rage.

**6.64.2**  Then that hero took up an iron club adorned with garlands and straps, its five-fingered grip fitted, fair to behold, equal in height to the summit of a great mountain.

**6.64.3**  It was bound with bands of gold, adorned with vajra and coral, dreadful as the staff of Yama, the destroyer of fear among the rākṣasas.

**6.64.4**  Whirling that club equal to Indra's banner in battle, Nikumbha of terrible prowess roared with open mouth, blazing with great energy.

**6.64.5**  With a pendant on his chest, armlets on his arms, a pair of gleaming earrings, and a variegated garland—

**6.64.6**  —Nikumbha shone with his ornaments and that iron club like a cloud adorned with the rainbow of Indra, accompanied by lightning and thunder.

**6.64.7**  The knot of air burst apart at the tip of the club of that great-souled one; it blazed with sound like fire without smoke.

**6.64.8**  Together with the city of Viṭapāvatī and the finest mansions of the gandharvas, together with Amarāvatī and all its palaces—

**6.64.9**  —the sky with its host of stars, its constellations, the moon, and the great planets seemed to spin and whirl, swept by the circling club of Nikumbha.

**6.64.10**  Unapproachable, blazing with the lustre of the club and his ornaments, the fire of Nikumbha, fuelled by wrath, arose like the fire at the end of an age.

**6.64.11**  The rākṣasas and monkeys alike could not stir for fear; but mighty Hanūmān, with chest bared, stood facing him.

**6.64.12**  That strong one, whose arms were like an iron club, brought down upon the chest of the powerful Nikumbha the club that shone like the sun.

**6.64.13**  Striking that firm, broad chest, the club was shattered into a hundred pieces and broke apart at once like a hundred meteors in the sky.

**6.64.14**  Shaken by that blow from the club, the great monkey reeled, as a mountain shakes in an earthquake.

**6.64.15**  Though struck thus, Hanūmān, best of the leapers, exceedingly powerful by nature, clenched his fist.

**6.64.16**  Raising it high, that great-splendoured, valiant one swift as the wind hurled it with full force against Nikumbha's chest.

**6.64.17**  Then his skin burst open and blood poured out; from that fist-blow a flame arose like lightning.

**6.64.18**  Though staggered by that blow, Nikumbha collected himself and seized the immensely powerful Hanūmān.

**6.64.19**  At that, the inhabitants of Laṅkā raised a terrible cry in the battle on seeing mighty Hanūmān lifted up by Nikumbha.

**6.64.20**  Even as he was being carried off by the son of Kumbhakarṇa, the son of the wind struck him with a fist swift as the thunderbolt.

**6.64.21**  Freeing himself and leaping to the ground, the son of the wind, Hanūmān, quickly wrestled Nikumbha down.

**6.64.22**  Hurling him with full exertion and crushing him to the ground, that valiant one sprang up swiftly and fell upon his chest.

**6.64.23**  Grasping him with both arms and twisting his neck, he tore away the great head of the roaring Nikumbha with a fearful wrench.

**6.64.24**  Then, as Nikumbha fell roaring, slain by the son of the wind in battle, the combat between the army of the son of Daśaratha and the army of the lord of the rākṣasas became far more terrible, both sides consumed with fury.

**6.65.1**  Hearing that Nikumbha had been slain and Kumbha struck down, Rāvaṇa, exceedingly wrathful, blazed up like fire.

**6.65.2**  That lord of the rākṣasas, overwhelmed by both anger and grief, urged on Makarākṣa of wide eyes, the son of Khara.

**6.65.3**  "Go, my son, dispatched by me, accompanied by your forces. Slay both Rāghava and Lakṣmaṇa together with the forest-dwellers."

**6.65.4**  Hearing Rāvaṇa's words, Makarākṣa, the heroic and proud son of Khara, the night-rover, said boldly: "So be it."

**6.65.5**  Having saluted the ten-necked one and circumambulated him, the mighty one departed from Rāvaṇa's bright dwelling by his command.

**6.65.6**  The son of Khara said this to the marshal of forces standing nearby: "Let a chariot be brought quickly, and let the army be assembled without delay."

**6.65.7**  Hearing those words, the marshal of forces, a night-rover, brought the chariot and the army near.

**6.65.8**  The night-rover circumambulated the chariot and mounted it, then urged the charioteer: "Drive my chariot quickly."

**6.65.9**  Then Makarākṣa addressed all those rākṣasas: "All of you rākṣasas—fight in front of me."

**6.65.10**  "I have been commanded by the great-souled king of the rākṣasas, Rāvaṇa, to slay both Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa in battle."

**6.65.11**  "Today I shall slay Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, O night-rovers, Sugrīva the tree-roaming animal, and the monkeys with my finest arrows."

**6.65.12**  "Today with my spear-casts I shall burn down the great army of the monkeys that has arrived, like fire consuming dry fuel."

**6.65.13**  Hearing those words of Makarākṣa, all those night-rovers, powerful and resolute, equipped themselves with various weapons.

**6.65.14**  Those heroes, able to take any form, fanged, tawny-eyed, hair dishevelled, fearsome, roared like elephants.

**6.65.15**  The great-bodied ones surrounded the great-bodied son of Khara and advanced joyfully, shaking the earth.

**6.65.16**  Then arose a great sound on all sides from thousands of conches and kettledrums beaten together, along with battle-cries and the slapping of arms.

**6.65.17**  Then the charioteer's goad slipped from his hand, and the banner of that rākṣasa fell suddenly to the ground.

**6.65.18**  The horses yoked to his chariot, stripped of their vigour, moved with stumbling feet, dejected, their mouths wet with foam and tears.

**6.65.19**  As the terrible, evil-minded Makarākṣa set forth, the wind blew against him, harsh and laden with dust.

**6.65.20**  Seeing those omens, the mightiest of the rākṣasas marched out without reflection, all of them, to where Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa stood.

**6.65.21**  Dark as clouds, elephants, and buffaloes in hue, scarred again and again by maces and swords in the fore of battle, each crying "I, I first!" those night-rovers skilled in war wheeled about, roaring.

**6.66.1**  Seeing Makarākṣa marching forth, all those bull-like monkeys leaped forward at once and stood ready, eager to fight.

**6.66.2**  Then commenced that great and hair-raising battle between the night-rovers and the monkeys, like the battle of the gods with the dānavas.

**6.66.3**  With hurled trees and spears, with thrown rocks and iron clubs, the monkeys and night-rovers crushed one another.

**6.66.4**  The night-rovers struck with śaktis, spears, maces, swords, lances, broad-bladed swords, slings, and volleys of arrows from every side—

**6.66.5**  —and with nooses, mallets, staffs, and thunderbolt-like weapons as well; those night-rovers wrought havoc among the lion-like monkeys.

**6.66.6**  The monkeys, tormented by the son of Khara's showers of arrows, became confused of mind and all fled, afflicted by fear.

**6.66.7**  Seeing those forest-dwellers fleeing, all the rākṣasas roared like lions, bold and wearing the air of conquerors.

**6.66.8**  As the monkeys fled on all sides, Rāma checked the rākṣasas with a rain of arrows.

**6.66.9**  Seeing the rākṣasas checked, Makarākṣa the night-rover, seized with a smouldering rage, spoke these words:

**6.66.10**  "Stand, Rāma! I offer you single combat. I shall make you abandon your life with sharp arrows loosed from my bow."

**6.66.11**  "When you slew my father in the Daṇḍaka forest before my very eyes, as he was engaged in his own deeds—remembering that, my rage increases."

**6.66.12**  "My limbs burn fiercely, O wicked Rāghava, because I could not find you at that time in the great forest."

**6.66.13**  "By good fortune you have come within my sight here, Rāma. You are as longed for as another animal is longed for by a hungry lion."

**6.66.14**  "Today, through the speed of my arrows, you will go to the realm of the king of the dead. You shall meet again with the heroes you yourself have slain."

**6.66.15**  "Why say more? Hear my words, Rāma. Let all the worlds witness you and me upon the battlefield."

**6.66.16**  "Whether with missiles, or with a mace, or with your bare arms in this great battle—let it be fought, Rāma, with whatever you have practised."

**6.66.17**  Hearing Makarākṣa's words, Rāma the son of Daśaratha replied with a smile to that boastful speaker:

**6.66.18**  "Fourteen thousand rākṣasas, and your father himself, and Triśirā and Dūṣaṇa as well—all were slain by me in the Daṇḍaka."

**6.66.19**  "Vultures, jackals, and crows with sharp beaks and hooked talons shall today be well-fed on your flesh, O wicked one."

**6.66.20**  Thus addressed by Rāma, the son of Khara, the night-rover, released showers of arrows against Rāghava on the battlefield.

**6.66.21**  Rāma cut down those arrows with his own rain of arrows in manifold ways; those severed shafts with golden feathers fell to the earth by the thousands.

**6.66.22**  There ensued that battle, each pressing the other with full force, between the son of the rākṣasa Khara and the son of Daśaratha.

**6.66.23**  Like the sound of two storm-clouds in the sky, the resounding clap of their bowstrings and the ringing of arrows loosed from the bow were heard on the battlefield.

**6.66.24**  Gods, dānavas, gandharvas, kiṃnaras, and great serpents—all gathered in the sky, eager to witness that marvel.

**6.66.25**  Wounded in each other's limbs, their strength grew twofold; each answering the other's blow with a blow, those two fought on the battlefield.
