**6.66.26**  The arrows loosed at Rāma were cut down by the rākṣasa in battle; and the arrows loosed by the rākṣasa were cut down by Rāma in many ways with his shafts.

**6.66.27**  All the cardinal and intermediate directions were filled with showers of arrows; the earth too was covered on all sides and could not be seen.

**6.66.28**  Then mighty-armed Rāghava in his fury cut the rākṣasa's bow, pierced the charioteer with eight iron arrows, shattered the chariot with his shafts, and brought down the chariot-horses.

**6.66.29**  Dismounted and standing on the ground, Makarākṣa the night-rover took in his hand a spear that was the terror of all living beings, blazing like the fire at the end of an age.

**6.66.30**  Whirling that great blazing spear, the night-rover hurled it in fury at Rāghava in the great battle.

**6.66.31**  That blazing spear flying toward him, loosed from the hand of Khara's son, was cut apart in the air by Rāghava with three arrows.

**6.66.32**  That spear, adorned with divine gold, cut asunder in many pieces, tormented by Rāma's arrows, scattered and fell to the earth like a great meteor.

**6.66.33**  Seeing that spear destroyed by Rāma of marvellous deeds, the beings moving in the sky exclaimed: "Well done! Well done!"

**6.66.34**  Seeing his spear destroyed, Makarākṣa the night-rover raised his fist and cried out to Kākutstha: "Stand! Stand!"

**6.66.35**  Seeing him rushing forward, the delight of the Raghu line, Rāma, laughed and then fitted the Fire-missile to his own bow.

**6.66.36**  The rākṣasa, struck by that missile by Kākutstha in battle, his heart cleft asunder, fell there and died.

**6.66.37**  Seeing the fall of Makarākṣa, all those rākṣasas, afflicted by the might of Rāma's arrows, fled back to Laṅkā.

**6.66.38**  The gods too beheld with great joy that night-rover, the son of Khara, slain by the speed of the arrows of the son of King Daśaratha—fallen like a mountain shattered by the thunderbolt.

**6.67.1**  Hearing that Makarākṣa had been slain, Rāvaṇa the conqueror in battle then commanded in fury his son Indrajit to go to the fight.

**6.67.2**  "Slay, O hero of mighty valour, those two brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa — whether invisible or visible, you in every way surpass them in strength."

**6.67.3**  "You have defeated Indra himself of unequalled deeds in battle — how then, having seen these mere mortals, will you not slay them in combat?"

**6.67.4**  Thus addressed by the lord of rākṣasas, Indrajit accepted his father's words and duly poured oblations into the sacred fire upon the sacrificial ground.

**6.67.5**  As he offered oblations into the fire there, rākṣasī women wearing red turbans came rushing in agitation to where Rāvaṇa's son stood.

**6.67.6**  Weapons, arrows as sacrificial leaves, vibhītaka wood as kindling, red garments, and a ladle of black iron were brought.

**6.67.7**  Having spread the fire on all sides with arrow-shafts as strewing material, he seized by the throat a living all-black goat.

**6.67.8**  Of the fire blazing for the oblation-offering, smokeless and of great flames, those signs appeared that presaged victory.

**6.67.9**  With its crest curling clockwise, resembling heated gold, the fire rose up of its own accord and received that oblation.

**6.67.10**  Having offered oblations into the fire and propitiated the gods, dānavas, and rākṣasas, he mounted the finest chariot that had the power of invisibility — that auspicious vehicle.

**6.67.11**  Drawn by four horses, furnished with sharp arrows, and with his great bow strung and raised, he shone magnificently upon that supreme chariot.

**6.67.12**  Blazing with bodily splendour, accoutred in gold ornaments, that chariot was fully adorned with arrows — both full-moon-crested and half-moon-headed shafts.

**6.67.13**  Indrajit's battle-standard, a great conch-shaped emblem of Jāmbūnada gold, resembling a blazing fire and adorned with vaidūrya gems, blazed forth.

**6.67.14**  Protected by that brahma-weapon equal in splendour to the sun, Rāvaṇa's son of tremendous might became invincible.

**6.67.15**  Indrajit, ever triumphant in battle, sallied forth from the city, offered oblations with rākṣasa mantras, rendered himself invisible, and declared:

**6.67.16**  "Today, by slaying in battle those two who roam the forest under a false pretence of renunciation, I shall bestow victory — an exceeding triumph in war — upon my father Rāvaṇa."

**6.67.17**  "Having made the earth free of Rāma by slaying Rāma together with Lakṣmaṇa, I shall bring my father the highest delight" — saying this, he vanished.

**6.67.18**  Then, urged on by the ten-necked one, the keen enemy of Indra fell upon them in fury, fierce in battle with his sharp bow and iron-shafted arrows.

**6.67.19**  He beheld those two heroes of mighty valour standing amid the monkey host, pouring forth networks of arrows like two three-headed serpents.

**6.67.20**  Recognising them as "these are those two," he strung his bow and poured down unceasing streams of arrows like a rain-cloud pouring rain.

**6.67.21**  Taking to the sky with his chariot, he stood beyond the range of their sight and pierced Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa with sharp arrows.

**6.67.22**  Assailed by the force of his arrows, Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa took up their bows with arrows nocked and discharged divine weapons.

**6.67.23**  Though the two mighty ones, resplendent as gods, covered the sky with nets of arrows, they could not touch him with their shafts or weapons.

**6.67.24**  For that glorious one created a darkness of smoke, veiling the sky, and the directions vanished, enveloped in misty gloom.

**6.67.25**  Neither the twang of his bowstring, nor the sound of his chariot's wheel-rims and hoofbeats could be heard as he moved; nor did his form become visible.

**6.67.26**  In the dense dark gloom, the long-armed one rained down what seemed a wondrous shower of arrows — a downpour of iron-shafted shafts.

**6.67.27**  Rāvaṇa's son, empowered by a boon, wounded Rāma grievously with arrows bright as the sun, striking every limb in fury during battle.

**6.67.28**  Struck by the iron-shafted arrows as if by torrents, those two tiger-men, though like mountains under assault, released sharp arrows with golden feathering.

**6.67.29**  Reaching into the sky to reach Rāvaṇa's son, those heron-feathered arrows were cut apart and fell to the earth soaked in blood.

**6.67.30**  Those two foremost of men, excessively tormented by the flood of arrows, cut apart those falling shafts with many broad-headed arrows.

**6.67.31**  Wherever those two sons of Daśaratha saw sharp arrows descending, there and thence they released supreme weapons.

**6.67.32**  But Rāvaṇa's son, a supreme chariot-warrior, roaming all directions with his chariot and swift in his weapons, pierced those two sons of Daśaratha with sharp arrows.

**6.67.33**  Those two heroes, the sons of Daśaratha, pierced excessively by him with well-compacted gold-feathered shafts, looked like two flowering kiṃśuka trees.

**6.67.34**  No one could discern his movements, nor his form, bow, or arrows — nothing else whatsoever could be perceived, as when the sun is engulfed by clouds.

**6.67.35**  Pierced by him, monkeys slain and bereft of life lay fallen there on the surface of the earth by the hundreds.

**6.67.36**  Lakṣmaṇa, greatly enraged, spoke to his brother: "I shall deploy the brāhma-weapon for the purpose of slaying all rākṣasas."

**6.67.37**  Then Rāma spoke to Lakṣmaṇa of auspicious marks: "For the sake of one alone you should not slay all rākṣasas on the earth."

**6.67.38**  "One who is not fighting, one concealed, one with palms joined, one who has sought refuge, one who is fleeing, or one who is heedless — you should not slay any such here."

**6.67.39**  "Let us make the effort for the slaying of this one alone, O mighty one — let us release swift weapons terrible as venomous serpents."

**6.67.40**  "This petty rākṣasa who wields illusion, his chariot hidden by force — the monkey troop-leaders will slay him once they spot him."

**6.67.41**  "Even if this one should enter the earth, or the sky, or the netherworld, or the vault of heaven — even thus concealed, he shall be scorched by my weapon and fall lifeless to the ground."

**6.67.42**  Having spoken thus, the great-souled foremost hero of the Raghus, surrounded by the best of the monkeys, then eagerly watched — the great-souled one — for the slaying of that ferocious and cruel-deeded one.

**6.68.1**  Perceiving the resolve of the great-souled Rāghava, Indrajit withdrew from that battle and re-entered the city.

**6.68.2**  That hero of great splendour, recalling the slaughter of those swift rākṣasas, went forth again with eyes reddened by anger.

**6.68.3**  Indrajit, of mighty valour, descendant of Pulastya, the scourge of the gods, went out through the western gate surrounded by rākṣasas.

**6.68.4**  Indrajit, seeing those two warrior brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa arrayed for battle, then conjured up an illusion.

**6.68.5**  Indrajit placed on his chariot an illusory Sītā wrought of māyā, and surrounded by a great army, resolved upon her slaying.

**6.68.6**  That one of utterly evil mind, with the intention of bewildering everyone and resolved to slay Sītā, set out toward the monkeys.

**6.68.7**  Seeing him sally forth from the city, the forest-dwellers sprang up, furious and eager for battle, rocks in their hands.

**6.68.8**  Hanūmān, that bull among monkeys, went in front of them all, grasping a mighty mountain peak that was difficult to assail.

**6.68.9**  He saw Sītā — stripped of joy — on Indrajit's chariot: her hair in a single braid, miserable, her face gaunt from fasting.

**6.68.10**  Clad in a single worn garment, unwashed, every limb smeared with dust and grime — that beloved of Rāghava, that most beautiful of women.

**6.68.11**  Having gazed upon the Maithilī for a moment and having made a firm judgment, Hanūmān became distressed, his face overwhelmed with tears.

**6.68.12**  He saw her — grief-stricken, joyless, wretched Sītā seated on the chariot under the sway of the son of the lord of rākṣasas — and spoke.

**6.68.13**  That great monkey, thinking "What does this one intend?", together with those foremost of monkeys charged toward the son of Rāvaṇa.

**6.68.14**  Seeing that monkey force, the son of Rāvaṇa, overcome with rage, drew his gleaming sword and grasped Sītā by the hair.

**6.68.15**  As they all watched, the son of Rāvaṇa struck that woman — the one joined to the chariot by illusion — who cried out "Rāma! Rāma!"

**6.68.16**  Seeing her seized by the hair, the son of the Wind, Hanūmān, overcome by anguish and shedding tears born of grief from both eyes, spoke harsh words in fury to the son of the lord of rākṣasas:

**6.68.17**  "Evil-minded one, you seize a woman by her hair to bring about your own destruction! Born in the lineage of brahmarṣis yet fallen into the rākṣasa species — shame on you of sinful conduct, whose mind is of such a kind!"

**6.68.18**  "Cruel one, ignoble one, of evil conduct, petty one, whose valour is that of a sinner — such a deed is fit only for the base; you have no compassion, O merciless one!"

**6.68.19**  "The Maithilī, fallen from her home, from her kingdom, from Rāma's side — what wrong has she done you that you wish to slay her?"

**6.68.20**  "Having slain Sītā, you shall not live long by any means — by this deed worthy of death you have indeed come into my hands."

**6.68.21**  "And the worlds that await slayers of women — those loathsome worlds condemned by all — those shall you attain, having relinquished life here and gone to the hereafter."

**6.68.22**  Saying this, Hanūmān, surrounded by armed monkeys, charged in great fury toward the son of the lord of rākṣasas.

**6.68.23**  That host of terrible-swift rākṣasas checked the charging army of forest-dwellers, that host of mighty valour.

**6.68.24**  Having troubled the monkey-army with a thousand arrows, Indrajit then addressed Hanūmān, that foremost of monkeys:

**6.68.25**  "The Vaidehī — for whose sake Sugrīva, you yourself, and Rāma have come here — her I shall slay today, even as you watch."

**6.68.26**  "After slaying her, I shall then slay Rāma, Lakṣmaṇa, you, O monkey, Sugrīva, and that ignoble Vibhīṣaṇa."

**6.68.27**  "As for what you say, O leaping one — 'women must not be slain' — whatever would cause pain to one's enemies, that is what must be done."

**6.68.28**  Having spoken thus, Indrajit then personally struck that weeping illusory Sītā with his sharp-edged sword.

**6.68.29**  Cut by him along the line of the sacred thread, that wretched woman of broad hips, fair to behold, fell to the earth.

**6.68.30**  Having slain that woman, Indrajit said to Hanūmān: "Look — she has been struck down by me in anger before your very eyes, this one who was Rāma's."

**6.68.31**  Then Indrajit, having slain her personally with his great sword, mounted his chariot in delight and let out a mighty roar.

**6.68.32**  The monkeys stationed nearby heard that roar — the roar of one with gaping mouth, who had taken refuge in that stronghold.

**6.68.33**  That evil-minded son of Rāvaṇa, having thus slain Sītā, became joyful at heart; seeing him exultant in aspect, the monkeys became crestfallen in appearance and fled in all directions.

**6.69.1**  Hearing that terrible roar, equal in sound to Indra's thunderbolt, the bull-like monkeys gazed in all directions and fled.

**6.69.2**  Then Hanūmān, son of the Wind, spoke to all of them — their faces downcast, miserable, frightened, scattering in different directions:

**6.69.3**  "Why do you flee with downcast faces, O leaping monkeys, your ardour for battle abandoned? Where has your heroism gone?"

**6.69.4**  "Follow behind me as I go forward in battle — it is not fitting for those of heroic lineage to turn back."

**6.69.5**  Thus addressed by the wise son of the Wind, greatly enraged and with gladdened hearts, they seized mountain peaks and trees.

**6.69.6**  Those bull-like monkeys charged at the rākṣasas roaring, and surrounding Hanūmān they followed him in the great battle.

**6.69.7**  Hanūmān, encircled on all sides by those foremost monkeys, blazed like a fire with flames and burned the enemy's army.

**6.69.8**  That mighty great monkey, surrounded by the monkey army and resembling Yama the lord of death at the end of an age, wrought slaughter among the rākṣasas.

**6.69.9**  That great monkey Hanūmān, seized by grief and rage alike, hurled a great boulder at the chariot of the son of Rāvaṇa.

**6.69.10**  Seeing it hurtling down, the chariot — fitted with well-trained horses and its charioteer — was at once driven far away.

**6.69.11**  Not reaching Indrajit along with his chariot and charioteer, that boulder — raised in vain — split the earth and entered the ground.

**6.69.12**  When the boulder fell, the rākṣasa army was shaken; the forest-dwellers charged at them by the hundreds, roaring.

**6.69.13**  Those monkeys of massive bodies and terrible prowess, aroused, hurled trees and mountain peaks into the midst of the enemy.

**6.69.14**  Those night-wanderers of dreadful form, struck with force by those mighty-valorous monkeys with their trees, writhed on the battle-ground.

**6.69.15**  Seeing his own army tormented by the monkeys, Indrajit took up his weapons and in fury turned to face the enemy.

**6.69.16**  Releasing torrents of arrows, surrounded by his own army and displaying his valour, he slew very many of those tiger-monkeys.

**6.69.17**  The monkeys in turn slew his attendants in battle with spears, thunderbolt-weapons, swords, broad blades, and blunt iron clubs.

**6.69.18**  With śāla trees bearing their branches and boughs, and with boulders, the mighty Hanūmān wrought carnage among those rākṣasas of terrible deeds.

**6.69.19**  Checking the enemy's army, Hanūmān spoke to those forest-dwellers: "Turn back — this force cannot be overcome by us."

**6.69.20**  "We who fight abandoning our lives, wishing to do what is dear to Rāma — the daughter of Janaka for whose sake we fight has been slain."

**6.69.21**  "Having reported this matter to Rāma and to Sugrīva, we shall do whatever those two shall determine in response."

**6.69.22**  Having spoken thus, that foremost of monkeys, restraining all the monkeys, withdrew slowly and deliberately, unafraid, with his forces.

**6.69.23**  Seeing Hanūmān departing toward where Rāghava was, Indrajit repaired to Nikumbhilā and poured oblations into the fire.

**6.69.24**  Upon the sacrificial ground, the fire blazed up, ritually invoked by that rākṣasa, consuming the oblation of blood.

**6.69.25**  That flame, dense and radiant, was seen — satiated by the blood-oblation — rising up, that fierce fire, like the sun arrived at the twilight.

**6.69.26**  Then Indrajit, for the welfare of the rākṣasas, offered the oblation according to rule and ordinance; and seeing this, those rākṣasas took their stations in great multitudes — those who knew the rites of sound and unsound sacrifice.

**6.70.1**  Rāghava too, upon hearing that vast tumult of battle from the forest-dwelling rākṣasas, then spoke to Jāmbavān.

**6.70.2**  "Surely, gentle one, Hanūmān has performed a deed most difficult to accomplish, for that fearful, immense clash of weapons is heard even here.

**6.70.3**  Therefore go, lord of bears, surrounded by your own army, and render swift aid to that foremost of monkeys while he fights."

**6.70.4**  The king of bears, saying "so be it," surrounded by his own forces, went to the western gate where the monkey Hanūmān was.

**6.70.5**  Then the lord of bears saw, on the road, Hanūmān coming, surrounded by monkeys who had been fighting and were now panting with exhaustion.

**6.70.6**  Seeing on the road both Hanūmān and that upraised bear-army, dark as a rain-cloud and fearsome, he checked his advance and turned back.

**6.70.7**  He of great renown, having quickly come into the presence of that monkey-army, spoke these words to Rāma in grief:

**6.70.8**  "Even as we were fighting in battle and looking on, Indrajit, son of Rāvaṇa, slew Sītā who was weeping."

**6.70.9**  "Seeing her, O subduer of foes, my mind was overcome with agitation and I fell into despair; therefore I have come to inform you of this event."

**6.70.10**  Hearing those words of his, Rāghava fainted with grief and fell then to the ground like a tree whose roots have been severed.

**6.70.11**  Seeing Rāghava, resplendent as a god, fallen upon the ground, the foremost monkeys leaped forward and rushed to him from all sides.

**6.70.12**  They sprinkled him with water fragrant with lotuses and blue water-lilies, as he blazed with an unbearable anguish, suddenly arisen like fire.

**6.70.13**  Then Lakṣmaṇa, deeply grieved, embraced him with both arms and spoke to the unwell Rāma words full of reason and import:

**6.70.14**  "You who walk on the auspicious path and have mastered your senses like a noble man — dharma that is ineffectual cannot protect you from misfortunes.

**6.70.15**  "The existence of beings, both stationary and moving, is plainly perceived; but dharma is not so manifest — therefore in my view it does not exist.

**6.70.16**  "Just as the stationary and the moving are plainly seen, this matter is not equally apt — for one such as you should not meet with calamity.

**6.70.17**  "If adharma were truly existent, Rāvaṇa would go to hell; and you, endowed with dharma, would not thus suffer this misfortune.

**6.70.18**  "But since he knows no affliction while affliction has come to you, one must conclude from dharma that there is dharma and from adharma, adharma.

**6.70.19**  "If those who delight in adharma were bound by dharma, then dharma would indeed come to those who practise dharma, and such would be their fruit.

**6.70.20**  "Since wealth increases in those in whom adharma is established, while those of virtuous conduct are afflicted, both dharma and adharma are therefore without purpose.

**6.70.21**  "If evildoers are slain by adharma, O Rāghava, then dharma, itself slain by the act of slaying, is destroyed — whom then can it slay?

**6.70.22**  "Or if he is slain or slays another by divine ordinance, then fate is implicated in it and is not tainted by that sinful act.

**6.70.23**  "With dharma that is unseen in its recompense, unmanifest, both real and unreal — how, O crusher of enemies, can the highest be attained through it?

**6.70.24**  "If the real existed, O foremost of the righteous, nothing unreal would touch you; since you have met with such a thing, the real therefore does not hold.

**6.70.25**  "Or perhaps dharma is feeble and impotent, following in the wake of strength; the feeble, with its dignity stripped away, is not to be served — such is my view.

**6.70.26**  "If dharma is but an attribute of strength and valour, then abandon dharma and act — in strength as in dharma.

**6.70.27**  "If dharma is indeed truthfulness in speech, O scorcher of enemies, then why did you not restrain your father when he was cruel and untrue to you?

**6.70.28**  "If dharma truly existed, O scorcher of foes, or adharma — Vajrī who wields the thunderbolt would not have performed the hundred-sacrifice after slaying a sage.

**6.70.29**  "Dharma rooted in adharma destroys, O Rāghava; all this, O Kākutstha, a man does as he pleases.

**6.70.30**  "Such is my view, dear one — this is dharma — O Rāghava, when you renounced the kingdom you severed the very root of dharma.

**6.70.31**  "For from wealth that grows and multiplies on all sides, all undertakings proceed, as rivers flow from mountains.

**6.70.32**  "For the man bereft of wealth, whose energy is slight, all his undertakings are cut off, as a stream dries in summer.

**6.70.33**  "Thus he who, desiring pleasure and having been raised in comfort, abandons wealth, begins to do evil — and so fault sets in.

**6.70.34**  "He who has wealth has friends; he who has wealth has kinsmen; he who has wealth is a man in this world; he who has wealth is the learned one.

**6.70.35**  "He who has wealth is the heroic one; he who has wealth is the wise one; he who has wealth is the greatly fortunate; he who has wealth possesses great virtues.

**6.70.36**  "These faults of abandoning wealth have been declared by me — O hero, it was with such reasoning that you resolved to renounce the kingdom.

**6.70.37**  "For him who has wealth, dharma, desire, and wealth all revolve auspiciously around him; for one who is without wealth and yet seeks wealth, wealth cannot be gathered.

**6.70.38**  "Joy, desire, pride, dharma, anger, tranquillity, self-restraint — all these, O lord of men, proceed from wealth.

**6.70.39**  "For those who walk in dharma's path, this world perishes — those resources are not visible in you, as planets are invisible on stormy days.

**6.70.40**  "O hero, when you went into exile and abided by your father's word, your wife — dearer to you than life — was carried off by a rākṣasa.

**6.70.41**  "Therefore today, O hero, I shall remove by my deeds this great sorrow caused by Indrajit — so arise, O Rāghava.

**6.70.42**  "O sinless one, enraged for your sake, having witnessed the slaying of Janaka's daughter, I shall fiercely pierce Laṅkā with arrows — together with its cavalry, elephants, chariots, and all the rākṣasa lords."

**6.71.1**  While Lakṣmaṇa, devoted to his brother, was thus consoling Rāma, having posted sentinels at their proper stations, Vibhīṣaṇa arrived there.

**6.71.2**  He was attended by four counsellors, heroes armed with diverse weapons, resembling the bull-leader of a herd of elephants dark as a mass of collyrium.

**6.71.3**  Approaching, he saw the great-souled Rāghava languishing in sorrow, and the monkeys too with eyes clouded by tears.

**6.71.4**  He saw the great-souled Rāghava, delight of the Ikṣvāku lineage, overcome by bewilderment and resting in Lakṣmaṇa's lap.

**6.71.5**  Seeing Rāma ashamed and tormented by sorrow, Vibhīṣaṇa, his spirit dejected with inner pain, said, "What is this?"

**6.71.6**  Seeing Vibhīṣaṇa's face and Sugrīva and those monkeys, Lakṣmaṇa spoke these words, his voice choked with tears:

**6.71.7**  "Hearing, gentle one, from Hanūmān's report that Sītā had been slain by Indrajit, Rāghava straightway fell into a swoon."

**6.71.8**  Restraining Saumitri as he was speaking, Vibhīṣaṇa spoke these words of weighty import to the unconscious Rāma:

**6.71.9**  "What has been told to you by Hanūmān in the form of news fit for a lord of men — that I deem impossible, like the drying up of the ocean.

**6.71.10**  "I know the intention of the evil-souled Rāvaṇa regarding Sītā, O mighty-armed one — he will not bring about her killing.

**6.71.11**  "Though implored again and again by me, who sought his welfare, saying 'Release the princess of Videha,' he did not heed that counsel.

**6.71.12**  "She cannot be seen by conciliation, nor by sowing dissension, nor by bribery — far less by force of arms, nor by any other means whatsoever.

**6.71.13**  "That rākṣasa, having deluded the monkeys, has returned to the shrine called Nikumbhilā, where he will perform his oblation.

**6.71.14**  "For if he completes the oblation, this son of Rāvaṇa becomes invincible in battle even by the gods together with Vāsava.

**6.71.15**  "Surely this illusion was deployed by him — ever seeking to obstruct — to confound the valour of the monkeys, dear one. Let us go there with our armies before it is completed."

**6.71.16**  "Abandon, O tiger among men, this false anguish that has come upon you — for all the army is being crushed, seeing you wasted away by grief.

**6.71.17**  "Stay here with a serene heart, raised up in spirit; send Lakṣmaṇa with us who will draw the armies along.

**6.71.18**  "This tiger among men will, with sharp arrows, cause Rāvaṇa's son to abandon his rite — and thereafter he will be fit to be slain.

**6.71.19**  "His arrows, sharpened and keen, with feathers of wings, shafts of reeds, and horse-like fleetness, terrible as flying birds, shall drink blood.

**6.71.20**  "Therefore, O mighty-armed one, dispatch Lakṣmaṇa of auspicious marks for the destruction of that rākṣasa, as the thunderbolt-wielder hurls his thunderbolt.

**6.71.21**  "O foremost of men, there is no delay of the right moment when the slaying of the enemy is within reach — what you are able to do today, release the arrow-flight for the enemy's destruction, as great Indra smashed the demon's citadel."

**6.71.22**  "For when that lord of rākṣasas completes his rite he becomes invisible in battle even to gods and demons; if one were to fight with him after he has completed the rite, there would be great uncertainty even for the gods."

**6.72.1**  Hearing those words of his, Rāghava, wasted by grief, did not clearly comprehend what had been said by that rākṣasa.

**6.72.2**  Then Rāma, conqueror of enemy cities, steadying himself with composure, spoke to Vibhīṣaṇa who sat beside him, in the presence of the monkeys:

**6.72.3**  "O lord of the Nairṛtas, Vibhīṣaṇa, what you have said — I wish to hear it again; speak what you intend to say."

**6.72.4**  Hearing Rāghava's words, Vibhīṣaṇa, skilled in speech, once more spoke these words:

**6.72.5**  "O mighty-armed one, the stationing of the outposts as commanded by you, O hero, was carried out immediately after your order.

**6.72.6**  "All those divisions have been deployed on all sides, and the troop-commanders have been posted in proper order according to their assigned positions.

**6.72.7**  "But there is something more I must report — hear it, O greatly renowned one: with you tormented without cause, we are tormented in our hearts.

**6.72.8**  "Abandon, O king, this sorrow, this false anguish that has come upon you — let this anxiety be cast off, for it increases the enemy's joy.

**6.72.9**  "Let effort be made, O hero; let courage be cultivated — if Sītā is to be regained and the night-wanderers are to be slain.

**6.72.10**  "O joy of the Raghus, I shall speak — hear my beneficial words: let Saumitri go, attended by a great army, to Nikumbhilā, to slay Rāvaṇa's son in battle.

**6.72.11**  "That great archer, victor in battle, to slay Rāvaṇa's son with arrows released from the arc of the bow, venomous as the poison of a serpent.

**6.72.12**  "By that hero through his austerities and by boon from the Self-born, the Brahmaśiras weapon was obtained, as well as horses that go wherever desired.

**6.72.13**  "'The enemy who slays you, O enemy of Indra, before you have reached Nikumbhilā and before you have offered into the fire — that is your death' — thus, O king, the death of that wise one has been ordained.

**6.72.14**  "O Rāma, dispatch that mighty one for the slaying of Indrajit; with him slain, know that Rāvaṇa is slain together with his kinsmen."

**6.72.15**  Having heard Vibhīṣaṇa's words, Rāma then spoke: "I know the illusion of that terrible one, O you whose valour is truth.

**6.72.16**  "He is skilled in the Brahmā-weapon, wise, a great master of illusion, of great strength — in battle he renders even the gods together with Varuṇa unconscious.

**6.72.17**  "Of him moving through the sky in his chariot, O greatly renowned one, the course is not known, as the sun's course is unknown in a confluence of clouds.

**6.72.18**  But Rāghava, having understood the illusory power of that evil-souled enemy, spoke these words to Lakṣmaṇa endowed with glory:

**6.72.19**  "Surrounded by all of the monkey-king's forces, together with the troop-commanders headed by Hanūmān, O Lakṣmaṇa,

**6.72.20**  "surrounded by the army together with Jāmbavān, the lord of bears — slay that son of the rākṣasa, skilled in the power of illusion.

**6.72.21**  "This great-souled night-wanderer, who knows that terrain, will follow at your back together with his counsellors."

**6.72.22**  Hearing Rāghava's words, Lakṣmaṇa of fierce valour, together with Vibhīṣaṇa, took up another supreme bow.

**6.72.23**  Girded, clad in armour, bearing a sword, equipped with arrows, holding a golden bow, Saumitri touched Rāma's feet and spoke with delight:

**6.72.24**  "Today arrows loosed from my bow, piercing through Rāvaṇa's son, shall swoop upon Laṅkā as geese swoop upon a lotus-lake.

**6.72.25**  "This very day my arrows, released from the string of the great bow and slaying that terrible one, shall scatter his body."

**6.72.26**  Having thus spoken these words before his glorious brother, Lakṣmaṇa, eager for the slaying of Rāvaṇa's son, departed swiftly.

**6.72.27**  Having paid reverence to his elder's feet and circumambulated him, he marched to Nikumbhilā, the shrine guarded by Rāvaṇa's son.

**6.72.28**  The royal prince Lakṣmaṇa of great majesty, accompanied by Vibhīṣaṇa, having received his brother's blessing, went forth swiftly.

**6.72.29**  Hanūmān, surrounded by many thousands of monkeys, and Vibhīṣaṇa with his ministers, then followed after Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.72.30**  Swiftly surrounded by the great monkey-army, he also beheld on the road the forces of the king of bears standing arrayed.

**6.72.31**  Saumitri, delight of his friend, having traversed a great distance of road, saw from afar the army of the rākṣasa-lord drawn up in battle array.

**6.72.32**  He, bow in hand, arriving at the site of the illusion-rite, O subduer of foes, stood firm in accordance with the Brahman procedure, in order to conquer — that joy of the Raghu line.

**6.72.33**  He entered that army of enemies — blazing with the radiance of diverse immaculate weapons, dense with banners, vast with great charioteers, most terrifying, of immeasurable speed — like darkness.

**6.73.1**  Then, in that situation, the younger brother of Rāvaṇa [Vibhīṣaṇa] spoke to Lakṣmaṇa words that were purposeful and harmful to the enemy.

**6.73.2**  "Strive, O Lakṣmaṇa, to break through this great army — when it is broken, the son of the lord of rākṣasas will become visible here."

**6.73.3**  "Therefore, showering the foes with arrows like unto Indra's thunderbolts, charge swiftly until this task is accomplished."

**6.73.4**  "Slay Rāvaṇi — that wicked hero, that master of illusion, that unrighteous one of cruel deeds, the terror of all the worlds."

**6.73.5**  Having heard Vibhīṣaṇa's words, Lakṣmaṇa of auspicious marks poured down showers of arrows against the son of the lord of rākṣasas.

**6.73.6**  Bears and monkeys too, those foremost fighters with trees and boulders, charged together against that standing army.

**6.73.7**  And the rākṣasas, eager to slay the monkey army, came on with sharp arrows, swords, śaktis, and raised tomaras.

**6.73.8**  That tumultuous clash between the monkeys and rākṣasas arose, reverberating Laṅkā on all sides with a great roar.

**6.73.9**  The sky was filled with weapons of many forms, with sharp arrows, uprooted trees, and dreadful mountain peaks raised aloft.

**6.73.10**  Those rākṣasas with distorted faces and arms, driving their weapons into the monkeys, caused them very great terror.

**6.73.11**  Likewise the monkeys, with whole trees and mountain peaks, struck and smote the foremost rākṣasas in battle.

**6.73.12**  As the rākṣasas were being slaughtered by those great-bodied, mighty-armed chiefs of bears and monkeys, a great fear arose among them.

**6.73.13**  But hearing that his own army was distressed and afflicted by the enemy, the invincible one arose, that task yet unaccomplished.

**6.73.14**  Emerging from the darkness of the trees, that rākṣasa Rāvaṇi, filled with fury, mounted a chariot already yoked and ready.

**6.73.15**  He with his dreadful bow and arrows, resembling a mass of black collyrium, with red mouth and red eyes, cruel, shone like Death himself, the Ender.

**6.73.16**  And upon seeing him stationed in his chariot, that army of rākṣasas of fearful speed, eager to fight with Lakṣmaṇa, rallied about.

**6.73.17**  At that moment Hanūmān, the subduer of enemies, resembling a bearer of the earth, raising an enormous tree that was very hard to face —

**6.73.18**  — that monkey, like the fire of dissolution scorching the rākṣasas' army, rendered it insensible in battle with blow after blow of trees.

**6.73.19**  Seeing the son of the Wind-god destroying them with speed, thousands of rākṣasas showered down upon Hanūmān.

**6.73.20**  Those bearing sharp tridents with their tridents, those with swords in hand with their swords, those holding śaktis with their śaktis, those armed with paṭṭasas with their paṭṭasas —

**6.73.21**  — and with iron clubs, maces, and fine-looking lances, and with hundreds of śataghnīs and iron hammers —

**6.73.22**  — and with dreadful axes and bhiṇḍipālas, and with fists swift as thunderbolts and palms like lightning-bolts, those rākṣasas struck him.

**6.73.23**  Closing in from all sides they struck the one who resembled a mountain; and upon them too, in great fury, he wrought terrible carnage.

**6.73.24**  Indrajit beheld the best of monkeys, the son of the Wind, steadfast as a mountain, slaying enemies and routing foes.

**6.73.25**  He said to his charioteer: "Drive to where that monkey is — ignored, he would bring utter destruction upon us rākṣasas."

**6.73.26**  Thus addressed by him, the charioteer drove to where Māruti stood, bearing the supremely invincible Indrajit in the chariot.

**6.73.27**  That invincible rākṣasa, coming close, rained down upon the monkey's head arrows, swords, paṭṭasas, blades, and battle-axes.

**6.73.28**  Receiving those dreadful weapons, Māruti, blazing with great rage, spoke these words indeed.

**6.73.29**  "Fight, if you are a hero, O son of Rāvaṇa, O fool! Having come against the son of the Wind, you shall not return alive."

**6.73.30**  "Fight with your bare arms if you desire single combat with me in battle — bear my speed, O dim-wit! Then you would indeed be the foremost among rākṣasas."

**6.73.31**  Vibhīṣaṇa pointed out to Lakṣmaṇa the son of Rāvaṇa, intent on killing Hanūmān and with his bow raised.

**6.73.32**  "He who conquered Vāsava [Indra], the very son born of Rāvaṇa himself — that one has mounted his chariot and seeks to kill Hanūmān."

**6.73.33**  "Slay Rāvaṇi, O son of Sumitrā, with arrows of incomparable form, enemy-rending, life-ending, and dreadful."

**6.73.34**  Thus spoken to then by the great-souled Vibhīṣaṇa, who is terror to foes, he beheld Rāvaṇi stationed in his chariot — that one resembling a mountain, of fearful power, hard to approach.

**6.74.1**  Having thus addressed the son of Sumitrā, the joyful Vibhīṣaṇa, taking him who had his bow in hand, went in haste.

**6.74.2**  Going not far from there and entering the great forest, Vibhīṣaṇa revealed that act to Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.74.3**  The brother of Rāvaṇa, full of radiance, pointed out to Lakṣmaṇa a banyan tree resembling a dark rain-cloud, terrible to behold.

**6.74.4**  "Here the mighty son of Rāvaṇa offers oblations to the spirits, and thereafter advances to battle."

**6.74.5**  "After that, the rākṣasa becomes invisible to all beings; he slays enemies in battle and binds them with excellent arrows."

**6.74.6**  "Before the mighty son of Rāvaṇa enters the banyan tree, destroy him together with his chariot, horses, and charioteer, with sharp arrows."

**6.74.7**  Saying "So be it," the great-spirited son of Sumitrā, the delight of his friends, stood there twanging his wonderful bow.

**6.74.8**  Then the mighty son of Rāvaṇa, Indrajit, became visible there in his chariot the colour of fire, clad in armour, bearing a sword, with his banner raised.

**6.74.9**  The great-spirited one spoke to that undefeated descendant of Pulastya: "I challenge you in battle — give me a proper fight!"

**6.74.10**  Thus addressed, the great-spirited and noble son of Rāvaṇa spoke harsh words there, having seen Vibhīṣaṇa.

**6.74.11**  "You were born and raised here; you are in truth the brother of my father — how can you work against your nephew's son, O rākṣasa? You are my uncle!"

**6.74.12**  "Kinship is no authority for you, nor friendship, nor birth, nor brotherhood, nor dharma — O defiler of dharma, O fool!"

**6.74.13**  "You are pitiable, O fool, and censured by the righteous — you who, abandoning your own kin, have come to serve others."

**6.74.14**  "With your slack intellect you do not know the great difference — what a thing it is to dwell with one's own kin, and what a thing to take refuge with the base!"

**6.74.15**  "Whether the stranger be virtuous or one's own kin be without virtue, a kinsman without virtue is still better — for a stranger is a stranger indeed."

**6.74.16**  "And this pitilessness of yours, O night-wanderer, O younger brother of Rāvaṇa — that you can speak harshly against your own kind!"

**6.74.17**  Thus addressed by his brother's son, Vibhīṣaṇa replied: "As if unknowing of my character — why do you boast, O rākṣasa?"

**6.74.18**  "O son of the lord of rākṣasas, O wicked one — abandon harshness out of respect. Though I was born in a lineage of rākṣasas of cruel deeds, this is indeed the first virtue of men; and therefore my character is free of rākṣasa-nature."

**6.74.19**  "I do not delight in cruelty; I do not delight in unrighteousness. How can a brother be abandoned because of a brother's uneven conduct?"

**6.74.20**  "The seizure of others' wealth, the violation of others' wives, and excessive suspicion of friends — these three faults bring ruin."

**6.74.21**  "The dreadful slaughter of great seers, conflict with all the gods, arrogance, anger, making enemies, and contrariness —"

**6.74.22**  "— these faults of my brother, which destroy life and sovereignty, have covered over his virtues as rain-clouds cover the mountains."

**6.74.23**  "Because of these faults I have abandoned the brother who is your father. This city of Laṅkā is nothing, and neither are you, nor is your father."

**6.74.24**  "You are arrogant, immature, and ill-bred, O rākṣasa; you are bound by the noose of Time — say to me whatever you wish."

**6.74.25**  "Today disaster has come upon you — what more will you say here? You will not be able to enter the banyan tree, O vilest of rākṣasas."

**6.74.26**  "Having offended the two descendants of Kākutstha, you cannot live. Fight in battle with the god among men, Lakṣmaṇa; slain, you will serve the gods' purpose in Yama's realm."

**6.74.27**  "Show your own strength, fully roused; expend all your weapons and arrows. Having come within range of Lakṣmaṇa's arrows today, you and your forces shall not go away alive."

**6.75.1**  Having heard Vibhīṣaṇa's words, Rāvaṇi, stupefied with fury, spoke harsh words and leaped forward with speed.

**6.75.2**  Standing in the great, well-adorned chariot yoked with black horses, his weapon and sword raised, he resembled Death, the Ender, in his wrath.

**6.75.3**  Taking up his great-sized, broad, swift, strong, and dreadful bow, and grasping arrows that destroy enemies —

**6.75.4**  — he, filled with fury, addressed the son of Sumitrā along with Vibhīṣaṇa and those tiger-like monkeys: "Witness my prowess!"

**6.75.5**  "Today in battle will you ward off the shower of arrows loosed from my bow — hard to face, like rain released into the sky?"

**6.75.6**  "Today my arrows, issuing from my great bow, will scatter your limbs as fire does a heap of cotton."

**6.75.7**  "Pierced by sharp arrows, by tridents, śaktis, ṛṣṭis, and tomaras, I will today send you all to Yama's abode."

**6.75.8**  "Who will stand before me in battle as I rain down showers of arrows with my swift hand, roaring like a rain-cloud?"

**6.75.9**  Hearing that roar of the lord of rākṣasas, Lakṣmaṇa then with fearless face, filled with anger, spoke these words to Rāvaṇi.

**6.75.10**  "You have spoken of the far shore of deeds as hard to reach, O rākṣasa; but he is the wise one who reaches the far shore of deeds by action itself."

**6.75.11**  "You, who fall short of the goal, having spoken words about a purpose hard for anyone to attain, consider yourself accomplished — O fool!"

**6.75.12**  "The way you practised in battle back then — going invisible — that is the path followed by thieves; it is not the path heroes follow."

**6.75.13**  "Even as I stand here within range of your arrows, O rākṣasa — reveal that power today. Why do you boast only with words?"

**6.75.14**  Thus addressed, the mighty Indrajit, grasping his dreadful bow, released sharp arrows, victorious in battle.

**6.75.15**  Those arrows, released with great speed, equal to serpent-venom, reached Lakṣmaṇa and fell upon him as if hissing like snakes.

**6.75.16**  The swift son of Rāvaṇa, Indrajit, pierced in battle with arrows of very great speed the auspiciously-marked son of Sumitrā.

**6.75.17**  His limbs deeply pierced by arrows and drenched in blood, the glorious Lakṣmaṇa shone like a smokeless fire.

**6.75.18**  Indrajit, observing his own feat and exulting in it, let out a very great roar and spoke these words.

**6.75.19**  "These feathered, keen-edged arrows released from my bow, life-ending, will take your life today, O son of Sumitrā!"

**6.75.20**  "Today let the packs of jackals, the flocks of hawks, and the vultures descend upon you, O Lakṣmaṇa — lifeless, slain by me!"

**6.75.21**  "Rāma, ever noble, a mere kshatriya-aspirant, of utterly confused mind, will today see you his devoted brother — slain by me!"

**6.75.22**  "Your armour shattered, your bow cast aside on the ground, your head severed, O son of Sumitrā — today I shall slay you!"

**6.75.23**  As the son of Rāvaṇa spoke thus, furious and harsh, Lakṣmaṇa replied to him with words exceedingly reasoned.

**6.75.24**  "Without accomplishing the deed, why do you boast here, O rākṣasa? Do that deed by which I would believe your boasting."

**6.75.25**  "Without speaking a single harsh word, without belittling you, without boasting, I will slay you — watch, O devourer of men!"

**6.75.26**  Having spoken thus, Lakṣmaṇa drove five iron-tipped arrows, drawn to his ear and of great velocity, deep into the rākṣasa's chest.

**6.75.27**  Struck by those arrows, the son of Rāvaṇa, filled with rage, pierced Lakṣmaṇa in return with three well-aimed arrows.

**6.75.28**  There arose a most dreadful and tumultuous combat in battle between those two lions — the man and the rākṣasa — each seeking to slay the other.

**6.75.29**  Both were possessed of great strength, both were endowed with valour, both were supremely courageous, and both were skilled in all weapons and missiles.

**6.75.30**  Both supremely hard to conquer, both of incomparable strength and splendour, those two great heroes fought like two planets colliding in the sky.

**6.75.31**  Like Bala and Vṛtra, those two great-souled ones, truly invincible in battle, fought like two lions.

**6.75.32**  Releasing many torrents of arrows as they stood firm, those two lions — the man and the rākṣasa — fought with great exhilaration.

**6.75.33**  Greatly exhilarated, those two foremost beings — man and rākṣasa — each seeking victory, bearing arrows and bows, rained heavily upon each other with floods of arrows, like two rain-clouds.

**6.76.1**  Then the son of Daśaratha, crusher of enemies, fitted an arrow to his bow and discharged it at the lord of rākṣasas, hissing in rage like a serpent.

**6.76.2**  Hearing that twang of the bowstring, the son of Rāvaṇa turned pale in the face and stared at Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.76.3**  Seeing the rākṣasa, son of Rāvaṇa, with downcast face, Vibhīṣaṇa addressed Saumitri who was intent on battle.

**6.76.4**  "I observe the omens in this son of Rāvaṇa — hasten, O mighty-armed one; this one is broken, without doubt."

**6.76.5**  Then Saumitri fitted sharp arrows resembling flames of fire and released them all at him, each blazing with venom as it were.

**6.76.6**  Struck by Lakṣmaṇa's arrows, whose impact equalled Indra's thunderbolt, he became bewildered for a moment, all his senses convulsed.

**6.76.7**  Regaining consciousness after a moment, his senses restored, the hero beheld the hero, the son of Daśaratha, standing firm before him.

**6.76.8**  His eyes red with fury, he charged at Saumitri, and approaching him, spoke harsh words once again.

**6.76.9**  "Do you not remember my valor in that earlier battle, when you and your brother lay bound, writhing on the field?"

**6.76.10**  "Both of you in that great battle were struck down to the earth first by arrows equal to Indra's thunderbolt, lying unconscious together with your vanguard."

**6.76.11**  "Either you have no memory of it, I think, or you clearly wish to go to the abode of Yama, since you desire to challenge me."

**6.76.12**  "If you did not witness my valor in the first battle, today I shall show it to you — stand firm now, resolute!"

**6.76.13**  Having spoken thus, he pierced Lakṣmaṇa with seven arrows, and Hanūmant with ten excellent keen-edged shafts.

**6.76.14**  Then the valiant one, his fury doubled by wrath, pierced Vibhīṣaṇa with a hundred well-aimed arrows.

**6.76.15**  Seeing that act of Indrajit accomplished, Rāma's younger brother then, giving it no thought, laughed and declared: "This is nothing."

**6.76.16**  That bull among men, Lakṣmaṇa, undaunted of countenance and furious, drew and released terrible arrows at Rāvaṇi in battle.

**6.76.17**  "Heroes who have entered battle do not strike in this fashion, O night-wanderer — these arrows of yours are light, feeble in power, and easily endured."

**6.76.18**  "Heroes who aspire to victory in battle do not fight in such a manner." Saying this to him, Lakṣmaṇa covered him with a shower of arrows.

**6.76.19**  His gold-adorned armor, shattered by those arrows, crumbled onto the floor of the chariot like a net of stars falling from the sky.

**6.76.20**  His armor stripped by the iron shafts, his body covered with wounds, the valiant Indrajit in battle resembled a mountain overgrown with flowering plants.

**6.76.21**  Hissing repeatedly, they fought a tumultuous fight; the limbs of each were cut all over by arrows, and both were drenched in blood on every side.

**6.76.22**  Those two foremost knowers of weapons, displaying their missiles again and again, wove arrows of every shape and form into nets across the sky.

**6.76.23**  Shooting with flawless precision, swiftly and with wonderful skill, the two — man and rākṣasa — made a tumultuous and terrible combat.

**6.76.24**  From those two, one after the other, a dreadful clap of palms upon bows was heard — like two terrifying clouds thundering in the sky.

**6.76.25**  The gold-feathered arrows that fell upon their bodies in the fight, smeared with blood, flew out and sank into the earth.

**6.76.26**  With other sharpened weapons they clashed in the sky; their arrows shattered and split each other by the thousands.

**6.76.27**  That terrible pile of arrows heaped in the battle between them was like a pile of sacrificial kuśa grass kindled by two blazing fires.

**6.76.28**  The wound-covered bodies of those two great-souled warriors shone beautifully, like leafless but flowering śalmali and kuṃśuka trees in the forest.

**6.76.29**  Indrajit and Lakṣmaṇa, each desiring victory over the other, made a tumultuous and terrible clash again and again.

**6.76.30**  Lakṣmaṇa striking Rāvaṇi in battle, and Rāvaṇi striking Lakṣmaṇa — the two of them, smiting each other, felt no fatigue.

**6.76.31**  With networks of arrows lodged deep in their bodies, those two swift great warriors shone like two mountains overgrown with creepers.

**6.76.32**  All the limbs of those two, soaked in blood and thickly covered with arrows, blazed brilliantly, like fires aflame.

**6.76.33**  A great span of time passed as they fought, yet neither of them turned away from battle nor experienced fatigue.

**6.76.34**  Then, to dispel the battle-weariness and render service to the unvanquished Lakṣmaṇa at the fore of the fights, mighty Vibhīṣaṇa came forward and stood at the battlefront.

**6.77.1**  Seeing those two — the man and the rākṣasa — engaged in combat, that brave brother of Rāvaṇa stood at the head of the battle.

**6.77.2**  Taking his stand, he drew his great bow and discharged sharp-pointed great arrows at the rākṣasas.

**6.77.3**  Those arrows, resembling flames, fell with concentrated force and split the rākṣasas asunder, as thunderbolts split great mountains.

**6.77.4**  Vibhīṣaṇa's attendants too, those foremost rākṣasas, cut down the valiant rākṣasa warriors in battle with spears, swords, and broad-bladed weapons.

**6.77.5**  Vibhīṣaṇa surrounded by those rākṣasas shone like an elephant in the midst of exultant young elephants.

**6.77.6**  Then, urging on the monkeys who were eager for battle against the rākṣasas, that foremost of rākṣasas, knowing the right moment, spoke at the right time.

**6.77.7**  "This one alone stands here like the last refuge of the lord of rākṣasas — this is the remaining force of his army. Why do you lords of monkeys hold back?"

**6.77.8**  "When this wicked rākṣasa is slain at the head of battle, excluding Rāvaṇa himself, the remainder of his army will be destroyed."

**6.77.9**  "The valiant Prahasta has been slain, and the mighty Nikumbha, Kumbhakarṇa and Kumbha, and the night-wanderer Dhūmrākṣa —"

**6.77.10**  "— Akampana and Supārśva, the rākṣasa Cakramālī, Kampana, and those powerful ones Devāntaka and Narāntaka —"

**6.77.11**  "— having slain these many exceedingly powerful foremost rākṣasas, having crossed the ocean with their arms, let the rest be crossed like a mere cow's hoofprint!"

**6.77.12**  "This much alone remains for you to conquer here, O monkeys; all those rākṣasas proud of their strength have been slain when they came together."

**6.77.13**  "It is improper for me to cause the death of my elder brother's son; yet, setting aside compassion, for Rāma's sake I shall slay my brother's child."

**6.77.14**  "Even as I wish to kill him, tears well up and obstruct my eyes — but the mighty-armed Lakṣmaṇa himself shall settle that. O monkeys, gather together and slay his attendants who are near him!"

**6.77.15**  Urged on by that greatly renowned rākṣasa, the lords of monkeys rejoiced and brandished their tails.

**6.77.16**  Then those tiger-like monkeys, shrieking again and again, raised various cries — like peacocks at the sight of clouds.

**6.77.17**  Jāmbavān too, surrounded by all of them along with his own troops, struck the rākṣasas with rocks, claws, and teeth.

**6.77.18**  Those mighty rākṣasas, setting aside fear and bearing weapons of many kinds, surrounded the lord of bears as he was striking them down.

**6.77.19**  With arrows, sharp axes, broad-bladed swords, clubs, and javelins, they struck Jāmbavant in battle as he was smiting the rākṣasa army.

**6.77.20**  That tumultuous clash between the monkeys and rākṣasas arose, fierce and of great sound, like the terrible roar of the wrathful gods and asuras.

**6.77.21**  Hanūmant too, blazing with rage, uprooted a sāla tree from the mountain and, rushing forward, made a slaughter of the rākṣasas by the thousands.

**6.77.22**  Having given tumultuous battle to his paternal uncle Vibhīṣaṇa in the fight, Indrajit rushed again at Lakṣmaṇa, slayer of enemy heroes.

**6.77.23**  Those two heroes — Lakṣmaṇa and the rākṣasa — fought once again in battle, raining torrents of arrows and striking each other.

**6.77.24**  Those two mighty and swift ones repeatedly concealed each other with networks of arrows, like the moon and sun in the hot season hidden by clouds.

**6.77.25**  There was no perceiving the taking up of arrows, nor the fitting of them to the bow, nor the grasping of the bow itself, nor the releasing of shafts, nor the drawing back, nor any pause —

**6.77.26**  — nor the positioning of the fist, nor the aiming at the target could be seen in those two fighting there, such was the swiftness of their hands.

**6.77.27**  With nets of arrows launched at great speed from their bows covering the sky on all sides, no forms could be discerned — everything was covered in darkness, vast and all the more terrible.

**6.77.28**  At that time the wind did not blow, nor did fire blaze; the great sages murmured "may the worlds be safe!" and the gandharvas along with cāraṇas gathered and descended there.

**6.77.29**  Then Saumitri, with four arrows, pierced the four dark horses of that lion among rākṣasas, who were adorned with gold trappings.

**6.77.30**  Then with another broad-headed shaft, the glorious scion of the Rāghu line, with swift dexterity, severed the head of the charioteer who was about to drive away.

**6.77.31**  Seeing the charioteer slain in battle, the son of Rāvaṇa abandoned his battle-fury and was overcome with dejection.

**6.77.32**  Seeing the rākṣasa with downcast face, the leaders of the monkey troops were supremely jubilant and praised Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.77.33**  Then Pramāthī, Śarabha, Rabhasa, and Gandhamādana — those four lords of monkeys, unable to tolerate it, sprang into action.

**6.77.34**  Those monkeys of exceedingly great valor and terrible might leaped up swiftly and fell upon his four foremost horses.

**6.77.35**  Ridden down by those mountain-like monkeys, blood poured visibly from the mouths of the horses.

**6.77.36**  They slew his horses and smashed his great chariot, then leaped up again swiftly and stood at Lakṣmaṇa's side.

**6.77.37**  Rāvaṇi, leaping down from the chariot whose horses were slain and whose charioteer was destroyed, charged at Saumitri with a rain of arrows.

**6.77.38**  Then Lakṣmaṇa, resembling great Indra himself, repelled with massed shafts that foot-warrior who was discharging keen excellent arrows first — blocking him thoroughly with multitudes of arrows.

**6.78.1**  That night-wanderer of great splendor, Indrajit, his horses slain, standing on the ground, ablaze with supreme wrath, blazed with his own energy.

**6.78.2**  Those two bowmen, eagerly desiring to kill each other, came forth with victory as their purpose, like two bull-elephants emerging from a forest.

**6.78.3**  The rākṣasas and forest-dwelling monkeys, striking each other down, did not abandon their master in battle as they rushed hither and thither.

**6.78.4**  He, resorting to extreme swiftness, rained showers of arrows upon Lakṣmaṇa as Indra rains showers of rain.

**6.78.5**  But Lakṣmaṇa, crusher of enemies, without agitation, repelled that shower of arrows released by Indrajit, though it was exceedingly difficult to withstand.

**6.78.6**  Considering Lakṣmaṇa unpierceable by any weapon, the son of Rāvaṇa, Indrajit, supremely enraged, swiftly displaying his weapon-skill, pierced Lakṣmaṇa on the forehead with three well-feathered arrows.

**6.78.7**  With those arrows fixed in his forehead, the glorious scion of the Raghu line shone at the forefront of battle, as a mountain with three peaks.

**6.78.8**  Even so, though afflicted by the rākṣasa's arrows in the great battle, Lakṣmaṇa quickly shot him back with ten arrows.

**6.78.9**  Lakṣmaṇa and Indrajit, two heroes of mighty strength and bow, of terrible prowess, struck each other with pointed shafts.

**6.78.10**  Those two bowmen, advancing upon each other, pierced one another all over the body with terrible arrows, both of them resolved upon victory.

**6.78.11**  At him whose horses were slain, the more enraged Vibhīṣaṇa discharged five shafts upon his chest, equal in impact to the thunderbolt.

**6.78.12**  Those gold-feathered arrows, aimed true, piercing his body, emerged smeared with blood, red like great serpents.

**6.78.13**  Incensed at his paternal uncle, the mighty Indrajit, in the midst of the rākṣasas, took up a supreme arrow, bestowed by Yama.

**6.78.14**  Seeing that great arrow fitted to his bow, Lakṣmaṇa of great splendor and terrible valor took up another arrow of his own.

**6.78.15**  It was the one personally given by Kubera of immeasurable spirit in a dream — unconquerable, unbearable even by the gods and asuras together with Indra.

**6.78.16**  Those two excellent arrows, fitted to their excellent bows and being drawn taut by the two heroes, blazed mightily with glory.

**6.78.17**  Those two shafts, illuminating the sky as they flew from the bows, struck each other face to face and fell down together with force.

**6.78.18**  Those two, resembling great meteors, colliding with each other in battle, split into a hundred pieces and fell to the ground.

**6.78.19**  Seeing their arrows deflected at the head of battle, both Lakṣmaṇa and Indrajit were at once abashed and overcome with fresh rage.

**6.78.20**  Greatly enraged, Saumitri took up the water-weapon of Varuṇa; the great Indrajit, standing firm in battle, discharged the terrible weapon of Rudra.

**6.78.21**  A supremely tumultuous battle ensued between them, resembling a marvel; the beings dwelling in the sky surrounded Lakṣmaṇa on all sides.

**6.78.22**  In the dreadful battle, frightful with terrifying sounds, between the monkeys and rākṣasas, the sky appeared covered with many astonished beings.

**6.78.23**  Sages, ancestral spirits, gods, gandharvas, garuḍas, and serpents, placing Śatakratu (Indra) at their head, protected Lakṣmaṇa in battle.

**6.78.24**  Then the younger brother of Rāvaṇa (Vibhīṣaṇa) fitted another foremost shaft — one whose touch was like the sacred fire, and terrible for the son of Rāvaṇa.

**6.78.25**  A shaft with beautiful feathers, smooth-bodied, with fine joints, well-formed, wrought with gold — the hero fitted an arrow that would end a life.

**6.78.26**  Hard to ward off, hard to endure, inspiring fear in the rākṣasas, like the venom of a deadly serpent, honored by the hosts of gods —

**6.78.27**  — with which the mighty lord Indra, of great splendor, mounted on his bay steeds, had long ago conquered the dānavas in the battle of gods and asuras —

**6.78.28**  — that weapon of Indra, unconquered in battles, that foremost of shafts — the foremost of men, Saumitri, fitted it to his foremost bow.

**6.78.29**  Having fitted the enemy-destroying arrow, he drew the strung bow taut — unapproachable, like Time at the dissolution of the worlds.

**6.78.30**  Having fitted it to the excellent bow and drawing back, the glorious Lakṣmaṇa spoke these words that would accomplish his purpose.

**6.78.31**  "If Rāma, the son of Daśaratha, is truly righteous in soul and true to his vows, and if he is without rival in valor, then slay this son of Rāvaṇa!"

**6.78.32**  Having spoken thus, the heroic Lakṣmaṇa, slayer of enemy heroes, drew that straight arrow back to his ear and discharged it at Indrajit, joined with the Aindra weapon.

**6.78.33**  That head, along with its helmet and its glowing earrings in their glory, was torn from Indrajit's body and fell upon the ground.

**6.78.34**  The great head of that son of a rākṣasa, severed at the neck, resembling burnished gold, was seen on the ground soaked in blood.

**6.78.35**  Slain, the son of Rāvaṇa fell swiftly to the earth, clad in his armor, still wearing his head-guard, shattered, bow in hand.

**6.78.36**  Then all the monkeys together with Vibhīṣaṇa roared aloud, rejoicing at his slaying, as the gods had at the slaying of Vṛtra.

**6.78.37**  Then in the sky there arose the roar of the beings, of the great-souled sages, and of the gandharvas and apsarases as well.

**6.78.38**  Recognizing their lord as fallen, that great rākṣasa army, being slaughtered, fled in all directions from the monkeys who now shone with victory.

**6.78.39**  Slaughtered by the monkeys, those rākṣasas cast away their weapons and ran, all turned toward Laṅkā, their senses lost.

**6.78.40**  The rākṣasas, terrified, fled by hundreds in every direction, all abandoning their weapons — broad-bladed swords, axes, and halberds.

**6.78.41**  Some, stricken with panic and tormented by the monkeys, fled into Laṅkā; some fell into the ocean; some took refuge on the mountain.

**6.78.42**  Seeing Indrajit, who had been the protector in battle, lying slain, not a single one among thousands of rākṣasas was to be seen standing firm.

**6.78.43**  As the rays of the sun do not remain when the sun has set, so when he had fallen, those rākṣasas dispersed in all directions.

**6.78.44**  That one of great energy became like the sun with its rays extinguished, like a fire that has been quenched — cast aside, his life departed.

**6.78.45**  When the son of the lord of rākṣasas had fallen, the world was relieved of its torment in great measure, its enemy destroyed, full of jubilation.

**6.78.46**  The blessed Indra, together with all the foremost gods, was filled with joy when that rākṣasa of evil deeds was slain.

**6.78.47**  The waters became pure, and the sky rejoiced; the daityas and dānavas came forth when he who had been a terror to all the worlds had fallen.

**6.78.48**  And all of them — gods, gandharvas, and dānavas — said together, freed from fever, their impurities calmed: "Let the brāhmaṇas move about freely!"

**6.78.49**  Then, supremely delighted, the leaders of the monkey troops in battle applauded, seeing that foremost of rākṣasas who was unmatched in strength lying slain.

**6.78.50**  Vibhīṣaṇa, Hanūmant, and Jāmbavant the lord of the bear troops, felicitating him with victory, also praised Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.78.51**  The monkeys, shrieking, roaring, and thundering, having accomplished their aim, surrounded and attended upon the son of the Raghu line.

**6.78.52**  The monkeys, whirling their tails and clapping their hands, then proclaimed these words aloud: "Victory to Lakṣmaṇa!"

**6.78.53**  The monkeys, joyful at heart, embracing one another, engaged in tales of all kinds of valor rooted in their devotion to Rāghava.

**6.78.54**  Beholding that difficult feat of Lakṣmaṇa in battle, his beloved companions were filled with joy; and the gods, hearing that the enemy of Indra had been slain, achieved the highest delight of heart.

**6.79.1**  Lakṣmaṇa, auspiciously marked, his limbs drenched with blood, became joyful having slain Śakrajit in battle.

**6.79.2**  Then that mighty hero of great splendour, having turned back Jāmbavān, Hanūmant, and all those forest-dwellers,

**6.79.3**  came quickly to where Sugrīva and Rāghava were — Lakṣmaṇa, leaning on Vibhīṣaṇa and Hanūmant.

**6.79.4**  Then Saumitri approached Rāma, saluted him, and stood near his brother as Indra's younger brother stands by Indra; and the hero then reported the terrible slaying of Indrajit.

**6.79.5**  Vibhīṣaṇa, filled with joy, then reported to Rāma the severing of Rāvaṇa's son's head by the great-souled Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.79.6**  That bull among men seated him on his lap, embraced him warmly, smelled his head, and touching him again and again in haste, spoke words to comfort Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.79.7**  "A most auspicious deed has been done by you, performer of the most difficult tasks. Today I am made free of enemies. For Rāvaṇa will march forth with a great arrayed force upon hearing that his son has been slain."

**6.79.8**  "That lord of rākṣasas, scorched by the death of his son, when he sallies forth, I shall slay him — that unconquerable one — surrounding him with a great army."

**6.79.9**  "With you, Lakṣmaṇa, as my protector, neither Sītā nor the earth is beyond my reach — now that Śakrajit has been slain today in battle."

**6.79.10**  Having consoled and embraced his brother, Rāghava Rāma, filled with delight, addressed Suṣeṇa and said this:

**6.79.11**  "This great wise one, Saumitri, dear to his friends, still has arrows in him; treat him so that he fully recovers. Let Saumitri, together with Vibhīṣaṇa, be quickly freed of shafts."

**6.79.12**  "Also tend to the brave monkey warriors, fighters from trees, and to all others here who fought and remain wounded with arrows — let all of them be carefully made comfortable by you."

**6.79.13**  Thus addressed by Rāma, the great-souled Suṣeṇa, chief of the monkey troops, caused the supreme medicinal herb to be brought to the nose of Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.79.14**  Having inhaled its fragrance, Lakṣmaṇa became free of all arrows at once, free of pain, and his wounds were healed.

**6.79.15**  Then by Rāghava's command, Suṣeṇa treated Vibhīṣaṇa and those allied friends, and all the foremost monkey leaders.

**6.79.16**  Then, free of arrows, free of pain, restored to his natural state, Saumitri, joyful, was in an instant rid of fever.

**6.79.17**  Seeing Saumitri rise hale and healthy, Rāma, the king of the leaping monkeys, Vibhīṣaṇa, and Jāmbavān the lord of bears — along with their forces — rejoiced greatly and for a long time.

**6.79.18**  The great-souled son of Daśaratha honoured Lakṣmaṇa's most extraordinary deed; and the chiefs of the monkey troops became exuberant upon hearing that Śakrajit had been brought down in battle.

**6.80.1**  Then the ministers of Paulastya, upon hearing that Indrajit was slain, and recognising the event, reported it with anguish to the ten-necked one.

**6.80.2**  "O great king of great splendour, your son has been slain in battle by Lakṣmaṇa, aided by Vibhīṣaṇa, before our very eyes."

**6.80.3**  "Your heroic son, never defeated in battles, Vibudhendrajit — meeting hero against hero — has been slain by the hero Lakṣmaṇa."

**6.80.4**  Hearing that terrifying, dreadful report of his son's cruel slaying — Indrajit's killing in battle — he fell into a great swoon.

**6.80.5**  After a long time recovering his senses, the king, foremost among rākṣasas, tormented by grief for his son, wretched and his faculties overwhelmed, began to lament.

**6.80.6**  "Alas, O foremost of rākṣasa armies, my child, great chariot-warrior who had conquered Indra himself — how have you today come under the power of Lakṣmaṇa?"

**6.80.7**  "Could you not, when enraged, pierce even Yama and Death with your arrows, could you not shatter even the peaks of mount Mandara — how then Lakṣmaṇa in battle?"

**6.80.8**  "Today the king Vaivasvata is even more honoured by me — he by whom you, O mighty-armed one, have today been united with the law of time."

**6.80.9**  "This is the path of great warriors even among all the hosts of immortals: the man who is slain for his master's sake attains heaven."

**6.80.10**  "Today all the hosts of gods, the world-guardians, and the sages will sleep in peace and without fear, having seen Indrajit slain."

**6.80.11**  "Today the three entire worlds and the earth with all its forests, bereft of Indrajit alone, seem void to me."

**6.80.12**  "Today I shall hear in the inner apartments the wailing of the rākṣasa women, like the trumpeting of a herd of female elephants in a mountain cave."

**6.80.13**  "O scorcher of foes, the crown-princeship, Laṅkā, the rākṣasas, your mother, me, and your wife — where have you gone, abandoning us all?"

**6.80.14**  "O hero, you who have gone to the abode of Yama — the funeral rites must be performed in your name; yet you conduct yourself in the inverse order, for you have preceded me."

**6.80.15**  "While Sugrīva still lives, and Rāghava with Lakṣmaṇa, you have gone without removing the thorn from my heart — where have you gone, leaving us?"

**6.80.16**  Rāvaṇa, the lord of rākṣasas, afflicted with laments such as these, was then seized by a great rage born of the calamity of his son's death.

**6.80.17**  His form, dreadful by nature, now blazing and swollen with the fire of rage, became like the form of Rudra when wrathful — unapproachable.

**6.80.18**  From his enraged eyes, drops of tears fell — like drops of oil with flames from two burning lamps.

**6.80.19**  As he gnashed his teeth, the sound of his grinding was heard like that of a great machine being turned by dānavas.

**6.80.20**  Like the fire of Time when fully enraged, into whichever direction he looked, the rākṣasas there, terrified, crouched and hid themselves.

**6.80.21**  Like Death himself when wrathful, desirous of devouring the moving and stationary, as he gazed in all directions, no rākṣasa dared approach him.

**6.80.22**  Then Rāvaṇa, supremely enraged, the lord of rākṣasas, spoke in the midst of the rākṣasas, wishing to rally them for battle:

**6.80.23**  "Having performed for thousands of years the most difficult austerities in diverse retreats, I satisfied the Self-Born one."

**6.80.24**  "By the fruit of that very austerity and by the grace of the Self-Born, I have never had fear from asuras or from gods."

**6.80.25**  "The armour given me by Brahmā, equal in brilliance to the sun, has not been pierced in the conflicts of gods and asuras even by the vajra or the śakti-spear."

**6.80.26**  "Who today, equipped with that armour and standing on my chariot in this fight, shall face me in battle — even Purandara himself?"

**6.80.27**  "That great bow along with arrows, given to me by the propitious Self-Born one for use in conflicts of gods and asuras —"

**6.80.28**  "— let that great and formidable bow be brought forward today with hundreds of drums sounding, for the slaying of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa in the supreme battle."

**6.80.29**  That hero, scorched by the death of his son and overcome by rage, Rāvaṇa, after deliberating in his mind, resolved to kill Sītā.

**6.80.30**  With his copper-red eyes, he looked around and, terrible-visaged, wretched, with a wretched voice, he addressed all those night-wanderers:

**6.80.31**  "Through my son's illusion, in order to deceive the forest-dwellers, a semblance of slaughter was shown there, and Sītā was shown as if dead."

**6.80.32**  "Now I shall truly do what pleases me — I shall destroy Vaidehī, who faithfully follows that base kṣatriya." Having said this to his counsellors, he quickly seized his sword.

**6.80.33**  Drawing it — that sword full of virtues, as bright as the clear sky — he rushed forth swiftly from the assembly hall, surrounded by his counsellors.

**6.80.34**  Rāvaṇa, his mind greatly agitated by grief for his son, enraged, taking up his sword, rushed to where Maithilī was.

**6.80.35**  Seeing the rākṣasa striding forward, the rākṣasas roared a lion-roar and, embracing one another, spoke to each other, beholding him in his fury:

**6.80.36**  "Today those two brothers, seeing him, will be distressed. For the four world-guardians were conquered by this one in his fury, and many other enemies were laid low by him in battles."

**6.80.37**  While they were thus talking amongst themselves, Rāvaṇa, intoxicated with rage, rushed toward Vaidehī who was in the aśoka grove.

**6.80.38**  Though restrained by friends of good intent, he charged forward, fully enraged, like a planet seizing Rohiṇī in the sky.

**6.80.39**  Maithilī, the blameless one, who was being guarded by the rākṣasī women, then saw the rākṣasa, wrathful and bearing a fine sword.

**6.80.40**  Beholding him sword in hand, the daughter of Janaka was overcome with distress, seeing him who, though restrained many times by friends, would not turn back.

**6.80.41**  "As this one charges toward me himself, enraged, the evil-minded one will slay me — who has a protector — as if I were without one."

**6.80.42**  "He urged me many times: 'Be the wife of Rāvaṇa,' urging me who am devoted to my husband — but he was refused by me."

**6.80.43**  "This one, having clearly reached despair at my non-compliance, is now seized by rage and delusion, and is prepared to kill me."

**6.80.44**  "Or perhaps those two tiger-like men, the brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, have been struck down today in battle by this ignoble one on account of me. Alas, shame — this destruction of the two princes is on account of me!"

**6.80.45**  "That word of Hanūmant was not heeded by me in my wretchedness. Had I, blameless one, come then upon his back, I would not be lamenting thus today, seated in my husband's lap as a faithful wife."

**6.80.46**  "I think the heart of Kausalyā will be fulfilled — when she, who has but one son, hears that her son has perished in battle."

**6.80.47**  "She, weeping, will recall the birth, childhood, and youth of that great-souled one, and his acts of righteousness and his beauty."

**6.80.48**  "Without hope, her son slain, having performed the śrāddha rite, senseless, she will surely mount the funeral pyre or enter the waters."

**6.80.49**  "Shame upon the hunchback, the wicked Mantharā of evil resolve — because of whom Kausalyā will obtain this sorrow."

**6.80.50**  Thus seeing Maithilī, the wretched one, lamenting — like Rohiṇī separated from the moon and seized by a planet —

**6.80.51**  — the wise one named Supārśva spoke these words to Rāvaṇa, the lord of rākṣasas, as he was being restrained by the counsellors:

**6.80.52**  "How is it, O ten-necked one, O own brother of Vaiśravaṇa, that you wish to slay Vaidehī, casting aside dharma in your rage?"

**6.80.53**  "O hero, lord of rākṣasas, you who are versed in the Vedas, who have completed your studies and vows, who are always devoted to your own dharma — why do you contemplate the slaying of a woman?"

**6.80.54**  "Behold Maithilī endowed with beauty, O king. Together with us, direct your anger at Rāghava himself."

**6.80.55**  "Making preparations today on the fourteenth of the dark fortnight, go forth on the new-moon day, surrounded by your forces, for victory."

**6.80.56**  "You, a hero, wise, a chariot-warrior, sword-bearer, mounting the finest chariot — having slain Rāma the son of Daśaratha, you shall obtain Maithilī."

**6.80.57**  That wicked-souled Rāvaṇa accepted the righteous words communicated by his friend, and went home; then that mighty one again proceeded to the assembly hall, accompanied by his friends.

**6.81.1**  Entering the assembly hall, the king, wretched and deeply grieved, sat down in the foremost seat, breathing like an angry lion.

**6.81.2**  Then mighty Rāvaṇa, wasted by the calamity of his son's death, addressed all the chiefs of the army with joined palms, and spoke:

**6.81.3**  "Let all of you, accompanied by all the elephants and horses, go forth, adorned with hosts of chariots and infantry."

**6.81.4**  "Surrounding Rāma alone in battle, you ought to slay him, rejoicing, with a downpour of arrows — like rain clouds at the onset of the monsoon season."

**6.81.5**  "Or else I myself, tomorrow in the great battle, with sharp arrows, his body pierced by you, shall slay Rāma before the eyes of the world."

**6.81.6**  Taking these words of the lord of rākṣasas to heart, the rākṣasas marched forth swiftly in chariots, surrounded by elephant formations.

**6.81.7**  At sunrise, a most fearsome and tumultuous battle between the rākṣasas and the monkeys took place.

**6.81.8**  The monkeys and rākṣasas struck one another in battle with variegated maces, lances, swords, and battle-axes.

**6.81.9**  Rivers of blood flowed, carrying the flotsam of that massed host of elephants and chariots — with horses as fish, banners as trees, and heaps of bodies as their current.

**6.81.10**  The monkey chieftains, leaping and leaping again in battle, shattered banners, armour, chariots, horses, and various weapons.

**6.81.11**  The leaping monkeys tore apart the hair, ears, foreheads, and noses of the rākṣasas with their sharp teeth and nails.

**6.81.12**  In battle, a hundred foremost monkeys rushed upon each single rākṣasa, as birds rush upon a fruit-laden tree.

**6.81.13**  Similarly, the rākṣasas, mountain-like, slaughtered the fierce monkeys with heavy maces, lances, swords, and battle-axes.

**6.81.14**  The great army of monkeys, being slaughtered by the rākṣasas, sought refuge in Rāma the son of Daśaratha, who is worthy of refuge.

**6.81.15**  Then Rāma of great splendour, mighty, taking up his bow, entered the rākṣasa army and rained down a torrent of arrows.

**6.81.16**  But they could not approach Rāma as he entered — most dreadful, burning them with the fire of his arrows — just as clouds do not approach the sun in the sky.

**6.81.17**  The night-wanderers witnessed in battle the most dreadful deeds already wrought by Rāma, and his extraordinary feats.

**6.81.18**  They saw him shaking the great host, scattering the great warriors — yet they did not truly see Rāma, just as one does not see wind that has entered a forest.

**6.81.19**  They saw the army cut down, split apart, burned by arrows, shattered, afflicted by weapons — but they did not see Rāma, the swift in action.

**6.81.20**  Striking at their bodies, they did not perceive Rāghava — just as living beings do not perceive the soul of all existence dwelling in the objects of the senses.

**6.81.21**  "Here he slays the elephant formations, here he slays the great chariot-warriors, here he slays the infantry and cavalry together with sharp arrows!"

**6.81.22**  Thus all those rākṣasas, enraged with one another in battle, struck those who looked like Rāma — owing to their resemblance to Rāghava.

**6.81.23**  They did not see Rāma burning the enemy host, confused by the supreme Gāndharva weapon of that great-souled one.

**6.81.24**  The rākṣasas in battle saw thousands of Rāmas; then again in the great combat they saw only one Kākutstha.

**6.81.25**  They saw the whirling golden tip of that great-souled one's bow, resembling a revolving firebrand wheel — but they did not see Rāghava.

**6.81.26**  His body was the hub, the flame of his valour was the spokes, his body with bow was the rim, the sound of his bowstring and the clapping of his palm were its noise, brilliant with the qualities of his energy and intelligence —

**6.81.27**  — bounded at its edge by the qualities of divine weapons, that wheel of Rāma slaying rākṣasas in battle was seen by the people like the wheel of Time itself.

**6.81.28**  A force of ten thousand swift-as-wind chariots, eighteen thousand swift elephants,

**6.81.29**  fourteen thousand horsemen together with their riders, and a full two hundred thousand rākṣasa foot-soldiers —
