**6.81.30**  — all of these shape-shifting rākṣasas were slain, in one eighth part of the day, by Rāma alone with arrows resembling tongues of flame.

**6.81.31**  Those night-wanderers who remained alive, their horses slain, their chariots destroyed, exhausted, their banners scattered, fled back to the city of Laṅkā.

**6.81.32**  Strewn with slain elephants, infantry, and horses, that battle-ground became like the playground of Rudra the wielder of the Pināka bow, when wrathful.

**6.81.33**  Then the gods together with the gandharvas, the siddhas, and the supreme sages honoured Rāma's deed with cries of "Well done! Well done!"

**6.81.34**  And Rāma then said in reply to Sugrīva: "This power of divine weapons belongs to me or to Tryambaka — the Three-eyed one."

**6.81.35**  Having slain that rākṣasa host, Rāma — the great-souled one, equal to Śakra, triumphant in weapons and arms, his weariness overcome — was then praised by the exultant hosts of gods.

**6.82.1**  Those thousands of elephants with their riders, and those horses, and the fire-colored chariots with their banners — by the thousands —

**6.82.2**  and thousands of rākṣasas fighting with clubs and iron bars, adorned with golden banners, heroes who could assume any form at will —

**6.82.3**  all these, deployed by Rāvaṇa and adorned with ornaments of refined gold, were slain by the sharp arrows of Rāma, whose deeds know no effort.

**6.82.4**  Seeing and hearing this, the surviving night-wanderers were bewildered; the rākṣasī women gathered together, wretched and overwhelmed with grief.

**6.82.5**  Made widows, their sons slain, weeping, their kinsmen killed — the rākṣasī women came together in affliction and lamented aloud.

**6.82.6**  "How did Śūrpaṇakhā, that aged, fearsome, pot-bellied hag, ever approach Rāma in the forest — Rāma who is fair as the god of love himself?

**6.82.7**  She, vile and ill-favored, fit to be slain by the world, saw him — that tender, mighty-souled prince devoted to the welfare of all beings — and was seized with desire.

**6.82.8**  How could that rākṣasī, void of all virtues and ugly-faced, lust after Rāma — Rāma who is full of every virtue, of great brilliance, and of beautiful countenance?

**6.82.9**  By reason of the ill-fortune of this people, that withered, white-haired creature committed an act that was wrong, ridiculous, and condemned by all the world.

**6.82.10**  That unseemly one, for the destruction of the rākṣasas and of Dūṣaṇa and Khara, provoked Rāghava — and this was utterly unfit for her to do.

**6.82.11**  It was for that reason that Rāvaṇa, the ten-necked rākṣasa, kindled this great enmity; he abducted Sītā in order to bring about his own destruction.

**6.82.12**  Yet Daśagrīva will never obtain Sītā, the daughter of Janaka; the enmity he has forged with the mighty Rāghava is bound and inexhaustible.

**6.82.13**  The evidence is clear: the rākṣasa Virādha, who coveted Vaidehī, was slain single-handedly by Rāma — that alone should have sufficed as a warning.

**6.82.14**  Fourteen thousand rākṣasas of terrible deeds were slain in Janasthāna by arrows that blazed like tongues of fire —

**6.82.15**  and Khara was slain in battle, and Dūṣaṇa and Triśiras too, by arrows bright as the sun. That too was warning enough.

**6.82.16**  Kabandha, the blood-drinker with arms a yojana long, roaring in his fury, was killed — that too was proof enough.

**6.82.17**  Rāma slew mighty Bālin, the son of the Thousand-Eyed One, who was dark as a cloud. That alone was evidence enough.

**6.82.18**  Sugrīva, who lived in misery on Mount Ṛśyamūka with broken hopes, was installed as king — that too was sign enough.

**6.82.19**  The words of Vibhīṣaṇa — right in both dharma and worldly interest, beneficial to all the rākṣasas, and well-reasoned — he did not heed, out of delusion.

**6.82.20**  If the younger brother of Dhanada had followed Vibhīṣaṇa's counsel, this city of Laṅkā would not now be reduced to a charnel ground of grief.

**6.82.21**  Even hearing that mighty Kumbhakarṇa had been slain by Rāghava, and his dear son Indrajit — Rāvaṇa does not come to his senses.

**6.82.22**  "My son is slain in battle, my brother is slain, my husband is slain" — such is the cry that is heard in every household of the rākṣasas.

**6.82.23**  Hundreds of thousands of chariots, horses, elephants, and rākṣasa foot-soldiers have been slain in battle by the heroic Rāma.

**6.82.24**  It is Rudra, or Viṣṇu, or the great Mahendra of a hundred sacrifices, or perhaps Death himself in the form of Rāma, who is destroying us.

**6.82.25**  Our champions slain by Rāma, we have no hope of life; seeing no end to our fear, masterless, we cry out in lamentation.

**6.82.26**  Daśagrīva, though a hero with boons granted, does not perceive this great and terrible fear that has arisen from Rāma's hand in battle.

**6.82.27**  Neither gods nor gandharvas, neither piśācas nor rākṣasas are capable of rescuing one whom Rāma has set upon in battle.

**6.82.28**  And evil omens appear in battle after battle for Rāvaṇa — they will narrate the destruction of Rāvaṇa by Rāma.

**6.82.29**  Pitāmaha, well pleased, granted Rāvaṇa immunity from gods, dānavas, and rākṣasas — but Rāvaṇa did not ask for freedom from humans.

**6.82.30**  This is the fear that I believe has come from humans — without doubt, the terrible fear that will end the lives of both the rākṣasas and Rāvaṇa.

**6.82.31**  The gods, tormented by the powerful rākṣasa with his boon, worshipped Pitāmaha with blazing austerities.

**6.82.32**  For the welfare of the gods, the great-souled Pitāmaha, satisfied, spoke this great word to all the deities:

**6.82.33**  "From this day forth, all dānavas and rākṣasas will roam the three worlds forever enveloped in fear, for all eternity."

**6.82.34**  Then the gods assembled together, with Indra at their head, and propitiated Mahādeva — the bearer of the bull-banner, the destroyer of Tripura.

**6.82.35**  Mahādeva, well pleased, spoke this word to the gods: "A woman shall be born for your welfare — one who will bring about the destruction of the rākṣasas."

**6.82.36**  "This Sītā, set in motion by the gods, will consume us — rākṣasas together with Rāvaṇa — as hunger once consumed the dānavas of old; she is the slayer of rākṣasas."

**6.82.37**  By the arrogance of Rāvaṇa, that ill-disciplined and evil-minded one, this terrible destruction has come, and we are overwhelmed with grief.

**6.82.38**  We see no one in the world who could be a refuge for us — attacked by Rāghava as if by Time itself at the dissolution of an age.

**6.82.39**  Thus all the women of the night-wanderers, embracing one another with their arms, sank into despair, tormented by affliction and overwhelming fear, and then cried out with a truly terrible wailing.

**6.83.1**  Rāvaṇa heard the pitiful sound of lamentation from the afflicted rākṣasī women in every household of Laṅkā.

**6.83.2**  He breathed a long sigh, remained absorbed in thought for a moment, and then Rāvaṇa, fearsome to behold, became supremely wrathful.

**6.83.3**  Biting his lip with his teeth, his eyes reddened with rage, so terrible that even rākṣasas could scarcely look upon him, he blazed like the fire at the end of time.

**6.83.4**  The lord of rākṣasas spoke to the rākṣasas standing near him — those whose speech was broken with fear — as if scorching them with his gaze.

**6.83.5**  "Tell Mahodara, Mahāpārśva, and the rākṣasa Virūpākṣa: by my command, march out with the armies at once."

**6.83.6**  Hearing those words, those rākṣasas, afflicted with fear, roused those steadfast rākṣasas by the king's command.

**6.83.7**  All those rākṣasas, terrible to behold, said "So be it"; having performed the rites of benediction, they all went before Rāvaṇa.

**6.83.8**  Those great chariot-warriors honored Rāvaṇa according to due rite and stood with joined palms, all of them desiring victory for their lord.

**6.83.9**  Then Rāvaṇa, maddened with rage, laughed and spoke to Mahodara, Mahāpārśva, and the rākṣasa Virūpākṣa:

**6.83.10**  "Today with arrows loosed from my bow — arrows like the sun at the end of an age — I shall dispatch both Rāghava and Lakṣmaṇa to the abode of Yama.

**6.83.11**  Today I shall avenge the killing of Khara, Kumbhakarṇa, Prahasta, and Indrajit by slaying the enemy.

**6.83.12**  Neither the sky, nor the directions, nor the rivers, nor the ocean will be able to shine, covered as they will be by the clouds of my arrows.

**6.83.13**  Today I shall churn those troops of monkey-bands, troop by troop, with waves of arrows rising from the ocean of my bow.

**6.83.14**  Today, like an elephant, I shall trample those ponds of monkey-bands — their faces like open lotuses, bright as the filaments of lotuses.

**6.83.15**  Today the leaders of the monkey-bands, their faces pierced with arrows in battle, will adorn the earth like lotus-stems with stalks.

**6.83.16**  Today, releasing a single shaft in battle, I shall split hundreds upon hundreds of those fierce fighting monkeys who wield trees as weapons.

**6.83.17**  For those women whose husbands, brothers, and sons have been slain — today, by killing the enemy, I perform the act of wiping away their tears.

**6.83.18**  Today with monkeys pierced by my arrows — scattered, lifeless — I shall make the earth in battle a surface fit for careful scrutiny.

**6.83.19**  Today I shall satisfy all the jackals, vultures, and other flesh-eaters with the flesh of enemies tormented by my arrows.

**6.83.20**  Let my chariot be made ready quickly; let my bow be brought at once; and let whatever night-wanderers remain follow me into battle."

**6.83.21**  Hearing those words, Mahāpārśva spoke: "Let those army-officers standing here muster the troops without delay."

**6.83.22**  The army-officers, resolute and swift in their valor, went about Laṅkā rousing those rākṣasas from house to house.

**6.83.23**  Within a moment, rākṣasas of terrible prowess burst forth, roaring, with terrible faces, their arms bearing various weapons.

**6.83.24**  With swords, broadblades, spears, nooses, clubs, ploughs, śaktis with keen edges, and great heavy mallets —

**6.83.25**  with staves, bright discuses, sharp battle-axes, slings, śataghnīs, and other excellent weapons —

**6.83.26**  then the four army-officers, by Rāvaṇa's command, quickly brought the chariot yoked with eight horses and fitted with a charioteer.

**6.83.27**  Rāvaṇa mounted the divine chariot blazing with its own radiance — his weight and gravity seeming to split the earth asunder.

**6.83.28**  Permitted by Rāvaṇa, Mahāpārśva and Mahodara, and the irresistible Virūpākṣa, then mounted their chariots.

**6.83.29**  Elated, roaring, as if splitting the earth, releasing terrible battle cries, they marched out, eager for victory.

**6.83.30**  Then the splendid Rāvaṇa, surrounded by the forces of his rākṣasa host, marched out for battle, bow upraised, like Yama the ender of time.

**6.83.31**  Then that great chariot-warrior rode out through the gate by which Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa stood, his chariot drawn by swift horses.

**6.83.32**  Then the sun lost its luster, the directions were shrouded in darkness, birds cried out ominously, and the earth trembled.

**6.83.33**  The sky rained blood; the horses stumbled; a vulture alighted on the top of the banner; and jackals howled inauspiciously.

**6.83.34**  His left eye twitched, his right arm trembled, his face grew pale, and his voice faltered somewhat.

**6.83.35**  Such were the portents that arose, presaging death in battle, as Daśagrīva the rākṣasa set forth into the fight.

**6.83.36**  A meteor fell from the sky with a sound like thunder; vultures cried out inauspiciously, echoed by crows.

**6.83.37**  Heeding not these terrible omens that had arisen, Rāvaṇa marched out in his delusion, goaded by Time, seeking his own death.

**6.83.38**  At the thunderous chariot-roar of those great-souled rākṣasas, the army of the monkeys also advanced for battle.

**6.83.39**  A very tumultuous battle ensued between those monkeys and rākṣasas, each challenging the other, enraged, each desiring victory.

**6.83.40**  Then Daśagrīva, enraged, wrought great carnage in the ranks of the monkeys with arrows adorned with gold.

**6.83.41**  Some monkeys had their heads severed by Rāvaṇa; some were slain and lay breathless; some were ripped open in their flanks; some had their skulls split; some were robbed of their eyes.

**6.83.42**  Daśānana, his eyes rolling with fury — wherever he advanced with his chariot in the fray, from that direction the leaders of the monkey-bands could not endure the velocity of his arrows.

**6.84.1**  Thus, with the monkeys whose bodies were cut down by Daśagrīva's shafts, the earth there became strewn and covered with monkeys.

**6.84.2**  That unendurable, blazing shower of Rāvaṇa's arrows from one side — they could not withstand it, as moths cannot endure a blazing fire.

**6.84.3**  Tormented by his sharp arrows, crying out, they fled in all directions — like elephants enveloped in tongues of flame, being burned.

**6.84.4**  Rāvaṇa moved through that battle scattering the armies of the leaping monkeys with his arrows, as the wind scatters great storm-clouds.

**6.84.5**  Having wrought swift carnage upon the forest-dwellers, the lord of rākṣasas then swiftly encountered Rāghava in battle.

**6.84.6**  Sugrīva, seeing those monkeys broken and fleeing from the battle, assigned Suṣeṇa to command a division and swiftly turned his mind to the fight.

**6.84.7**  Having placed that monkey hero who was his equal in valor in charge, Sugrīva advanced to face the enemy, armed with a tree.

**6.84.8**  On both sides and behind him, all the leaders of the monkey-bands personally carried great boulders and various mighty trees.

**6.84.9**  Great Sugrīva, roaring in battle with his mighty voice, felling various others, slew eminent rākṣasas.

**6.84.10**  The lord of monkeys, great of body, crushed the rākṣasas as the wind at the end of an age crushes the swollen deep-rooted trees.

**6.84.11**  He rained a downpour of mountains upon the rākṣasa armies, as a cloud rains a shower of stones upon flocks of birds in a forest.

**6.84.12**  Struck by those mountain-showers hurled by the king of monkeys, the rākṣasas fell with their heads scattered, like mountains with their peaks cut off.

**6.84.13**  Then, as the rākṣasas were being annihilated on all sides, broken by Sugrīva, falling and crying out —

**6.84.14**  the rākṣasa Virūpākṣa, a bowman, proclaiming his own name, leaped from his chariot and, irresistible, mounted the shoulder of an elephant.

**6.84.15**  Mounting that two-tusked elephant, Virūpākṣa the great chariot-warrior, roaring with a terrible thunder, charged at the monkeys.

**6.84.16**  He discharged terrible arrows at Sugrīva at the head of the army; he steadied the agitated rākṣasas and filled them with excitement.

**6.84.17**  The lord of monkeys, pierced through by those sharp arrows of that rākṣasa, blazed with great fury and set his mind upon killing him.

**6.84.18**  Then the heroic monkey, uprooting a tree, charged and struck that great elephant full in its face.

**6.84.19**  That great elephant, struck by the blow of Sugrīva, reeled back a bowshot's length, sank down, and bellowed.

**6.84.20**  Quickly dismounting from the stricken elephant, that valiant rākṣasa turned to face the enemy and advanced toward the monkey.

**6.84.21**  Seizing a bull-hide shield and sword, swift in his valor, he advanced upon Sugrīva who stood firm, as if threatening him.

**6.84.22**  Then Sugrīva, enraged at him, seizing an enormous boulder like a thundercloud, hurled it at Virūpākṣa.

**6.84.23**  That foremost of rākṣasas, seeing the boulder flying toward him, stepped aside with great agility and then struck with his sword.

**6.84.24**  With that sword, enraged, he knocked away Sugrīva's armor at the head of the army; struck by the sword, it fell to the ground.

**6.84.25**  The monkey, fallen but rising again, delivered at him a slap of his palm equal to a thunderbolt, with a fearsome sound.

**6.84.26**  The rākṣasa, with great skill, evading that palm-blow aimed by Sugrīva, struck him on the chest with his fist.

**6.84.27**  Then Sugrīva, lord of the monkeys, grew even more enraged, seeing that his own blow had been evaded by that rākṣasa.

**6.84.28**  The monkey saw an opening in Virūpākṣa's guard and then, in fury, brought down his great palm on his temple.

**6.84.29**  Struck by that palm-blow equal to Mahendra's thunderbolt, he fell to the earth soaked in blood, vomiting blood.

**6.84.30**  They beheld Virūpākṣa, his eyes rolled back in fury, drenched in frothy blood — made even more "virūpa" (grotesque) than his name.

**6.84.31**  The monkeys beheld their foe twitching, rolling to one side, drenched in blood, crying out piteously.

**6.84.32**  Thus those two swift warriors, joined in combat, the ocean-like forces of monkeys and rākṣasas, roared terribly — like two great oceans with their shores broken asunder.

**6.84.33**  Beholding that mighty Virūpākṣa slain by the lord of monkeys, the entire combined army of monkeys and rākṣasas became like the Gaṅgā in flood.

**6.85.1**  As their armies were swiftly slaughtering each other in that great battle, those two forces became like a pond in the height of summer, greatly diminished on both sides.

**6.85.2**  At the destruction of his own army and at the slaying of Virūpākṣa, Rāvaṇa, lord of the rākṣasas, became doubly enraged.

**6.85.3**  Seeing his army decimated, being slaughtered by the vānaras, anguish arose in him in the battle as he beheld the reversal of fortune brought by destiny.

**6.85.4**  He then spoke to Mahodara, the subduer of foes, who stood nearby: "At this moment, O mighty-armed one, my hope of victory rests in you."

**6.85.5**  "Destroy the enemy's army, O hero; show your valor today. This is the time to repay the master's bread — fight well!"

**6.85.6**  Thus addressed, Mahodara said "So be it" to the lord of rākṣasas, and plunged into the enemy's army like a moth into fire.

**6.85.7**  Then that mighty, brilliant one wrought devastation among the vānaras, driven on by his master's words and by his own valor.

**6.85.8**  Seeing the great army of the vānaras being broken in battle, Sugrīva immediately charged at Mahodara.

**6.85.9**  Seizing a vast, terrible boulder equal to a mountain, the lord of monkeys, blazing with great energy, hurled it for his destruction.

**6.85.10**  Seeing that boulder rushing upon him, Mahodara, unperturbed, shattered it with arrows — that thing so difficult to withstand.

**6.85.11**  Cut to pieces by that rākṣasa with volleys of arrows, the boulder fell to the ground in a thousand fragments, like a swirling cluster of vultures scattered in confusion.

**6.85.12**  Seeing that boulder shattered, Sugrīva, overcome with rage, uprooted a sāla tree and hurled it at the rākṣasa in the forefront of battle, and that hero, conqueror of enemy cities, also tore into him with arrows.

**6.85.13**  Then in his rage he spotted an iron club lying on the ground; whirling that blazing iron club and displaying it, he swiftly struck Sugrīva's finest horses with its tip.

**6.85.14**  The heroic rākṣasa Mahodara, intensely enraged, leaped down from that great chariot whose horses had been slain, and seized his mace.

**6.85.15**  Those two warriors, each with mace and iron club in hand, came together in battle, roaring like bull-elephants, like two storm-clouds accompanied by lightning.

**6.85.16**  The lord of monkeys struck Mahodara's mace with his iron club; split apart by the mace, that club of his fell to the ground.

**6.85.17**  Then the radiant Sugrīva picked up from the ground a terrible iron pestle adorned all over with gold.

**6.85.18**  Raising it, he threw it; Mahodara likewise hurled another mace at him. Striking each other, both shattered and fell to the ground.

**6.85.19**  Then, with their weapons broken, those two came together with bare fists, both possessed of equal blazing energy, like two flaming fires.

**6.85.20**  Those two struck each other and roared again and again; striking each other with their palms, they fell to the ground.

**6.85.21**  They quickly sprang up again and struck each other; those two unconquered warriors threw each other with their arms.

**6.85.22**  Then the swift Mahodara, the rākṣasa, seized a sword that lay near at hand, together with a shield.

**6.85.23**  Similarly, the foremost of vānaras, Sugrīva, even swifter, seized a great sword along with its fallen shield.

**6.85.24**  Those two, their bodies overcome with fury, roaring, charged at each other with swords raised in battle, both exhilarated and expert in weaponry in combat.

**6.85.25**  Both swiftly circled the right-hand arc; both were intensely enraged at each other, both intent on victory.

**6.85.26**  That hero, the swift Mahodara, boastful of his valor but wicked-minded, brought his sword down upon Sugrīva's great shield.

**6.85.27**  As Mahodara was pulling free his sword embedded in the shield, the foremost among monkeys severed his head along with its protective helmet adorned with earrings.

**6.85.28**  Seeing him fall to the ground with his head severed, the army of the lord of rākṣasas did not remain there.

**6.85.29**  Having slain him, that monkey roared joyfully together with the other vānaras; the ten-headed Rāvaṇa was furious, while Rāghava shone with delight.

**6.86.1**  When Mahodara had been slain, the mighty Mahāpārśva began to harass Aṅgada's formidable army with his arrows.

**6.86.2**  He caused the heads of the foremost among the vānaras to fall from their bodies on all sides, as the wind knocks fruit from its stalk.

**6.86.3**  That rākṣasa, greatly enraged, cut off the arms and shoulders of some vānaras with his arrows, and tore open the flanks of others.

**6.86.4**  Those vānaras, tormented by the shower of arrows from Mahāpārśva, all turned away in despair, their senses gone.

**6.86.5**  Observing his army distressed and afflicted by the rākṣasa, mighty-armed Aṅgada surged forward like the ocean at the full moon.

**6.86.6**  Seizing an iron club equal in brilliance to the rays of the sun, the foremost of vānaras brought it down upon Mahāpārśva in battle.

**6.86.7**  By that blow, Mahāpārśva was rendered unconscious; together with his charioteer, senseless, he fell from his chariot to the ground.

**6.86.8**  Then the splendid lord of the bears, resembling a heap of blue collyrium, of surpassing valor, leaped forth from his own troop that was like a thunder-cloud.

**6.86.9**  Seizing a huge boulder resembling a mountain-peak, he in his fury swiftly struck the horses down and smashed that chariot to pieces.

**6.86.10**  But after a moment, mighty Mahāpārśva regained consciousness and pierced Aṅgada again with many arrows.

**6.86.11**  He struck Jāmbavān with three arrows in the middle of his chest, and struck the king of the bears, Gavākṣa, with many arrows.

**6.86.12**  Seeing Gavākṣa and Jāmbavān tormented by arrows, Aṅgada, overcome with rage, seized his terrible iron club.

**6.86.13**  Aṅgada, greatly enraged at that rākṣasa who stood at a distance, seized that iron club of his, equal in radiance to the rays of the sun,

**6.86.14**  grasping it with both arms, and whirling it, the swift son of Vālin hurled it at Mahāpārśva for his destruction.

**6.86.15**  That club hurled by the powerful one knocked the rākṣasa's bow together with its arrow from his hand, and also struck off his helmet.

**6.86.16**  Swiftly closing in, the powerful son of Vālin, in his rage, struck him with his open palm at the root of the ear adorned with its earring.

**6.86.17**  Enraged, the swift Mahāpārśva of great splendor seized in one hand a very large battle-axe.

**6.86.18**  That axe — burnished with oil, gleaming, made of the finest stone, hard — the rākṣasa, extremely furious, brought down upon the son of Vālin.

**6.86.19**  When it landed violently on his left shoulder-blade, Aṅgada, enraged, wrenched the battle-axe free.

**6.86.20**  That hero Aṅgada, equal in valor to his father, intensely enraged, drew back his fist resembling a thunderbolt,

**6.86.21**  and, knowing the vital points, he brought down that fist — equal in impact to Indra's thunderbolt — squarely upon the rākṣasa's chest, aimed at the heart.

**6.86.22**  By that blow in that great battle, the rākṣasa's heart instantly burst, and he fell slain to the ground.

**6.86.23**  When he fell to the ground, that army was thrown into turmoil, and great rage arose in Rāvaṇa during the battle.

**6.87.1**  Seeing the two rākṣasas Mahodara and Mahāpārśva slain, and the mighty warrior Virūpākṣa also killed,

**6.87.2**  great rage entered Rāvaṇa in that mighty battle. He urged on his charioteer and spoke these words:

**6.87.3**  "I shall remove this grief of mine for my slain ministers and my besieged city by killing both Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa."

**6.87.4**  "I shall fell the tree that is Rāma in battle — that tree bearing Sītā as its flower and fruit, whose branches are Sugrīva, Jāmbavān, Kumuda, and Nala."

**6.87.5**  That supreme chariot-warrior, that great one, making all ten directions resound with the thunder of his chariot, swiftly proceeded and charged toward Rāghava.

**6.87.6**  The earth entire, with its rivers, mountains and forests, filled with that sound, shook — together with its boars, deer, and elephants.

**6.87.7**  He unleashed the dark weapon, immensely terrible and most dreadful, which consumed all the monkeys utterly; they fled in every direction.

**6.87.8**  Seeing those many armies broken into hundreds of fragments by Rāvaṇa's excellent arrows, Rāghava stood firm there.

**6.87.9**  He then saw Rāma standing unconquered together with his brother Lakṣmaṇa, as Viṣṇu stands with Vāsava.

**6.87.10**  Rāma seemed to be drawing lines in the sky as he grasped his great bow — broad-eyed as lotus petals, long-armed, the subduer of foes.

**6.87.11**  Observing the monkeys broken in battle and Rāvaṇa charging, Rāghava, exhilarated, gripped his bow in the midst of it all.

**6.87.12**  Then he began to draw that excellent bow of mighty force and great sound, as if cleaving the very earth.

**6.87.13**  Coming within arrow-range of those two princes, Rāvaṇa became as Rāhu approaching the moon and sun together.

**6.87.14**  By Rāvaṇa's volleys of arrows and by the sound of Rāma's bowstring being drawn, the rākṣasas fell by hundreds at that time.

**6.87.15**  Wishing to fight him first, Lakṣmaṇa, drawing his bow, released sharp arrows resembling tongues of fire.

**6.87.16**  Those arrows barely released into the sky by the bow-bearing Lakṣmaṇa were intercepted by Rāvaṇa of great splendor with his own arrows.

**6.87.17**  One arrow with one arrow, three with three, ten with ten — he severed Lakṣmaṇa's arrows, displaying the lightness of his hands.

**6.87.18**  Passing beyond Saumitri, the battle-victorious Rāvaṇa then approached Rāma, who stood immovable as a mountain.

**6.87.19**  Closing in on Rāma in combat, Rāvaṇa with eyes red from rage let loose showers of arrows upon Rāghava.

**6.87.20**  Seeing the streams of arrows loosed from Rāvaṇa's bow rushing upon him, Rāma swiftly seized broad-headed arrows without delay.

**6.87.21**  Then Rāghava cut those volleys of arrows with his sharp broad-heads — those blazing, swift, angry arrows resembling venomous serpents.

**6.87.22**  Rāghava swiftly showered Rāvaṇa, and Rāvaṇa likewise showered Rāghava; those two rained upon each other with diverse sharp arrows.

**6.87.23**  Those two unconquered ones in battle circled long in wondrous arcs, left and right, watching the speed of each other's arrows.

**6.87.24**  All beings were terrified by those two fighting simultaneously — both terrible, both discharging arrows, both resembling Death and the Ender of all things.

**6.87.25**  The sky became completely filled with their various arrows, as if at the end of the hot season it were crowded with storm-clouds wreathed with chains of lightning.

**6.87.26**  The sky appeared as though latticed by the showers of arrows from those heroes — swift, with very sharp tips, well-fletched with vulture feathers.

**6.87.27**  Those two created a terrible, supreme darkness of arrows at that time, even as the sun had set, like two great storm-clouds rising up.

**6.87.28**  The battle between those two, each seeking the other's death, was tumultuous, unassailable, and inconceivable — like the battle between Vṛtra and Vāsava.

**6.87.29**  For both were supreme archers, both expert in arms, both foremost among those versed in missiles; both ranged freely in battle.

**6.87.30**  For wherever either of those two moved, in that very direction waves of arrows sped — like waves of two oceans driven by the wind.

**6.87.31**  Then Rāvaṇa, the weeper of the world, with hands engaged without pause, discharged a garland of iron arrows upon Rāma's forehead.

**6.87.32**  Wearing upon his head those arrows launched from Rāvaṇa's fearsome bow — radiant as petals of the blue lotus — Rāma did not yield to pain.

**6.87.33**  Then Rāma, filled with anger, muttering mantras and invoking the terrible weapon, took up his arrows once more,

**6.87.34**  and blazing with great energy, that valiant one, drawing his bow, discharged those arrows at the lord of rākṣasas — his arrows unbroken.

**6.87.35**  Those arrows, striking the armor of the lord of rākṣasas that resembled a great dark cloud, caused him no pain at that time, for he was invulnerable.

**6.87.36**  Then Rāma, skilled in all weapons, struck again at that lord of rākṣasas standing in his chariot, splitting him in the forehead with a supreme missile.

**6.87.37**  Those arrows, having pierced through their arrow-forms like five-headed serpents hissing, entered the earth, turned back by Rāvaṇa.

**6.87.38**  Having destroyed Rāghava's weapon, Rāvaṇa, overwhelmed with rage, took up another weapon — an āsura weapon, immensely terrible.

**6.87.39**  He sent forth arrows with the faces of lions and tigers, others with the faces of herons and crows, others with the faces of vultures and hawks, and still others with the faces of jackals.

**6.87.40**  He also released sharp arrows with the faces of wolves — gaping-mouthed and terrifying — and five-headed arrows darting their tongues.

**6.87.41**  Other arrows with the faces of asses, arrows with the faces of boars, arrows with the faces of dogs and cocks, arrows with the faces of crocodiles and venomous serpents —

**6.87.42**  these and many other sharp arrows of illusion he, blazing with great energy, released against Rāma, hissing like an enraged serpent.

**6.87.43**  Penetrating that āsura weapon, the joy of the Raghu line, with immense energy, like fire itself, released the fire-weapon.

**6.87.44**  Arrows with faces blazing like fire, also arrows with faces of the sun, arrows with faces of the full moon and the half-moon, and arrows with faces of comets —

**6.87.45**  arrows of the color of planets and asterisms, arrows with faces of great meteors, and others resembling lightning-tongues — these sharp arrows he released.

**6.87.46**  Those terrible arrows of Rāvaṇa, struck by Rāghava's weapon, dissolved in the sky and went away by the thousands.

**6.87.47**  Seeing that weapon destroyed by Rāma of tireless deeds, all the monkeys of changeable form then roared with delight.

**6.88.1**  When that weapon was repelled, Rāvaṇa, lord of the rākṣasas, doubled his fury, and from that fury he immediately discharged another missile.

**6.88.2**  That great-resplendent one, Rāvaṇa, made ready to release against Rāghava another terrible weapon fashioned by Maya, dreadful in its power.

**6.88.3**  Then from his bow there flew out tridents, maces, and pestles blazing with fire, all hard as the thunderbolt, on every side.

**6.88.4**  Hammers with knobbed heads, nooses, and burning thunderbolts also streamed forth, sharp and varied, like the winds at the dissolution of an age.

**6.88.5**  That illustrious Rāghava, the foremost among the greatest knowers of weaponry, of immense splendour, countered that weapon with the supreme Gāndharva missile.

**6.88.6**  When that weapon was neutralised by the great-souled Rāghava, Rāvaṇa, his eyes reddened with rage, discharged the Solar missile.

**6.88.7**  Then blazing discs, vast and brilliant, flew out from the bow of the ten-necked one, that intelligent being of terrible velocity.

**6.88.8**  With those discs flashing hither and thither and falling in all directions, the sky was ablaze, as if with the fiery orbs of the moon, the sun, and the planets.

**6.88.9**  Rāghava shattered all those discs and the varied weapons of Rāvaṇa in the forefront of battle with floods of arrows.

**6.88.10**  Seeing that weapon destroyed, Rāvaṇa, lord of the rākṣasas, pierced Rāma in all his vital spots with ten arrows.

**6.88.11**  Though pierced by those ten arrows shot from the great bow of Rāvaṇa, the greatly lustrous Rāghava did not tremble.

**6.88.12**  Then Rāghava, triumphant in battle, filled with great wrath, pierced Rāvaṇa in all his limbs with many arrows.

**6.88.13**  Meanwhile, the mighty Lakṣmaṇa, Rāghava's younger brother, who was enraged and a slayer of enemy heroes, took up seven arrows.

**6.88.14**  With those arrows of great velocity, the illustrious Lakṣmaṇa, of immense splendour, split Rāvaṇa's banner bearing human skulls into many pieces.

**6.88.15**  And with another arrow the illustrious and greatly powerful Lakṣmaṇa struck off the head of the charioteer of the lord of the rākṣasas—that head adorned with gleaming earrings.

**6.88.16**  Lakṣmaṇa also shattered the bow of the lord of the rākṣasas—that bow resembling the rut-post of an elephant—with five sharp arrows.

**6.88.17**  And Vibhīṣaṇa, leaping forward, slew with his mace the excellent horses of Rāvaṇa—those horses dark as rain-clouds and huge as mountains.

**6.88.18**  With his horses slain, Rāvaṇa swiftly leaped down from the great chariot and directed his fierce rage toward his own brother.

**6.88.19**  Then the powerful lord of rākṣasas, of great might, hurled a blazing śakti lance at Vibhīṣaṇa—a lance blazing like a flaming thunderbolt.

**6.88.20**  Before it even reached him, Lakṣmaṇa cut it down with three arrows. Then a great shout arose from the monkeys in the battle.

**6.88.21**  That śakti lance, garlanded with gold and cut in three pieces, fell sparkling and blazing like a great meteor fallen from the sky.

**6.88.22**  Then he seized another great śakti, still more formidable, hard to withstand even by Time itself, blazing with its own inherent radiance.

**6.88.23**  In the swift and powerful hands of the wicked Rāvaṇa it blazed with supremely dreadful fire, its brilliance equal to Indra's thunderbolt.

**6.88.24**  In that very instant the heroic Lakṣmaṇa swiftly went to Vibhīṣaṇa, who had fallen into danger of losing his life.

**6.88.25**  To free him, the heroic Lakṣmaṇa drew his bow and covered Rāvaṇa, who held the śakti in hand, with a shower of arrows.

**6.88.26**  Overwhelmed by the flood of arrows released by the great-souled Lakṣmaṇa, his valour checked and turned aside, Rāvaṇa's mind could not fix itself on striking a blow.

**6.88.27**  Seeing his brother freed by Lakṣmaṇa, Rāvaṇa turned to face Lakṣmaṇa and spoke these words.

**6.88.28**  "You who boast of your strength—since you have freed Vibhīṣaṇa the rākṣasa in this way, this śakti of mine shall now be aimed at you instead.

**6.88.29**  This śakti, red with its iron marks, hurled from my arm-like iron bar, shall pierce your heart and carry away your life."

**6.88.30**  Having spoken thus, he took up that śakti fitted with eight great bells and loud-sounding, fashioned by Maya's magic, never failing, a slayer of enemies—

**6.88.31**  —aiming it at Lakṣmaṇa, blazing as it were with its own energy. Rāvaṇa, supremely wrathful, hurled it and roared aloud.

**6.88.32**  Hurled with terrible velocity, sounding like Indra's thunderbolt, that śakti flew swiftly at Lakṣmaṇa in the forefront of battle.

**6.88.33**  Rāghava cried after that oncoming śakti: "May Lakṣmaṇa be safe! Be futile—your assault is destroyed!"

**6.88.34**  That śakti of great velocity fell blazing with immense radiance upon the broad chest of Lakṣmaṇa, like the tongue of the king of serpents.

**6.88.35**  Then Lakṣmaṇa, his heart pierced through by the śakti driven deep by Rāvaṇa's force, fell upon the earth.

**6.88.36**  Standing close by and seeing Lakṣmaṇa in that condition, the greatly resplendent Rāghava, out of his love for his brother, became heavy of heart.

**6.88.37**  He brooded for a moment, his eyes blurred with tears, then blazed forth with greater ferocity, like the fire at the dissolution of an age.

**6.88.38**  Reflecting that "this is not the time for despair," Rāghava resolved on the slaying of Rāvaṇa and waged battle with tremendous uproar.

**6.88.39**  Rāma then saw Lakṣmaṇa in the great battle, pierced by the śakti, smeared with blood, lying like a mountain with its serpents.

**6.88.40**  Even those foremost monkeys could not destroy that śakti hurled by the mighty Rāvaṇa, try as they might—they were themselves tormented by floods of arrows from the swift-handed rākṣasa.

**6.88.41**  After piercing Saumitri it had entered the earth. Rāma, enraged in battle, seized that dreadful śakti with both hands and forcefully broke and extracted it.

**6.88.42**  Even as he drew it out, the mighty Rāvaṇa rained down arrows that pierced him in all his limbs, cutting through his vital points.

**6.88.43**  Paying no heed to those arrows, Rāghava embraced Lakṣmaṇa and then spoke to Hanūmat and Sugrīva: "Surround and guard Lakṣmaṇa here, O best of monkeys."

**6.88.44**  "The moment for valour has arrived—long desired by me. This wicked-souled ten-necked one, resolute in evil, shall be slain. I have longed for this as the chataka bird longs to see the cloud at summer's end."

**6.88.45**  "In this very moment, very soon, I swear truly to you: you monkeys shall see a world either without Rāvaṇa or without Rāma."

**6.88.46**  "The loss of the kingdom, the sojourn in the forest, the wandering through Daṇḍaka, the seizure of Vaidehī, and the encounter with the rākṣasas—

**6.88.47**  "—all that great and dreadful suffering, all that affliction resembling hell—I shall today put an end to all of it by slaying Rāvaṇa in battle."

**6.88.48**  "For this very purpose I assembled this monkey army, and for this I made Sugrīva king by slaying Vālin in battle."

**6.88.49**  "For this very purpose the ocean was crossed and the bridge built over the sea. That wicked one has now come within the range of my sight in battle."

**6.88.50**  "Having come within range of my gaze, Rāvaṇa does not deserve to live—as no creature can survive the gaze of a serpent whose very sight is venom."

**6.88.51**  "Be at ease, O invincible chiefs of monkeys, and watch from the mountain-tops this battle between me and Rāvaṇa."

**6.88.52**  "Today let the three worlds, together with gandharvas, gods, sages, and cāraṇas, behold the Rāma-ness of Rāma in my combat."

**6.88.53**  "Today I shall perform a deed that all the worlds, with all their moving and unmoving creatures, together with the gods, shall speak of as long as the earth endures."

**6.88.54**  Having spoken thus, Rāma, composed and focused, struck the ten-necked one in battle with sharp arrows adorned with heated gold.

**6.88.55**  Then Rāvaṇa poured down upon Rāma blazing iron arrows and maces like a rain-cloud pouring streams.

**6.88.56**  The noise of the arrows of Rāma and Rāvaṇa, flying from both sides and striking each other, was tremendous.

**6.88.57**  Those arrows of Rāma and Rāvaṇa, broken and scattered, with their tips aflame, fell from the sky to the surface of the earth.

**6.88.58**  The great twanging sound of the bowstrings of those two—Rāma and Rāvaṇa—which struck terror into all beings, was beyond compare in its wonder.

**6.88.59**  Pelted with torrents of arrow-nets by the great-souled one, tormented by him who held a blazing bow, Rāvaṇa fled out of fear, even as he had come, like a rain-cloud struck and driven off by the wind.

**6.89.1**  Having given fierce battle to the wicked-souled Rāvaṇa and still releasing floods of arrows, he spoke these words to Suṣeṇa.

**6.89.2**  "Lakṣmaṇa has fallen to the ground by the force of Rāvaṇa's blow; the hero writhes like a serpent, rousing anguish in me."

**6.89.3**  "Seeing this hero drenched in blood—he who is dearer to me than life—what power have I to fight, when my spirit is so distraught?"

**6.89.4**  "This is my brother who gloried in battle, marked with auspicious signs. If he has attained the five elements, what use to me are my own life or happiness?"

**6.89.5**  "My heroic energy seems to shrink with shame, my bow seems to slip from my hand, my arrows falter, my sight is overcome with tears; my anguish intensifies sharply, and the desire to die arises in me."

**6.89.6**  Seeing his brother struck down by the wicked-souled Rāvaṇa, he fell into deep despair and lamented with senses overwhelmed.

**6.89.7**  "Neither battle nor life nor even Sītā concerns me, having seen my brother Lakṣmaṇa struck down in the dust of combat."

**6.89.8**  "What use to me is a kingdom? What use my life? I have no purpose in battle while Lakṣmaṇa lies struck down in the forefront of the fight."

**6.89.9**  The heroic Suṣeṇa, comforting Rāma, spoke these words: "This great-armed Lakṣmaṇa, increaser of glory, is not dead."

**6.89.10**  "His face is not distorted, nor dark, nor without radiance. His face can be seen to be bright and serene."

**6.89.11**  "His palms are red as lotus flowers, and his eyes are calm and clear. Such appearance does not belong to the dead, O lord of men. Do not despair, O hero; he lives yet, O subduer of foes."

**6.89.12**  "Of one who lies as if asleep on the ground with relaxed limbs, his heart heaving with breath, trembling again and again—this tells us he is alive."

**6.89.13**  Having spoken those words to Rāghava, Suṣeṇa, skilled in speech, spoke these further words in haste to Hanūmat who stood near.

**6.89.14**  "O gentle one, go swiftly from here to the Oṣadhi mountain—the auspicious peak that was described before by the heroic Jāmbavat."

**6.89.15**  "On the southern peak of that mountain grows an herb; bring it here—the Viśalyakaraṇī, the auspicious herb known as Viśalyakaraṇī."

**6.89.16**  "Bring also the Sauvarṇakaraṇī and the Saṃjīvanī, and the Saṃdhānakaraṇī—go quickly and bring them here for the restoration of the heroic and great-souled Lakṣmaṇa."

**6.89.17**  Thus instructed, Hanūmat, the illustrious one, went to the Oṣadhi mountain and fell into bewilderment, not recognising those great medicinal herbs.

**6.89.18**  Then a thought arose in the mind of that son of the Wind of immeasurable might: "I shall take the very peak of the mountain and carry it there."

**6.89.19**  "If I go without taking the Viśalyakaraṇī, time will have passed beyond saving, and great confusion will result."

**6.89.20**  Having thus deliberated, the mighty Hanūmat went quickly and, seizing the peak of the mountain, leaped up into the air with it.

**6.89.21**  "I could not identify those herbs, O foremost of monkeys, so I have brought the whole peak of that mountain."

**6.89.22**  Praising the wind-god's son who spoke thus, Suṣeṇa, best of monkeys, plucked and took the herbs from it.

**6.89.23**  Then Suṣeṇa, best of monkeys and of great splendour, crushed that herb and held it to Lakṣmaṇa's nostrils to inhale.

**6.89.24**  Lakṣmaṇa, the slayer of enemy heroes, had breathed in the herb even while the lance was still in him; then, the lance freed and pain gone, he swiftly rose up from the earth.

**6.89.25**  Seeing Lakṣmaṇa rise up from the ground, the monkeys, greatly delighted, cried "Well done! Well done!" and honoured Suṣeṇa.

**6.89.26**  Rāma said "Come, come" to Lakṣmaṇa, that slayer of enemy heroes, and embraced him with deep affection, his eyes brimming with tears.

**6.89.27**  Embracing Saumitri, Rāghava said: "By good fortune I see you, O hero, returned from death."

**6.89.28**  "My life would have no purpose, nor would Sītā, nor victory—what purpose could my life have had if you had been dissolved into the five elements?"

**6.89.29**  As the great-souled Rāghava was speaking thus, Lakṣmaṇa, distressed and with halting speech, answered him with these words.

**6.89.30**  "You who are resolute in truth and made that vow in days past—you should not speak so here, like some slight and unworthy man."

**6.89.31**  "The good do not make vows in vain, O sinless one; the keeping of a vow is the great mark of such a man."

**6.89.32**  "It is not proper for you to fall into despair on my account, O sinless one. Today, uphold your vow by slaying Rāvaṇa."

**6.89.33**  "No enemy who enters the path of your arrows shall depart alive—no more than a great elephant can depart alive from a roaring lion with sharp fangs."

**6.89.34**  "And I desire the swift slaying of that wicked-souled one—before the sun, whose deeds are done, descends to set."

**6.90.1**  Hearing those words spoken by Lakṣmaṇa, Rāghava released terrible arrows at Rāvaṇa in the forefront of battle.

**6.90.2**  The ten-necked one, standing in his chariot, struck Rāma with exceedingly dreadful arrows resembling thunderbolts, like a rain-cloud with its streams.

**6.90.3**  Rāma, composed and focused, pierced the ten-necked one in battle with arrows blazing like fire and adorned with gold.

**6.90.4**  "The battle is not equal—Rāma standing on the ground and the rākṣasa standing in a chariot," so said the gods, gandharvas, and dānavas.

**6.90.5**  Then a chariot with limbs ornamented with gold, adorned with hundreds of small bells, resembling the newly risen sun, with a yoke-pole made of vaidūrya gems—

**6.90.6**  —yoked with excellent horses wearing golden headpieces, with white scattered manes, tawny horses bright as the sun, adorned with a net of gold—

**6.90.7**  —bearing a banner-staff of gold, the auspicious and supreme chariot of the king of the gods, descended from the heavens and drove up to Kākutstha.

**6.90.8**  And Mātali, the charioteer of the thousand-eyed Indra, standing in the chariot with his goad, addressed Rāma with joined palms and spoke these words.

**6.90.9**  "This chariot, O Kākutstha, has been given by the thousand-eyed Indra for your victory—this auspicious chariot that destroys enemies, O great being."

**6.90.10**  "Here is the great bow of Indra, and armour resembling fire, and arrows bright as the sun, and a śakti lance, pure and keen."

**6.90.11**  "Mount this chariot, O hero, and slay the rākṣasa Rāvaṇa, with me as your charioteer, O Rāma—even as great Indra slew the dānavas."

**6.90.12**  Thus addressed, Rāma circumambulated the chariot, saluted it reverently, and mounted it—illuminating the worlds with his splendour.

**6.90.13**  Then there was a wondrous battle between two chariots, hair-raising, between the great-armed Rāma and the rākṣasa Rāvaṇa.

**6.90.14**  That Rāghava, supreme knower of missiles, countered the Gāndharva weapon with the Gāndharva, and the divine weapon of the rākṣasa king with the divine.

**6.90.15**  Then the lord of rākṣasas, that night-wanderer, supremely enraged, released once more a most terrible and dreadful rākṣasa weapon.

**6.90.16**  Those golden-adorned arrows released from Rāvaṇa's bow advanced upon Kākutstha having transformed into great venomous serpents.

**6.90.17**  With blazing mouths vomiting fire and flame, they advanced upon Rāma alone, with gaping jaws, terrifying.

**6.90.18**  With those blazing-bodied, greatly venomous ones whose touch was like Vāsuki's, all the directions and the intermediate directions were covered and filled.

**6.90.19**  Seeing those serpents rushing toward him in battle, Rāma manifested the dreadful and fear-inspiring Gārutmat missile.

**6.90.20**  Those arrows released from Rāghava's bow, with golden nocks and flame-bright tips, became golden suparṇas—enemies of serpents—and flew forth.

**6.90.21**  Those shape-shifting arrows of Rāma, now in the form of suparṇas, slew all those swift-moving arrows that had taken the form of serpents.

**6.90.22**  His weapon neutralised, Rāvaṇa, lord of the rākṣasas, enraged, then showered Rāma with dreadful torrents of arrows.

**6.90.23**  Having tormented Rāma, the tireless worker, with a thousand arrows, Rāvaṇa then struck Mātali with a flood of shafts.

**6.90.24**  Felling the golden banner from the chariot to its floor, Rāvaṇa also struck Indra's horses with a net of arrows.

**6.90.25**  Gods, gandharvas, and dānavas together with cāraṇas became despondent, and the siddhas and the great sages too, seeing Rāma distressed at that moment.

**6.90.26**  The chiefs of the monkeys together with Vibhīṣaṇa also became troubled, seeing the moon of Rāma seized by the Rāhu of Rāvaṇa.

**6.90.27**  Mercury, boding ill for creatures, stood having occupied Rohiṇī, the moon's beloved constellation, which belongs to Prajāpati.

**6.90.28**  The ocean, its waves encircled by smoke and blazing as if on fire, leaped up in fury as if touching the sun.

**6.90.29**  The sun, its colour like a weapon, harsh and of dim radiance, appeared as if headless, conjoined with a comet.

**6.90.30**  Mars stood clearly having occupied Viśākhā in the sky—the constellation presided over by Indra and Agni that belongs to the Kosalas.

**6.90.31**  The ten-faced, twenty-armed one, holding his bow drawn, appeared—the ten-necked one visible like Mount Maināka.

**6.90.32**  Rāma, being driven back by the ten-necked rākṣasa, was unable to fix his arrows in the forefront of battle.

**6.90.33**  Then he, furrowing his brow in anger, his eyes slightly suffused with red, was seized with supreme wrath, as if scorching all things with his gaze.

**6.91.1**  Beholding the face of wise Rāma blazing with fury, all living beings trembled with fear, and the earth itself shook.

**6.91.2**  A mountain haunted by lions and tigers trembled, its trees shaking though it stood still; the ocean, lord of rivers, was likewise thrown into turmoil.

**6.91.3**  Birds with harsh cries and rough voices, portending ill omens, screamed and wheeled about on every side across the sky.

**6.91.4**  Seeing Rāma seething with rage and witnessing those dreadful portents, all creatures were seized with terror, and fear entered into Rāvaṇa.

**6.91.5**  At that moment the gods standing in their celestial cars, the Gandharvas and great serpents, the sages, Dānavas, and Daityas, the Garutmats and sky-dwellers—

**6.91.6**  —all witnessed that battle, which had the character of a cosmic dissolution, as the two heroes fought each other with many fearsome weapons.

**6.91.7**  All the gods and asuras assembled to witness the conflict, and beholding the great battle, spoke words of joyful devotion.

**6.91.8**  The assembled asuras cried "Victory to Daśagrīva!", while the gods cried out to Rāma again and again, "Victory to you!"

**6.91.9**  Meanwhile, that wicked-souled Rāvaṇa, enraged at Rāghava and eager to strike, laid hold of a great weapon—

**6.91.10**  —a spear with the hardness of the thunderbolt, resounding mightily, the destroyer of all foes, adorned with tips like mountain peaks, terrifying to behold—

**6.91.11**  —with sharp points as though wreathed in smoke, comparable to the fire at the end of a cosmic age, supremely terrible, unapproachable, difficult to withstand even for Death himself—

**6.91.12**  —a trident that inspired terror in all beings, that split and pierced. Rāvaṇa seized it, blazing as it were with his own fury.

**6.91.13**  Supremely enraged, that mighty one grasped the trident by its middle, surrounded in battle by many valiant rākṣasas.

**6.91.14**  Raising it aloft, that great-bodied one let out a fearsome battle-roar, his eyes red with rage, exhilarating his own army.

**6.91.15**  That dreadful roar of the lord of rākṣasas caused the earth, the atmosphere, the cardinal directions, and the intermediate directions to tremble.

**6.91.16**  At that tremendous roar of the evil-souled one, all living creatures trembled and the ocean was thrown into agitation.

**6.91.17**  Taking up that great trident in his might, Rāvaṇa uttered a tremendous cry and spoke harsh words to Rāma:

**6.91.18**  "This spear, Rāma, hard as the thunderbolt, raised by me in fury, shall instantly snatch away the life of you together with your brother and allies."

**6.91.19**  "Having slain you today, O boaster in battle, I shall swiftly even the score for the valiant rākṣasas slain at the forefront of the army."

**6.91.20**  "Stand now — I shall slay you with this trident, O Rāghava!" Having spoken thus, the lord of rākṣasas hurled that trident.

**6.91.21**  Rāghava checked that oncoming trident with a volley of arrows, even as Vāsava checks a rising fire at the end of the age with a deluge of water.

**6.91.22**  But Rāvaṇa's great trident burned up those arrows shot from Rāma's bow, just as fire burns up moths.

**6.91.23**  Seeing those arrows reduced to ash, pulverized by contact with the trident while still in midair, Rāghava took hold of his wrath.

**6.91.24**  Supremely enraged, Rāghava, the joy of the Raghu line, seized the śakti-weapon brought by Mātali and fashioned by Vāsava.

**6.91.25**  Swung by the mighty one, the śakti rang like a bell and blazed across the sky, radiant as a comet at the end of a cosmic age.

**6.91.26**  Hurled against the lord of rākṣasas, it struck that trident; shattered by the śakti, the great trident fell, its brilliance gone.

**6.91.27**  Then Rāma pierced his swift horses with sharp arrows — swift, powerfully driven, keen, comparable to thunderbolts.

**6.91.28**  Then Rāghava, fully focused, pierced Rāvaṇa in the chest with sharp arrows, and struck him on the forehead with three arrow-shafts.

**6.91.29**  His entire body pierced by arrows, blood flowing from his limbs, the lord of rākṣasas standing amid his host appeared like a blossoming aśoka tree.

**6.91.30**  His body pierced through and through by Rāma's arrows, his limbs wet with blood, the lord of night-stalkers felt distress in the midst of the assembly and then burned with intense rage.

**6.92.1**  Then Rāvaṇa, that boaster in battle, tormented in combat by Kākutstha with such fury, was overcome by towering rage.

**6.92.2**  His eyes blazing with anger, that mighty one drew his bow and, filled with violent fury, assailed Rāghava in the supreme clash.

**6.92.3**  With thousands of streams of arrows, Rāvaṇa filled Rāghava with shafts like a cloud from the sky filling a lake.

**6.92.4**  Though filled with a net of arrows released from the bow in combat, Kākutstha did not waver — like a great mountain, immovable.

**6.92.5**  Standing firm in battle, warding off showers of arrows with his own arrows, that mighty one received them as the sun receives its own rays.

**6.92.6**  Then the night-stalker, quick-handed and enraged, struck great-souled Rāghava in the chest with thousands of arrows.

**6.92.7**  Smeared with blood in battle, Lakṣmaṇa's elder brother appeared like a great kiṃśuka tree in full bloom in the forest.

**6.92.8**  Roused to fury by the impact of arrows, Kākutstha — of very great splendour, radiant as the sun at the end of the age — seized his own arrows in turn.

**6.92.9**  Then those two — Rāma and Rāvaṇa — deeply enraged with each other, could no longer make each other out in that battle amid the darkness of arrows.

**6.92.10**  Then Rāma, son of Daśaratha, possessed by wrath, spoke harsh words to Rāvaṇa with a contemptuous laugh:

**6.92.11**  "Because you abducted my helpless wife from Janasthāna by stealth, O vilest of rākṣasas, you are therefore no hero."

**6.92.12**  "You forcibly carried off Vaidehī, wretched and separated from me, dwelling in the great forest — and you think yourself a hero?"

**6.92.13**  "You are a hero against defenceless women, a violator of another's wife — having done the deed of a coward, you think yourself a hero?"

**6.92.14**  "Transgressor of all bounds, shameless one, unstable in conduct — you have embraced death through arrogance and still you think yourself a hero?"

**6.92.15**  "What you did with the help of your heroic brother Dhanada and his forces is indeed glorious and praiseworthy — what a great deed!"

**6.92.16**  "Now reap today the very great fruit of that deed — a deed approached through arrogance, a deed that is contemptible and harmful."

**6.92.17**  "You reckon yourself a hero, O evil-minded one, yet you feel no shame at having dragged away Sītā like a thief."

**6.92.18**  "Had you violated Sītā by force in my presence, you would then have beheld your brother Khara slain by my arrows."

**6.92.19**  "Fortunately, O evil-souled one, you have come within the range of my sight — today I shall despatch you to the abode of Yama with sharp arrows."

**6.92.20**  "Today let flesh-eating creatures drag away your head, severed by my arrows, its blazing earrings scattered in the dust of the battlefield."

**6.92.21**  "Let vultures alight on your chest, O Rāvaṇa, as you lie cast on the ground, and drink thirstily the blood oozing from the wounds of arrow-shafts."

**6.92.22**  "Today let birds tear out your entrails as you lie slain, pierced by my arrows and fallen — as Garutmats tear serpents."

**6.92.23**  Speaking thus, the heroic Rāma, destroyer of foes, rained down a shower of arrows on the lord of rākṣasas who stood nearby.

**6.92.24**  Rāma's valour, strength, exhilaration in combat, and the power of his weapons all doubled as he longed for the death of his foe.

**6.92.25**  All weapons appeared of their own accord for that self-knowing one, and in his elation that greatly splendid hero became swifter of hand.

**6.92.26**  Recognising these auspicious signs as dwelling within himself, Rāma, the destroyer of rākṣasas, assailed Rāvaṇa with renewed vigour.

**6.92.27**  Struck by the monkeys' showers of rocks and by Rāghava's showers of arrows, Daśagrīva reeled at heart.

**6.92.28**  When he could no longer wield a weapon, could no longer draw his bow, could no longer display his valour — his inner spirit overcome with distress—

**6.92.29**  —even the arrows and various weapons he had hurled no longer availed him in battle, for the time of death was approaching.

**6.92.30**  His charioteer, the driver of his car, observing him in that condition, quietly and without panic drove his chariot away from the battle.

**6.93.1**  But Rāvaṇa, deeply enraged out of his delusion, impelled by the power of Fate, his eyes reddened with anger, spoke to his charioteer:

**6.93.2**  "As though I were of diminished valour, as though powerless, as though deprived of manhood, as though a coward, as though of little account, as though destitute of spirit, as though stripped of splendour—

**6.93.3**  —as though abandoned by my powers of illusion, as though expelled by weapons — you disregard me, O evil-minded one, and act according to your own judgment!"

**6.93.4**  "For what reason, ignoring me and disregarding my will, have you driven my chariot away in the presence of my enemy?"

**6.93.5**  "You, ignoble wretch, have today destroyed my fame, my valour, my splendour, and my reputation — all accumulated over long ages."

**6.93.6**  "Before the eyes of the enemy, whose valour is renowned and whose courage inspires admiration, you have made me — eager for battle — appear a coward."

**6.93.7**  "You who in your delusion do not drive this chariot forward, O evil-minded one — my suspicion is confirmed: you have been suborned by the enemy."

**6.93.8**  "This act is not that of a friend who seeks one's good; it resembles the act of enemies; and what you have done is not properly done."

**6.93.9**  "Turn the chariot back quickly before my enemy escapes — if you have any decency left, if you still remember your virtues."

**6.93.10**  Thus harshly addressed by the senseless one, the charioteer — who sought his master's good — spoke beneficent and conciliatory words to Rāvaṇa:

**6.93.11**  "I am not afraid, nor am I confused, nor have I been suborned by enemies; I am not negligent, nor am I lacking in affection, nor have I forgotten my duty."

**6.93.12**  "Rather, by me who desires your welfare and guards your glory, whose mind overflows with affection — what seemed unpleasant was done out of love."

**6.93.13**  "In this matter, O great king, you should not fault me who am devoted to your good — treating me like some ignoble wretch of low account."

**6.93.14**  "Hear me — I shall tell you the reason why I turned the chariot back in battle, like a current of water turned back by a flood."

**6.93.15**  "I perceived the great fatigue you suffer from the exertion of battle; I do not observe in you, O hero, either brightness of countenance or exhilaration."

**6.93.16**  "And these chariot-horses, wearied by the effort of pulling the car, are spent, exhausted by the heat, like cattle beaten down by rain."

**6.93.17**  "And the many omens that keep appearing around us — in each and every one of them I discern inauspiciousness."

**6.93.18**  "Place and time must be gauged, as must signs and indications, the dejection, exhilaration, and fatigue of the chariot-warrior, and the strength and weakness of both sides."

**6.93.19**  "The high and low ground, the level and uneven terrain, the right moment for battle — these must be assessed, as must the watching for an opening in the enemy."

**6.93.20**  "Advance and retreat, standing firm and falling back — all of this must be understood by one who rides a chariot, by one who governs a chariot."

**6.93.21**  "For your rest and for that of these chariot-horses, avoiding a terrible reverse — what I have done here is proper."

**6.93.22**  "This chariot was not driven away by me out of self-will, O hero; what I have done, O lord, was done out of love for my master."

**6.93.23**  "Command me as you truly see fit, O destroyer of enemies — I shall do it with a heart free of all obligation."

**6.93.24**  Satisfied with those words of his charioteer, Rāvaṇa, eager for battle, praised him in many ways and spoke thus:

**6.93.25**  "Quickly, O charioteer, turn this chariot to face Rāghava — Rāvaṇa will not turn back without slaying his enemies in battle."

**6.93.26**  Having spoken thus, Rāvaṇa, the satisfied lord of rākṣasas, gave him a single auspicious and supremely fine arm-ornament.

**6.93.27**  Then swiftly, urged on by Rāvaṇa's command, the charioteer drove the horses forward; and the great chariot of the lord of rākṣasas was, in a moment, once again at the fore of the battle against Rāma.

**6.94.1**  The king among men beheld the chariot of the king of the rākṣasas rushing forward suddenly, resounding loudly, bearing great banners.

**6.94.2**  Yoked with dark horses, endowed with a fierce radiance, thick with banners like lightning-flashes, displaying weapons like Indra's own rainbow-bow, it poured streams of arrows like a cloud discharging a torrent of rain.

**6.94.3**  Seeing the chariot of his enemy rushing forward, resembling a storm-cloud, with a sound like that of a mountain cleaving under the thunderbolt's stroke, Rāma spoke to Mātali, charioteer of the thousand-eyed Indra.

**6.94.4**  "Mātali, see the chariot of the foe rushing forward in great fury — by coming at such tremendous speed from the inauspicious side, he has resolved, it seems, to destroy himself in battle."

**6.94.5**  "Therefore be alert; advance to meet the foe's chariot. I wish to scatter it as the wind scatters a risen cloud."

**6.94.6**  "Without hesitation, without confusion, with heart and eyes undistracted, keeping firm hold of the reins, drive the chariot swiftly."

**6.94.7**  "You who are accustomed to Indra's chariot need not be instructed; I, bent solely on battle, only remind you — I do not teach you."

**6.94.8**  Pleased by those words of Rāma, Mātali, foremost among the charioteers of the gods, drove the chariot forward.

**6.94.9**  Then, circling Rāvaṇa's great chariot from the auspicious side and throwing up dust from the wheel, he shook Rāvaṇa.

**6.94.10**  Then the ten-headed one, enraged, his eyes wide and copper-red, assailed Rāma, who stood facing him in the chariot, with a volley of arrows.

**6.94.11**  Rāma, of exceeding splendour, stung with indignation at that assault, his steadiness overpowered by wrath, took up in battle the bow of Indra of immense velocity, along with arrows blazing bright as the sun's rays.

**6.94.12**  Then a great battle was joined between those two, each eager to slay the other, facing one another like two proud lions.

**6.94.13**  Then the gods together with the Gandharvas, the Siddhas and the great seers assembled to witness the chariot-duel, all longing for Rāvaṇa's destruction.

**6.94.14**  Then dreadful and hair-raising portents arose, presaging the destruction of Rāvaṇa and the victory of Rāghava.

**6.94.15**  The sky rained blood upon Rāvaṇa's chariot; violent whirlwinds circled in the inauspicious direction.

**6.94.16**  A great flock of vultures, hovering in the sky, flew in whatever direction his chariot moved.

**6.94.17**  Laṅkā was enshrouded in a reddish twilight hue like the hibiscus flower; even by day the earth appeared as if ablaze.

**6.94.18**  Enormous meteors accompanied by thunderous crashes fell with great roaring sounds, despairing the rākṣasas and boding ill for Rāvaṇa.

**6.94.19**  Wherever Rāvaṇa stood, the earth trembled; and the arms of the striking rākṣasas felt as if seized and held.

**6.94.20**  Copper-red, yellow, pale, and white rays of the sun fell upon Rāvaṇa's body, appearing like the mineral veins of a mountain.

**6.94.21**  Followed by vultures and spewing fire from their mouths, jackals shrieked ominously, looking toward his face with fury.

**6.94.22**  A contrary wind blew in the battle, raising dust and blinding the eyes of that king of the rākṣasas.

**6.94.23**  Indra's thunderbolts fell all around his army with terrible, unbearable sounds, not accompanied by any sound of clouds.

**6.94.24**  All the cardinal and intermediate directions became shrouded in darkness; with a great rain of dust the sky became difficult to see.

**6.94.25**  Making dreadful quarrelsome cries, hundreds of śārikā birds fell upon his chariot, terrible and uttering terrible sounds.

**6.94.26**  His horses shed sparks from their haunches and a constant stream of tears from their eyes — fire and water equally.

**6.94.27**  Thus many dreadful and fearful portents of this kind arose for the destruction of Rāvaṇa.

**6.94.28**  And for Rāma too, omens that were gentle and auspicious appeared on all sides, proclaiming victory.

**6.94.29**  Then Rāghava, skilled in reading omens, observing those omens that had come to him, felt supreme joy and delight, and redoubled his valour in battle.

**6.95.1**  Then there commenced between Rāma and Rāvaṇa a very great chariot-duel, utterly fierce, striking fear into all the worlds.

**6.95.2**  Then both the rākṣasa army and the great host of monkeys stood weapons raised yet motionless, transfixed.

**6.95.3**  Seeing those two mighty combatants, the man and the rākṣasa, locked in battle, all were overcome in their hearts and seized with the greatest wonder.

**6.95.4**  With arms busy wielding various weapons, their minds astounded, they stood watching the battle and did not strike one another.

**6.95.5**  The army appeared like a painting — the rākṣasas with eyes fixed in wonder upon Rāvaṇa, the monkeys with eyes fixed in wonder upon Rāghava.

**6.95.6**  But those two, Rāghava and Rāvaṇa, having observed the omens and made up their minds, steady in their fury, fought without fear.

**6.95.7**  Kākutstha resolved "I must conquer" and Rāvaṇa resolved "I must die fighting" — both staking the whole of their valour, they displayed it in battle.

**6.95.8**  Then the mighty ten-headed one, in anger, fitting arrows to his bow, released them aimed at the standard mounted on Rāghava's chariot.

**6.95.9**  Those arrows, failing to reach the standard of Indra's chariot, merely grazed the red-painted lance-staff and fell to the ground.

**6.95.10**  Then Rāma, greatly enraged, the mighty one, stretching his bow, resolved in his mind to return blow for blow.

**6.95.11**  He released a sharp arrow aimed at Rāvaṇa's standard — irresistible as a great serpent, blazing with its own splendour.

**6.95.12**  That arrow, piercing through, struck the ground; the standard of Rāvaṇa's chariot was severed and fell to the earth.

**6.95.13**  Seeing his standard smashed, the immensely powerful Rāvaṇa seemed to blaze in battle as if ignited by the fire of his own wrath.

**6.95.14**  Overcome by fury and discharging a great rain of arrows, Rāvaṇa pierced Rāma's divine horses with his shafts.

**6.95.15**  Those horses of his, though struck, neither stumbled nor swerved; they remained composed at heart, as if struck merely by lotus stalks.

**6.95.16**  Seeing the horses' lack of agitation, Rāvaṇa then grew yet more fiercely enraged and discharged another rain of arrows.

**6.95.17**  Maces, iron clubs, discuses, pestles, mountain peaks, trees, javelins, and battle-axes —

**6.95.18**  — all this storm of weapons conjured by māyā he hurled down; then, with heart and effort unwearied, he loosed arrows by the thousands.

**6.95.19**  The battle became tumultuous, terror-inspiring, fearful, with fearful reverberations, irresistible — a great conflict composed of many weapons.

**6.95.20**  Releasing shafts all around at Rāghava's chariot and upon the monkey army, and filling the sky continuously with arrows, the ten-headed one discharged them with an utterly free and unencumbered spirit.

**6.95.21**  Seeing Rāvaṇa straining with such total absorption in the battle, Kākutstha, almost smiling, fitted sharp arrows to his bow.

**6.95.22**  He then released arrows in the battle by hundreds of thousands; seeing them, Rāvaṇa with his own shafts made the sky into an unbroken mass.

**6.95.23**  Then, by the blazing rain of arrows loosed by those two, the sky appeared as a second luminous sky, bound together with shafts.

**6.95.24**  As Rāma and Rāvaṇa thus discharged arrows in battle, not one arrow was released without cause, not one failed to penetrate, not one was without effect.

**6.95.25**  Those two fought without pause, shooting left and right, and with swarms of arrows they made the sky breathless, as it were.

**6.95.26**  Rāma slew Rāvaṇa's horses and Rāvaṇa slew Rāma's horses — the two of them then doing and undoing each other's deeds in turn.

**6.96.1**  Rāma and Rāvaṇa thus fighting in battle were beheld by all living beings with wonder in their innermost hearts.

**6.96.2**  As they afflicted each other in battle, those two foremost chariots of theirs, bent on mutual destruction, became terrible in aspect.

**6.96.3**  Their two charioteers displayed many kinds of charioteer's movement — circles, straight rushes, advances and retreats.

**6.96.4**  Rāma assailing Rāvaṇa and Rāvaṇa assailing Rāghava, the two of them matched in speed of movement, charging forward and wheeling back.

**6.96.5**  As the two of them hurled webs of arrows, those two foremost chariots roamed the battlefield like two rain-clouds drenching the earth.

**6.96.6**  Having displayed many kinds of movement in battle, those two then halted once more facing each other.

**6.96.7**  As they stood, the yoke-poles of their chariots met yoke-pole to yoke-pole, the horses' muzzles met muzzle to muzzle, and the banners met banner to banner.

**6.96.8**  Then Rāma with four sharp arrows released from his bow drove back Rāvaṇa's four blazing horses.

**6.96.9**  Overcome by rage at the turning back of his horses, that rover of the night released sharp arrows against Rāghava.

**6.96.10**  Though deeply pierced by the mighty ten-headed one, Rāghava underwent no change and was not distressed.

**6.96.11**  And that night-roamer hurled arrows again, equal in sound to the fall of Indra's thunderbolt, aimed directly at the charioteer of the thunderbolt-wielder.

**6.96.12**  But those arrows of great velocity, falling upon the body of Mātali, produced not even the slightest confusion or distress in battle.

**6.96.13**  Angered by that assault — on behalf of Mātali rather than himself — Rāghava turned the enemy back with a net of arrows.

**6.96.14**  The heroic Rāghava released twenty, thirty, sixty, then hundreds and thousands of arrows upon the foe's chariot.

**6.96.15**  The seven oceans were agitated by the sounds of maces, pestles, iron clubs, and by the wind from the fletched ends of arrows.

**6.96.16**  By the agitation of the oceans, all the serpents dwelling on the floor of Pātāla were distressed, as were thousands of Dānavas.

**6.96.17**  The entire earth shook with its mountains, forests, and groves; the sun became without lustre, and the wind did not blow.

**6.96.18**  Then the gods together with the Gandharvas, the Siddhas and the great seers, all together with the Kinnaras and the great serpents, fell into anxious worry.

**6.96.19**  "May there be welfare for cows and Brāhmaṇas; may the worlds endure forever; may Rāghava conquer Rāvaṇa, lord of the rākṣasas, in battle."

**6.96.20**  Then Rāma, the mighty-armed increaser of the glory of the Raghus, fitting to his bow a crescent-bladed arrow like a venomous serpent, severed Rāvaṇa's noble head with its blazing earring.

**6.96.21**  That head fell to the ground and was seen by the three worlds; yet another head, equal to that one, rose up upon Rāvaṇa.

**6.96.22**  That too was swiftly severed in battle by Rāma, swift of hand and swift in action — a second head of Rāvaṇa cut off with arrows.

**6.96.23**  No sooner was that head severed than yet another appeared; that too was cut off by Rāma with arrows like thunderbolts.

**6.96.24**  In this same way a hundred heads of equal splendour were severed, yet no end was to be seen in the diminishment of Rāvaṇa's life.

**6.96.25**  Then the heroic Rāghava, increaser of Kausalyā's joy, knower of all weapons, laden with many arrows, fell into deep thought.

**6.96.26**  "Those by which Mārīca was slain, those by which Khara and Sudūṣaṇa were slain, Virādha in the Krañca forest, and Kabandha in the Daṇḍaka forest —"

**6.96.27**  "— all those arrows have been my trusted weapons in battle; what then is the reason that they grow feeble against Rāvaṇa?"

**6.96.28**  Thus absorbed in thought, yet unrelenting in battle, Rāghava poured rains of arrows upon Rāvaṇa's chest.

**6.96.29**  And Rāvaṇa too, enraged, the lord of the rākṣasas standing in his chariot, afflicted Rāma in battle with a rain of maces and pestles.

**6.96.30**  Before the watching eyes of gods, Dānavas, Yakṣas, Piśācas, serpents, and rākṣasas, that great battle continued through the entire night.

**6.96.31**  Neither by night nor by day, neither for a moment nor for the twinkling of an eye did the battle between Rāma and Rāvaṇa find any pause.

**6.97.1**  Then Mātali reminded Rāghava at that moment, saying as if in ignorance: 'O hero, why do you keep following him?'

**6.97.2**  'Release upon him, O lord, the weapon descended from Brahmā for his destruction — the hour of his annihilation that was foretold by the gods is now at hand.'

**6.97.3**  Then Rāma, reminded by those words of Mātali, took up that blazing arrow that hissed like a serpent.

**6.97.4**  That great, infallible arrow, bestowed by Brahmā and given first to him by the revered sage Agastya the mighty — that arrow which never misses in battle —

**6.97.5**  — had been fashioned of old by Brahmā of boundless might for Indra's sake, and given formerly to the lord of gods who desired the conquest of the three worlds.

**6.97.6**  In its feathers dwells the wind-god, in its tip fire and the sun; its body is made of sky, and in weight it equals Meru and Mandara.

**6.97.7**  Blazing in its form, beautifully fletched, adorned with gold, it was made with the radiance of all living beings and shone with the brilliance of the sun.

**6.97.8**  Like the fire of universal dissolution with its smoke, like a blazing venomous serpent, swift in action, it could cleave through hosts of chariots, elephants, and horses.

**6.97.9**  It could break through gates and iron bars and even mountains; dreadful, smeared with fat, its body sprinkled with many kinds of blood.

**6.97.10**  Hard as a thunderbolt, roaring mightily, fearsome in many battles, terrifying to all, dreadful, hissing like a snake.

**6.97.11**  In battle it was ever a feast for flocks of herons, vultures, jackals, and rākṣasas; bearing the form of Yama, inspiring dread.

**6.97.12**  A delight to the monkey-lords, a destroyer of rākṣasas, it was feathered with the manifold, beautifully variegated plumes of Garuḍa.

**6.97.13**  That supreme arrow of the worlds, destroyer of the fears of the Ikṣvāku line, stripping the glory from enemies, filling himself with joy —

**6.97.14**  — the mighty, powerful Rāma consecrated that great arrow with the rite prescribed by Vedic injunction and fitted it to his bow.

**6.97.15**  Deeply enraged at Rāvaṇa, he drew the bow fully taut and released that arrow that strikes the vital points, exerting his utmost force.

**6.97.16**  Irresistible as the thunderbolt hurled from Indra's arm, unstoppable as Fate itself, it fell upon Rāvaṇa's chest.

**6.97.17**  Released with tremendous velocity, that body-ending arrow pierced the heart of the evil-souled Rāvaṇa.

**6.97.18**  Blood-smeared, that life-ending arrow, bearing away Rāvaṇa's vital breath with its speed, entered into the earth.

**6.97.19**  That arrow, having slain Rāvaṇa, its surface wet with blood, its task accomplished, quietly returned to its own quiver.

**6.97.20**  From the hand of the struck one, the bow with its arrow fell quickly to the ground along with his life-breath as it departed from him.

**6.97.21**  His life-breath gone, the lord of the rākṣasas of terrific speed and great splendor fell from his chariot to the ground, like Vṛtra struck by the thunderbolt.

**6.97.22**  Seeing him fallen to the ground, the surviving night-roamers, bereft of their master and trembling with fear, fled in all directions.

**6.97.23**  The tree-fighting monkeys, roaring, fell upon them, having witnessed the slaying of the ten-necked one and Rāghava's victory.

**6.97.24**  Tormented by the exultant monkeys, the rākṣasas fled in fear toward Laṅkā, their refuge destroyed, their faces pitiful, streaming with tears.

**6.97.25**  Then the joyful monkeys, wearing the look of conquerors, let out a great cry, proclaiming Rāghava's victory and the death of Rāvaṇa.

**6.97.26**  Then the auspicious divine kettledrums of the gods resounded in the sky, and a sweet breeze bearing celestial fragrances blew there.

**6.97.27**  And from the sky a rain of flowers fell to the earth, scattering the charming, hard-to-obtain blossoms over Rāghava's chariot.

**6.97.28**  In the sky, joined with praises of Rāghava, there was heard the excellent speech of the great-souled gods: 'Well done! Well done!'

**6.97.29**  Great joy filled the gods together with the cāraṇas at the slaying of the fierce Rāvaṇa who had been the terror of all the worlds.

**6.97.30**  Then Rāghava, delighted, having slain the foremost of rākṣasas, fulfilled the wishes of Sugrīva and the mighty Aṅgada.
