**6.97.31**  Then the wind-hosts grew calm, the directions became clear, the sky became cloudless, the earth did not tremble, the winds ceased to blow, and the sun became steady in its brilliance.

**6.97.32**  Then Sugrīva, Vibhīṣaṇa, and the other dear friends, together with Lakṣmaṇa, came together in joy at the victory and honored Rāghava, who was splendid in battle, with due ceremony.

**6.97.33**  He, the enemy slain, his vow fulfilled, surrounded by the forces of his own people in battle, shone forth — the glorious son of the king of the Raghu line — surrounded by hosts of gods, like Indra himself.

**6.98.1**  Hearing that Rāvaṇa had been slain by the great-souled Rāghava, the rākṣasī women poured out from the inner apartments, emaciated with grief.

**6.98.2**  Though restrained again and again, weeping, rolling in the dust of the earth, their hair unbound, agonized with grief — like cows whose calves have been killed.

**6.98.3**  Going out through the northern gate together with the rākṣasas, they entered the dreadful battlefield, searching for their slain lord.

**6.98.4**  Crying 'O noble son!' and 'Alas, my master!' on every side, they rushed about over the earth thick with headless trunks, mired in blood.

**6.98.5**  Their eyes filled with tears, overcome by grief for their husband, they cried out like she-elephants whose herd-bull has been slain.

**6.98.6**  They beheld Rāvaṇa, of massive body, great might, and great splendor, slain upon the ground, resembling a heap of dark collyrium.

**6.98.7**  Suddenly seeing their husband lying in the dust of the battlefield, they fell upon his limbs like severed forest creepers.

**6.98.8**  Out of reverence one embraced him and wept; another clasped his feet; yet another hung about his neck.

**6.98.9**  One raised his arms and rolled on the ground; another, gazing upon the face of the slain one, fell into a swoon.

**6.98.10**  One, placing his head in her lap and gazing at his face, wept, bathing his face with tears as with cold dew upon a lotus.

**6.98.11**  Thus afflicted, seeing their husband Rāvaṇa slain upon the earth, they cried out repeatedly in many ways and lamented again and again in their grief.

**6.98.12**  He by whom Śakra was terrified, by whom Yama was terrified, by whom king Vaiśravaṇa was separated from the Puṣpaka —

**6.98.13**  — he who gave great fear to the gandharvas, the sages, and the great-souled gods — that same one now lies slain in battle.

**6.98.14**  He who knew no fear from asuras, from gods, or even from serpents — for him has come this fear from a human being.

**6.98.15**  He who was unkillable by the gods, by dānavas and rākṣasas alike — that same one lies slain in battle by a human fighting on foot.

**6.98.16**  He who could not be killed by gods, yakṣas, or asuras — this one, as if a trifling thing, has been brought to death by a mortal.

**6.98.17**  Speaking thus in many ways, his women wept; and further overwhelmed with grief, they lamented again and again.

**6.98.18**  'By your not heeding the friends who ever counseled your good, you have now brought down together — these women, us, and yourself.'

**6.98.19**  'Even your dear brother Vibhīṣaṇa, speaking words of benefit, was boldly treated harshly by you in your delusion, you who sought your own destruction.'

**6.98.20**  'Had Sītā the Maithilī been restored by you to Rāma, this dreadful, root-destroying great calamity would not have befallen us.'

**6.98.21**  'Your brother's wish would have been fulfilled, Rāma would have become a friendly lineage, all of us would remain unwidowed with our desires fulfilled, and the enemies would not have prevailed.'

**6.98.22**  'But by you, cruel one, forcibly detaining Sītā, three have been cast down into one scale — the rākṣasas, ourselves, and you yourself.'

**6.98.23**  'O foremost of rākṣasas, this was not your free will or your desire — fate drives everything; what is destroyed is destroyed by fate.'

**6.98.24**  'O mighty-armed one, this destruction of monkeys and your rākṣasas in battle, and indeed of yourself, has come about through the conjunction of fate.'

**6.98.25**  'The course of fate, once set in motion, cannot be reversed in this world — not by wealth, not by desire, not by valor, not by command.'

**6.98.26**  Thus those pitiable wives of the lord of rākṣasas lamented, grief-stricken like ospreys, their eyes clouded with tears.

**6.99.1**  Among those lamenting rākṣasī women, his chief and beloved wife, pitiable, gazed upon her husband.

**6.99.2**  Seeing the ten-necked one slain by Rāma of inconceivable deeds, Mandodarī there lamented pitiably for her husband.

**6.99.3**  'O mighty-armed one, O younger brother of Vaiśravaṇa — even Purandara trembles to stand before you when you are angered.

**6.99.4**  The sages, the earthly gods, the glorious gandharvas, and the cāraṇas — did they not flee to the directions in terror of you?

**6.99.5**  And yet you, O king, O bull among rākṣasas, are defeated in battle by a mere human named Rāma — are you not ashamed? What is this?

**6.99.6**  How did a human who wanders in the forest kill you — who had subjugated the three worlds endowed with glory and valor, you who were invincible?

**6.99.7**  Your destruction at the hands of Rāma in battle is not fitting for one who roams beyond the reach of humans, who can assume any form at will.

**6.99.8**  I cannot believe this is Rāma's own doing — that he, facing you in battle, you who were assailed from all sides, should have struck you down.

**6.99.9**  Having once conquered the senses you conquered the three worlds; now, as if those senses remembered that enmity, you were yourself conquered by the senses.

**6.99.10**  Or rather, Vāsava himself has come in the form of Rāma, contriving an illusion for your destruction that was beyond reckoning.

**6.99.11**  The very moment your brother Khara, surrounded by many rākṣasas, was slain at Janasthāna — from that moment alone, he was no human being.

**6.99.12**  The very moment Hanūmān by his might entered the city of Laṅkā — difficult of entry even for gods — from that very moment we were distressed.

**6.99.13**  'Let there be no enmity with Rāghava' — so I said to you, and you would not accept it; the consequence of that has now arrived.

**6.99.14**  You conceived an unprovoked desire for Sītā, O foremost of rākṣasas — for the destruction of your sovereignty, your body, and your own people.

**6.99.15**  By violating Sītā, who surpasses even Arundhatī and Rohiṇī, O foolish one, by molesting that venerable woman, you have done what is unbecoming of you.

**6.99.16**  The Maithilī is not superior to me nor even my equal in birth, beauty, or gracious conduct — but you in your delusion did not perceive this.

**6.99.17**  In every way, no death of any creature is without its mark — and your death, for one, bears the mark made by Maithilī.

**6.99.18**  Maithilī will sport with Rāma free of grief, while I, of meager merit, have fallen into a dreadful ocean of sorrow.

**6.99.19**  Having sported with you on Kailāsa, Mandara, and Meru, and in the Caitraratha forest and all the divine gardens —

**6.99.20**  — I who used to travel in a fitting aerial car with incomparable splendor, adorned with bright garlands and garments, beholding those various lands — I am cast down from all pleasures of desire by the slaying of you, my hero.

**6.99.21**  True indeed were the words spoken by my greatly fortunate brother-in-law: 'The destruction of the foremost rākṣasas is at hand.'

**6.99.22**  Through a calamity arising from desire and wrath, through entanglement with vice, you have done this — made the entire lineage of rākṣasas without a protector.

**6.99.23**  You of renowned strength and manhood are not truly to be mourned by me; but by the nature of women my mind turns to compassion.

**6.99.24**  Taking with you both your good deeds and your evil deeds, you have gone to your own final course; I mourn for myself, who am made wretched by separation from you.

**6.99.25**  Dark as a blue storm-cloud, adorned with bright golden armlets and yellow garments, your limbs flung wide, you lie drenched in blood — as if asleep. Why do you not speak to me who am tormented with grief?

**6.99.26**  Why do you not look up at me, the granddaughter of the great-valiant, capable, never-retreating in battle rākṣasa?

**6.99.27**  This bow of yours, with which you used to destroy enemies in battle, shining like the sun — like the thunderbolt of the thunderbolt-wielder — this ever-honored weapon of yours —

**6.99.28**  — your iron club, which struck enemies in battle, adorned with networks of gold, lies scattered, cut by arrows into a thousand pieces.

**6.99.29**  Shame upon this heart of mine that it does not burst into a thousand pieces — tormented with grief as it is — now that you have attained the five elements.

**6.99.30**  At this juncture Rāma said to Vibhīṣaṇa: 'Let the funeral rites be performed for your brother, and turn back these women.'

**6.99.31**  Then Vibhīṣaṇa, humbly hearing those words, the knower of righteousness, having deliberated with his mind on a reply consistent with both dharma and purpose, spoke in deference to Rāma.

**6.99.32**  'I am not fit to perform the rites for one who abandoned the vow of righteousness, who is cruel, brutal, false, and who violated another man's wife.'

**6.99.33**  'This Rāvaṇa, who took the form of a brother, was ever devoted to universal harm; he does not deserve honor, though he might be honored on account of reverence due to an elder.'

**6.99.34**  'O Rāma, people on earth will call me cruel; but all, hearing of his qualities, will speak of his good deeds again.'

**6.99.35**  Hearing that, Rāma, foremost among those who uphold righteousness, supremely pleased, spoke thus to Vibhīṣaṇa who was skilled in speech and an expert in counsel.

**6.99.36**  'What is dear to you is what I must do, and through you this conquest has been mine; yet, O lord of rākṣasas, I must necessarily say something fitting to you.'

**6.99.37**  'Though this night-rover was joined with unrighteousness and falsehood, he was nonetheless glorious, strong, brave, and ever so in battles.

**6.99.38**  'The great-souled Rāvaṇa, cause of lament to the world, endowed with strength — it is heard that he was not defeated even by gods led by the Hundred-Sacrificed Indra.

**6.99.39**  'Enmities end with death; our purpose is accomplished. Let his funeral rites be performed — he is as much mine as yours.'

**6.99.40**  'O mighty-armed, O knower of dharma, he deserves a proper funeral rite to be performed quickly by you; you will thereby become a sharer of glory.'

**6.99.41**  Hearing Rāghava's words, Vibhīṣaṇa, hastening, provided Rāvaṇa with the fitting funeral rite.

**6.99.42**  Vibhīṣaṇa offered the sacred fire to him according to the prescribed rite, and consoled those women, speaking words of comfort again and again.

**6.99.43**  When all the rākṣasī women had gone back inside, Vibhīṣaṇa came to Rāma's side and stood there in a humble manner.

**6.99.44**  And Rāma too, together with his army, with Sugrīva and Lakṣmaṇa, attained joy at having slain his enemy, as the Hundred-Sacrificed Indra had upon slaying Vṛtra.

**6.100.1**  Having witnessed the slaying of Rāvaṇa, the gods, gandharvas, and dānavas departed in their various celestial vehicles, relating auspicious tales to one another.

**6.100.2**  They narrated the terrible slaying of Rāvaṇa, the valor of Rāghava, the glorious battle of the monkeys, and the counsel of Sugrīva—

**6.100.3**  —and the devotion and heroism of Saumitri Lakṣmaṇa; thus those greatly blessed ones departed rejoicing by the same way they had come.

**6.100.4**  Rāghava, that greatly blessed one, then dismissed the divine chariot given by Indra — radiant as a flame — and duly honored Mātali.

**6.100.5**  Granted leave by Rāghava, Mātali — charioteer of Śakra — mounted that divine chariot and ascended directly to heaven.

**6.100.6**  When that best of the gods' charioteers had ascended to heaven, Rāghava, filled with supreme joy, embraced Sugrīva.

**6.100.7**  Having embraced Sugrīva, saluted by Lakṣmaṇa, and honored by the foremost of the monkeys, he came to the army's encampment.

**6.100.8**  Then Rāma spoke to Saumitri Lakṣmaṇa — endowed with great virtue and blazing with splendor — who stood close by his side:

**6.100.9**  "O gentle one, consecrate Vibhīṣaṇa here as king of Laṅkā — he who is devoted to me, loyal to me, and has rendered me great service."

**6.100.10**  "This is my deepest wish, O gentle one: to see this younger brother of Rāvaṇa, Vibhīṣaṇa, consecrated as ruler of Laṅkā."

**6.100.11**  Thus addressed by the great-souled Rāghava, Saumitri replied "So be it" with great joy and took up a golden vessel.

**6.100.12**  With that vessel Saumitri consecrated Vibhīṣaṇa as king amid the rākṣasas of Laṅkā, acting on Rāma's command.

**6.100.13**  That righteous-souled one consecrated the pure-souled Vibhīṣaṇa, and his ministers and the rākṣasas devoted to him rejoiced greatly.

**6.100.14**  Seeing Vibhīṣaṇa consecrated as lord of the rākṣasas in Laṅkā, Rāghava together with Lakṣmaṇa was filled with supreme delight.

**6.100.15**  Having obtained that great kingship bestowed by Rāma, Vibhīṣaṇa pacified his subjects and then came before Rāma.

**6.100.16**  Then the joyous night-roaming citizens brought him unbroken grains, sweetmeats, parched rice, divine flowers, and garlands.

**6.100.17**  The invincible and valiant Vibhīṣaṇa accepted those auspicious and propitious offerings and presented them all to Rāghava and Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.100.18**  Seeing Vibhīṣaṇa's purpose accomplished and his aim fulfilled, Rāma accepted all of it solely out of desire to please him.

**6.100.19**  Then Rāghava addressed the monkey Hanūmant — that hero who resembles a mountain, standing at his side with palms joined in reverence:

**6.100.20**  "O gentle one, with the permission of this great king Vibhīṣaṇa, enter the abode of Rāvaṇa and, approaching with humility—"

**6.100.21**  "—tell Vaidehī that I am well, together with Sugrīva and Lakṣmaṇa, O foremost among the victorious, and that Rāvaṇa has been slain by me."

**6.100.22**  "Having delivered this joyful news to the Maithilī, O lord of the monkeys, and having received her message in return, you should come back."

**6.101.1**  Thus commissioned, Hanūmān — son of the wind-god — entered the city of Laṅkā, honored by the night-roaming rākṣasas along the way.

**6.101.2**  Entering the dwelling of Rāvaṇa, that one of great splendor saw Sītā bereft of her moon, like Rohiṇī separated from the moon and filled with terror.

**6.101.3**  Approaching her quietly, bowing low and stooping respectfully, he saluted her and began to relate in full the message of Rāma.

**6.101.4**  "O Vaidehī, Rāma is well, together with Sugrīva and Lakṣmaṇa; the subduer of foes, his purpose accomplished and his enemy slain, sends you his greetings."

**6.101.5**  "O goddess, Rāvaṇa has been slain by Rāma aided by Vibhīṣaṇa, together with the monkeys, and by the wise strategy of Lakṣmaṇa."

**6.101.6**  "The heroic Rāma, the delight of the Raghu line, having inquired after your welfare, spoke with supreme joy in his heart, his purpose fulfilled:"

**6.101.7**  "'I bring you joyful tidings, O goddess, and I honor you all the more: by good fortune you live, O one who knows righteousness, through my victory in battle.'"

**6.101.8**  "'Victory has been won by us, O Sītā — be at ease, let your anguish pass. That enemy Rāvaṇa is slain, and this Laṅkā has been brought under control.'"

**6.101.9**  "'This vow made by me — who went without sleep and remained steadfast in winning your liberation — has been fully fulfilled by building a bridge across the great ocean.'"

**6.101.10**  "'You who dwell in the abode of Rāvaṇa need have no anxiety, for this sovereignty of Laṅkā has been placed under the authority of Vibhīṣaṇa.'"

**6.101.11**  "'Therefore be at ease, confident that you are now in your own home; and this one comes joyfully, eager for the sight of you.'"

**6.101.12**  Thus addressed, Sītā — whose face resembles the moon — leapt up, but overcome with surging joy she spoke not a single word.

**6.101.13**  The foremost of monkeys said to Sītā, who remained silent and unresponsive: "Why do you ponder, O goddess? Why do you not speak to me?"

**6.101.14**  Thus addressed by Hanūmān, Sītā — steadfast in righteousness — spoke with supreme joy, her voice choked and trembling with delight:

**6.101.15**  "Having heard this joyful news concerning my lord's victory, I was overcome with a rush of delight and rendered speechless for a moment."

**6.101.16**  "For I cannot find, however much I ponder, O monkey, a fitting reward here to offer you in return for having conveyed this joyful tidings of mine."

**6.101.17**  "Nor do I see, O gentle monkey, anything on this entire earth that could be given to you as equal recompense for conveying this joyful news to me."

**6.101.18**  "Silver or gold, or gems of many kinds, or sovereignty over the three worlds — none of these is worthy even to speak of in this regard."

**6.101.19**  Thus addressed by Vaidehī, the monkey replied, standing before Sītā with his palms joined together in reverence:

**6.101.20**  "You alone are worthy to speak such words of tender affection — you who are devoted to your lord's welfare and happiness, and who desire your lord's victory."

**6.101.21**  "Your words, O gentle one, are full of substance and tender feeling; they surpass a multitude of gems of every kind, and even sovereignty over the realm of the gods."

**6.101.22**  "And in substance I have already obtained the goods of sovereignty over the gods and more, in that I behold Rāma victorious and his enemy slain — this is my reward."

**6.101.23**  "But if you grant permission, I wish to kill all these rākṣasī women who formerly threatened and terrorized you—"

**6.101.24**  "—those of dreadful appearance and behavior, cruel and with even crueler eyes, who tormented you — who hold your husband as your deity — when you were in the Aśoka grove."

**6.101.25**  "Those rākṣasīs of terrible speech — grant me this boon: I wish to slay these utterly dreadful ones by various means of destruction."

**6.101.26**  "With fists, with hands, and with feet, O beautiful one, with terrible blows of the knee, and by knocking out their teeth—"

**6.101.27**  "—by biting off their ears and noses, by tearing out their hair, and by striking those dried-mouthed ones with terrible blows and leaping upon them—"

**6.101.28**  "—by many such acts of punishment, O renowned goddess, I wish to kill these ones who have done you wrong, O mistress."

**6.101.29**  Thus addressed by the great-minded one, the renowned Vaidehī — daughter of Janaka — spoke to Hanūmān in words consonant with righteousness:

**6.101.30**  "Who would be angry at slave-women who are subject to royal authority, who act by another's command, and who must comply with orders? O foremost of monkeys—"

**6.101.31**  "All this has come upon me through the working of adverse fate and through past misdeeds; for one experiences the fruits of one's own actions."

**6.101.32**  "I am certain that I had to suffer this through the conjunction of circumstance. I, being helpless, endure the conduct of Rāvaṇa's slave-women."

**6.101.33**  "These rākṣasīs threatened me under Rāvaṇa's orders; now that he is slain, O foremost of monkeys, they would not threaten me anymore."

**6.101.34**  "There is an ancient verse in keeping with righteousness, sung by a bear in the presence of a tiger — hear it from me, O monkey:"

**6.101.35**  "'Another does not incur the sin belonging to those who perform sinful acts; the noble, who are adorned by righteous conduct, must observe the proper code of behavior.'"

**6.101.36**  "Whether toward the wicked or the virtuous, even those deserving of death, O monkey, a noble one must show compassion — for there is no one who never transgresses."

**6.101.37**  "Even toward rākṣasas who roam the worlds delighting in harm and who take whatever form they wish, even while committing sins, nothing unworthy should be done."

**6.101.38**  Thus addressed by Sītā, Hanūmān — skilled in speech — replied to Sītā, the renowned wife of Rāma:

**6.101.39**  "You are truly the righteous and renowned wife of Rāma, befitting him. Give me your message to carry back, O goddess — I shall go to where Rāghava is."

**6.101.40**  Thus addressed by Hanūmān, Vaidehī — daughter of Janaka — said: "I desire to see my husband, O foremost of monkeys."

**6.101.41**  Hearing those words of hers, Hanūmān — son of the wind-god, of great radiance — spoke these words, filling the Maithilī with joy:

**6.101.42**  "You shall see Rāma, O noble lady — his face like the full moon — together with Lakṣmaṇa, firm in friendship and his enemy destroyed, as Śacī sees the lord of the thirty gods."

**6.101.43**  Having spoken thus to Sītā — who shone like Śrī herself made manifest — the swift Hanūmān came to where Rāghava was.

**6.102.1**  The monkey, skilled in meaning, approached Rāma — most wise and best among all bowmen — and spoke these words to him:

**6.102.2**  "She on whose account this entire undertaking began and its fruits have ripened — that goddess Maithilī, scorched by sorrow, you should now behold."

**6.102.3**  "For she — the Maithilī — though overwhelmed with grief and her eyes clouded with tears, upon hearing of your victory, was filled with joy."

**6.102.4**  "And she, trusting in our earlier bond of confidence, said to me: 'I desire to see my husband, his purpose accomplished, together with Lakṣmaṇa.'"

**6.102.5**  Thus addressed by Hanūmān, Rāma — best among upholders of righteousness — suddenly fell into deep contemplation, his eyes flooded with tears.

**6.102.6**  Sighing long and deeply, his gaze cast down to the earth, he spoke to Vibhīṣaṇa — who resembles a cloud — standing at hand:

**6.102.7**  "Have Vaidehī anointed with divine fragrant pastes, adorned with divine ornaments, and bathed with her hair washed, and bring Sītā here without delay."

**6.102.8**  Thus addressed by Rāma, the eager Vibhīṣaṇa entered the inner apartments and urged Sītā through his own women attendants:

**6.102.9**  "O Vaidehī, anointed with divine fragrant pastes and adorned with divine ornaments, mount the palanquin — may it be auspicious for you; your husband desires to see you."

**6.102.10**  Thus addressed, Vaidehī replied to Vibhīṣaṇa: "O lord of the rākṣasas, I wish to see my husband without bathing first."

**6.102.11**  Hearing those words of hers, Vibhīṣaṇa replied: "As your husband Rāma has said, so it is fitting that you act."

**6.102.12**  Hearing those words of his, the Maithilī — who holds her husband as her deity, who has taken the vow of devotion to her husband, that virtuous one — replied "So be it."

**6.102.13**  Then Sītā, her hair bathed, adorned by young women, wearing precious ornaments and clothed in costly garments—

**6.102.14**  —was placed upon a blazing-bright palanquin covered with the most precious cloth and guarded by many rākṣasas, and Vibhīṣaṇa brought her forward.

**6.102.15**  Approaching the great-souled Rāma and perceiving that he was absorbed in deep thought, Vibhīṣaṇa bowed humbly and joyfully announced that Sītā had arrived.

**6.102.16**  Upon hearing of her arrival — she who had long dwelt in the house of the rākṣasas — three emotions simultaneously seized Rāghava: joy, dejection, and anger.

**6.102.17**  Then, seeing Vibhīṣaṇa standing at his side and deliberating with grave reflection, the joyless Rāghava spoke these words:

**6.102.18**  "O gentle lord of rākṣasas, ever devoted to my victory — let Vaidehī come swiftly into my presence."

**6.102.19**  Understanding that command of Rāghava, Vibhīṣaṇa quickly set about clearing the way on all sides.

**6.102.20**  Men wearing armor-coats and turbans, bearing rattling cane staffs, moved about on all sides clearing and dispersing the crowd.

**6.102.21**  The crowds of bears, monkeys, and rākṣasas being driven back on all sides were dispersed far away from there.

**6.102.22**  From all of them as they were being dispersed arose a roar like the sound of the ocean being churned up by the wind.

**6.102.23**  Seeing them being driven away on all sides in a commotion, Rāghava — out of both courtesy and impatience — ordered them to stop.

**6.102.24**  And Rāma, agitated, his eyes burning as if with fire, spoke these reproachful words to the greatly wise Vibhīṣaṇa:

**6.102.25**  "Why, disregarding me, are these people being harassed by you? Stop this effort — these people are my own people."

**6.102.26**  "Not houses, not garments, not walls are screens of concealment — the proper veil of a woman is her conduct; such is not the way a woman is honored by a king."

**6.102.27**  "In times of misfortune, in hardship, in battle, at a svayaṃvara, at a sacrifice, or at a wedding — the sight of a woman is not a fault."

**6.102.28**  "This Sītā has been through war and has stood in great hardship; there is no fault in seeing her, especially in my presence."

**6.102.29**  "Therefore bring her swiftly into my presence, O Vibhīṣaṇa — let Sītā see me standing here surrounded by the company of friends."

**6.102.30**  Thus addressed by Rāma, the reflective Vibhīṣaṇa humbly and respectfully led Sītā into Rāma's presence.

**6.102.31**  Then Lakṣmaṇa, Sugrīva, and the monkey Hanūmān, having heard Rāma's words, became greatly distressed.

**6.102.32**  From his harsh manner and gestures showing indifference toward his wife, they surmised that Rāghava seemed displeased with Sītā.

**6.102.33**  The Maithilī, shrinking with shame into her own limbs, accompanied by Vibhīṣaṇa, moved toward her husband.

**6.102.34**  She, her face half-concealed in her garment, wept with shame in that assembly of people as she drew near to her husband, speaking the words "O noble son."

**6.102.35**  With wonder, with surging joy, with love, and with lament she gazed up at her husband's gentle face — she whose own face was yet more gentle.

**6.102.36**  Then, gazing long upon the beloved face of her husband — radiant as the risen full moon in its beauty — she whose own face resembled the pure moon put aside the weariness of her heart, and thus she stood.

**6.103.1**  Seeing Maithilī standing humbly at his side, Rāma — his anger held within his heart — began to speak.

**6.103.2**  "You have been won back, fair one, with my enemy vanquished in battle; what a man of honour was bound to do, that I have accomplished.

**6.103.3**  I have put an end to my indignation; the affront has been wiped clean; dishonour and enemy — both have been uprooted by me at the same time.

**6.103.4**  Today my manly vigour stands proven; today my toil has borne fruit; today, having fulfilled my vow, I am master of myself.

**6.103.5**  You were carried off by a fickle-minded Rākṣasa — that fault wrought by fate has been overcome by me, a mortal.

**6.103.6**  What purpose of man does that man of feeble spirit serve, who does not wipe away with his valour the dishonour he has received?

**6.103.7**  Today that praiseworthy act of Hanūmat — the crossing of the ocean and the trampling of Laṅkā — has borne its fruit.

**6.103.8**  Today the exertion of Sugrīva, who strove valiantly in battle and counselled my welfare together with his army, has borne its fruit.

**6.103.9**  Today the effort of the devoted Vibhīṣaṇa — who, abandoning his worthless brother, came to me of his own accord — has borne its fruit.

**6.103.10**  As Rāma spoke these words, Sītā — her eyes wide open like a doe's — was flooded with tears.

**6.103.11**  As Rāma looked upon her, his anger flared up afresh, blazing like a fire fed with copious ghee.

**6.103.12**  With furrowed brow and eyes cast askance, he spoke harshly to Sītā in the midst of the monkeys and Rākṣasas:

**6.103.13**  "Whatever a man must do to wipe away an outrage, all of that has been done, O Sītā, in my fury against the enemy.

**6.103.14**  As the southern quarter was subdued by the sage Agastya — his soul perfected by asceticism — hard to overcome in all the world, so has the foe been vanquished.

**6.103.15**  Know well, fair one, that this toil of battle was undertaken and brought to completion through the valour of friends — not for your sake by me.

**6.103.16**  It was undertaken by me to safeguard my conduct, to ward off reproach on every side, and to wipe clean the stain on my celebrated lineage.

**6.103.17**  Now that suspicion has arisen about your chastity, your standing before me is as offensive to me as a lamp is to one whose eyes are afflicted.

**6.103.18**  Therefore, O daughter of Janaka, you are dismissed — go wherever you wish, to these ten directions; I have no further need of you.

**6.103.19**  For what man of noble birth and self-respect, his heart guided by good counsel, would take back a woman who has lived in another's house?

**6.103.20**  How could I take you back again — you who have fallen from Rāvaṇa's lap and been beheld by his lustful eyes — I, who declare my lineage to be great?

**6.103.21**  The war was fought for that purpose; my honour has been retrieved by me; I have no attachment to you — depart hence wherever you please.

**6.103.22**  This, fair one, I have declared with a settled mind. Set your heart, if you wish, on Lakṣmaṇa or on Bharata.

**6.103.23**  Or fix your mind upon Sugrīva, king of the monkeys, or upon Vibhīṣaṇa, lord of the Rākṣasas, O Sītā — or wherever your own happiness lies.

**6.103.24**  For Rāvaṇa, beholding you of divine form and captivating beauty dwelling in his own abode, would not have endured it long without consummating his desire.

**6.103.25**  Then Maithilī, worthy to receive love at his feet, hearing those unwelcome words from her beloved after so long, burst into profuse weeping, trembling like a vine struck by the trunk of a lordly elephant.

**6.104.1**  Thus addressed with harsh and hair-raising words by the wrathful Rāghava, Vaidehī was deeply distressed.

**6.104.2**  Hearing those rough words of her husband — words never heard before — in that great assembly, Maithilī was overwhelmed with shame.

**6.104.3**  The daughter of Janaka, as though pierced to the quick by those verbal shafts, seemed to sink into herself, and shed copious tears.

**6.104.4**  Then, wiping her face wet with tears, she spoke these words to her husband in a slow, choked voice:

**6.104.5**  "Why do you cause me to hear such unworthy and ear-searing harsh words, O hero — as a vulgar man speaks to a common woman?

**6.104.6**  I am not as you imagine, O great-armed one. Know me truly — I swear to you by my own conduct.

**6.104.7**  You suspect the whole class on account of the ways of lowly women; abandon this suspicion — I am one who has been tested.

**6.104.8**  If, O lord, I came into bodily contact against my will, the fault is not mine there — it is fate that is to blame.

**6.104.9**  But what is under my control — my heart — that abides in you; what could I do, powerless, over a body under another's control?

**6.104.10**  If I am not known to you through our shared upbringing and intimacy, O giver of honour, then I am forever destroyed by that.

**6.104.11**  When your hero Hanūmat was sent to find me and I was in Laṅkā, why were you not pleased to have me abandoned then, O hero?

**6.104.12**  In the presence of the lord of monkeys, immediately upon receiving your message, I — being abandoned by you, O hero — would have given up my life.

**6.104.13**  Your toil would not have been in vain, having laid down my life in doubt, and the suffering of your allies would not have been fruitless.

**6.104.14**  But you, O tiger among men, following only your anger, have given precedence — as a shallow man would — merely to the fact of my womanhood.

**6.104.15**  My origin not from Janaka but from the earth's surface, and my conduct — you who are learned in conduct — has not been given weight by you.

**6.104.16**  The hand clasped in childhood, pressed by a child, my devotion and my character — all of this you have cast behind you.

**6.104.17**  Speaking thus, weeping, her voice faltering with sobs, Sītā addressed Lakṣmaṇa, who stood absorbed in thought, downcast:

**6.104.18**  "Build me a pyre, O son of Sumitrā — that is the remedy for this calamity. Struck down by a false accusation, I have no wish to live.

**6.104.19**  Forsaken in a public assembly by a husband who takes no pleasure in my virtues, the only course befitting me is to enter the fire."

**6.104.20**  Addressed thus by Vaidehī, Lakṣmaṇa — the slayer of enemy heroes — fell under the sway of indignation and looked towards Rāghava's face.

**6.104.21**  Understanding Rāma's wish as indicated by his demeanour, the valiant son of Sumitrā built the pyre in accordance with Rāma's intent.

**6.104.22**  Then Vaidehī, after slowly circumambulating Rāma with his face cast downward, approached the blazing fire.

**6.104.23**  Maithilī, bowing to the gods and the Brāhmaṇas, stood near the fire with hands folded in reverence and spoke thus:

**6.104.24**  "As my heart has never strayed from Rāghava, so may Pāvaka, the witness of all worlds, protect me on every side."

**6.104.25**  Having spoken thus and circumambulated the fire, Vaidehī entered the blazing flames with a heart utterly serene and free of attachment.

**6.104.26**  That vast assembly gathered there — thronged with young and old alike — witnessed Maithilī entering the fire.

**6.104.27**  As she entered the fire, a great cry of "Alas! Alas!" arose among the Rākṣasas and the monkeys — a sound beyond all wonder.

**6.105.1**  Then Vaiśravaṇa the king, and Yama the afflicitor of foes, and the thousand-eyed Mahendra, and the scorching Varuṇa —

**6.105.2**  — and the glorious three-eyed Mahādeva whose banner bears the bull, and Brahmā the creator of all worlds, best among knowers of Brahman —

**6.105.3**  — all of these assembled together and, arriving at the city of Laṅkā in celestial chariots bright as the sun, approached Rāghava.

**6.105.4**  Then the chiefs of the thirty gods, raising their broad arms adorned with bracelets, addressed Rāghava as he stood with hands reverently joined:

**6.105.5**  "You are the creator of all worlds, the foremost among the wise, best of all. How can you disregard Sītā as she falls into the fire? How do you not know yourself to be the chief among the hosts of gods?"

**6.105.6**  "You were formerly Ṛtadhāman, a Vasu, lord of the Vasus — you are the primordial creator of the three worlds, self-luminous.

**6.105.7**  You are the eighth Rudra among the Rudras, and the fifth among the Sādhyas; the two Aśvins are your ears, and the moon and sun your two eyes.

**6.105.8**  You are seen at the beginning and end of the worlds, O scorcher of foes — yet you ignore Vaidehī as though you were an ordinary mortal.

**6.105.9**  Thus addressed by those guardians of the worlds, Rāghava — lord of the worlds, Rāma, best of those who uphold dharma — spoke to the chiefs of the thirty gods:

**6.105.10**  "I consider myself a mortal man, Rāma the son of Daśaratha. Who I am, whose I am, and whence I am — let the blessed one tell me that."

**6.105.11**  To Kākutstha speaking thus, Brahmā — best among knowers of Brahman — said: "Hear the truth from me, O Rāma of true valour:

**6.105.12**  You are the god Nārāyaṇa, the glorious, the all-pervading lord who wields the discus; you are the boar with a single tusk, the vanquisher of foes past and future.

**6.105.13**  You are the imperishable, the truth of Brahman, in the middle and at the end, O Rāghava; you are the supreme dharma of the worlds, Viṣvaksena, four-armed.

**6.105.14**  You are Śārṅgadhanvan, Hṛṣīkeśa, the Puruṣa, Puruṣottama, the Unconquered, the sword-bearer, Viṣṇu, Kṛṣṇa, and the immensely powerful one.

**6.105.15**  You are the general of armies and the village chief; you are intellect, being, patience, and restraint; you are the source and dissolution; you are Upendra and Madhusūdana.

**6.105.16**  You are the one who acts like Indra, great Mahendra, Padmanābha, the destroyer of enemies in battle; the divine great sages call you the refuge and the shelter.

**6.105.17**  You are the thousand-horned, the soul of the Vedas, the hundred-tongued, the great bull; you are the sacrifice, you are the vaṣaṭ-call, you are the syllable Oṃ, O scorcher of foes.

**6.105.18**  None knows your origin or your end, nor who you are; you are seen in all beings, in Brāhmaṇas, and in cows.

**6.105.19**  In all directions, in the sky, in mountains and in forests — you of a thousand feet, the glorious, the hundred-headed, the bearer of a thousand weapons.

**6.105.20**  You sustain all beings and the earth with its mountains; at the end of the earth you appear in the waters as the great serpent.

**6.105.21**  You uphold the three worlds, O Rāma — gods, Gandharvas, and Dānavas; I, Brahmā, am your heart, O Rāma, and the goddess Sarasvatī your tongue.

**6.105.22**  The gods were fashioned as the hairs of your body by Brahmā, O lord; your closing of the eyes became night, your opening of the eyes became day.

**6.105.23**  The Vedas became your sacraments; nothing exists apart from you; the entire universe is your body, and the steadiness of the earth is your steadfastness.

**6.105.24**  Fire is your wrath and Soma your serenity, O bearer of the Śrīvatsa mark; in ancient times the three worlds were traversed by you with three strides.

**6.105.25**  Mahendra was made king by you after binding Bali the great Asura; Sītā is Lakṣmī, you are Viṣṇu the god, Kṛṣṇa, Prajāpati.

**6.105.26**  You entered a human body here for the purpose of slaying Rāvaṇa; that purpose of ours has been accomplished by you, O best of those who uphold dharma.

**6.105.27**  Rāvaṇa is slain, O Rāma — ascend to heaven rejoicing; your strength and valour are unfailing, and your heroism is unfailing.

**6.105.28**  Those mortals who are devoted to you, who are steadfastly devoted to you — the ancient, eternal god, Puruṣottama — will never be frustrated.

**6.105.29**  Those mortals who shall glorify you — for them there is no defeat."

**6.106.1**  Having heard this auspicious speech uttered by the Grandfather (Brahmā), Vibhāvasu (Agni, the fire-god) leapt forth, taking Vaidehī in his arms.

**6.106.2**  She resembled the rising sun; she was adorned with ornaments of refined gold; she wore red garments; she was young, with dark and curling hair—

**6.106.3**  —bearing unfaded garlands and ornaments, lovely in form, and noble of spirit: Vibhāvasu, placing Vaidehī upon his lap, presented her to Rāma.

**6.106.4**  And then Pāvaka (Agni), the witness of all the worlds, spoke to Rāma: "Here is your Vaidehī, O Rāma; no sin exists in her.

**6.106.5**  Neither by speech, nor by mind, nor by contemplation, nor by glance has this virtuous woman, proud of her good conduct, ever transgressed against you.

**6.106.6**  She was carried off by Rāvaṇa, that demon swollen with arrogance; separated from you, she was wretched and helpless, taken from the desolate forest.

**6.106.7**  Imprisoned within the inner chambers, guarded closely, her mind fixed on you alone, wholly devoted to you, she was watched over by bands of deformed and dreadful-looking rākṣasīs.

**6.106.8**  Though tempted in various ways and repeatedly threatened, Maithilī gave no thought to that demon, for her innermost soul was wholly given over to you.

**6.106.9**  Take back this woman of pure heart and free of sin, O Rāghava. There is nothing to be said against her — I command you thus.

**6.106.10**  Thus addressed, Rāma — of great splendour, firm resolve, and steadfast valour, the foremost among upholders of dharma — spoke to that chief of the thirty gods (Agni):

**6.106.11**  "Sītā must certainly undergo purification before the three worlds, for this auspicious woman has long dwelt in Rāvaṇa's inner chambers.

**6.106.12**  For the virtuous would say of me, 'Rāma, son of Daśaratha, is foolish and enslaved by desire,' if I were to take back Jānakī without purifying her.

**6.106.13**  I too know Maithilī, daughter of Janaka, to be undivided in her heart, devoted, and scrupulously guarded in her mind toward me.

**6.106.14**  But for the conviction of the three worlds — I who am anchored in truth — I allowed Vaidehī to enter the sacrificial fire.

**6.106.15**  Even this wide-eyed woman, protected by her own radiance, could not be transgressed by Rāvaṇa, just as the great ocean cannot overstep its shore.

**6.106.16**  That evil-souled one was not capable of violating Maithilī even in thought — she who was beyond his reach, blazing like a tongue of fire.

**6.106.17**  This auspicious woman does not deserve the taint of Rāvaṇa's dominion over her, for Sītā is inseparable from me as radiance is from the sun.

**6.106.18**  Maithilī, daughter of Janaka, is pure in all three worlds; she is no more to be abandoned than a self-possessed man can abandon his own fame.

**6.106.19**  And I must comply with the salutary word of all of you — affectionate, honoured in all the worlds, and speaking thus for my welfare."

**6.106.20**  Having spoken these words and being praised by the mighty gods for his own deeds, Rāma the mighty — Rāghava who was worthy of happiness — was reunited with his beloved and experienced joy.

**6.107.1**  Having heard this auspicious speech spoken eloquently by Rāghava, Maheśvara (Śiva) uttered these still more auspicious words:

**6.107.2**  "O lotus-eyed one, mighty-armed one, broad-chested one, scorcher of foes — by good fortune this deed has been accomplished by you, O foremost among those who bear arms!

**6.107.3**  By good fortune, O Rāma, the dreadful darkness that had spread over all the worlds, the terror born of Rāvaṇa, has been rolled back by you in battle.

**6.107.4**  Console Bharata who is afflicted with grief; and visit the glorious Kausalyā, and Kaikeyī, and Sumitrā — the mother of Lakṣmaṇa;

**6.107.5**  regain the kingdom of Ayodhyā, delight your well-wishers, and establish the lineage in the family of the Ikṣvākus, O mighty one;

**6.107.6**  having performed the horse-sacrifice and attained incomparable glory, having given wealth to the brāhmaṇas — then you shall be worthy to go to the heavenly realm."

**6.107.7**  "O Kākutstha, there stands your father, King Daśaratha, seated in his celestial vehicle — your revered elder in the human world, of great and illustrious fame.

**6.107.8**  The glorious one has reached Indra's realm, ferried across by you, his son. Pay your respects to him together with your brother Lakṣmaṇa."

**6.107.9**  Hearing the words of Mahādeva (Śiva), Kākutstha together with Lakṣmaṇa bowed in reverence to his father who stood at the summit of the celestial vehicle.

**6.107.10**  The lord saw his father blazing with his own radiance, clothed in spotless garments, together with his brother Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.107.11**  Then Daśaratha, that ruler of the earth, seated in the celestial vehicle, was seized with great joy upon beholding his son who was dearer to him than life.

**6.107.12**  The mighty-armed lord, seated on a splendid throne, lifted him onto his lap, embraced him with both arms, and then began to speak:

**6.107.13**  "Heaven is not dear to me, nor the honour shown by gods and sages, when I am deprived of you, O Rāma — this I promise you in truth.

**6.107.14**  The words spoken by Kaikeyī, O foremost of speakers, those words aimed at sending you into exile — they have weighed upon my heart.

**6.107.15**  But now, seeing you well and embracing you together with Lakṣmaṇa, I am this day freed from sorrow, as the sun is freed from mist.

**6.107.16**  I have been ferried across by you, O son, by a noble son of great soul — as the righteous brahmin was once ferried across by Aṣṭāvakra.

**6.107.17**  And now I understand, O gentle one, how the highest among men was appointed here by the lords of the gods for the purpose of slaying Rāvaṇa.

**6.107.18**  Truly, Kausalyā has achieved her purpose — she who, filled with joy, will behold you, O slayer of enemies, returned from the forest and come home.

**6.107.19**  Truly, those men have achieved their purpose, O Rāma, who will behold you as lord of the earth, entered into the city and anointed with consecration-waters.

**6.107.20**  I wish to see you united with Bharata, who is devoted to you, mighty, pure, and devoted to dharma.

**6.107.21**  Fourteen years, O gentle one, have been spent by you dwelling in the forest together with Sītā and the wise Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.107.22**  Your forest-exile is ended, your vow has been fulfilled; having slain Rāvaṇa in battle, you have given satisfaction to the gods.

**6.107.23**  A praiseworthy deed has been accomplished and glorious renown has been attained by you, O slayer of enemies. Established in the kingdom together with your brothers, may you enjoy a long life."

**6.107.24**  To the king speaking thus, Rāma spoke with folded hands: "O knower of dharma, grant your grace to Kaikeyī and to Bharata.

**6.107.25**  The dreadful curse you pronounced upon Kaikeyī — 'I abandon you together with your son' — let that curse, O lord, not touch Kaikeyī together with her son."

**6.107.26**  The great king said "So be it" to Rāma who stood with joined palms; then, embracing Lakṣmaṇa, he spoke again:

**6.107.27**  "By serving Rāma with devotion, together with Vaidehī Sītā, you have given me great joy, and you have obtained the fruit of dharma.

**6.107.28**  You shall attain dharma, O knower of dharma, and abundant glory on earth; and, with Rāma pleased with you, you shall also attain heaven and greatness.

**6.107.29**  Serve Rāma — be blessed, O increaser of Sumitrā's joy! Rāma is always devoted to the welfare of all the worlds.

**6.107.30**  These three worlds together with Indra, the Siddhas, and the supreme sages, approach and worship this great-souled one, Puruṣottama (the highest of beings).

**6.107.31**  This is he of whom it is spoken as the unmanifest, the imperishable, the one fashioned by Brahman, the heart of the gods, O gentle one, the secret — Rāma, the scorcher of foes.

**6.107.32**  By serving Rāma with devotion together with Vaidehī Sītā, you have obtained the practice of dharma and abundant glory.

**6.107.33**  Having spoken thus to Lakṣmaṇa who stood with joined palms, that righteous king of mighty arms then spoke these auspicious words to Vaidehī:

**6.107.34**  "Do not feel anger regarding this abandonment, O Vaidehī; this was done by Rāma, who seeks your good, for the purpose of establishing your purity.

**6.107.35**  You, O fair-browed one, need no instruction regarding devotion to your husband; yet it must be said by me: he is your supreme deity.

**6.107.36**  Having thus instructed both sons, Sītā, and his daughter-in-law, the blazing Daśaratha departed to Indra's realm in his celestial vehicle.

**6.108.1**  After Kākutstha (Daśaratha) had departed, Mahendra — the chastiser of Pāka — filled with the highest delight, spoke to Rāghava who stood with joined palms:

**6.108.2**  "Your sight to us, O Rāma, O scorcher of foes, is never in vain. I am filled with affection on that account — speak, tell me whatever your mind desires."

**6.108.3**  Thus addressed, Kākutstha — together with his brother Lakṣmaṇa and his wife Sītā — replied with joined palms:

**6.108.4**  "If affection has arisen for me, O lord of all the gods, then I shall speak — fulfil my word truly, O foremost of speakers.

**6.108.5**  All those vānaras who fought valiantly on my behalf and went to the abode of Yama — let them all rise again, having obtained life.

**6.108.6**  They are devoted to my beloved ones and did not reckon with death; by your grace, let them be reunited with their kin. This is the boon I choose.

**6.108.7**  I wish to see them — the golāṅgūlas (tailed monkeys) and likewise the ṛkṣas (bears) — free of disease, free of wounds, fully endowed with strength and vigour, O granter of honour.

**6.108.8**  And even out of season, let choice roots and fruits be available, and let clear rivers stand wherever the vānaras may be."

**6.108.9**  Having heard those words of that great-souled Rāghava, Mahendra replied with words marked by affection:

**6.108.10**  "Great is this boon you have named, O dear one, O joy of the Raghus: the monkeys shall rise again, as those who slept wake at the passing of slumber.

**6.108.11**  They shall all be reunited with their friends, kinsmen, relatives, and own people, joined together with the highest joy.

**6.108.12**  Even out of season, the trees shall be filled with blossoms and laden with fruit, O great archer, and the rivers shall be full of water."

**6.108.13**  The vānaras whose limbs had first been covered with wounds became again free of wounds; all of them arose, astonished and asking, "What is this?"

**6.108.14**  All the foremost gods, seeing Kākutstha whose purpose was fully accomplished, first praised him who was worthy of praise together with Lakṣmaṇa, and then spoke:

**6.108.15**  "Go hence to Ayodhyā, O hero; dismiss the vānaras; and comfort Maithilī, this devoted and long-suffering woman.

**6.108.16**  See your brother Bharata who has been observing vows in his grief for you; anoint yourself as king, and going to the citizens, bring them joy."

**6.108.17**  Having spoken thus and taking leave of Rāma together with the son of Sumitrā, the gods, filled with joy, departed to heaven in celestial vehicles blazing like the sun.

**6.108.18**  And Kākutstha, having paid his respects to all those foremost of the thirty gods, then commanded his army to encamp, together with his brother Lakṣmaṇa.

**6.108.19**  Then that great army, guarded by Lakṣmaṇa and Rāma, its people filled with joy, glorious and blazing with splendour, shone resplendently on every side — even as the moon, led forth by night, blazes with cool radiance.

**6.109.1**  Having passed the night, Rāma — the subduer of foes — rose at ease; Vibhīṣaṇa approached with folded hands, offered felicitations on his victory, and addressed him.

**6.109.2**  "Bathing waters, unguents for the body, garments and ornaments, divine sandalwood paste, and garlands of various kinds —

**6.109.3**  — and these women skilled in adornment, with eyes like lotuses, stand ready to bathe you duly according to rite, O Rāghava."

**6.109.4**  Thus addressed, Kākutstha replied to Vibhīṣaṇa: "Invite the monkeys headed by Sugrīva to bathe as well."

**6.109.5**  "That righteous-souled one, who deserves comfort, tender-limbed and long-armed, that prince of true vow —

**6.109.6**  — without that son of Kaikeyī, the righteous Bharata, who pines on my account, bathing, garments, and ornaments hold no great value for me.

**6.109.7**  Let us proceed swiftly by this very route back to that city, Ayodhyā — for this road, though traversed, is exceedingly difficult."

**6.109.8**  Thus addressed, Vibhīṣaṇa replied to Kākutstha: "I shall bring you to that city within a single day, O son of a king.

**6.109.9**  The aerial chariot called Puṣpaka — brilliant as the sun — which was forcibly taken from my brother Kubera by Rāvaṇa, bodes well for you.

**6.109.10**  That chariot, resembling a cloud, stands here; by it, O conqueror of the lordly elephant, you shall travel to Ayodhyā.

**6.109.11**  If I am worthy of your grace, if you remember my merits, if you have friendship for me — then, O wise one, dwell here for a time.

**6.109.12**  Honoured together with your brother Lakṣmaṇa and your wife Vaidehī with every comfort and desire, then, O Rāma, you shall depart."

**6.109.13**  "With your army and the assembly of your friends, O Rāma — please accept this hospitality that has been arranged and offered by me with joy.

**6.109.14**  Out of affection, deep regard, and friendship, O Rāghava, I am making this plea — I am your servant; I am not issuing you a command."

**6.109.15**  Thus addressed, Rāma then replied to Vibhīṣaṇa within the hearing of all the Rākṣasas and the monkeys:

**6.109.16**  "I have been honoured by you, O hero, O scorcher of foes, through your ministership, through every deed with body and mind, and through your supreme friendship.

**6.109.17**  I would not fail to carry out this word of yours, O lord of Rākṣasas — yet my heart is eager to see my brother Bharata.

**6.109.18**  He who came to Citrakūṭa to turn me back, bowing his head in supplication — whose entreaty I did not grant —

**6.109.19**  — and Kausalyā, Sumitrā, and the illustrious Kaikeyī, my elders and my friends, and the citizens together with their sons —

**6.109.20**  — provide for me the chariot swiftly, O lord of Rākṣasas; now that my purpose is fulfilled, residence here is hardly agreeable to me.

**6.109.21**  Grant me leave, O gentle one; I have been honoured, Vibhīṣaṇa — you must not take offence; I take my leave hastily and beg your indulgence.

**6.109.22**  Then — adorned with golden decorations, furnished with platforms of lapis lazuli, surrounded on all sides by pavilions, radiant with the lustre of silver —

**6.109.23**  — decked with white pennants and standards, beautified with golden mansions, embellished with golden lotuses,

**6.109.24**  — strewn with clusters of small bells, with windows inlaid with pearl and gem, encircled by networks of bells giving forth sweet sounds on every side —

**6.109.25**  — shaped like the peak of Mount Meru, fashioned by Viśvakarman, adorned with many mansions gleaming white with pearl and silver —

**6.109.26**  — its floors decorated with crystal, with choice seats of lapis lazuli, furnished with rich and costly coverings, endowed with great wealth —

**6.109.27**  — that irresistible chariot, swift as thought, was presented to Rāma, and Vibhīṣaṇa stood before him.

**6.110.1**  Seeing the Puṣpaka chariot — adorned with flowers — standing near at hand, Vibhīṣaṇa addressed Rāma.

**6.110.2**  That lord of Rākṣasas, hands folded, bowing humbly and deferentially, asked Rāghava with urgent solicitude: "What shall I do?"

**6.110.3**  The great-souled Rāghava, reflecting, spoke these words filled with affection within the hearing of Lakṣmaṇa:

**6.110.4**  "The forest-dwelling monkeys who have laboured and exerted themselves — honour them, Vibhīṣaṇa, with jewels, wealth of various kinds, and ornaments.

**6.110.5**  Together with them, O lord of Rākṣasas, Laṅkā has been conquered — by those who, rejoicing, cast aside all fear for their lives and never turned back in battle.

**6.110.6**  When honoured thus — as they deserve to be honoured — these chiefs of the monkey hosts, O bestower of honour, will become content, gratified by your grateful heart.

**6.110.7**  Since they know you as one who is generous, who gathers allies, who is compassionate and illustrious — for that very reason I remind you of this."

**6.110.8**  Thus addressed by Rāma, Vibhīṣaṇa honoured all those monkeys, distributing jewels and wealth to each.

**6.110.9**  Then, having seen the troop-leaders thus honoured with jewels and wealth, Rāma mounted that supreme chariot.

**6.110.10**  Taking the illustrious Vaidehī, who was modest with shyness, onto his lap, and accompanied by his heroic, bow-bearing brother Lakṣmaṇa,

**6.110.11**  Kākutstha, seated in the chariot, addressed all the monkeys and the mighty Sugrīva and the Rākṣasa Vibhīṣaṇa:

**6.110.12**  "The duty of friends has been performed by you, O foremost of monkeys; you are all dismissed by me — depart, each as you wish."

**6.110.13**  "O scorcher of foes, Sugrīva — all that befits a comrade and a friend has been done by you, who are ever reverent of dharma; return swiftly to Kiṣkindhā, surrounded by your own army.

**6.110.14**  Dwell in sovereignty over Laṅkā, given by me to you, Vibhīṣaṇa — not even the gods together with Indra are capable of assailing you.

**6.110.15**  I am about to return to Ayodhyā, my father's royal capital; I wish to take your leave — I bid farewell to you all."

**6.110.16**  Thus addressed by Rāma, those immensely powerful monkeys and the Rākṣasa Vibhīṣaṇa replied with folded hands: "We wish to go to Ayodhyā — let your honour lead all of us."

**6.110.17**  "Having seen you consecrated with the coronation waters, and having paid homage to Kausalyā, we shall before long return to our own homes, O son of the king."

**6.110.18**  Thus addressed by the monkeys together with Vibhīṣaṇa, the righteous-souled, illustrious Rāghava spoke to them — Sugrīva and Vibhīṣaṇa among them:

**6.110.19**  "What is dearer than the dear has been gained by me — accompanied by my friends and together with all of you, I shall experience joy upon reaching my city.

**6.110.20**  Board the chariot swiftly, Sugrīva, together with your monkeys; you too ascend, Vibhīṣaṇa, lord of Rākṣasas, along with your ministers."

**6.110.21**  Then Sugrīva together with his army swiftly and quickly ascended that divine Puṣpaka chariot, as did Vibhīṣaṇa with his ministers.

**6.110.22**  When all of them had boarded, that supreme vehicle of Kubera, permitted to rise by Rāghava, soared aloft into the sky.

**6.110.23**  Rāma, filled with delight and elation, travelled in that resplendent chariot yoked with swans, and shone like Kubera himself.

**6.111.1**  That supreme chariot, set in motion by Rāma, soared upward like a great cloud lifted by the wind.

**6.111.2**  Then the joy of the Raghu line, Rāma, cast his gaze in every direction and addressed Maithilī Sītā, whose face resembled the moon:

**6.111.3**  "Look, O Vaidehī, upon Laṅkā, standing upon the peak of Trikūṭa whose shape resembles the summit of Kailāsa — built by Viśvakarman.

**6.111.4**  Behold this battlefield, O Sītā — turned to a mire of flesh and blood — the great slaughter-ground of the monkeys and Rākṣasas.

**6.111.5**  For your sake, O large-eyed one, Rāvaṇa was slain by me; Kumbhakarṇa was slain here, and the night-roaming Prahasta as well.

**6.111.6**  Indrajit, son of Rāvaṇa — also called Rāvaṇi — was slain in battle here by Lakṣmaṇa, and also the dreadful-eyed Virūpākṣa, Mahāpārśva, and Mahōdara.

**6.111.7**  Akampana too was slain here, and other powerful Rākṣasas — Triśiras, Atikāya, Devāntaka, and Narāntaka.

**6.111.8**  Here Mandodarī, his wife by name, lamented for him, surrounded by a thousand fellow-wives, all weeping.

**6.111.9**  That ford of the ocean is visible here, O fairest one, where, having crossed the sea, we spent the night.

**6.111.10**  This bridge was built by me across the ocean, the water-flood — for your sake, O large-eyed one — the bridge of Nala, most difficult to construct.

**6.111.11**  Behold the imperturbable ocean, O Vaidehī — the abode of Varuṇa — roaring boundlessly, frequented by conches and oysters.

**6.111.12**  Look, O Maithilī, at that golden mountain, king of mountains, with a golden navel — which rose out of the ocean after splitting it, to give Hanūmat a place to rest.

**6.111.13**  Here came this king of Rākṣasas, Vibhīṣaṇa, to offer himself.

**6.111.14**  That city visible yonder, O Sītā, is Kiṣkindhā, with its colourful woodlands — Sugrīva's charming city, where Vālī was slain by me.

**6.111.15**  That great mountain gleaming like a cloud with lightning is visible yonder, O Sītā — Ṛśyamūka, finest of mountains, clad in golden minerals.

**6.111.16**  Here I met Sugrīva, lord of monkeys, and here, O Sītā, the compact was made by me for the slaying of Vālī.

**6.111.17**  That is the Pampā lake visible yonder, adorned with lotuses and colourful woodlands — where I, bereft of you, lamented in deep anguish.

**6.111.18**  On the bank of this lake I encountered the righteous Śabarī; and here Kabandha — with arms extending a yojana — was slain by me.

**6.111.19**  That splendid forest tree in Janasthāna is visible yonder, O Sītā, O graceful one — the place where the great battle occurred for your sake between the cruel Rāvaṇa and the great-souled Jaṭāyus.

**6.111.20**  There Khara was slain in combat, Dūṣaṇa was laid low, and the mighty Triśiras was felled by me with unerring arrows.

**6.111.21**  That lovely, beautiful leaf-hut is also visible there — the place from which you were violently abducted by Rāvaṇa, lord of Rākṣasas.

**6.111.22**  This is the lovely Godāvarī, with clear, auspicious waters; and this visible here is the hermitage of Agastya — behold it, O Maithilī.

**6.111.23**  The great hermitage of Śarabhaṅga is also visible here, O Vaidehī — the place to which the thousand-eyed Śakra, the smasher of citadels, himself came.

**6.111.24**  These dwelling-places of ascetics are visible yonder, O slender-waisted one — where Atri the patriarch of his lineage shines with the splendour of sun and fire; and here, O Sītā, you encountered the righteous ascetic woman.

**6.111.25**  In this very place the great-bodied Virādha was slain by me.

**6.111.26**  That slender and majestic peak gleaming yonder is Citrakūṭa — the place where the son of Kaikeyī came to placate me.

**6.111.27**  That is the Yamunā visible in the distance, with its picturesque woodlands; and here the splendid hermitage of Bharadvāja gleams before us.

**6.111.28**  This is the Gaṅgā flowing in three streams, visible to you, O fairest of complexion; and this is Śṛṅgaverapura, the place where we met Guha.

**6.111.29**  That visible yonder is Ayodhyā, my father's royal capital — make your obeisance to Ayodhyā, O Vaidehī, for you have returned.

**6.111.30**  Then all those monkeys and the Rākṣasa Vibhīṣaṇa, leaping up again and again, beheld that beautiful city.

**6.111.31**  Then those monkeys beheld the city of Ayodhyā — encircled by white mansions, with its broad enclosures thronged with elephants and horses — even as the gods behold the city of Mahendra, Amarāvatī.

**6.112.1**  At the completion of the fourteenth year plus five nights, the elder brother of Lakṣmaṇa arrived at the hermitage of Bharadvāja and reverently saluted the sage with self-restraint.

**6.112.2**  Having paid homage to Bharadvāja, the treasure of asceticism, he asked him: "O blessed one, do you hear any news of prosperity and health in the city? And is Bharata well? Do my mothers still live?"

**6.112.3**  Thus addressed by Rāma, the great sage Bharadvāja replied to the foremost of the Raghu clan with a smile, appearing joyful.

**6.112.4**  "Bharata, smeared with mud and wearing matted locks, awaits you, having placed your sandals before him in honour; all is well in the household."

**6.112.5**  "You who had entered the great forest wearing bark garments long ago — fallen from the kingdom with a woman as your third companion, devoted solely to righteousness —"

**6.112.6**  "— going on foot, having renounced everything, acting upon your father's command, bereft of the enjoyments of heaven, like an immortal fallen from heaven —"

**6.112.7**  "— seeing you thus, O conqueror in battle, compassion arose in me before, subsisting on forest roots and fruits because of Kaikeyī's command —"

**6.112.8**  "— but now, seeing you who have achieved your full purpose, surrounded by companions and kinsmen, with your enemies conquered, my joy is unsurpassed."

**6.112.9**  "All your joys and sorrows are known to me, O Rāghava — all that you have abundantly endured, beginning with the slaughter at Janasthāna."

**6.112.10**  "Your deployment in the service of the brāhmaṇas, your protection of all the ascetics, the encounter with Mārīca, and the abduction of Sītā —"

**6.112.11**  "— the meeting with Kabandha, your approach to the Pampā lake, your alliance with Sugrīva, and how Vālī was slain by you —"

**6.112.12**  "— the search for the daughter of Videha, the deeds of the son of the wind-god, and upon the discovery of the daughter of Videha, how the bridge of Nala was built, and how Laṅkā was set ablaze by the exultant monkey leaders —"

**6.112.13**  "— and how Rāvaṇa, the tormentor of the gods, was slain in battle along with his sons, kinsmen, ministers, forces, and vehicles —"

**6.112.14**  "— and the meeting with the thirty gods, and the boons granted to you — all of this is known to me by my ascetic power, O beloved of righteousness."

**6.112.15**  "I too grant you a boon here, O best among those who bear weapons — accept this guest-offering; tomorrow you shall go to Ayodhyā."

**6.112.16**  Accepting those words with a bowed head, the glorious son of the king, greatly rejoiced, said "So be it" and then requested a boon.

**6.112.17**  "May all the trees along the road as I travel toward Ayodhyā, O blessed one, bear fruit out of season and drip with honey."

**6.112.18**  Those trees that were fruitless bore fruit; those without flowers bloomed; the dry trees became fully leafed; and all those trees dripped with honey.

**6.113.1**  Gazing toward Ayodhyā, Rāghava fell into reflection; having reflected, he then turned his gaze upon the monkeys.

**6.113.2**  Rāma, the wise and resplendent one, wishing to do what was dear, then spoke to Hanūmān the monkey, swift in his stride, for a cherished purpose.

**6.113.3**  "Go swiftly to Ayodhyā, O best of leaping ones; quickly find out whether the people in the king's palace are well."

**6.113.4**  "Reaching Śṛṅgaverapura, tell Guha who moves in the forest thickets — the lord of the Niṣādas — of my well-being, conveying my words."

**6.113.5**  "Upon hearing that I am well, free from illness and free from anxiety, Guha will be pleased — he is a friend dear to me as my own self."

**6.113.6**  "The pleased lord of the Niṣādas, Guha, will show you the road to Ayodhyā and inform you of the news concerning Bharata."

**6.113.7**  "You must tell Bharata of my well-being in my own words; announce to him that I have accomplished my purpose, along with my wife and together with Lakṣmaṇa."

**6.113.8**  "Tell him also of the abduction of the daughter of Videha by the mighty Rāvaṇa, of my alliance with Sugrīva, and of the slaying of Vālī in battle."

**6.113.9**  "Tell him of the search for the daughter of Mithilā, and how she was found by you after leaping over the great waters of the eternal ocean-lord."

**6.113.10**  "Tell of the approach to the ocean and the sighting of the sea, how the bridge was caused to be built, and how Rāvaṇa was slain."

**6.113.11**  "Tell of the granting of boons by great Indra, by Brahmā, by Varuṇa, and of my meeting with my father through the grace of Mahādeva."

**6.113.12**  "Rāma, having conquered the hosts of enemies and having attained unsurpassed glory, comes having fully accomplished his purpose, with his mighty allies."

**6.113.13**  "Observe what expression Bharata assumes upon hearing this; and you must also ascertain everything regarding him and whatever pertains to me."

**6.113.14**  "All of Bharata's circumstances and gestures must be discerned truly — by his facial colour, his glance, and his manner of speaking."

**6.113.15**  "For whose mind would not be turned by a kingdom filled with every desire, teeming with elephants, horses, and chariots — the realm inherited from father and grandfather?"

**6.113.16**  "It may be that the glorious Bharata, by circumstance, has himself come to desire the kingdom; let the son of the Raghu line rule the entire earth without exception."

**6.113.17**  "Having ascertained his intention and resolve, O monkey, you should return quickly, before we have travelled far."

**6.113.18**  Having thus been instructed, Hanūmān the son of the wind-god, assuming a human form, went swiftly to Ayodhyā.

**6.113.19**  He leapt over the path of the ancestors, the beautiful abode of the lord of serpents, and crossed the fearsome confluence of the Gaṅgā and the Yamunā.

**6.113.20**  Reaching Śṛṅgaverapura and finding Guha, the mighty Hanūmān, filled with joy, spoke these words with auspicious speech:

**6.113.21**  "Your friend Kākutstha Rāma, whose valour is truth, together with Sītā and with the son of Sumitrā, sends you his greetings of well-being."

**6.113.22**  "Having spent tonight, the fifth night, on the word of the sage, with the leave of Bharadvāja, you shall see Rāghava this very day."

**6.113.23**  Having spoken thus, the greatly resplendent one, with his bodily hairs thrilling with delight, the swift one leapt up swiftly and without hesitation.

**6.113.24**  He saw the ford of Rāma, and also the sandy river Vālukinī; he saw too the Gomatī, and the fearsome forest of sāla trees.

**6.113.25**  Having travelled a great distance in haste, that elephant among monkeys came upon the blossoming trees growing near Nandigrāma.

**6.113.26**  At a distance of barely one krośa from Ayodhyā, he saw Bharata, emaciated and sorrowful, dwelling like a forest hermit, wearing bark cloth and black antelope skin.

**6.113.27**  He was wearing matted locks, his body smeared with grime, emaciated by grief over his brother, subsisting on fruits and roots, self-controlled, an ascetic, walking the path of righteousness.

**6.113.28**  He bore a high mass of matted locks, wore garments of bark and antelope skin, was disciplined in conduct, had a cultivated inner self, and possessed a brilliance equal to that of a brahmarṣi.

**6.113.29**  Having placed your sandals before him as regent, he was governing the earth and protecting the world of all four classes from every fear.

**6.113.30**  He was attended by pure ministers, by the court priest, and by the chiefs of the army, all of them disciplined and wearing ochre robes.

**6.113.31**  The citizens, devoted to righteousness, were indeed not willing to let that prince go — he who wore bark cloth and black antelope skin.

**6.113.32**  To him who was like righteousness incarnate, knowing righteousness, like another one accompanied by the gods, Hanūmān the son of the wind-god spoke with joined palms:

**6.113.33**  "The Kākutstha whom you mourn — the one who dwelt in the Daṇḍaka forest wearing bark garments and matted locks — that one sends his greetings of well-being to you."

**6.113.34**  "I announce joyful news to you, O lord — you will cast off your terrible grief; at this very moment you shall be united with your brother Rāma."

**6.113.35**  "Rāma, having slain Rāvaṇa and having recovered the daughter of Mithilā, comes having fully accomplished his purpose, together with his mighty allies."

**6.113.36**  "The greatly resplendent Lakṣmaṇa and the illustrious Vaidehī Sītā come together with Rāma — as Śacī comes with the great Indra."

**6.113.37**  Thus addressed by Hanūmān, Bharata the son of Kaikeyī suddenly fell down in joy; overcome by rapture he fell into a swoon.

**6.113.38**  Then, after a moment, Bharata the Rāghava rose up and, having recovered, spoke these words to Hanūmān who had spoken such cherished news:

**6.113.39**  The glorious Bharata, in his excitement, embraced the monkey and, overjoyed as though freed from sorrow, drenched him with copious drops of tears.

**6.113.40**  "Whether you are a god or a human who has come here out of compassion, O gentle one, I give you whatever you desire as a reward for announcing this cherished news."

**6.113.41**  "A hundred thousand cows, a hundred excellent villages, sixteen wives who are nobly born and well-mannered, adorned with earrings —"

**6.113.42**  "— women of golden complexion, with beautiful noses and thighs, with faces as gentle as the moon, adorned with all ornaments, and endowed with noble birth."

**6.113.43**  Having heard the wonderful account of Rāma's coming from that best of monkeys, the son of the king was filled with delight and with eagerness to see Rāma; and again, from sheer joy, he spoke these words:

**6.114.1**  "For many years now, since my lord went to the great forest, I hear the glad recounting of my lord's deeds with joy."

**6.114.2**  "Auspicious indeed does this popular saying appear to me: joy comes to a man still living, even after a hundred years."

**6.114.3**  "How did the meeting of Rāghava and the monkeys come about? In what region, through reliance on what? Tell me who am asking you this."

**6.114.4**  Being asked by the prince and seated on a cushioned seat, Hanūmān then recounted the full story of Rāma's deeds in the forest.

**6.114.5**  How Rāma was exiled when the boon was granted to your mother; and how King Daśaratha died of grief for his son —

**6.114.6**  How you were quickly summoned by messengers from the royal household, O lord; and how, upon your return to Ayodhyā, you refused to accept the kingdom —

**6.114.7**  How you went to Mount Citrakūṭa and invited your brother who was afflicting enemies with the kingdom; how he practised the conduct of the good —

**6.114.8**  How, steadfast in the king's command, he renounced the kingdom; and how you returned, having taken the sandals of your elder brother —

**6.114.9**  "All of this, O great-armed one, is known to you as it happened; but now hear from me what occurred after your departure."

**6.114.10**  After your departure at that time, they entered the great and deserted Daṇḍaka forest, where the deer and birds were startled and scattered.

**6.114.11**  Before them as they went through the dense forest, Virādha appeared, powerful and roaring with a great roar.

**6.114.12**  Lifting him up while he roared loudly, with his arms raised and his face downward, they hurled him into a pit dug in the ground — like an elephant bellowing.

**6.114.13**  Having accomplished that difficult deed, the two brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, at evening, went to the beautiful hermitage of Śarabhaṅga.

**6.114.14**  When Śarabhaṅga had gone to heaven, Rāma of true valour, having reverenced all the sages, went on to Janasthāna.

**6.114.15**  Fourteen thousand rākṣasas of fearsome deeds were slain while the great-souled Rāghava dwelt there.

**6.114.16**  After that, Śūrpaṇakhā came to Rāma's side; then, directed by Rāma, Lakṣmaṇa swiftly rose up —

**6.114.17**  — and that mighty one, drawing his sword, cut off her ears and nose. Tormented by him, that wretched woman then went to Rāvaṇa.

**6.114.18**  Mārīca, a terrible rākṣasa and follower of Rāvaṇa, enticed the daughter of Videha by becoming a gem-studded deer.

**6.114.19**  She, the daughter of Videha, upon seeing it, said to Rāma: "Capture it — ah, what a beautiful creature it would be in our hermitage, my beloved!"

**6.114.20**  Then Rāma, bow in hand, ran after it as it ran; he struck it as it ran with an arrow with straight joints.

**6.114.21**  Then, O gentle one, when Rāghava had gone after the deer and Lakṣmaṇa too had gone out, the ten-necked one entered the hermitage and seized Sītā by force — as the planet Rāhu seizes Rohiṇī in the sky.

**6.114.22**  Then, after slaying in battle the vulture Jaṭāyu who sought to protect her, Rāvaṇa seized Sītā and swiftly departed at once.

**6.114.23**  Then monkeys of wondrous appearance, standing on the mountain peak, resembling mountains themselves, saw there with amazement Rāvaṇa the lord of rākṣasas, carrying Sītā and departing.

**6.114.24**  Then Rāvaṇa who makes the world cry out entered Laṅkā.

**6.114.25**  Having brought the daughter of Mithilā into that auspicious and great mansion surrounded by gold, Rāvaṇa tried to console her with words.

**6.114.26**  Kākutstha, returning, was filled with anguish when he saw the vulture.

**6.114.27**  Having cremated the slain vulture, his father's dear friend, Rāma, wandering along the Godāvarī and through the flowering forest regions, together with his companion came upon a rākṣasa in the great forest named Kabandha.

**6.114.28**  Then, on Kabandha's counsel, Rāma of true valour went to Mount Ṛśyamūka and met Sugrīva.

**6.114.29**  Their meeting at first was cordial from the heart; through mutual conversation a deep affection grew between those two.

**6.114.30**  Rāma, by the strength of his own arms, restored his kingdom to him, having slain in battle the great-bodied and mighty Vālī.

**6.114.31**  Sugrīva, installed in the kingdom together with all the monkeys, pledged to Rāma the search for the princess.

**6.114.32**  Commanded by Sugrīva, the great-souled lord of the monkeys, ten crores of monkeys were dispatched in all directions.

**6.114.33**  As for us who had gone astray and lost our way, on the Vindhya, best of mountains, deeply afflicted by grief, a great span of time passed by.

**6.114.34**  But Saṃpāti the valiant, the brother of the king of vultures, reported to us the dwelling-place of Sītā in Rāvaṇa's abode.

**6.114.35**  I, removing the sorrow of those kinsmen who were overwhelmed with sorrow, mustering my own strength, leapt a hundred yojanas.

**6.114.36**  There I saw her alone, having entered the Aśoka grove, wearing silken garments that were soiled, joyless but firm in her resolve.

**6.114.37**  Having met with her according to proper form, having inquired of that blameless one about everything, having received the jewel as a token of recognition, I returned with my purpose accomplished.

**6.114.38**  And upon my return, I gave that resplendent great jewel as the token of recognition to Rāma of unwearied action.

**6.114.39**  Hearing of that daughter of Mithilā, he rejoiced and affirmed his will to live — he who had reached the end of life's purpose, like a sick man who has drunk nectar.

**6.114.40**  Rousing his army to exertion, he set his mind on the destruction of Laṅkā — like fire wishing to consume all those worlds.

**6.114.41**  Then reaching the ocean, he caused Nala to build a bridge; the army of heroic monkeys crossed over by that bridge.

**6.114.42**  Nīla slew Prahasta; Rāghava slew Kumbhakarṇa; Lakṣmaṇa slew the son of Rāvaṇa; and Rāma himself slew Rāvaṇa.

**6.114.43**  Having met with Śakra, with Yama, with Varuṇa, and with the divine sages, Kākutstha the scorcher of enemies received boons.

**6.114.44**  He, the recipient of those boons, with gladness and accompanied by the monkeys, came to Kiṣkindhā by the aerial chariot Puṣpaka.

**6.114.45**  Him — who is now again on the Gaṅgā, dwelling in the proximity of the sage, under the auspicious Puṣya conjunction without impediment — you shall see tomorrow.

**6.114.46**  Then he, Bharata, having heard the great and true words of Hanūmān, rejoiced with joined palms and spoke words that delighted his heart: "My heart's desire, long cherished, is now at last fulfilled."

**6.115.1**  Hearing the supreme joy, Bharata of true valor, filled with delight, commanded Śatrughna, the slayer of enemy heroes.

**6.115.2**  "Let all the deities and the sacred shrines of the city be worshipped with fragrant garlands and musical instruments by pure men."

**6.115.3**  "Let the royal ladies, the ministers, the soldiers and the women of the army companies go forth to behold Rāma's face, which resembles the moon."

**6.115.4**  Hearing Bharata's words, the mighty Śatrughna, slayer of enemy heroes, dispatched many thousands of laborers.

**6.115.5**  "Level the low places and the uneven ground; clear away the obstacles on the road beyond Nandigrāma."

**6.115.6**  "Sprinkle the entire earth with water cool as snow." Then others scattered parched grain and flowers on all sides.

**6.115.7**  "Let the streets of this finest city be adorned with raised banners, and let the dwellings be decorated before the rising of the sun."

**6.115.8**  "Let hundreds of men strew the royal road, unobstructed, with garlands and loose flowers of five colors and sweet fragrances."

**6.115.9**  With thousands of rutting elephants adorned with gold ornaments, and with female elephants in golden girths, and with great chariot-warriors in chariots, they went forth in swift haste.
